Motivation: Conventional multi-task networks rely on fixed sharing structures or eager expansion, which limits adaptability to varying task demands. There is a need for data-driven, gradual growth that activates a second pathway only when justified by optimization signals, without incurring unnecessary overhead.

Method: EMAN uses latent relative phases to induce an antisymmetric growth direction without instantiating a second computational path. It monitors multiple decision signals during training to convert local optimization evidence into a structural decision, materializing two equal-capacity independent paths only after certification. The framework allocates shared and task-specific capacity adaptively to match task requirements.

Result: Empirical validation on controlled rank settings, PASCAL-Context, and NYUv2 shows improved performance with competitive computational cost, indicating effective emergent modular growth and adaptive capacity allocation.

Conclusion: EMAN demonstrates a principled, optimization-driven route to emergent modular architectures in multi-task learning, allowing gradual, evidence-driven growth of an independent path and adaptive sharing that improves performance while controlling cost; the approach offers generality to other multi-task settings and data-driven architectural design.

Abstract: Existing multi-task learning methods rely on hard sharing, multiple paths or experts, adaptive sharing, and dynamic expansion. However, their capacity changes are usually constrained by predefined structures or triggered by task boundaries and conflict signals. This raises a fundamental question: can a network start from exact single-path computation and grow a new independent path only when persistent optimization evidence appears? We propose the Emergent Modular Atomic Network (EMAN), an optimization-driven framework for exposing an antisymmetric growth direction through latent relative phases without instantiating a second path, and for monitoring multiple decision signals during training to transform local optimization evidence into a structural decision. EMAN materializes two equal-capacity independent paths only after certification. EMAN adaptively allocates shared and task-specific representation capacity to accommodate varying task requirements. Extensive experiments on controlled rank settings, PASCAL-Context, and NYUv2 validate its effectiveness, achieving improved performance at a competitive computational cost.

</details>


### [97] [SW-ProxyCE: Zero-Query Adversarial Transfer from Public EEG Encoders to Private Downstream Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16931)
*Linhua Cong,Dingkun Liu,Dongrui Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Shrinkage-Whitened Proxy Cross-Entropy (SW-ProxyCE) to perform query-free, task-aware adversarial attacks on EEG foundation models with public encoders; demonstrates transferable adversarial examples to private downstream models and outperforms task-agnostic attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public EEG foundation encoders create a security risk by enabling attackers with limited resources to craft transferable adversarial examples against private downstream models; understanding and mitigating this risk is crucial for deployment.

Method: SW-ProxyCE recovers task-level decision geometry from a small labeled reference set using shrinkage-whitened class prototypes, enabling adversarial generation without training a surrogate classifier. The approach is query-free and applicable to both linear-probing and full-fine-tuning downstream models, evaluated across three EEG tasks with multiple foundation encoders and a paradigm-specific pre-trained encoder in cross-subject and within-subject settings.

Result: Adversarial examples generated from the public encoder and limited references effectively transfer to inaccessible downstream models; SW-ProxyCE outperforms task-agnostic representation-shift attacks, indicating strong transferability of EEG foundation models and limited inherent adversarial robustness.

Conclusion: EEG foundation models exhibit strong transferability that does not ensure adversarial robustness; defenses are needed for deployment of public encoders; code will be released on GitHub.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) foundation models have recently emerged as a promising paradigm for EEG decoding by learning reusable representations from large-scale heterogeneous neural recordings. However, the open release of EEG foundation encoders, while facilitating downstream developments, also introduces a previously unexplored security risk: publicly available representations may make private downstream models vulnerable. This paper investigates adversarial transfer attacks in EEG foundation model deployment in a public-encoder and private-downstream setting, where attackers have white-box access to a released encoder and a small task-matched labeled reference set, but no access or query to victim parameters, outputs, or gradients. We propose Shrinkage-Whitened Proxy Cross-Entropy (SW-ProxyCE), a query-free task-aware attack framework that recovers task-level decision geometry from a small labeled reference set through shrinkage-whitened class prototypes, enabling transferable adversarial generation without training an additional surrogate classifier. We evaluated SW-ProxyCE across three EEG tasks using three general-purpose foundation encoders and a paradigm-specific pre-trained encoder, covering both linear-probing and full-fine-tuning downstream models in cross-subject and within-subject scenarios. Results demonstrated that adversarial examples generated from the public encoder and limited labeled references can effectively transfer to inaccessible downstream models. SW-ProxyCE consistently outperformed task-agnostic representation-shift attacks, revealing that the strong transferability of EEG foundation models does not necessarily lead to adversarial robustness. Our code will be available on GitHub.

</details>


### [98] [DOW-KE: Anchor-Free Multi-Layer Knowledge Editing via Direct End-to-End Weight Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16932)
*Ran Chen,Junbo Zhang,Qianli Zhou,Xinyang Deng,Wen Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DOW-KE: an end-to-end, anchor-free knowledge-editing method that backpropagates through the full model to jointly optimize all edited layers, addressing propagation and closure gaps and improving Score and neighborhood specificity across model-dataset settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-layer locate-then-edit approaches optimize target residual activations (anchors) but do not optimize the joint effect of edits as deployed, leaving a gap between anchor targets and realized edits due to propagation attenuation/distortion.

Method: Backpropagate the final editing objective through the entire model and jointly optimize updates across all edited layers; embed the preservation projection inside the update parameterization within the computation graph so every gradient acts on the deployed update; anchor-free design ensures optimization aligns with deployment and enforces safety constraints.

Result: In large-scale sequential editing across two datasets and three models, DOW-KE achieves the highest overall Score and neighborhood Specificity in five of six model-dataset settings compared with evaluated baselines.

Conclusion: Anchor-free, end-to-end optimization closes the gap between what is optimized and what is deployed, improving editing efficacy while maintaining preservation constraints; results indicate strong, though not universal, gains across settings.

Abstract: Multi-layer locate-then-edit methods for knowledge editing first optimize target residual-stream activations (anchors) at selected layers, then realize them layer by layer as weight updates. This pipeline optimizes an intermediate representation but deploys multi-layer weight updates whose joint effect through the true forward pass is never itself optimized: regardless of how anchors are set or propagated, each update comes from a local solve, so propagation-induced attenuation and distortion go uncorrected, leaving a closure gap between anchor targets and realized edits. We propose DOW-KE, an anchor-free method built on a single principle: what is optimized must be exactly what is deployed. DOW-KE backpropagates the final editing objective through the complete model, jointly optimizing the updates of all edited layers so cross-layer propagation and coupling enter every gradient step. The same principle dictates where preservation resides: embedding the preservation projection in the update parameterization, inside the computation graph, makes every gradient act on the deployed update; post-hoc constraints would reopen the gap, and the constrained search keeps edits clear of protected knowledge. In large-scale sequential editing on two datasets and three models, DOW-KE achieves the highest overall Score and neighborhood Specificity in five of six model-dataset settings among the evaluated baselines.

</details>


### [99] [Study-Strategy Clusters from EdNet Logs Track Engagement, Not Mastery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16963)
*Qingchuan Lyu,Yingxin Li,Albert Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Unsupervised ITS-use–pattern clustering yields a hierarchy of learner-style strategies; early clusters predict engagement but not late unassisted accuracy; behavior styles offer limited predictive value for knowledge gains; knowledge tracing shows only modest gains from style signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether unsupervised learner-type clusters derived from ITS logs predict later engagement and knowledge gains, and whether these clusters align with or add to knowledge-tracing signals.

Method: Cluster EdNet-KT3 study-strategy features (resource use, revision, video, problem practice) for 5,000 active learners using silhouette to select k=5, yielding four contrast poles (reading-focused, video-heavy, revision-heavy, problem-first) plus a large near-mean residual. Reclustering the residual produces eight finer styles. Timeline split by respond count so clusters use early half and outcomes late half. Evaluate early cluster predictiveness on late engagement (persistence, completion) and late unassisted accuracy; compare to volume-based clustering (ARI). Apply SAKT to seven TOEIC sections to predict next correctness vs a baseline of section difficulty; measure ARI between mastery signal and behavior styles; report AUC lift.

Result: Early clusters predict later engagement with sizeable effect sizes (persistence eta^2 ≈ 0.106; completion eta^2 ≈ 0.021). Late unassisted accuracy shows only weak, non-significant improvement (p_adj ≈ 0.093). Volume-based clustering yields ARI ≈ 0.064, i.e., weak alignment with strategy labels. SAKT yields modest AUC lift of +0.051 (CI [0.045, 0.058]); mastery signal is nearly independent of behavior styles (ARI ≈ 0.007).

Conclusion: Behavioral clustering captures study styles and engagement patterns, but provides limited insight into or prediction of knowledge gains; knowledge-tracing performance is largely independent of these behavior-derived styles.

Abstract: Learning analytics often treats unsupervised clusters of intelligent tutoring system (ITS) logs as learner types that should predict learning. We test that assumption on EdNet-KT3. Clustering study-strategy features (resource use, revision, video, problem practice) for 5{,}000 active learners yields a silhouette-selected parent cut ($k=5$) with 4 contrast poles (reading-focused, video-heavy, revision-heavy, and problem-first) plus a large near-mean residual ($\sim$64.9\%). Reclustering that residual adds four finer styles, giving a bootstrap-stable hierarchy of 8 named strategies. We split each learner's timeline by respond count so clusters use only the early half and outcomes only the late half. Early clusters predict later engagement (continuing to practice and finishing late sessions, especially persistence, $η^{2}\approx 0.106$; completion $η^{2}\approx 0.021$) but not later unassisted accuracy (correctness on late first-attempts without help; $p_{\mathrm{adj}}\approx 0.093$). Volume rises with some styles, yet volume-only clustering barely matches strategy labels (ARI$=0.064$). A knowledge-tracing model (SAKT) on the seven TOEIC exam sections predicts next correctness only modestly better than a baseline that knows only how hard each section usually is (AUC lift $+0.051$; CI $[+0.045,+0.058]$), and that mastery signal is nearly independent of behavior styles (ARI$=0.007$). Behavioral clustering here describes study styles and engagement, not knowledge gains.

</details>


### [100] [RoBell-RVFL: A Robust Generalized Bell Random Vector Functional Link Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16965)
*A. Rahaman,A. Quadir,M. Tanveer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A robust, quality-aware RVFL variant (RoBell-RVFL) for imbalanced and noisy data using minority-preserving unit weights and a probability-weighted gbell kernel weighting, preserving efficiency with closed-form learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Class imbalance and label noise bias decision boundaries; existing methods neglect intra-class distribution and are vulnerable to noise; need a lightweight, robust alternative.

Method: RoBell-RVFL with dual strategy: minority samples given unit weight; majority samples weighted by probability-weighted generalized bell membership in kernel-induced feature space; integrates local class probability and distribution; retains closed-form learning of RVFL.

Result: Extensive experiments on UCI and KEEL; robustness to up to 40% label noise; outperforms recent state-of-the-art RVFL variants.

Conclusion: Adaptive, quality-aware sample weighting is crucial for robust RVFL learning; global weighting schemes are ineffective in noisy, imbalanced contexts.

Abstract: The dominance of majority classes in real-world datasets poses a fundamental challenge to randomized neural networks, often biasing decision boundaries and overlooking critical minority samples. Existing remedies, such as synthetic minority over-sampling (SMOTE) and class-weighted loss functions, primarily address class proportions while neglecting intra-class distribution, making them vulnerable to label noise and outliers. In this paper, we propose \textbf{RoBell-RVFL}, a robust and lightweight \emph{quality-aware} generalized bell random vector functional link network that redefines how randomized models handle class imbalance and noisy data. RoBell-RVFL employs a dual-strategy, sample-level weighting mechanism that strictly preserves minority class information using unit weights, while adaptively regulating the influence of majority class samples through a probability-weighted generalized bell (gbell) membership function in a kernel-induced feature space. This design effectively suppresses noisy, boundary, and outlier samples within the majority class, enabling the network to learn from informative samples rather than merely abundant ones. By explicitly incorporating local class probability and class distribution information into the learning process, RoBell-RVFL achieves adaptive control over sample contributions without sacrificing the closed-form learning efficiency of RVFL networks. Extensive evaluations on UCI and KEEL benchmark datasets, along with robustness tests under up to 40\% label noise, demonstrate that RoBell-RVFL consistently and significantly outperforms recent state-of-the-art RVFL variants. The results indicate that adaptive, quality-aware sample weighting is essential for robust RVFL learning, rendering conventional global weighting schemes ineffective in noisy and imbalanced environments.

</details>


### [101] [MultiSigBERT: Beyond Survival Analysis through Multimodal and Sequential Modeling in Oncology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16972)
*Paul Minchella,Stéphane Chrétien,Guillaume Metzler,Loïc Verlingue,Rémi Vaucher*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A multimodal sequential survival model (MultiSigBERT) leverages path signature representations to integrate narrative text, structured data, and temporal trajectories for oncology survival prediction, achieving competitive concordance on real-world data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve survival analysis by jointly modeling heterogeneous EHR modalities (free-text, structured vars) and temporal dynamics, addressing the limitations of single-modality and non-temporal models.

Method: Convert free-text to sentence embeddings; apply modality-specific PCA; concatenate with structured covariates to form joint temporal trajectories; apply the Signature transform from Rough Paths to capture higher-order temporal interactions; use resulting high-dimensional Signature features in a LASSO-regularized Cox model to estimate risk.

Result: Evaluated on a real-world oncology cohort from Léon Bérard Center with >120k reports and >2,500 patients; concordance index 0.743 (sd 0.029) on an independent test set.

Conclusion: Multimodal temporal dynamics and geometric structure improve survival prediction, as demonstrated by the reported CI and the integration framework.

Abstract: Machine learning has become an essential component of modern healthcare, where the integration of heterogeneous data sources offers unprecedented opportunities to improve clinical decision-making. Electronic Health Records (EHR) contain complementary information -- including narrative clinical reports, numerical measurements, and structured variables -- yet most survival models remain limited to a single modality or fail to exploit the temporal nature of patient trajectories. We propose MultiSigBERT, a unified framework for multimodal sequential survival modeling in oncology based on path signature representations. Here, narrative medical reports (free-text) are converted into sentence embeddings by extracting and averaging contextual word embeddings. These representations are then compressed via modality-specific PCA and concatenated with structured covariates to form joint temporal trajectories which are then encoded using the Signature transform, a tool from Rough Paths theory that efficiently captures higher-order temporal interactions across modalities without supervision needed. The computed Signature features are finally incorporated as high dimensional features into a LASSO-regularized Cox model to estimate individualized risk scores. The performance of our novel MultiSigBERT pipeline is illustrated on the analysis of a real-world oncology cohort from the Léon Bérard Center, comprising over 120,000 medical reports and structured records from more than 2,500 patients. The model achieves a concordance index of 0.743 (sd 0.029) on an independent test set, demonstrating the benefit of jointly modeling multimodal temporal dynamics together with patient-level geometric structure for survival prediction.

</details>


### [102] [Position: Fairness Failure in Generative Models is an Evaluation Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16974)
*Mariia Vladimirova,Jean-Yves Franceschi,Thibaut Issenhuth*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Fairness Cards to standardize and document fairness evaluation in generative models, enabling reproducibility and comparability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current fairness evaluations are inconsistent across papers and not readily actionable for deployment, hindering fair and responsible use of generative models.

Method: Diagnose recurring empirical and conceptual failure modes in current evaluation practice and advocate a shift from ad-hoc bias checks to standardized, generative-specific evaluation; introduce Fairness Cards that document prompt families, counterfactual protocols, metrics, and refusal handling.

Result: Conceptual framework and design of Fairness Cards as a minimal reporting artifact to enable reproducibility, comparability, and accountability in fairness assessments of generative models; project page provided.

Conclusion: Calls for a paradigm shift in evaluation standards toward standardized reporting and actionable evaluation practices, with recommendations to adopt Fairness Cards more broadly.

Abstract: Despite groundbreaking advancements in generative models during the last decade, concerns about their lack of fairness, reinforcing societal inequalities and harming marginalized groups, remain under-addressed and difficult to act upon. This position paper argues that fairness failures in generative models, albeit driven by multiple factors, are ultimately stemming from an evaluation problem: fairness findings are rarely comparable across papers or actionable for deployment decisions. This paper diagnoses recurring empirical and conceptual failure modes in current practice and motivates a shift from ad-hoc bias checks to standardized, generative-specific evaluation. We propose Fairness Cards as a minimal reporting artifact that makes evaluation choices explicit (prompt families, counterfactual protocols, metrics, and refusal handling) enabling reproducibility, comparability, and accountability. We conclude with additional recommendations towards a paradigm shift in evaluation standards. Our project page can be found at https://mariiavladimirova.github.io/fairness-cards .

</details>


### [103] [Agents unlock new capabilities through Switching LoRA Adapters as a Tool (SLAaaT)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17034)
*Kenneth Ge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method to avoid forgetting by switching between LoRA adapters mid-trace, enabling long-horizon agents to combine multiple specialized skills with up to 18x reduction in capability tax and better performance than subagents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prevent catastrophic forgetting when fine-tuning models for multiple tasks; enable long agent trajectories to compose diverse capabilities without tradeoffs.

Method: Propose a mid-trace adapter-switching mechanism. Use two synthetic coding tasks requiring specialization. Evaluate autonomous switching, new strategies, baseline comparisons.

Result: Model can solve tasks previously unsolved; autonomous switching; new strategy beating human heuristic baseline; up to 18x reduction in capability tax; outperforms subagents in capabilities and token usage.

Conclusion: Mid-trace adapter switching is effective for multi-capability agents; reduces forgetting cost and improves efficiency compared to single adapter or subagent approach.

Abstract: Post-training can unlock new capabilities and improve performance on specialized tasks, but sometimes at the cost of catastrophic forgetting in other domains. This poses a problem in long agent trajectories that compose different capabilities. We reject this tradeoff by giving an agent a tool to switch between specialized LoRA adapters mid-trace. To test its effectiveness, we compose two synthetic coding tasks that are logically simple but require specialization. We find that this allows the model to solve problems it previously could not, that the model is able to switch autonomously (and find a new strategy that beats our human heuristic baseline on one task), and that this incurs an up to an 18x reduction in capability tax compared to an agent using only one specialized adapter. Our approach also substantially outperforms spawning subagents in both task capabilities and token usage.

</details>


### [104] [J-Miner: Recovering Executable Decision Knowledge from Language-Model Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17063)
*Yunfan Gao,Xinyi Huang,Tao Sheng,Haorui Song,Yun Xiong,Haofen Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: J-Miner extracts explicit, executable decision rules from fine-tuned classifiers by mining internal signals across layers and token positions, enabling inspection, validation, and reuse beyond the source model. It yields high fidelity to the original decisions and transfers to lightweight students with minimal parameter counts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuned classifiers typically expose only final labels, leaving decision knowledge implicit. There is a need to extract and encode this latent decision knowledge into an inspectable, portable representation for validation, reuse, and transfer to smaller models.

Method: Aggregate vocabulary-aligned internal signals across layers and token positions to identify text-level named concepts. Use the classifier's own predictions to learn executable decision rules over these concepts, distilling local internal readouts into an explicit classifier-level knowledge representation (J-Miner).

Result: J-Miner rules reproduce up to 98.3% of source-classifier decisions and achieve 6.0–29.5 percentage points higher behavioral fidelity than rules learned from input words. Named concepts align with internal semantic evidence used in decisions, and learned rules consolidate signals into inspectable decision structures. The knowledge transfers to lightweight standalone students with about 1/24 the parameters, preserving 99.8% of the source mean task accuracy.

Conclusion: Task-specific decision knowledge can be faithfully represented in an explicit, executable form and reused beyond the original classifier.

Abstract: Large language models can be fine-tuned into specialized classifiers that perform well across diverse text tasks and make complex judgments, but they typically expose only final labels, leaving the decision knowledge acquired through fine-tuning implicit within the model. We study how to mine this internal decision knowledge from a fine-tuned classifier and encode it in an executable representation that can be inspected, validated, and reused beyond the source classifier. We introduce J-Miner, which mines text-level named concepts by aggregating vocabulary-aligned internal signals across layers and token positions, and uses the classifier's own predictions to learn executable decision rules over them. This process distills local internal readouts into an explicit classifier-level knowledge representation. Across multiple classification tasks, J-Miner rules reproduce up to 98.3\% of source-classifier decisions and achieve 6.0--29.5 percentage points higher behavioral fidelity than equally compact rules learned from input words. Further analysis shows that the named concepts reflect internal semantic evidence associated with task decisions, while the learned rules consolidate these distributed signals into inspectable decision structures. The resulting decision knowledge also transfers to lightweight standalone students: using about 1/24 as many parameters as the source classifiers, they reconstruct and execute the representation from raw text while retaining 99.8\% of the source classifiers' mean task accuracy. These findings show that task-specific decision knowledge can be faithfully represented in an explicit, executable form and reused beyond the classifier in which it was learned.

</details>


### [105] [Certified but Private: Scalable Zero-Knowledge Proofs for Neural Network Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17070)
*Youwei Zhong,Ben Merbaum,Timos Antonopoulos,Ning Luo,Charalampos Papamanthou,Katerina Sotiraki,Ruzica Piskac*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PANDA enables zero-knowledge proofs to certify robustness and fairness of neural networks without revealing private parameters, built atop CROWN. It introduces a lightweight, polynomial-time algorithm for linear relaxation bounds, enabling local robustness proofs for very large networks (∼2.9M params) in minutes with fast verification, outperforming prior ZKP approaches by several orders of magnitude in scalability and prover overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In safety-critical and legal contexts, formal guarantees about ML models are essential, but parameters are often trade-secret and cannot be disclosed to auditors. ZKPs offer a way to prove properties without revealing the model. Existing ZKP-based robustness methods are computationally prohibitive, limiting practicality.

Method: PANDA integrates a novel algorithm for proving linear relaxation bounds for nonlinear activation layers within the CROWN robustness certification framework, producing simple, lightweight ZK proofs. It achieves polynomial-time scaling in the number of neurons, enabling large networks to be certified locally.

Result: The system can generate local-robustness proofs for networks with more than 2.9 million parameters in about 5 minutes and verify them in about 10 seconds, with polynomial scaling in network size and orders-of-magnitude improvement in prover overhead compared to prior ZKP approaches.

Conclusion: PANDA demonstrates the feasibility of scalable, privacy-preserving robustness and fairness certification for large neural networks using ZKPs, dramatically expanding practical applicability of formal guarantees in settings where model secrecy is required.

Abstract: With the growing deployment of machine learning models, formal guarantees of the robustness and fairness of these models have become increasingly important in safety-critical and legal-compliance settings. However, model parameters are often commercial secrets that cannot be disclosed to auditors or end users. To this end, we present PANDA, a scalable system that uses zero-knowledge proofs (ZKPs) to prove the robustness and fairness properties of a model without revealing its private parameters. PANDA is built on top of CROWN, an efficient robustness certification framework that is used in many state-of-the-art formal verification tools for neural networks. The core contribution of PANDA is a novel algorithm for proving linear relaxation bounds for non-linear activation layers, yielding simple, lightweight proofs. Remarkably, our system can generate proofs of local robustness for neural networks with more than 2.9M parameters in 5 minutes, and can verify them in 10 seconds. Prior ZKP-based robustness system rely on exponential-time algorithms that cannot scale to nontrivial networks. In contrast, PANDA scales polynomially in the number of neurons in a network, allowing us to support neural networks 4 orders of magnitude larger than previous approaches with significantly reduced prover overhead.

</details>


### [106] [Dynamic Regime-Aware Conformal Calibration for Reliable Economic Forecast Intervals under Multiple Distribution Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17079)
*Bogdan Oancea*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DRACP delivers a dynamic, regime-aware conformal prediction framework that remains calibrated under covariate shift by combining density-ratio weighting, localized kernels, and online regime-aware weights with a self-tuning significance controller; it achieves near-nominal coverage and robust calibration, but with looser intervals compared to some online CP baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Replace exchangeability-based conformal prediction in economic forecasting where covariate shift and regime changes violate exchangeability, by a framework that preserves coverage and calibration under nonstationarity.

Method: A unified weighted conformal calibration framework incorporating density-ratio weighting, localized kernel estimates, probabilistic regime-aware weights, and a self-tuning online significance controller; provides finite-sample validity under oracle weights, a coverage-gap bound for estimated weights, and deterministic/regret guarantees for the online controller.

Result: Empirical evaluation on 48 forecasting series across euro-area, EU-27 HICP, US macroeconomic and energy indicators, and daily financial series shows DRACP achieves coverage close to 0.90 (0.890) with no series dropping below 0.80, outperforming in calibration versus baselines; however, the method is not the most interval-efficient, with strongly-adaptive online CP delivering narrower intervals and best interval scores; DRACP shows strongest robustness during the 2021-2023 inflation surge; ablation indicates the online controller and conditional-scale normalization drive most gains while weighting components contribute modestly.

Conclusion: DRACP provides a principled calibration-then-coverage approach under covariate shift, offering a reliable calibration backbone when intervals must meet coverage standards, at the cost of reduced efficiency relative to the narrowest-existing methods; future work could target efficiency gains and deeper analysis of weighting components.

Abstract: Conformal prediction provides distribution-free prediction intervals but relies on exchangeability, an assumption often violated in economic forecasting because of covariate shift, concept drift, local heterogeneity and latent regimes. We propose Dynamic Regime-Aware Conformal Prediction (DRACP), which combines density-ratio, localized kernel and probabilistic regime-aware weighting with a self-tuning online significance controller in a unified weighted conformal calibration framework. We distinguish three theoretical results: finite-sample validity under oracle importance weights, a coverage-gap bound for estimated weights with rates in effective sample size, and deterministic or regret guarantees for the online controller. We evaluate DRACP against six baselines on 48 real forecasting series covering euro-area and EU-27 HICP inflation, US macroeconomic and energy indicators, and daily financial series. Recent online methods (FACI, strongly-adaptive online conformal prediction and conformal PID) were verified against the authors' implementations. DRACP is not the most efficient method: strongly-adaptive online conformal prediction achieves the best interval score and intervals about 20% narrower. Instead, DRACP provides the most reliable calibration, achieving coverage closest to the nominal 0.90 (0.890), never falling below 0.80 on any series, maintaining the best coverage at all forecast horizons, and performing best during the 2021-2023 inflation surge. The strongly-adaptive method undercovers on 20 of 48 series versus 10 for DRACP. DRACP therefore offers a principled trade-off between calibration and efficiency, favoring reliable coverage when prediction intervals must satisfy coverage standards. An ablation study shows that the online controller and conditional-scale normalization provide most of the performance gain, whereas the weighting components make a smaller contribution.

</details>


### [107] [Backward through Time, Algebraically](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17087)
*Konstantinos Kogkalidis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces an algebra-generic, differentiable evaluation engine for linear temporal logic (LTL) semantics, implemented in PyTorch telos, avoiding fixed shallow-embedding semantics by providing an executable specification of multiple algebras and auditing their forward/backward behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete-valued LTL semantics are ill-suited for softly-valued systems (neural policies, adaptive controllers, etc.). Differentiability is essential for training, but existing implementations lock into a single semantic algebra—this work seeks a flexible, differentiable, algebra-agnostic framework.

Method: Develop an evaluation engine that is algebra-generic and differentiable. Provide an executable specification of acceptable algebras. Implement and audit a variety of algebras for both forward evaluation and backward differentiation. Package and integrate within PyTorch as the telos library (repo: https://github.com/konstantinosKokos/telos).

Result: A flexible framework where each algebra embodies a specific trade-off in interpreting LTL (satisfaction, disappointment directions). The engine supports differentiable semantics across multiple algebras and can be extended with new ones; concrete implementations and tests are provided within telos.

Conclusion: The work delivers a principled, extensible path to differentiable LTL semantics—algebras encode design choices about how to relax or approximate satisfaction, enabling training of soft temporal logic in neural systems. It shifts from fixed shallow embeddings to a generic, auditable, differentiable semantic engine.

Abstract: Linear temporal logic is a modal extension of propositional logic that allows one to state how a system should behave over time. Its canonical domain is the booleans, but discretely-valued judgements are of little use in steering softly-valued systems (neural policies, adaptive controllers, sequence models, etc). In such cases, the goal formula's (dis)satisfaction becomes a training signal, and differentiability becomes a prime concern. Candidate differentiable semantics abound, but navigating them is tricky. Implementations, where available, are shallow embeddings, demanding an upfront commitment to a single semantic algebra and its (usually implicit) conduct. The paper casts the reader as a functional programmer asked to come to terms with this predicament, and refusing. Out of that refusal comes an evaluation engine that is algebra-generic and amenable to differentiation, together with an executable specification of the algebras it can accept. Various algebras are implemented and audited for their behavior, both forward and backward. Each algebra turns out to be a choice of which direction to disappoint, and how. Everything described (and more) is part of the PyTorch library telos, to be found at https://github.com/konstantinosKokos/telos.

</details>


### [108] [Deep Learning for Cross-Border Electricity Price Forecasting: A Comparative Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17091)
*Hadeer Elashhab,Sai Srijan Papineni,Marvin Dorn,Veit Hagenmeyer,Benjamin Schäfer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a reproducible benchmark for day-ahead electricity price forecasting using public data across markets, comparing six DL models and examining few-shot regimes, showing N-HiTS/NBEATSx strong in low-data settings, with transformers competitive but requiring tuning; emphasizes feature selection and hyperparameters, and notes small differences between top models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the lack of standardized benchmark datasets in EPF, enabling consistent cross-market evaluation and reproducibility.

Method: Standardized public dataset for Germany-Luxembourg 2024; six DL models spanning state-space, MLP, RNN, and Transformer; zero-shot, one-shot, few-shot simulations; calendar, price, and market features; systematic hyperparameter tuning and feature selection.

Result: N-HiTS and NBEATSx excel in low-data regimes; transformer models can achieve comparable accuracy but need more adaptation; performance improves with careful feature engineering and tuning; differences among top models are often small.

Conclusion: Proposes a reproducible evaluation framework for EPF, facilitating cross-market generalization and progress tracking; highlights practical considerations (data regime, features, tuning) that influence model rankings.

Abstract: While publicly available electricity market data presents a valuable resource for forecasting research, the field lacks established benchmark datasets for standardized comparison. As a result, many studies have relied on different datasets and metrics to evaluate methods in isolated settings, making it difficult to assess progress and compare state-of-the-art approaches consistently. In this work, we use public data to evaluate deep learning models for electricity price forecasting (EPF) across multiple market settings. Our goal is to establish a reproducible framework that enables a consistent evaluation of forecasting models. Although deep learning has been explored for day-ahead EPF, many prior studies are limited to single-market settings, narrow feature sets, or fixed training regimes. This work presents a comparative evaluation of six deep learning models--covering state-space, MLP, RNN, and Transformer-based architectures--emphasizing generalization across markets. We simulate low-data target-market conditions using zero-shot, one-shot, and few-shot learning. Our test set focuses on the Germany-Luxembourg (DE-LU) bidding zone in 2024 using a standardized dataset with calendar, historical price, and market-derived features. Our findings suggest that N-HiTS and NBEATSx perform competitively in limited-data scenarios, while transformer-based models can reach comparable accuracy but tend to require more adaptation and tuning. Model performance also benefits from careful feature selection and hyperparameter tuning, and we note that the differences between the strongest models are often small.

</details>


### [109] [From Abductive Explanations to Global Logical Rules for Node Classification in SGCs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17103)
*Bryan Lima Cavalcante,Thiago Alves Rocha*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Minimal abductive explanations used to derive compact global rules for SGC-based node classification via decision trees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce redundancy in explanation subgraphs that are node-specific and improve generalization by extracting global logical rules from minimal explanations rather than from individual subgraphs.

Method: For each node, compute a minimal set of node-feature pairs sufficient to preserve the predicted class; train decision trees on these explanations; extract global logical rules from the trees.

Result: Experiments show compact global rules that faithfully reproduce the SGC model (high fidelity) on benchmark datasets.

Conclusion: Abductive explanation-based rule extraction yields interpretable, generalizable global rules for GNN node classification by leveraging minimal explanations and tree-based rule extraction.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have achieved remarkable performance in node classification tasks, motivating growing interest in methods capable of explaining their predictions. Recent logic-based approaches, such as LogicXGNN, derive global logical rules for Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) from collections of explanatory subgraphs. While informative, these subgraphs may contain redundant structural information that is specific to individual nodes, potentially limiting the generality of the extracted rules. In this work, we propose a logic-based framework for node classification in Simple Graph Convolution (SGC) networks that uses minimal abductive explanations as an intermediate representation for rule extraction. For each node, we compute a minimal set of node-feature pairs sufficient to preserve the predicted class. These explanations are then used to train decision trees from which global logical rules are extracted. Experiments on benchmark datasets show that the proposed framework produces compact global rules while maintaining high fidelity to the original SGC model.

</details>


### [110] [Causal Discovery in Equal Variance Linear Gaussian DAGs via SURE-Tuned Ridge Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17132)
*Sambit Mishra,Urbashi Mitra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Non-iterative, closed-form estimator (SURE-Ridge) for equal-variance linear Gaussian SEM DAG recovery; parallel node-wise regressions with SURE-selected regularization and adaptive thresholding, yielding strong small-sample accuracy and fast runtimes versus NOTEARS, DAGMA, GBNSL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address sample-limited and compute-limited regimes in causal DAG discovery from observational data, where iterative gradient-based methods and per-problem hyperparameter tuning are inefficient.

Method: SURE-Ridge performs parallel node-wise regressions with regularization parameters chosen adaptively via Stein's unbiased risk estimate (SURE); employs adaptive thresholding on the soft adjacency matrix to extract a DAG.

Result: Numerical experiments show lowest structural Hamming distance in the small-sample regime and the lowest run time across tested sample sizes compared with NOTEARS, DAGMA, GBNSL baselines.

Conclusion: SURE-Ridge offers an effective, scalable solution for equal-variance linear Gaussian SEM DAG recovery, excelling in low-sample and compute-constrained settings.

Abstract: Recovering the directed acyclic graph (DAG) of a structural equation model (SEM) from observational data is a central problem in causal discovery. The iterative gradient descent and per-problem hyperparameter tuning of continuous-optimization methods are poorly suited to two practically important regimes: the sample-limited regime, where the number of samples is comparable to or smaller than the number of nodes in the DAG, and the compute-limited regime. This work proposes SURE-Ridge, a non-iterative, closed-form estimator for equal variance linear Gaussian SEM. The method performs parallel node-wise regressions with regularization parameters chosen adaptively by Stein's unbiased risk estimate (SURE), and applies an adaptive thresholding procedure to extract a DAG from the resulting soft adjacency matrix. Numerical results show that SURE-Ridge achieves the lowest structural Hamming distance in the small-sample regime and the lowest run time across all sample sizes tested, compared with NOTEARS, DAGMA, and GBNSL baselines.

</details>


### [111] [Iterative tensor network transformations for element-wise evaluation of elementary and filtering functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17135)
*Xiao Wang,Tomohiro Hashizume,Pia Siegl,Dieter Jaksch*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ITNTs enable element-wise nonlinear processing directly in tensor-train compressed form, allowing efficient computations on huge data and enabling large-scale optimization, demonstrated on nonlinear filtering in 3D flow and Max-SAT search over 2^70 configs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlinear operations are challenging in tensor networks; need a general, compressed-domain framework to perform nonlinear filtering and optimization across very high-dimensional data.

Method: Introduce Iterative Tensor Network Transformations (ITNTs), an algorithmic framework that applies elementary nonlinear functions to TT-encoded data through iterative, in-network transformations operating entirely in the TT representation, controlling rank growth and computation.

Result: Two demonstrations: (I) accurate evaluation of nonlinear elementary/filters on a 3D reactive flow field for reaction-rate computation and region filtering; (II) extremum finding in combinatorial optimization, exemplified by Max-SAT on spaces up to 2^70 configurations.

Conclusion: ITNTs provide a foundational, general-purpose toolkit for nonlinear processing and optimization in tensor networks, enabling large-scale data science within compressed representations.

Abstract: Tensor networks are powerful formats for compressing large-scale data. However, their application to general data processing has been limited by the difficulty of performing nonlinear operations. Here, we introduce iterative tensor network transformations (ITNTs), a general algorithmic framework for the element-wise evaluation of elementary and nonlinear filtering functions on data encoded as tensor trains (TTs), a class of tensor networks. Our approach operates entirely in the compressed domain, enabling efficient computation on exponentially large datasets while maintaining a controlled computational cost. We demonstrate its power in two key areas: (I) evaluating highly nonlinear elementary and filtering functions on a 3D reactive flow field, enabling high-fidelity reaction rate computation and region filtering, and (II) finding extrema in complex optimization problems, such as solving Max-SAT instances on spaces up to $2^{70}$ configurations. These results establish ITNT as a foundational tool that provides tensor network methods with the capability for general-purpose data science and large-scale optimization.

</details>


### [112] [OraclePhys: A Systematic Framework for LLM Fine-Tuning on Structural Mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17162)
*Mingyu Li,Guorui Song,Jing Lin,Haoqian Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OraclePhys shows that the form of the label and the chosen objective, not data size, governs what LLMs learn during fine-tuning; certain forms yield usable physics understanding while others do not; an 8B model trained under this framework achieves specialist-level spatial-structural capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond post-hoc diagnosis of fine-tuning by treating label form as an experimental variable and quantify how fine-tuning signals shape the learned computation and its transfer across domains.

Method: Introduce OraclePhys with three components: OraclePhys-Bench (an exactly-graded finite-element benchmark with an oracle scoring every answer and counterfactual edits), OraclePhys-30K (a supervision dataset of seven answer forms over byte-identical structure descriptions), and a controlled training study across the seven forms and three verifier roles; evaluate across seven forms, three verifier roles, and objective types (ranking, scalar, boolean), with GRPO as an advantage-weighted scoring regime.

Result: The label form causally determines what is learned: ranking objectives produce an out-of-distribution forward model with the untrained base at guessing prior; scalar objectives offer at best a partial learning; boolean objectives yield no detectable learning; the effect persists across physics domains, model families, and paraphrased evaluation surfaces. Written/score-filtered answers enable this capability, while advantage-weighted scores raise reward but do not improve held-out physics performance, only routing. An 8B model trained in this framework reaches the data-precision frontier for spatial structural response, exceeding a frontier LLM at zero- and 32-shot settings and attaining a specialist level.

Conclusion: What the label encodes about the target computation is what the model learns during fine-tuning; what you train on determines how the model is routed in deployment.

Abstract: What a language model internalizes from fine-tuning is usually diagnosed after the fact. We make it an experimental variable. OraclePhys is a systematic fine-tuning framework with three components: OraclePhys-Bench, an exactly-graded structural-mechanics benchmark whose finite-element oracle scores every answer and counterfactual edit -- no human labels, no LLM judging; OraclePhys-30K, a supervision dataset of seven answer forms over byte-identical structure descriptions; and a controlled training study across the seven forms and three verifier roles. The study yields two findings. First, the label's answer form -- not its bit count -- causally determines what fine-tuning teaches: a ranking objective installs an out-of-distribution forward model where the untrained base sits at the guessing prior, a scalar objective at best a partial one, a boolean nothing detectable; the vector-scalar gulf survives a second physics domain, a second model family, and a paraphrased evaluation surface. Second, written or score-filtered answers install this capability, while advantage-weighted scores (GRPO) raise reward yet leave the model statistically equivalent to its start on held-out physics -- within the recipes and budgets tested -- sufficing only for routing. The trained 8B -- the first LLM on spatial structural response -- reaches the task's data-precision frontier: above a frontier LLM at zero- and 32-shot, at a specialist's level. What the label spells out about the target computation is what fine-tuning teaches; what you train on is what you route.

</details>


### [113] [Q-Learning With World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17163)
*Perry Dong,Yueru Jia,Chelsea Finn,Dorsa Sadigh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QWM integrates world models with Q-learning by doing test-time search over imagined trajectories to select actions, while training solely on real transitions; this avoids compounding model bias while retaining world-model sample-efficiency benefits, achieving strong gains on Robomimic and LIBERO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers aim to improve sample efficiency in off-policy RL and scale world-model methods to high-dimensional, long-horizon tasks, such as real robotics, without the bias from training on imagined data.

Method: QWM uses a world model to perform test-time rollout planning on top of Q-learning. Actions are chosen by searching imagined trajectories at decision time, but the policy and value networks are trained only on real transitions, preventing model-induced bias.

Result: QWM significantly outperforms strong prior state-of-the-art methods in both sample efficiency and policy performance on challenging manipulation benchmarks Robomimic and LIBERO.

Conclusion: Leverage world-model-based predictive search on top of online Q-learning can boost sample efficiency and performance without succumbing to bias from imagined data, enabling scalable, high-dimensional control.

Abstract: Off-policy reinforcement learning (RL) has become increasingly sample-efficient, enabling applications such as RL fine-tuning of Vision-Language-Action models into reliable, high-performing policies. World models offer a further lever for sample efficiency, as they predict state changes rather than actions alone, but their success has largely been confined to supervised policy learning. Prior model-based RL methods often optimize the policy or value function directly on imagined rollouts, which is prone to compounding bias and struggles to scale to large, high-dimensional problems such as real-world robotics, a problem that worsens with task horizon and visual complexity. In this work, we instead ask whether we can leverage world models directly on top of standard Q-learning to improve performance, while remaining trained and grounded in the real, online setting. We propose QWM, a framework that leverages world models to perform test-time search over imagined trajectories on top of Q-learning to select high-value actions during both online rollouts and evaluation. Since the policy and value function are trained only on real transitions, QWM avoids compounding model bias while still gaining the sample-efficiency benefits of predictive search. On challenging manipulation benchmarks Robomimic and LIBERO, QWM significantly outperforms strong prior state-of-the-art methods on both sample efficiency and performance.

</details>


### [114] [SCENARIODIFF: A Scenario-level Guidance Framework for Multimodal Time Series Forecasting--Extended Version](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17164)
*Tuan-Binh Tran,Dat Nguyen Cong,Duc-Trong Le,Thanh Trung Huynh,Tung Kieu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A hierarchical, interpretable multimodal forecasting framework (SCENARIODIFF) using three contextual agents to condition a diffusion-based model on text, with anchor-guided sampling for trajectory refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: External textual signals can inform forecasting, but existing methods struggle with interpretability and controllability when fusing text with time series.

Method: SCENARIODIFF components: Historical Context Agent extracts incremental evidence from raw documents; Scenario Agent builds qualitative horizon scenarios; Anchor Guidance Agent proposes sparse future anchors; a Multimodal Diffusion Transformer fuses signals; Anchor Blended Sampling refines trajectories without retraining.

Result: Empirical results on Time-MMD show strong performance in event-driven domains, highlighting the utility of explicit hierarchical scenario guidance; full code available.

Conclusion: Explicit, hierarchical scenario guidance improves interpretability and effectiveness of multimodal time series forecasting under noisy, weakly aligned textual context.

Abstract: Textual context such as news, reports, and logs can provide valuable signals for time series forecasting, especially when future dynamics are driven by external events that are not yet visible in historical values. Existing multimodal forecasting methods often either ask large language models (LLMs) to predict numerical values directly or fuse text and time series implicitly, making contextual influence difficult to interpret and control. We propose SCENARIODIFF, a hierarchical contextual reasoning framework for multimodal time series forecasting under noisy and weakly aligned documents. SCENARIODIFF organizes contextual information into three levels: a Historical Context Agent extracts stepwise evidence from raw documents, a Scenario Agent produces a qualitative scenario description for the forecast horizon, and an Anchor Guidance Agent generates sparse anchor points for event-relevant future regions. These structured signals condition a Multimodal Diffusion Transformer, while Anchor Blended Sampling locally refines generated trajectories without retraining. Experiments on the Time-MMD benchmark show that SCENARIODIFF is especially effective in event-driven domains, demonstrating the value of explicit hierarchical scenario guidance for multimodal time series forecasting. Our full implementation is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/ScenarioDiff_ICDM-2C4C

</details>


### [115] [Population Health-Based Machine Learning Reveals Associations Between Psychosocial Factors and Chronic Kidney Disease](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17174)
*Md. Atik Shams,David Eisenberg,Sumaiya Fatema,Asma Sultana,D. M Hasibul Islam,Junnatul Mawa,Anindita Datta,Nafiya Ahmed,Danastan Tasaouf Mridula,SK. Sazid Mahmud,Simon Bin Akter,Tanjila Helaly,Jorge Fresneda Fernandez,Humayera Islam,Tanmoy Sarkar Pias*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A two-part study using BRFSS and NHIS survey data to classify self-reported CKD status via a stacked ensemble, tackling missing data with nine imputation methods and class imbalance with sampling. Achieves balanced accuracy ~72.6–76.1% and AUROC ~79.6–82.3%, with SHAP-guided identification of predictors like regular medical check-ups, age, blood pressure, and mental health stress, yielding an interpretable CKD risk stratification framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CKD progresses silently and early detection is crucial for outcome improvement. Leveraging large-scale, real-world telehealth survey data with advanced ML aims to enable accurate, interpretable CKD risk stratification and identification of modifiable risk factors.

Method: Data sources: BRFSS 2021 (438,693 samples) and BRFSS 2019 (418,268); NHIS 2021 (29,482) and NHIS 2020 (31,568). Preprocessing includes nine state-of-the-art imputation methods to address missingness and sampling strategies to mitigate class imbalance. A customized stacked ensemble classifier is trained and evaluated. Model interpretability is provided via SHAP analysis, followed by clinical review to validate key predictors.

Result: The model achieves balanced accuracy of 72.56–76.12% and AUROC of 79.59–82.29%. SHAP analysis highlights critical predictors—regular medical check-ups, age, blood pressure, and mental health stress—with clinical review supporting their relevance.

Conclusion: Provides a robust, interpretable CKD risk stratification framework applicable to large-scale telehealth data, offering actionable insights into CKD-associated factors and potential targets for early screening and intervention.

Abstract: Chronic kidney disease (CKD) progresses silently and severely undermines quality of life, making early detection critical for improving patient outcomes. We present a two-part study that combines large-scale telehealth data with advanced machine learning to both classify self-reported CKD status and identify key drivers of disease. Using selected features from the Behavioral Risk Factor Surveillance System (BRFSS 2021: 438,693 samples; BRFSS 2019: 418,268 samples) and the National Health Interview Survey (NHIS 2021: 29,482 samples; NHIS 2020: 31,568 samples), we addressed missing data with nine state-of-the-art imputation methods and mitigated class imbalance via sampling strategies. Our customized stacked ensemble model achieved balanced accuracy of 72.56-76.12%, with corresponding AUROC scores of 79.59-82.29%. SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) analysis, followed by clinical review, highlighted critical predictors, including regular medical check-ups, age, blood pressure, and indicators of mental health stress. These findings deliver a robust and interpretable framework for CKD risk stratification and provide actionable insights into its associated factors.

</details>


### [116] [Task Specialization Fine-Tuning for Contextual Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17180)
*Jianan Zhou,Jung-Hoon Cho,Tianyue Zhou,Han Zheng,Jie Zhang,Roy Dong,Yining Ma,Cathy Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TSFT: a pretrain-first, then task-specific fine-tuning framework for contextual RL that uses a simple predictive model to allocate a fixed fine-tuning budget across task regions via integer linear programming, improving task coverage and approaching oracle performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CRL requires broad task coverage under limited resources; heterogeneous marginal returns across contexts make naive fine-tuning inefficient. A unified pretrain-finetune strategy with principled budget allocation can improve sample efficiency and coverage.

Method: Introduce Task Specialization Fine-Tuning (TSFT): an online framework that (1) uses a simple parametric model to predict fine-tuning gains per task region, and (2) solves an exact discrete budget allocation problem via integer linear programming to allocate finite fine-tuning resources across regions.

Result: Empirical validation across combinatorial optimization, continuous control, and LLM fine-tuning shows TSFT outperforms baselines in task coverage and approaches oracle performance.

Conclusion: TSFT establishes a model-based CRL direction compatible with a pretrain–finetune paradigm, enabling efficient, budget-aware specialization across task regions.

Abstract: Contextual Reinforcement Learning (CRL) seeks to generalize classical RL by maximizing task coverage across a context space of related tasks. While prior works often train from scratch and rely on either multi-task learning for a single policy or strategically training multiple policies, we advocate for a unified alternative: pretraining a single policy with good initial performance, followed by fine-tuning multiple policies for task specialization. This new paradigm, however, introduces unique challenges, such as heterogeneous marginal returns and sample inefficiency. This raises a critical research question: given a pretrained policy and a constrained budget, how much fine-tuning should each task region receive to enable sample-efficient CRL? To this end, we propose Task Specialization Fine-Tuning (TSFT), an online framework that predicts fine-tuning performance with a simple parametric model and exactly solves the resulting discrete budget allocation problem via integer linear programming. Extensive experiments across diverse decision domains, including combinatorial optimization, continuous control, and LLM fine-tuning, demonstrate that TSFT significantly outperforms baselines in task coverage and approaches oracle performance. Our work charts a new direction for model-based CRL, aligning with the modern pretrain-finetune era.

</details>


### [117] [Reinforcement Learning as (Discrete) Potential Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17181)
*Christopher Connolly*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Presents a potential-theoretic framework for RL under a fixed-policy, linking probability theory and potential theory to inform representations and algorithms; extendable to nonlinear RL when the policy is variable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge probability theory and potential theory to enrich RL representations and algorithms, aiming for better sample efficiency and formal constraints under a fixed-policy assumption; provide a path to nonlinear extension.

Method: Theoretical review and framework development that maps RL constructs (Markov chains, value functions, etc.) to potential-theoretic constructs; analyzes the linear fixed-policy setting and outlines extensions to nonlinear regimes when the policy is relaxed.

Result: Articulates a coherent potential-theoretic viewpoint for core RL representations and algorithms, highlighting potential gains in sample efficiency and formal constraints; shows a natural route to extend the linear theory to nonlinear RL when the fixed-policy assumption is relaxed.

Conclusion: The potential-theoretic viewpoint offers a principled foundation for RL, bridging probability and potential theory, with practical implications for representation learning and algorithm design; applicable to both linear (fixed-policy) and nonlinear RL via extension.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) theory fundamentally depends on probability theory through the Markov chain. There is a deep connection between probability theory and potential theory. This paper reviews that connection and explores the potential-theoretic viewpoint for core reinforcement learning representations and algorithms under a fixed-policy assumption. This viewpoint may offer a path for improved sample efficiency and formal constraints that can be applied to RL. When the fixed-policy assumption is relaxed, the linear potential theory framework can be naturally extended to the nonlinear case.

</details>


### [118] [How smoothing the affinity matrix affects neighborhood preservation in t-SNE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17190)
*Shirin Mohebi,Guillaume Bied,Jefrey Lijffijt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a row-wise gamma power transform on t-SNE affinities, enabling per-point control of local scale; equivalent to varying perplexity but with point-dependent effective perplexities; sharpening strengthens very local neighborhoods, smoothing improves mid-local neighborhoods, outperforming multiscale approaches in that range.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global perplexity in t-SNE limits faithful neighborhood preservation across scales. A per-point adaptive affinity scale could better capture local structure without sacrificing global relationships.

Method: Apply a row-wise power transform controlled by gamma to the t-SNE affinity matrix before symmetrization, preserving sparsity and rank order. Show that this is equivalent to rescaling Gaussian bandwidth and altering perplexity, but results in point-dependent effective perplexities. Compare empirically to baseline and multiscale affinity constructions.

Result: Sharpening (gamma < 1) improves preservation of the very nearest neighbors; smoothing (gamma > 1) improves preservation of broader local neighborhoods. The method outperforms alternative affinity constructions, including multiscale approaches, in the mid-local range.

Conclusion: Per-point adaptive control of affinity scale via a row-wise gamma transform offers improved neighborhood preservation in t-SNE and provides a principled alternative to fixed global perplexity, while maintaining sparsity and rank-order of affinities.

Abstract: Dimensionality reduction methods are instrumental to visualize high-dimensional data, and t-SNE stands as one of the most widely used methods due to its emphasis on local neighborhood preservation. A central component of t-SNE is the affinity matrix, which expresses pairwise similarities in the form of symmetrized probabilities, over which the optimization problem of t-SNE is defined. We study how the sharpness of this probability distribution affects neighborhood preservation at different scales. We introduce a row-wise power transform controlled by a parameter gamma that can smooth or sharpen each row of the affinity matrix while preserving sparsity and rank order. We show that this transform is equivalent to rescaling the Gaussian bandwidth and thus to changing the perplexity. However, as the sharpness of the probability distribution varies per point, a fixed gamma leads to point-dependent effective perplexities, making it distinct from changing the global perplexity. Empirically, we find that sharpening improves preservation of the very nearest neighbors, while smoothing improves preservation of broader local neighborhoods, outperforming alternative affinity constructions including multiscale methods in the mid-local range.

</details>


### [119] [Pessimistic Meta-Induction and Its Limits: Lessons from Frequentist Statistics and Machine Learning Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17213)
*Hanti Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Meta-induction fails to converge almost everywhere to truth, whereas ordinary enumerative induction can converge everywhere; in the problematic domain, no method reaches almost-everywhere convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reassess the inductive basis of scientific realism by focusing on convergence properties of induction; unify insights from frequentist statistics, ML, and formal epistemology to evaluate the inductive step rather than historical premises.

Method: Theoretically analyze induction using a convergence framework drawn from statistics and formal epistemology; compare enumerative (everywhere convergence) vs meta-inductive approaches (almost everywhere convergence); argue that in the challenging domain, no inference method attains almost-everywhere convergence.

Result: Enumerative induction can achieve everywhere convergence to truth, while meta-induction fails to achieve even almost-everywhere convergence; in the critical context, no method can achieve almost-everywhere convergence.

Conclusion: The inductive step underpinning pessimistic meta-inductive arguments against realism is undermined; the domain exhibits fundamental convergence limitations, calling for a reevaluation of the prospects for scientific realism under such inductive conditions.

Abstract: This paper challenges the pessimistic meta-inductive argument against scientific realism by undermining its inductive step rather than its historical premise. Although related challenges already exist, I develop a new one. Drawing on a general epistemology of scientific inference developed in frequentist statistics, machine learning, and formal epistemology, I evaluate induction in terms of convergence to the truth. I argue that ordinary enumerative induction can achieve everywhere convergence, whereas meta-induction fails even to achieve almost everywhere convergence. Indeed, in the problem context where meta-induction arises, the failure is deeper: no inference method whatsoever achieves almost everywhere convergence.

</details>


### [120] [Delta2Gamma: Band-Wise Adaptive Contrastive Learning of EEG for Alzheimer's Disease Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17231)
*Chanwoo Park,Chanwoo Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Self-supervised EEG Delta2Gamma with per-band encoders yields 92.4% AD vs CN accuracy on ADFTD under leave-one-subject-out.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for low-cost, scalable dementia screening. EEG is inexpensive but noisy and label-scarce; use self-supervised learning to build robust representations from unlabeled data, accounting for cross-subject variability by band-wise processing.

Method: Decompose EEG into five canonical bands (delta, theta, alpha, beta, gamma); assign separate encoder and projection head per band; adaptively predict temperature per band during contrastive training; apply augmented views and a contrastive loss; evaluate with strict leave-one-subject-out on the ADFTD cohort.

Result: Achieves 92.4% accuracy in distinguishing Alzheimer's disease from cognitively normal controls; outperforms supervised baselines and recent EEG methods.

Conclusion: Delta2Gamma demonstrates effective self-supervised, multi-band EEG representation learning for scalable dementia screening, showing strong cross-subject generalization with limited labeled data.

Abstract: Low-cost, scalable screening for dementia remains an open problem. Imaging-based diagnosis is costly and hard to deploy widely. Electroencephalography (EEG) is portable and inexpensive, but its recordings are noisy, vary widely across subjects, and carry few clinical labels. We tackle this with Delta2Gamma, a self-supervised framework that learns EEG representations from unlabeled data by contrasting augmented views of each signal. Rather than treat EEG as a single stream, Delta2Gamma decomposes every recording into the five canonical neural rhythms (delta, theta, alpha, beta, gamma). Each band gets its own encoder and projection head. Each also gets a temperature that is predicted adaptively during contrastive training, so bands with different signal statistics are balanced automatically. On the ADFTD cohort under a strict leave-one-subject-out protocol, Delta2Gamma separates Alzheimer's disease from cognitively normal controls with 92.4\% accuracy. This exceeds both supervised backbones and recent dedicated EEG methods.

</details>


### [121] [Physics-Informed and Hybrid Machine Learning in Additive Manufacturing: Application to Fused Filament Fabrication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17246)
*Berkcan Kapusuzoglu,Sankaran Mahadevan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hybrid physics-informed ML approaches to predict bond quality and porosity in FFF; physics-constrained losses, physics-model inputs, and physics pretraining; eight strategy combinations; improved accuracy with limited data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need to reliably predict porosity and bond quality in FFF parts with limited experimental data; enforce physical consistency between porosity, bond quality, and tensile strength; leverage multi-physics simulations to guide learning.

Method: Three integration strategies: (1) embed physics constraints in DNN loss, (2) feed physics model outputs as extra inputs, (3) pre-train DNN on physics model input-output pairs and refine with experimental data; eight combinations of strategies tested.

Result: Found that combining multiple strategies yields accurate ML models even with limited data; physical consistency between bond quality and tensile strength achieved; enhances porosity predictions.

Conclusion: Physics-informed hybrid ML is effective for FFF prediction tasks, enabling reliable predictions with sparse data, and can be extended to other multi-physics materials problems.

Abstract: This article investigates several physics-informed and hybrid machine learning strategies that incorporate physics knowledge in experimental data-driven deep-learning models for predicting the bond quality and porosity of fused filament fabrication (FFF) parts. Three types of strategies are explored to incorporate physics constraints and multi-physics FFF simulation results into a deep neural network (DNN), thus ensuring consistency with physical laws: (1) incorporate physics constraints within the loss function of the DNN, (2) use physics model outputs as additional inputs to the DNN model, and (3) pre-train a DNN model with physics model input-output and then update it with experimental data. These strategies help to enforce a physically consistent relationship between bond quality and tensile strength, thus making porosity predictions physically meaningful. Eight different combinations of the above strategies are investigated. The results show how the combination of multiple strategies produces accurate machine learning models even with limited experimental data.

</details>


### [122] [Co-RL: Unsupervised Reasoning Emerges from Diverse Cohort in Multi-agent RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17253)
*Yunhao Yang,Yuexin Bian,Yunjie Tian,Di Fu,Tianjin Huang,Yuanyuan Shi,Ziang Xiao,Nuno Vasconcelos,Yijiang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Co-RL trains multiple decoupled models with rewards derived from peers in a cooperative, diverse cohort, enabling unsupervised reasoning that outperforms label-free baselines and competes with supervised methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the dependence on costly ground-truth rewards in RL for reasoning, mitigate self-reinforcing biases in self-generated feedback, and promote robust, diverse reasoning in language and vision-language models.

Method: A cooperative multi-agent RL framework (Co-RL) where several decoupled models are trained in parallel with rewards derived from their peers. Diversity is increased via heterogeneous model families, sizes, and rephrased training samples to reduce correlated errors and feedback loops.

Result: Empirical gains of 3.0–8.6% (text-only LLMs across seven benchmarks) and 2.3–7.2% (multimodal VLMs across four benchmarks); Co-RL outperforms base and prior label-free approaches and matches or surpasses supervised methods without ground-truth labels; code provided.

Conclusion: Co-RL demonstrates that peer-derived rewards and intentional diversity can foster robust unsupervised reasoning in both text and multimodal settings, mitigating training collapse and offering competitive performance without labels.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a powerful approach for improving reasoning in language and vision-language models, yet its strongest successes still depend heavily on ground-truth supervision (e.g., verifiable reward). Such annotations are costly to obtain and become increasingly scarce as reasoning capabilities advance beyond what humans can reliably evaluate. Self-rewarding RL reduces this dependence by enabling models to derive reward signals from their own completions. However, training solely on self-generated feedback can reinforce existing biases and suboptimal behaviors, reduce response diversity, and ultimately lead to homogenized responses and training collapse. In this work, we show that unsupervised reasoning can emerge through cooperative multi-agent training. We introduce Co-RL, a framework in which multiple decoupled models, sharing no parameters, are simultaneously optimized through RL using rewards derived from their peers. We further show that increasing cohort diversity, through heterogeneous model families, sizes, and rephrased training samples, reduces the correlated errors that drive self-reinforcing feedback loops. This diversity consistently improves reasoning performance, maintains behavioral diversity, and mitigates training collapse. Across text-only and multimodal domains, Co-RL consistently outperforms the base models and prior label-free approaches, while matching or surpassing supervised methods, without access to any ground-truth labels. Concretely, Co-RL yields average gains of 3.0-8.6% across seven text-only benchmarks for LLMs and 2.3-7.2% across four multimodal benchmarks for VLMs. Code is available at https://github.com/DrStranded/Co-RL.

</details>


### [123] [Understanding Curriculum Learning in Large Language Models via Cross-Difficulty Optimization Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17268)
*Zhikai Ding,Ziyi Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A relative transfer-based curriculum learning framework (TDCS) adaptively samples data by estimating cross-difficulty transfer, yielding improved reasoning performance across tasks and scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Curriculum learning in LLM pretraining shows inconsistent benefits across tasks; understanding when and why it helps is crucial for designing effective curricula.
