Method: Introduce Relative Transfer as a metric of cross-difficulty knowledge transfer. Analyze optimization dynamics under different curricula, and derive TDCS to adjust sampling distributions dynamically based on estimated transfer throughout training.

Result: TDCS consistently outperforms representative curricula across multiple reasoning benchmarks, model scales, and training paradigms, with a unified optimization-based explanation grounded in cross-difficulty transfer.

Conclusion: Cross-difficulty transfer governs curriculum effectiveness; TDCS provides a principled, adaptive approach that unifies and explains curriculum learning in LLM optimization.

Abstract: Curriculum learning has been widely adopted in the post-training of large language models by organizing training data from easy to hard. However, its effectiveness varies substantially across reasoning tasks, suggesting that no single curriculum is universally optimal and raising a fundamental question: what determines when curriculum learning works? In this paper, we answer this question by analyzing the optimization dynamics induced by different curriculum schedules. We show that the transfer relationship between different difficulty levels characterizes the optimization dynamics induced by curriculum learning, which in turn explains the effectiveness of different curriculum schedules, and formalize this relationship as Relative Transfer, a principled measure of cross-difficulty knowledge transfer. Based on this measurement, we derive Transfer-aware Dynamic Curriculum Sampling (TDCS), which dynamically adjusts the sampling distribution according to the estimated transfer relationship throughout training. Extensive experiments on multiple reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that TDCS consistently outperforms representative scheduling strategies across different tasks, model scales, and training paradigms. More importantly, our work provides a unified optimization-based explanation of curriculum learning through cross-difficulty transfer.

</details>


### [124] [Rethinking Irregular Time Series Forecasting from the Perspective of Basis Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17284)
*Rongwen Li,Changjian Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Debiased Neural Basis-Function Network (DNBNet) for irregular time series forecasting, debiasing via importance sampling, adaptive neural basis-functions, multi-scale average-pooling fusion, and a dual-branch decoder, yielding strong performance and generalizability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Irregular time series with sparse observations and non-uniform timestamps cause asymptotic bias and limit basis-function adaptivity; existing methods ignore timestamp density and rely on fixed basis functions.

Method: Introduce debiased neural basis-function response mechanism using importance sampling to correct asymptotic bias; parameterize basis functions with neural networks; multi-scale decomposition via average pooling; mass-aware fusion; dual-branch decoder for forecasting.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple real-world datasets show effectiveness and strong generalizability across irregular time series scenarios; code released.

Conclusion: DNBNet effectively debiases and adapts to diverse temporal patterns, with novel modules improving representation of sparse irregular data and accurate forecasting.

Abstract: Irregular time series forecasting is crucial in many domains, such as healthcare and meteorological observation. However, due to the inherent characteristics of irregular time series, including sparse observations and non-uniform sampling, accurately predicting future dynamics remains challenging. In light of these two characteristics, many existing methods aggregate irregular observations into fixed-dimensional estimated response coefficients through predefined basis functions and use these coefficients as sequence representations. Nevertheless, this modeling paradigm still suffers from two key limitations: (i) a potential non-vanishing asymptotic bias caused by ignoring the sampling density of timestamps; and (ii) the limited adaptability of predefined basis functions to diverse temporal patterns. In this study, we propose a Debiased Neural Basis-Function Network (DNBNet) to address these challenges. Its core is a debiased neural basis-function response mechanism, which corrects asymptotic bias through importance sampling while parameterizing basis functions with neural networks to adapt to diverse temporal patterns. In addition, considering the sparsity of irregular data, we design a novel multi-scale decomposition module based on average pooling, together with a mass-aware fusion mechanism, to obtain richer representations. Finally, a dual-branch decoder is employed for forecasting. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of DNBNet and its strong generalizability across diverse irregular time series scenarios. Our code can be obtained at https://github.com/hnu-vis/DNBNet.

</details>


### [125] [Abra: Scaling Diffusion Image Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17286)
*Kyle Chickering,Wei-An Lin,Swayam Bhanded,Dan Saunders,Akshat Tripathi,Jiaming Song,Shyamal Buch,Xinchen Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Compute-optimal scaling laws for text-to-image diffusion models show diffusion models scale predictably like LLMs but require much more data per parameter; optimal data per parameter is ~200 image tokens, an order of magnitude more than LLMs per Chinchilla; models robust to overtraining, data-centric recommendations; training curves and performance metrics collapse onto a universal form.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend compute-optimal scaling laws from language models to visual generation, identifying data-model tradeoffs for diffusion models at frontier compute and informing practical training budgets.

Method: Systematic scaling study using Abra, a controlled family of flow-matching transformers, trained across 1e19 to 1e22 FLOPs. Varies model size and data, evaluates training loss and a suite of generative quality metrics, optimal CFG settings, representation quality, and the form of training curves; analyzes cross-metric predictability and curve collapse.

Result: Diffusion models exhibit compute-optimal scaling behavior similar to LLMs, but require substantially more data for optimal performance. The compute-optimal data-to-parameter ratio is ~200 image tokens per parameter, about 10x the Chinchilla prescription for LLMs. Unlike LLMs, diffusion models are robust to overtraining. The scaling predictability extends to generative quality metrics, CFG settings, representation quality, and the shape of training curves, all collapsing onto a universal form.

Conclusion: For diffusion models, prioritize data over increasing model size at frontier compute, as scaling laws generalize across metrics and training curves. These findings guide data-centric budgeting and model design for high-cost visual generation systems and suggest a universal scaling framework akin to LLMs.

Abstract: Compute-optimal scaling laws guide the training of frontier language models yet remain largely unexplored for visual generation. We present a systematic scaling law study for text-to-image diffusion models using Abra, a controlled family of flow-matching transformers trained across three orders of magnitude worth of compute ($10^{19}$ to $10^{22}$ FLOPs), reaching significantly larger compute budgets than previous works. We demonstrate that diffusion models scale just as predictably as language models but require far more data to train optimally: compute optimality occurs at approximately $200$ image tokens per parameter, ten times the Chinchilla compute-optimal prescription for LLMs. We show that unlike language models, diffusion models are robust to overtraining and that practitioners should err on the side of more data rather than a larger model. Finally, we show that this predictability extends beyond training loss to generative quality metrics, optimal CFG settings, representation quality, and even the shape of the training curves, which collapse onto a universal form.

</details>


### [126] [Beyond MSE: Rethinking the Evaluation Metric and Benchmarking for Irregular Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17293)
*Rongwen Li,Haixin Xie,Xiao Wang,Changjian Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Continuous-time Squared Error (CSE) with importance weighting to remove timestamp sampling bias in irregular time-series forecasting, provides theoretical guarantees that CSE's asymptotic error is not worse than MSE, and presents a benchmark across synthetic, semi-synthetic, and eight real datasets showing CSE better reflects continuous-time risk; code is available on GitHub.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MSE, used in irregular time-series benchmarks, is influenced by sample-specific timestamp sampling distributions, biasing evaluations of continuous-time predictive performance. A metric that isolates the true continuous-time risk is needed.

Method: Propose Continuous-time Squared Error (CSE) with importance weighting to cancel the effect of timestamp sampling distributions. Provide theoretical proof that asymptotic estimation error of CSE with respect to continuous-time risk is no larger than that of MSE. Build a systematic benchmark spanning synthetic, semi-synthetic, and eight real-world datasets to evaluate CSE and continuous-time predictive performance.

Result: CSE recovers continuous-time risk more accurately than MSE; relying on MSE can misrepresent models’ continuous-time predictive performance in real-world scenarios.

Conclusion: CSE offers an unbiased evaluation of continuous-time predictive performance in irregular time-series forecasting and should be adopted in benchmarks; the authors provide code for replication.

Abstract: Existing research on irregular time-series forecasting has primarily focused on model design, while evaluation metrics remain insufficiently studied. Existing benchmarks typically use mean squared error (MSE) as the evaluation metric. We show that, in irregular forecasting, MSE is determined not only by the model prediction but also by the sample-specific timestamp sampling distributions, leading to a biased assessment of the models' continuous-time predictive performance. To address this issue, we propose the Continuous-time Squared Error (CSE), which employs importance weighting to eliminate the influence of the timestamp sampling distributions. We further theoretically prove that CSE's asymptotic estimation error with respect to continuous-time risk is no greater than that of MSE. Finally, we construct a systematic benchmark covering synthetic, semi-synthetic, and eight real-world datasets to validate the effectiveness of CSE and systematically evaluate models' continuous-time predictive performance. Experiments show that CSE can recover continuous-time risk more accurately than MSE, while relying solely on MSE may not fully reflect models' continuous-time predictive performance in real-world scenarios. Our code can be obtained at https://github.com/hnu-vis/ITS-Bench.

</details>


### [127] [Repetition as Reinforcement: Enhancing Sample Efficiency via Instant Episode Repetition in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17347)
*Hoda Yamani,Yuning Xing,Koen van Rijnsoever,Bruce A. MacDonald,Henry Williams*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IER is a simple data-collection mechanism that boosts sample efficiency by repeating action sequences from high-reward episodes across a fixed number of subsequent episodes, integrated into SAC/TD3 and evaluated on continuous-control benchmarks; it outperforms standard and self-imitation baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Repetition strengthens successful experiences and can improve learning efficiency by directly shaping data collection, addressing sample efficiency in reinforcement learning.

Method: Identify a high-reward episode; replay its action sequence for a fixed number of subsequent episodes (data-influencing repetition); integrate into SAC and TD3; evaluate on continuous-control tasks (MuJoCo, DeepMind Control Suite) and a real-world robotic manipulation task.

Result: IER improves learning performance over standard SAC/TD3 and self-imitation baselines across evaluated benchmarks.

Conclusion: IER is a simple, effective mechanism for enhancing sample efficiency by propagating successful behaviors through repeated subsequent interactions; it can complement existing off-policy RL algorithms and warrants further exploration and ablations.

Abstract: Repetition is a fundamental mechanism in human learning, where revisiting successful experiences strengthens memory, consolidates skills, and improves future performance. Motivated by this biological principle, we introduce Instant Episode Repetition (IER), a simple and novel mechanism that improves sample efficiency by immediately repeating action sequences from successful episodes during environment interaction. Unlike conventional approaches such as Experience Replay and Self-Imitation Learning (SIL), which passively reuse past experience during training updates, IER directly influences the data collection process. Upon identifying a high-reward episode, the agent repeats its action sequence for a fixed number of subsequent episodes, reinforcing valuable behaviors through renewed interaction with the environment. We integrate IER into state-of-the-art SAC and TD3 algorithms and evaluate its effectiveness on continuous-control benchmarks, including MuJoCo, the DeepMind Control Suite, and a real-world dynamic object translation task with a robotic manipulator. Experimental results demonstrate that this simple mechanism improves learning performance over standard and self-imitation-based baselines.

</details>


### [128] [Agentic ESOpt: Fine-Tuning Long-Horizon LLM Agents with Minimal GPU Requirements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17310)
*Zhi Zheng,Rongsheng Chen,Yunpeng Ba,Zhenkun Wang,Yee Whye Teh,Wee Sun Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Agentic ESOpt uses evolution strategies for full-parameter fine-tuning of long-horizon LLM agents, offering scalability, flexibility, and improved performance over RL baselines; shows gains on WebArena-Lite and online prompt-parameter co-evolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL struggles with long-horizon, sparse rewards, high computational cost for large LLMs; ES offers black-box optimization and trajectory-level credit attribution for such settings.

Method: Iterative perturbation around current parameters, evaluate rewards, online reward-weighted update; cosine decay of perturbation scale σ; optionally online prompt-parameter co-evolution; full-parameter optimization.

Result: On WebArena-Lite with Qwen-3.5-27B: 6.69% improvement over No Skill; online prompt-parameter co-evolution improved 28/36 settings.

Conclusion: ES-based full-parameter agentic fine-tuning with parameter-context co-evolution is scalable and effective for long-horizon LLM agents, enabling better performance and easier integration with prompt-space evolution.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) has been promising in single-turn LLM fine-tuning. However, long-horizon agentic reasoning introduces increasingly branching interactions and sparse rewards, exposing several limitations of RL: its heavyweight backpropagation-based training stack makes it impractical to fine-tune larger LLMs, and longer-horizon trajectories make credit assignment in RL substantially harder. This paper argues that evolution strategies (ES) can be a better choice for fine-tuning long-horizon LLM agents. Compared with agentic RL, ES offers three key advantages: 1) Model Scalability: ES enables full-parameter optimization with only minimal, inference-level GPU memory, making it possible to fine-tune large LLMs. 2) Flexibility: its lightweight, black-box feedback interface makes ES fine-tuning easy to compose with prompt-space evolution (e.g., skill optimization & test-time compute); and 3) Long-Horizon Scalability: ES performs trajectory-level parameter attribution without decomposing rewards across horizons, yielding better scalability than Agentic RL as the horizon length grows. Based on this insight, we propose Agentic ESOpt, a full-parameter agentic fine-tuning framework tailored to flexible parameter--context co-evolution. At each step, Agentic ESOpt samples perturbations around the current LLM parameters, evaluates the resulting agents with rewards, and applies an online reward-weighted update. To improve the exploration--adaptation trade-off, Agentic ESOpt further introduces a cosine decay schedule of the perturbation scale $σ$. On WebArena-Lite, full-parameter optimization of Qwen-3.5-27B improves the No Skill baseline by 6.69%. In test-time automatic heuristic design, Agentic ESOpt performs online prompt--parameter co-evolution, improving its matched baseline in 28 of 36 settings.

</details>


### [129] [MoFE: A Novel Mixture-of-Experts Framework with Fourier Neural Operators for Cryptocurrency Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17342)
*Bowen Liu,Mingming Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MoFE is a Fourier-Neural-Operator-based mixture-of-experts framework that models crypto volatility as multi-frequency components and achieves SOTA forecasting for T+1 and T+5; improves directional accuracy and information coefficient, translating to excess returns and high Sharpe in simulated trading.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cryptocurrency prices exhibit non-stationarity, regime shifts, and multi-scale stochastic dependencies; standard DL struggles and suffers phase lag.

Method: MoFE integrates adaptive Fourier Neural Operators (AFNO) and convolutional dual-domain experts within a dynamic gating MoE. Grounded in SDE theory, it models volatility as a superposition of multi-frequency components (fundamental growth, mining-cost-driven seasonality, market sentiment chaos). The model learns continuous function-to-function mappings to capture global spectral trends, cyclical adjustments, and microstructure; a dynamic MoE selects strategies across regimes.

Result: Empirical study on BTC data (2020-01 to 2025-12) shows SOTA performance for both T+1 and T+5 horizons, reduced phase lag, higher Directional Accuracy and Information Coefficient; in high-fidelity trading simulations, yields excess returns and improved risk-adjusted metrics with high Sharpe.

Conclusion: The MoFE framework effectively addresses non-stationarity and regime shifts in crypto prices via spectral-decomposition and regime-aware MoE, delivering superior predictive accuracy and profitable trading signals; potential for generalization to other assets.

Abstract: Forecasting cryptocurrency prices remains a formidable challenge due to inherent non-stationarity, abrupt regime shifts, and multi-scale stochastic dependencies. Conventional deep learning models often struggle to capture complex underlying dynamics, frequently resulting in persistent phase-lagged predictions. To address these limitations, we propose MoFE, a novel deep learning framework that integrates Fourier Neural Operators (FNOs) within a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture. Rooted in the theoretical framework of stochastic differential equations, MoFE conceptualizes cryptocurrency volatility as a superposition of multi-frequency components, which includes user network based fundamental growth, mining costs and halving mechanism caused seasonal volatility, and market sentiment-induced chaos. Specifically, specialized adaptive FNO (AFNO) and Convolution dual-domain experts learn continuous function-to-function mappings to encapsulate global spectral trends, cyclical adjustments and microstructures, while a dynamic gating based MoE mechanism enables adaptive strategy switching across diverse market regimes. Extensive experiments on Bitcoin datasets spanning January 2020 to December 2025 demonstrate that MoFE achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance in both T+1 and T+5 forecasting horizons. Notably, the model effectively mitigates the phase-lag effect, delivering superior Directional Accuracy (DA) and Information Coefficient (IC). In high-fidelity simulated trading environments, these predictive gains transfer into significant excess returns and robust risk-adjusted performance, characterized by a high Sharpe ratio.

</details>


### [130] [Tight Bounds for Data-driven Multiple Hyper-parameter Tuning with Structured Loss Function](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17343)
*Anh Tuan Nguyen,Viet Anh Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Establishes tight pseudo-dimension bounds for multi-dimensional data-driven hyperparameter tuning by refining upper bounds via invariant connected sign cells in real algebraic geometry, and by introducing a multi-regime lower-bound framework; extends to bi-level validation-loss tuning and semi-algebraic applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generalization guarantees for data-driven hyperparameter tuning are challenging due to implicit, non-smooth dependence on hyperparameters; existing multi-dimensional bounds under piecewise-polynomial assumptions are loose and lack tight lower bounds.

Method: 1) Refine learning-theoretic upper bounds using real algebraic geometry by analyzing invariant connected sign cells during block elimination to avoid topological over-counting. 2) Propose a multi-regime lower-bound framework by constructing shattered problem instances across distinct regimes to show upper bounds are tight. 3) Extend the topological framework to accommodate bi-level validation-loss tuning and broader semi-algebraic applications.

Result: Tighter pseudo-dimension bounds for multi-dimensional data-driven tuning with strictly sharper sample complexities; lower-bound constructions that saturate the upper bounds; a generalized topological framework extended to bi-level validation-loss tuning and semi-algebraic settings.

Conclusion: Provides a tight, comprehensive theory for multi-dimensional data-driven hyperparameter tuning, delivering sharper upper bounds, matching lower bounds, and a flexible extension to more general semi-algebraic and bi-level tuning problems.

Abstract: Data-driven algorithm design frames hyperparameter tuning as a statistical learning problem, but establishing generalization guarantees remains challenging due to the implicit, non-smooth dependence of model performance on hyperparameters. Existing multi-dimensional bounds under piecewise-polynomial assumptions remain theoretically loose and lack comprehensive lower bounds. We resolve this by establishing tight pseudo-dimension bounds for multi-dimensional data-driven tuning. First, we refine the learning-theoretic upper bound using real algebraic geometry; by analyzing invariant connected sign cells during block elimination rather than isolated sign vectors, we avoid topological over-counting to derive strictly sharper sample complexities. Second, we present a multi-regime lower-bound framework that disentangles combinatorial and algebraic capacities. By constructing shattered problem instances across distinct regimes, we prove our upper bounds are tightly saturated. Finally, we extend our topological framework to accommodate general bi-level validation-loss tuning and broader semi-algebraic applications.

</details>


### [131] [CORAM: Coherent Orthogonal Rotation for Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17366)
*Xinyi Sui,Ziran Liu,Nam Ling,Wei Wang,Wei Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CORAM introduces manifold-based, row-slice SVD-factor merging for finetuned models, with amplification and dispersion-based restoration, outperforming OrthoMerge and matching strong weight-space baselines across diverse suites.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Weight-space linear combinations used by many model merging methods fail to preserve the geometry of updates, particularly singular values; OrthoMerge is limited by its orthogonal transforms. A geometry-aware, manifold-based merging approach is needed to better combine expert updates.

Method: Partition each target weight matrix into row slices. Represent every expert slice via its singular value decomposition within the base-model SVD frame. Merge task-specific factors on their corresponding manifolds. Apply an amplification coefficient lambda = kappa * c_hat, where c_hat is estimated from expert and merged update norms and scales roughly as sqrt(N) for N comparable experts. Choose restoration strength kappa from the dispersion of expert updates without evaluating candidate models. Use spread slicing to distribute highly updated rows across slices and include a residual pathway for non-target layers.

Result: CORAM reduces the gap to the best swept value to within 0.72 points and improves over OrthoMerge by 0.25 to 1.35 points across four evaluation suites, spanning 3B–9B models and language as well as vision-language experts, matching or exceeding strongest weight-space baselines.

Conclusion: CORAM provides a robust, scalable, geometry-aware alternative to weight-space merging, achieving consistent gains over state-of-the-art orthogonal approaches and competitive performance with traditional baselines across diverse model families and tasks.

Abstract: Merging finetuned models combines specialized capabilities without joint training or access to the original data. Most methods operate by linear arithmetic in Euclidean weight space, which cannot carry the geometry of the update. Orthogonal Model Merging (OrthoMerge) uses a single orthogonal transform for each weight matrix, but such a transform cannot change singular values. We propose CORAM, which partitions each target matrix into row slices, represents every expert slice by its singular value decomposition in the corresponding base-model SVD frame, and merges the task-specific factors on their corresponding manifolds. Because manifold averaging contracts the merged update, CORAM applies an amplification coefficient $λ=κ\hat{c}$. The scale c_hat is estimated from the expert and merged update norms and is approximately $\sqrt{N}$ for $N$ experts with comparable update magnitudes. The restoration strength kappa is selected from the dispersion of expert updates without evaluating candidate merged models. This rule remains within 0.72 points of the best swept value on all evaluated suites. CORAM also includes spread slicing to distribute highly updated rows across slices and a residual pathway for non-target layers. Across four suites covering three model families, 3B to 9B scales, and language and vision-language experts, CORAM improves over OrthoMerge by 0.25 to 1.35 points and matches or exceeds the strongest weight-space baselines.

</details>


### [132] [Pathology Transport: Optimal-Transport Explanations for Clinical Data, and When Their Heatmaps (Fail to) Localize Disease](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17370)
*Lalit Kumar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Generative models via an optimal-transport rectified flow between healthy and diseased distributions yield per-patient counterfactuals, unsupervised malignancy scores, and label-free heatmaps. They perform well on tabular data but fail to localize real lesions in chest X-rays, exposing a synthetic-to-real gap and raising questions about heatmap interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide an explainable clinical AI framework by modeling distributions of health and disease instead of probing a classifier, and to test whether resulting heatmaps truly localize pathology.

Method: Train an optimal-transport rectified flow between two clinical distributions (healthy vs diseased). For tabular Breast Cancer Wisconsin data, derive per-patient counterfactuals, an unsupervised malignancy score, and a label-free attribution; compare heatmaps to a supervised classifier. For chest X-rays, generate transport heatmaps and a reconstruction-based identity-preserving variant, evaluate localization on synthetic lesions (pointing game) and RSNA radiologist boxes, and compare to Grad-CAM.

Result: Tabular data: AUROC of 0.91 for malignancy score (0.93±0.01 across five seeds); label-free attribution correlates with a supervised classifier (r≈0.5) but does not exceed logistic regression. Chest X-rays: transport heatmaps resemble population-level signals and do not localize real lesions. The identity-preserving variant localizes synthetic lesions (pointing game ≈0.52) but fails on real RSNA boxes, while Grad-CAM remains superior. The central finding is a synthetic-to-real gap where plausible heatmaps on planted lesions do not evidence real localization.

Conclusion: Provides a reusable optimal-transport-based explainability pipeline and a controlled benchmark to assess whether heatmaps truly localize disease; highlights the need for rigorous localization tests beyond visual plausibility and notes limitations in real-world Chest X-ray localization compared to supervised methods.

Abstract: Generative models promise a route to explainable clinical AI: rather than probe a classifier, model the distributions of healthy and diseased patients and read explanations off the geometry between them. We build such a system - an optimal-transport rectified flow trained between two clinical distributions - and use it to ask a pointed question the field too rarely tests: do the resulting explanation heatmaps actually localize disease? On tabular tumour biomarkers (Breast Cancer Wisconsin) a single flow yields per-patient counterfactuals, an unsupervised malignancy score (AUROC 0.91; 0.93 +/- 0.01 across five seeds), and a label-free attribution that agrees with a supervised classifier (r ~ 0.5) - a compact, honest interpretability engine, though it never out-predicts logistic regression. Moving to chest X-rays, we show the transport heatmap is a population-level signal, not a localiser; a reconstruction-based, identity-preserving variant does localize synthetic lesions (pointing game 0.52), yet on real RSNA radiologist boxes it collapses to chance while only supervised Grad-CAM stays above it. The central result is a synthetic-to-real gap: label-free heatmaps that look compelling on planted lesions are not evidence of real localisation. We contribute a reusable optimal-transport recipe for generative explanations and a controlled benchmark for stress-testing whether they localize.

</details>


### [133] [Integrating Novelty and Surprise for Experience Prioritization and Exploration in Image-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17373)
*Hoda Yamani,Henry Williams,Bruce A. MacDonald*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NSPER introduces novelty and surprise signals into prioritized experience replay for image-based RL; NSPER+R extends this by incorporating these signals as intrinsic rewards; both methods improve sample efficiency and convergence on the DeepMind Control Suite.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sample inefficiency in RL with high-dimensional visual inputs and the partial effectiveness of existing approaches like PER and intrinsic rewards when used separately. A unified mechanism to prioritize informative experiences while promoting exploration is desirable.

Method: NSPER uses two intrinsic signals—novelty to emphasize underrepresented states and surprise to reveal environment model gaps—to drive prioritization in the replay buffer. NSPER+R further integrates these signals as intrinsic rewards during learning to jointly enhance replay quality and exploration.

Result: Empirical evaluation on image-based DeepMind Control Suite tasks shows that NSPER and NSPER+R achieve faster learning and better convergence than existing baselines.

Conclusion: Jointly leveraging novelty and surprise for replay prioritization and intrinsic rewards provides improved sample efficiency in image-based RL, with potential for broader applicability; future work could include ablations, comparison to more baselines, and extensions to other domains.

Abstract: Sample efficiency is a central challenge in reinforcement learning (RL), particularly in image-based domains where agents must learn from high-dimensional visual inputs. Traditional sampling often relies on random or suboptimal experience selection, leading to redundant updates and slow learning. Improving efficiency requires mechanisms that prioritize informative experiences while also encouraging effective exploration. Prioritized Experience Replay (PER) addresses part of this challenge by reusing high-value transitions, while intrinsic rewards promote the exploration of novel or uncertain states. However, their integration has not been extensively studied. This paper introduces Novelty and Surprise Prioritized Experience Replay (NSPER), which uses novelty to capture underrepresented states and surprise to expose gaps in the agent's understanding of the environment. We further extend this with NSPER+R, integrating these signals as intrinsic rewards to jointly improve replay quality and exploration. Experiments on DeepMind Control Suite tasks show that NSPER and NSPER+R improve training efficiency and convergence speed compared to existing methods in image-based RL.

</details>


### [134] [GUPO: Gradient Uncertainty-aware Policy Optimization for Post-Training Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17411)
*Peizheng Guo,Jianqi Zhang,Xingyu Zhang,Yun Fan,Jiahuan Zhou,Changwen Zheng,Wenwen Qiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GUPO introduces gradient uncertainty into Group Relative Policy Optimization by treating each group gradient as a Bayesian random variable and calibrating its contribution via a Dirichlet-based uncertainty measure, addressing conflicting group gradients in GRPO and improving policy updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Group gradients within a mini-batch in GRPO can be conflicting, reducing the quality of the aggregated policy update; existing methods treat gradients deterministically and ignore reliability differences.

Method: Model each group gradient as a random variable under a Bayesian framework; estimate its probability distribution; compute gradient uncertainty using a Dirichlet-based formulation; use the uncertainty to weight/group-gradient contributions during aggregation.

Result: Empirical evaluation across several benchmarks shows that GUPO yields more effective and robust policy updates than standard GRPO by accounting for gradient reliability during aggregation.

Conclusion: Incorporating gradient uncertainty into GRPO via a Dirichlet-based Bayesian model leads to more reliable aggregated updates under conflict, improving post-training LLM reasoning policies; the approach has potential applicability to other grouped-gradient optimization settings.

Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has become a widely used approach for post-training Large Language Models (LLMs) for reasoning. In GRPO, the group gradients induced by different queries within the same mini-batch are directly averaged to form the policy update. However, these group gradients can point in conflicting directions. Our empirical analysis suggests that group-gradient conflicts tend to be associated with less effective policy updates, motivating the need for a reliable aggregated update direction under such conflicts. Standard GRPO aggregation treats the realized group gradients as deterministic contributions and does not account for differences in their reliability during aggregation. To address this issue, we propose Gradient Uncertainty-Aware Policy Optimization (GUPO), which models each group gradient as a random variable under a Bayesian formulation and estimates its probability distribution. GUPO then derives gradient uncertainty using a Dirichlet-based formulation and uses it to calibrate the contribution of each group gradient during aggregation. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of GUPO.

</details>


### [135] [General Semantic Knowledge Infusion for Spatio-Temporal Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17440)
*Mattis thor Straten,Yannick Wolker,Steffen Strohm,Prathvish Mithare,Ralf Krestel,Matthias Renz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Augment GNN-based traffic forecasting with semantic embeddings from general-purpose knowledge graphs (e.g., Wikidata) by constructing semantic subgraphs around sensors, generating KG embeddings, and fusing them as additional adjacency matrices to sensor graphs, improving accuracy and potential interpretability without new GNN architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the limitation of relying solely on sensor proximity/topology in spatio-temporal traffic forecasting by incorporating external semantic knowledge to enrich context and improve prediction accuracy.

Method: Construct semantic subgraphs around traffic sensors using a general Knowledge Graph (e.g., Wikidata). Generate KG embeddings capturing relationships such as nearby POIs, administrative hierarchies, and location roles. Fuse KG-derived adjacency matrices with conventional sensor graphs to provide semantics-informed connections. Evaluate using established traffic forecasting baselines to isolate the impact of external knowledge rather than architectural novelty.

Result: Knowledge-augmented graphs improve prediction accuracy over sensor-only approaches. External knowledge provides information not captured by street networks alone and can enhance interpretability by revealing semantic relationships underlying traffic patterns.

Conclusion: External knowledge graphs can meaningfully augment GNN-based traffic forecasting without altering core GNN architectures, offering improved accuracy and potential interpretability; this approach is generalizable across datasets and forecasting methods.

Abstract: Although Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have made significant advances in spatio-temporal traffic forecasting, their performance is limited when they rely solely on sensor proximity or road-network topology. This paper presents a spatio-temporal prediction framework, developed to incorporate knowledge in various forms. This framework aims to improve sensor-level, contextual understanding of the environment. A general-purpose knowledge graph (e.g., Wikidata) is used to create semantic subgraphs around traffic sensors and generate knowledge graph embeddings that capture meaningful relationships, such as nearby points of interest, administrative hierarchies, and the functional roles of locations. These embeddings are then fused with conventional traffic sensor graphs to provide additional adjacency matrices informed by semantics. This allows GNNs to learn the semantic context beyond physical connectivity. This study differs from previous research in two key ways. Firstly, rather than proposing a novel GNN architecture, it demonstrates the general impact of external knowledge on prediction accuracy. Secondly, experiments with well-established traffic forecasting approaches show that external knowledge provides additional information that street network data alone cannot convey. The results show that integrating data from general-purpose knowledge graphs and sensor networks through data fusion can enhance the prediction accuracy of traffic forecasting models, and offers a potential pathway toward improved interpretability.

</details>


### [136] [Causal Local States: Scalable Simultaneous Causal Network Inference and Forecasting for Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17452)
*Jonas Braun,Fabian Fischbach,Daniel Köglmayr,Sebastian Baur,Christoph Räth*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CLS jointly infers Granger-causal interaction networks and forecasts system dynamics by per-node selection of smallest predictive neighbor sets, enabling explainable, scalable forecasting without a global hyperparameter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ML models are often black boxes; causal discovery alone lacks predictive validation; existing methods relying on global hyperparameters cannot capture structure in heterogeneous systems; a method is needed to integrate causal structure learning with forecasting across heterogeneous dynamics.

Method: Introduce causal local states (CLS). For each node, identify the smallest set of neighbors that yields near-optimal predictive performance; combine the resulting local neighborhoods to forecast the full system.

Result: On three benchmark datasets of increasing difficulty, CLS achieves high-fidelity reconstruction of the underlying interaction networks and forecasts comparable to a model given the true network.

Conclusion: CLS offers explainable and scalable forecasting by jointly recovering causal structure and predicting dynamics, addressing heterogeneity and avoiding reliance on a single global hyperparameter.

Abstract: Machine learning methods predict many real-world systems with remarkable accuracy, but they are typically treated as black boxes that offer no insight into which interactions drive the dynamics. Causal discovery methods reconstruct the interaction network from observational data, but without regard to whether the inferred structure supports prediction. Existing approaches combining both tasks rely on a single global hyperparameter, such as a causal threshold or a fixed neighborhood size, which cannot recover the structure of heterogeneous systems. Here we introduce causal local states (CLS), a framework that simultaneously infers an approximate Granger-causal interaction network and forecasts the system dynamics. For each node independently, we select the smallest set of neighbors that allows a predictive model to forecast the node near-optimally, and the resulting neighborhoods are then combined for a forecast of the full system. On three benchmarks of increasing difficulty, we achieve reconstruction of the underlying networks with high fidelity and forecasts on par with a model that is supplied with the true network, providing a step toward explainable and scalable forecasting of complex systems.

</details>


### [137] [Evaluating RL Explainability Methods by How Much They Help Fix Bugs in Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17524)
*Ram Rachum,Yotam Amitai,Bálint Gyevnár,Reuth Mirsky,Cameron Allen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes EvalXRL, a benchmark to evaluate explainable RL methods by diagnosing and repairing malfunctioning RL agents via an interactive, closed-loop process using an LLM coding agent; evaluates across environment x malfunction x XRL method tuples, scoring by resulting RL reward.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current XRL evaluations rely on functionally-grounded or human-grounded proxies; there is a need for end-to-end, task-oriented evaluation of explanations that reflects their impact on diagnosing and fixing real RL agents.

Method: Introduce EvalXRL: a held-out malfunction in an RL agent is diagnosed and repaired by an LLM-based coding agent that can use different XRL methods iteratively. The benchmark explores tuples of (environment, malfunction, XRL method) and uses the RL agent's reward as the final score. The process mirrors the scientific method in a closed-loop manner.

Result: Conceptual, self-contained framework for evaluation; no empirical results yet. Enables head-to-head comparison of multiple XRL methods in closed-loop usage and outlines a plan for future experiments.

Conclusion: If realized, EvalXRL could standardize end-to-end evaluation of XRL explanations by measuring their effectiveness in diagnosing and repairing malfunctioning agents, potentially accelerating practical advances in XRL.

Abstract: This preliminary paper outlines a planned evaluation benchmark for Explainable Reinforcement Learning (XRL) methods. Current evaluations rely on functionally-grounded metrics like faithfulness and compactness, and on human-grounded proxies like subjective ratings or prediction accuracy. We suggest evaluating XRL methods by how effectively their generated explanations help to diagnose and fix malfunctioning reinforcement learning (RL) agents. We propose EvalXRL, a benchmark in which a Large Language Model (LLM) coding agent uses different XRL methods to diagnose a held-out malfunction in an RL agent, and then repair it.
  Our proposed benchmark iterates across (environment $\times$ malfunction $\times$ XRL method) tuples and uses the reward signal of the RL agents to form a final score for each XRL method. The coding agent may use the method interactively: invoke the XRL method, process its output, form new hypotheses on what is broken, and invoke the method again with parameters adjusted for testing these hypotheses. This closed-loop structure may be described as a simplified version of the scientific method. Some XRL methods provide self-evaluations that follow this pattern; we propose the first head-to-head comparison of multiple XRL methods in closed-loop usage.

</details>


### [138] [No Gaussian Required: Contrastive Inverse Dynamics for JEPA World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17542)
*Jack Boylan,Chris Hokamp*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AC-MTM adds a training-time inverse-dynamics contrastive objective to LeWM's JEPA, providing a distribution-free anti-collapse signal that eliminates the need for reconstruction or target networks, and yields stable training with strong performance, especially on challenging multi-object scenes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: JEPAs are prone to collapse (constant encoder). Existing anti-collapse methods (e.g., SIGReg) impose a target latent distribution; a data-driven anti-collapse signal derived from action-conditioned transitions could be more robust and simpler.

Method: Extend LeWM's forward latent-prediction objective with a training-only inverse-dynamics head trained via Action-NCE. The head identifies the action that produced each latent transition among batch actions (a discrimination task). After training, the inverse branch is discarded; test-time remains unchanged: encoding, forward prediction, planning.

Result: AC-MTM trains stably from scratch on four standard pixel-control tasks and matches SIGReg on average under a matched planning protocol. On the harder OGBench Visual Scene task, it achieves 80.0±2.0% success vs 58.0±2.0% with SIGReg, a 20–24 point improvement per seed. A 50-episode random-policy baseline yields ~52%. The approach provides a distribution-free anti-collapse signal without target networks, stop-gradient, pretrained encoders, or reconstruction objectives.

Conclusion: Contrastive inverse dynamics offers an effective, data-driven anti-collapse mechanism for JEPAs. It relies on realistic action-space and observability assumptions, requires no auxiliary targets during deployment, and is accompanied by public code for reproduction.

Abstract: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) learn world models by predicting future embeddings, but the objective admits a trivial solution of a constant encoder, so every practical system adds an anti-collapse mechanism (LeCun, 2022; Assran et al., 2023; Bardes et al., 2022; 2024). LeWorldModel (LeWM) prevents collapse with SIGReg, a regularizer that forces the latent distribution to match an isotropic Gaussian: the representation is stabilized by prescribing what it must look like, independently of the environment it models. We argue that the anti-collapse pressure can instead come from the transition data itself. Action-Contrastive Masked Transition Modeling (AC-MTM) keeps LeWM's forward latent-prediction objective and adds a training-only inverse-dynamics head trained with Action-NCE: each latent transition must identify the action that produced it among the other actions in the batch, a discrimination task that a collapsed encoder provably fails. The inverse branch is discarded after training, leaving test-time encoding, forward prediction, planning, and compute identical to LeWM. On four standard pixel-control tasks under a matched planning protocol, AC-MTM trains stably from scratch and matches SIGReg on average. On the harder multi-object OGBench Visual Scene task, results are consistent with the prescribed geometry becoming a bottleneck: AC-MTM reaches 80.0$\pm$2.0% success versus 58.0$\pm$2.0% for SIGReg, improving by 20-24 points in each training seed. A single 50-episode random-policy run gives a 52% baseline estimate. Contrastive inverse dynamics thus provides a distribution-free anti-collapse signal that requires no target network, stop-gradient, pretrained encoder, or reconstruction objective, and we characterize the action-space and observability assumptions under which it holds. We make our code available at https://github.com/jackboyla/action-contrastive-jepa

</details>


### [139] [Domain-Adapted Molecular Language Models for Efficient Search of Make-on-Demand Libraries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17567)
*Henrik Wille,Luis-Finley Schütz,Felix Strieth-Kalthoff*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Domain-adapted molecular language model representations outperform vanilla embeddings in domain-specific virtual screening; fine-tuning on target libraries improves sample efficiency and yields top-performing encoders, while fingerprints remain a strong baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess practical utility of pretrained molecular LMs for discovery across diverse chemical domains and quantify domain-representation mismatch, with the goal of identifying effective adaptation strategies.

Method: Benchmark four molecular language models across six virtual libraries spanning drug discovery, organic materials, and catalysis. Compare native LM embeddings to fingerprints. Apply explicit domain adaptation and fine-tune encoders on structures from target libraries. Evaluate representation quality and sample efficiency across tasks.

Result: Native LM embeddings vary substantially across libraries; fingerprints provide a robust baseline. Explicit domain adaptation substantially improves representation performance. Fine-tuning LM encoders on target library structures consistently enhances sample efficiency, with adapted encoders frequently becoming top performers across benchmark tasks. Domain matters for representation quality; adaptation broadens practical utility.

Conclusion: Domain-adapted molecular representations are a promising strategy for sample-efficient, adaptive decision making in virtual screening and self-driving laboratories, and explicit adaptation can significantly improve the usefulness of molecular foundation models.

Abstract: Pretrained molecular language models are increasingly used as molecular encoders for learning structure-property relationships. However, their practical suitability for molecular discovery within and beyond their pretraining domain remains unclear. Herein, we systematically benchmark four molecular language models across six virtual molecular libraries spanning drug discovery, organic materials, and catalysis. Native molecular language model embeddings show substantial variation in discovery performance across libraries, whereas molecular fingerprints provide a consistently strong and robust baseline. Consistent with a potential domain-representation mismatch, we show that explicit domain adaptation substantially improves representation performance. Fine-tuning molecular language model encoders on structures from the target virtual library consistently improves sample efficiency, with several adapted encoders emerging as the top-performing representations across the benchmark tasks. These results show that molecular representation quality depends strongly on the target domain and that explicit adaptation can improve the practical utility of molecular foundation models. More broadly, our findings establish domain-adapted molecular representations as a promising strategy for sample-efficient adaptive decision making in virtual screening and self-driving laboratories.

</details>


### [140] [OOD Detection for EEG-based Machine Learning in High-Risk Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17620)
*Philipp Bomatter,Henry Gouk*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes an EEG OOD detection benchmark and evaluates diverse methods, illustrating that disentangling OOD detection from model uncertainty and combining complementary approaches improves reliability for downstream clinical tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG analyses are vulnerable to distribution shifts, leading to overconfident failures; EEG-specific OOD evaluation and its impact on downstream clinical tasks are underexplored.

Method: Introduce a benchmark for EEG OOD detection; benchmark a broad range of OOD detection methods and uncertainty estimation approaches; assess their value in two clinical downstream prediction tasks.

Result: Finds that OOD detection and uncertainty estimation are distinct capabilities; identifies state-of-the-art methods for EEG OOD detection; shows how complementary methods can be combined to provide a robust safety net for EEG deployments in real-world applications.

Conclusion: Establishes EEG OOD detection benchmarking as a foundation; demonstrates the practical benefit of integrating detection and uncertainty estimation for safer EEG-based machine learning in clinical settings.

Abstract: Machine learning models for electroencephalography (EEG) analysis show great promise across a wide range of applications, but their deployment in high-risk domains is hindered by their vulnerability to distribution shifts. Encountering out-of-distribution (OOD) data can lead to catastrophic, overconfident predictive failures. While OOD detection methods can mitigate these risks, they remain heavily under-explored for EEG. Moreover, evaluations in the broader literature typically evaluate OOD detection performance in isolation, ignoring their practical impact on downstream applications. To bridge this gap, we introduce a benchmark for EEG OOD detection, evaluate a broad range of methods, and furthermore evaluate their value in two clinical downstream prediction task. Our results disentangle OOD detection and model uncertainty estimation capabilities, which are frequently conflated in the literature, provide actionable insights about the current state of the art for EEG OOD detection and model uncertainty estimation, and demonstrate how complementary methods for both aspects can be combined to form a robust safety net for the deployment of EEG-based machine learning models in real-world applications.

</details>


### [141] [rl-triton: High-Performance Triton GPU Kernels for Reinforcement Learning Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17641)
*Lars Simon Zehnder*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Unifies seven RL credit-assignment estimators via a single associative-scan recurrence in Triton, enabling O(log T) parallel computation and achieving 1.6–5.7x speedups over a Torch baseline in large-scale parallel RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve performance and consistency of RL credit-assignment computations by collapsing disparate estimators into a common primitive, leveraging GPU parallelism and minimizing memory traffic while correctly handling episode termination/truncation.

Method: Introduce rl-triton library with a single associative scan operator; implement seven algorithms by constructing on-chip recurrence coefficients within fused Triton kernels; verify algebraically; explicitly handle terminated and truncated episodes; evaluate on GPUs across various sequence lengths and truncation modes; complexity O(log T) with parallel steps.

Result: Reported speedups range 1.6–5.7x full-call over vectorized torch.compile baseline in massively parallel regime; speedups tend to increase with longer sequences; consistent across all seven algorithms and GPUs, with/without per-step truncation handling.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a unified, high-performance approach to RL credit assignment that scales with sequence length, reduces kernel diversification, and provides an open-source tool for efficient RL simulations.

Abstract: We present rl-triton, an open-source library of high-performance GPU kernels for reinforcement learning credit assignment, implemented in Triton. The core contribution is a unified associative scan framework that recasts seven distinct RL estimation algorithms - Generalized Advantage Estimation (GAE), V-Trace, Retrace($λ$), TD($λ$) returns, discounted returns, eligibility traces, and episodic prefix sums - as instances of a single first-order linear recurrence solved in $O(\log T)$ parallel steps. All algorithms share the same associative scan operator, with algorithm-specific fused Triton kernels constructing their recurrence coefficients on-chip. We verify the associative operator algebraically and define the treatment of terminated and truncated episodes explicitly. Benchmarks show a 1.6-5.70$\times$ full-call speedup over a vectorized torch.compile baseline in the massively parallel simulation regime (thousands of environments, short rollouts). The reported range covers all seven algorithms on both GPUs, both with and without per-step truncation handling. For most algorithms, speedups increase at longer sequence lengths, as the baseline requires more scan stages as $\log T$ grows, each adding an intermediate HBM round-trip. The library is available at https://github.com/simonsays1980/rl-triton.

</details>


### [142] [Elimination Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17646)
*Mian Huang,Xueqin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A formal framework (Elimination Geometry) to analyze when a shared deployment rule can realize locally optimal objects, distinguishing local solvability, global realizability, and finite-sample certifiability, with tools, results, and applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To diagnose and repair losses induced by elimination and compression in prediction, inference, control, or representation, and to provide certificates and validation for architecture repair under fixed deployment contracts.

Method: Integrates geometry, optimization, information theory, statistics, and machine learning to define native defects, separate architecture obstruction from model-related errors, and produce certificates. It establishes deployment contracts, native endpoints, competing explanations, budgets, and validation rules, and derives formal results for various mechanisms with explicit antecedents and boundaries.

Result: Formal results characterizing when local solvability implies global realizability and certifiability; diagnostics and interfaces for integrability, representation admissibility, and resource constraints; identification of obstruction sources; reproducible synthetic and real-data studies illustrating how certificates guide architecture repair and document unresolved cases.

Conclusion: Elimination Geometry provides a comprehensive framework to diagnose and repair deployment-induced defects, enabling mechanism-matched interventions, independent validation, and robust performance floors under fixed deployment contracts.

Abstract: This monograph develops elimination geometry (EG), a typed, native-loss, audit-oriented framework for studying when locally optimal objects can be realized by a shared deployment rule. Elimination and compression may erase distinctions required by prediction, inference, control, or representation. EG asks which distinctions are lost, whether the induced defect is visible to the declared task, and whether changing information, architecture, action space, or deployment domain can repair it. EG separates local solvability, global realizability, and finite-sample certifiability. It derives native defects from the original objective and distinguishes architecture obstruction from model approximation, generalization, and implementation error. The monograph synthesizes tools from geometry, optimization, information theory, statistics, and machine learning into interfaces for integrability, representation admissibility, resource constraints, observational overlap, and common deployment. Formal results address regular, coordination, singular, compositional, and resource-limited mechanisms with explicit antecedents and claim boundaries. Applications include sparse model selection, distribution-free prediction, observational treatment policies, routed expert and retrieval systems, and learned score fields. Obstruction-Aware Learning and Inference links structural diagnosis to finite-data authorization, mechanism-matched intervention, and independent validation. Reproducible synthetic and real-data studies illustrate how certificates can guide architecture repair while recording failed gates and unresolved cases. The framework requires the deployment contract, native endpoint, competing explanations, information and compute budgets, and validation rule to be fixed before a persistent performance floor is attributed to architecture.

</details>


### [143] [Picard Proximal Monte Carlo for Parallel Bayesian Imaging with Score-Based Generative Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17666)
*Deliang Wei,Evan Bell,Wenhan Guo,Yifan Chen,Yu Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PiX-MC provides time-parallel Bayesian sampling for imaging using proximal Langevin dynamics plus Picard iteration, with multi-block and annealing variants. It offers convergence guarantees under broad assumptions and achieves substantial wall-clock speedups (e.g., up to 50x on 512×512×80 sparse-view CT with 8 GPUs) while preserving reconstruction quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the inherent sequential bottleneck of diffusion/score-based Bayesian priors in high-dimensional imaging by introducing a parallelizable sampling scheme that leverages proximal operators and Picard refinement.

Method: A proximal-likelihood formulation where the likelihood admits efficient proximal operators; Picard iterations to expose parallelism across discretization nodes; time-parallel Langevin sampling with multi-block and annealing variants; convergence guarantees under transparent assumptions, accommodating non-log-concave posteriors, imperfect learned score models, multi-block implementations, and annealing schedules.

Result: Demonstrates substantial reductions in wall-clock time while maintaining reconstruction quality across diverse imaging inverse problems; on a 512×512×80 sparse-view CT problem, annealed multi-block PiX-MC achieves up to 50× runtime speedup over standard Langevin sampling using eight GPUs.

Conclusion: PiX-MC is a scalable, time-parallel Bayesian sampling framework for imaging that combines proximal Langevin dynamics and Picard refinement to enable multi-GPU acceleration and robust performance across non-ideal conditions; it shows strong practical scalability and preserves reconstruction fidelity.

Abstract: Bayesian imaging inverse problems often require sampling from high-dimensional posterior distributions. While recent score-based and diffusion models provide expressive Bayesian priors, their sampling procedures remain inherently sequential and computationally expensive for large-scale imaging applications. We propose PiX-MC, a time-parallel posterior sampling framework based on proximal Langevin dynamics and Picard iteration. The proximal-likelihood formulation exploits the fact that many imaging likelihoods admit efficient, problem-specific proximal operators, while Picard refinement exposes parallelism across discretization nodes and naturally supports multi-GPU implementation. To further improve practical scalability and sampling performance, we develop multi-block and annealed variants of the proposed framework. We establish convergence guarantees under transparent assumptions, accommodating non-log-concave posteriors, imperfect learned score models, multi-block implementations, and annealing schedules. Experiments on a diverse collection of imaging inverse problems demonstrate that PiX-MC substantially reduces wall-clock time while preserving reconstruction quality. On a $512\times512\times80$ sparse-view computed tomography (CT) problem, annealed multi-block PiX-MC achieves up to a $50\times$ runtime speedup over the standard Langevin sampler using eight GPUs.

</details>


### [144] [Conformal Prediction for Molecular Properties under Label Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17678)
*Hyeonsu Lee,Juyeon Kim,Erkhembayar Jadamba,Seungjin Choi,Hyunjin Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Conformal prediction under label shift for drug property prediction; weights conformal scores by marginal label probability ratios to produce prediction intervals without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable uncertainty quantification in AI-driven drug discovery amid distribution drift and high-stakes experimental design requirements; align with regulatory transparency.

Method: Conformal prediction framework adapted for label shift by weighting conformity scores with marginal label probability ratios; does not require retraining of models; applicable to properties like solubility, potency, and toxicity.

Result: Produces statistically rigorous prediction intervals with valid coverage under label shift; robust uncertainty quantification despite distribution drift; enhances trust and decision-making in drug development pipelines.

Conclusion: Algorithm offers a practical, transparent uncertainty quantification tool for AI in drug discovery, addressing a key barrier to real-world deployment under distribution shifts and regulatory demands.

Abstract: Drug discovery and development underpins healthcare but remains costly and failure-prone. A critical bottleneck lies in predicting molecular properties such as solubility, potency, and toxicity, which directly determine whether a candidate can advance from preclinical to clinical trials. Artificial Intelligence (AI) has accelerated this process, yet its reliability is often undermined by distribution shift, as experimental conditions frequently diverge from training data. In addition, conventional point predictions provide only single-value estimates, offering limited guidance for high-stakes experimental design. We address these challenges with a conformal prediction framework tailored to label shift. By weighting conformal scores using marginal label probability ratios, our method produces statistically rigorous prediction intervals without retraining. This enables robust uncertainty quantification even when property distributions drift, directly tackling one of the most pervasive obstacles to applying AI in real-world drug development. By moving beyond accuracy alone to provide actionable confidence measures, our approach enhances the trustworthiness of AI-driven predictions. This further aligns predictive modeling with regulatory demands for transparency and uncertainty reporting and ultimately supports more reliable decision-making in billion-dollar development pipelines.

</details>


### [145] [Cross-View Correspondence Is a Measurement Intervention: Two-Sided Validation for Agent Evaluation and Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17713)
*Zhen Zhang,Ahmad Hafez,Amr Alanwar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cross-view correspondence in agent evaluation acts as a measurement intervention; without it, comparisons can be biased by the chosen correspondence. The paper proposes a three-part validity framework (nuisance removal validation, all-optima identification, uncertainty propagation), analyzes feasibility and optima of correspondences, and presents empirical audits showing substantial impact of correspondence on credit and conclusions, arguing for mandatory declaration and propagation of cross-view correspondence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prevent spurious sensitivity/invariance and mislabeling of mechanisms in cross-view evaluation; provide a formal validity theory and audit methodology to ensure trustworthy agent assessment and credit assignment.

Method: Develop a three-component validity framework: two-sided validation of nuisance removal and response preservation; all-optima identification of downstream conclusions; uncertainty propagation after validity. Analyze linear feasibility boundaries for nuisance removal preserving responses; characterize exact-optimum correspondence sets; provide a distribution-free certificate. Empirically test across public code/SQL pipelines with tracebacks and frozen audits (800-rollout tool-use, replication) and a pre-registered transport gate, including benign vs harmful response controls.

Result: Found that two deterministic optimal tracebacks disagree on temporal localization in 55.9% of 1,586 nonzero trajectory pairs; exact-optimum reversals of credit observed; some audits show reversals, while a clean subset shows none; calibrations on benign data can erase harmful responses; two-sided validation identifies response-preserving alternatives.

Conclusion: Cross-view correspondence must be declared, validated, and propagated into uncertainty before drawing point conclusions or assigning credit; failing to do so risks biased evaluations and misattributed learning signals.

Abstract: Agent evaluations and trace-based learning often compare outputs across transformed views through a post-response correspondence treated as neutral preprocessing. We show that this correspondence is a measurement intervention: omitting it can manufacture sensitivity, an over-aggressive map can manufacture invariance, and multiple optimal correspondences can leave mechanism labels and signed learning credit unidentified. We develop a validity theory and audit with three components: two-sided validation of nuisance removal and response preservation, all-optima identification of downstream conclusions, and uncertainty propagation after validity is established. We characterize the linear feasibility boundary for response-preserving nuisance removal, compute sharp ranges over exact-optimum correspondence sets, and give a distribution-free certificate that retains a credit coordinate only when all exact optima agree on its nonzero sign. Across public code and SQL pipelines, two deterministic optimal tracebacks disagree on temporal localization for 55.9% of 1,586 nonzero trajectory pairs; two frozen 800-rollout tool-use audits, including a task-and-seed-disjoint replication, expose exact-optimum reversals of intended turn-level credit, although a clean public quick-start subset shows none. A pre-registered transport gate failed on natural responses; frozen corrected and held-out controls then show that a map calibrated only on benign examples erases every retained harmful response, while two-sided validation selects response-preserving alternatives. Cross-view correspondence must therefore be declared, validated, and propagated into uncertainty before agent evaluation or credit assignment supports a point conclusion.

</details>


### [146] [MAGPIE-Net: Predicting short-duration heavy-rainfall events in station neighborhoods from multitemporal FY-4A AGRI observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17753)
*Xiang Lin,Yunying Li,Chengzhi Ye,Zitong Chen,Jing Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MAGPIE-Net introduces a geographically adaptive, differentiable grid-to-station mapping to translate multitemporal satellite signals into station-neighborhood heavy-rainfall warnings, outperforming gridded-output baselines for 0–3 h nowcasting in China.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the disconnect between satellite-derived gridded rainfall predictions and local, station-neighborhood warning targets by directly supervising the satellite-to-station pathway with station-specific losses.

Method: A pathway that fuses convection-initiation features, multiscale encoding, and auxiliary gridded precipitation diagnosis, with a differentiable, geographically adaptive grid-to-station mapping and station-neighborhood event losses guiding 0–3 h predictions.

Result: Independent 2023 warm-season tests in central/eastern China show CSI of 0.371, 0.304, 0.238 for 0–1, 1–2, 2–3 h; detection rate 65.1% and mean lead time 64.6 min vs baselines 23.6% and 18.3 min. MAGPIE-Net also performs better for smaller neighborhoods and 50 mm h⁻¹ events, and achieves 51.9% detection with 38.5 min lead in early warning when near-threshold antecedent rainfall is small.

Conclusion: Event-oriented satellite-to-station modeling translates multitemporal geostationary observations into local heavy-rainfall warnings more effectively than traditional gridded-precipitation modeling.

Abstract: Short-duration heavy-rainfall warning determines whether 1 h rainfall will exceed a threshold within a target-station neighborhood over the next few hours. Multitemporal infrared and water-vapor observations from the Fengyun-4A Advanced Geostationary Radiation Imager (FY-4A AGRI) capture cloud-top cooling, moisture evolution, and cloud expansion before substantial surface rainfall develops. However, most deep-learning nowcasting methods convert these signals into local warnings by post-processing gridded precipitation predictions, preventing station-neighborhood event targets from directly supervising the satellite-to-station learning pathway. We propose MAGPIE-Net, which embeds a geographically adaptive, differentiable grid-to-station mapping in a pathway combining convection-initiation features, multiscale encoding, and auxiliary gridded precipitation diagnosis. Station-neighborhood event losses thereby constrain the satellite representation and its mapping to irregular station locations for 0-3 h event prediction. In independent 2023 warm-season tests over central and eastern China, critical success index (CSI) values under the primary 40 km/20 mm h-1 definition were 0.371, 0.304, and 0.238 at 0-1, 1-2, and 2-3 h. Across episodes, MAGPIE-Net achieved a detection rate of 65.1% and a mean lead time of 64.6 min, compared with 23.6% and 18.3 min for the best gridded-output baseline, and remained superior for smaller neighborhoods and the 50 mm h-1 threshold. During the critical early-warning stage, when antecedent 1 h rainfall within 40 km remained below 1 mm, MAGPIE-Net detected 51.9% of episodes with a mean lead time of 38.5 min. These results show that event-oriented satellite-to-station modeling converts multitemporal geostationary cloud and moisture observations into local heavy-rainfall warnings more effectively than gridded-precipitation modeling.

</details>


### [147] [Training-Free Human-in-the-Loop Anomaly Detection via Memory Bank Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17775)
*Ayusha Abbas,Saram Abbas,Kabita Adhikari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Training-free, human-in-the-loop memory-bank editing for PatchCore anomaly detection shows strong gains with only 10 golden samples and no retraining; corrections close the gap to a fully trained bank, improving most categories with reduced labeling effort.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable anomaly detection deployment on new production lines with scarce labeled data and no access to original training data, reducing retraining and data collection costs.

Method: Operator-corrected normalization patches are inserted into the memory bank via a self-calibrating novelty gate. False positives are corrected using a gating threshold based on the median pool-normal nearest-neighbor distance. Evaluation uses 20 splits per category with Holm-corrected Wilcoxon tests; comparisons are made against an uncorrected fully trained bank; passive vs active querying is analyzed; deployment-time labeling cost is compared to exhaustive review.

Result: From only 10 golden samples, operator corrections close a median 66% of the gap to a fully trained bank (mean 80%), improving 12 of 15 MVTec AD categories and harming none. Ten samples plus corrections outperform hundreds of samples without them. On pre-trained banks, remaining headroom is smaller, with modest gains in certain categories (toothbrush +0.10, metal nut +0.09, zipper +0.05, screw +0.05) and no significant harm except for grid. Evaluation confirms gains are not due to memorization; corrected images inflate AUROC if not properly controlled. Passive and active querying are statistically similar; a matched-label-budget control indicates deployment-time labeling is ~43% of exhaustive-review cost. A defect-memory extension fails decisively. Feedback is simulated; live expert trials are future work.

Conclusion: Memory-bank editing via user corrections offers a practical, data-efficient path to improve anomaly detectors without retraining, with robust gains across most categories and a clear framework for evaluating deployment-time labeling costs. Cautions include evaluation biases, transferability, and the need for live expert validation.

Abstract: Anomaly detectors are hardest to deploy exactly where training data is scarcest: a newly commissioned production line has a handful of verified "golden" samples and no machine-learning engineer on the factory floor. We present a training-free human-in-the-loop framework in which a domain expert corrects a PatchCore detector by direct memory bank editing: no retraining, no gradients, no original training data. A false-positive correction inserts the reviewed image's normal patches through a self-calibrating novelty gate admitting only those beyond the median pool-normal nearest-neighbour distance. From a bank built on only ten golden samples, operator corrections close a median 66% of the gap to an uncorrected fully trained bank (mean 80%, raised by three categories that overshoot parity), significantly improving 12 of 15 MVTec AD categories and harming none: ten samples plus corrections outperform hundreds of samples without them. On already-trained banks the headroom is smaller and concentrated where the bank undersamples normal appearance (gated: toothbrush +0.10, metal nut +0.09, zipper +0.05, screw +0.05), and no category except grid is significantly harmed. Evaluation uses a held-out protocol (20 splits per category, Holm-corrected Wilcoxon), because corrected images entering the bank inflate naive evaluation toward AUROC 1.0 by memorisation. Passive and active querying are statistically indistinguishable; a matched-label-budget control attributes gains to deployment-time label production at 43% of exhaustive-review cost; a defect-memory extension fails decisively. Feedback is simulated from ground truth; live expert trials, where mislabelling is costliest on small banks, remain future work.

</details>


### [148] [Debate Training Reduces Reward Hacking in RLAIF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17776)
*Zachary Kenton,Lili Janzer,Rory Greig,Tian Huey Teh,Kirill Tyshchuk,Jonah Brown-Cohen,Harri Edwards,Senthooran Rajamanoharan,Noah Y. Siegel,Natasha Jaques,Rohin Shah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Debate-based RL finetuning of LLMs (generator vs. critic) reduces reward hacking compared to RLAIF when the judge is weaker than the policy. In math tasks with verifiable final answers, debate yields higher peak validation accuracy and more stable judge performance; adding debate rounds and word-limited critique helps mitigate hacking, though at expressiveness costs. Overall, balance in multi-agent training is crucial for robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reward hacking is a key failure mode in RL from AI feedback. When the judge is weaker than the policy, the system is prone to exploiting judge biases.
