The paper investigates whether a two-player debate framework can alleviate this by making the critic's judgment more robust through adversarial interaction.

Method: Train a Gemini 2.5 Flash-class policy with a frozen Gemini 2.5 Flash Lite judge. Compare RL with AI feedback (RLAIF) baseline to a debate setup where a generator and a critic argue, adjudicated by the weaker judge. Evaluate on mathematics tasks with verifiable final answers. Explore: (1) effect of judge strength on hacking; (2) adding more debate rounds; (3) prompting alignment versus critique strategies; (4) learning to critique using ground-truth labels. Include constraints like word-limited critique (~150 words).

Result: Debate reduces reward hacking and maintains judge performance during RL training. The debate setup achieves a larger peak validation accuracy (recovering ~45% of the gap relative to baseline) and preserves performance across many RL steps. When the judge is weakened, hacking accelerates, but a second debate round compensates. Debate incentives can override prompts misalignment. RL with an LLM judge shows a smaller train/validation reward gap than RLAIF with verifiable rewards. Ground-truth critique is possible but slow. Word-limited critique (≈150 words) balances the game and mitigates hacking, though it limits critic expressiveness.

Conclusion: Debate is a promising enhancement to RL with LLMs, offering robustness against reward hacking and improved performance, but requires careful balancing of multi-agent dynamics. Without constraints, adversarial training risks critic-hacking; small, measured critique channels (word-limits) help stabilize training while trading off some expressive clarity.

Abstract: We demonstrate that RL finetuning an LLM using debate, a two-player adversarial game between a generator and a critic adjudicated by a weaker LLM judge, reduces reward hacking compared to a reinforcement learning from AI feedback (RLAIF) baseline. Reward hacking is a central obstacle in RLAIF: as training progresses, the policy learns to exploit systematic errors in its AI judge, degrading task performance, a problem that worsens precisely when the judge is weaker than the policy, the setting most relevant to overseeing increasingly capable AI systems. We study mathematics tasks, where final-answer correctness is verifiable, allowing us to measure reward hacking dynamics. We train a Gemini~2.5 Flash-class policy with a frozen, weaker Gemini~2.5 Flash Lite judge, comparing a single-player RLAIF baseline against debate. While the baseline quickly hacks the judge, debate maintains judge performance throughout training, leading to a higher peak validation accuracy (45\% performance gap recovered) that persists through many RL steps. Additional experiments show that: 1) further weakening the judge leads to faster hacking, but this can be compensated by adding an additional debate round; 2) debate incentives override prompted misalignment; 3) RL using an LLM judge has a smaller train/validation reward gap than RL from verifiable rewards; 4) learning to critique to convince the judge using ground truth labels is possible but slow. Taken together, our results are a positive update on the feasibility of debate, while highlighting that balancing multi-agent training is critical: without player constraints, adversarial training risks defaulting to critic judge-hacking. We show that critique word limits (effective up to 150 words) successfully balance the game and avoid judge hacking, though this introduces a trade-off by restricting critic expressive clarity.

</details>


### [149] [Fourth-Moment Geometry of Rademacher Sums](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17802)
*Peigan Gao,Jian Qian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sharp moment inequalities for normalized Rademacher sums controlled by fourth-order mass; establish Gaussian stability for p≥4; determine sharp L_p/L_4 Khintchine constants for p≥5 with flat-vector extremizers; resolve conjectures of Jakimiuk and Barański–Murawski–Nayar–Oleszkiewicz; prove p=3 quadratic stability; bounds preserve sparsity/effective dimension; Laplace-transform form yields tail bounds; proofs aided by ChatGPT 5.6 Sol.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify how higher moments of a normalized Rademacher sum depend on its fourth-order mass; derive sharp moment envelopes and stability results across p; settle conjectures on Khintchine-type constants and their extremizers; provide coefficient-sensitive tail bounds and sparsity-aware insights.

Method: Develops a sharp fixed-q moment envelope and a separate convexity-threshold argument; introduces a fourth-order framework tying moments to the mass; derives Gaussian stability for the full p≥4 range; computes sharp finite-dimensional L_p/L_4 Khintchine constants for p≥5 with flat coefficient vectors as extremizers; validates conjectures and proves quadratic stability at p=3; expresses results via Laplace transforms to obtain tail bounds; notes that ChatGPT-assisted discovery informed the proofs.

Result: Gaussian stability inequality for p≥4; sharp finite-dimensional L_p/L_4 Khintchine constants for p≥5 with flat-vector extremizer; resolution of Jakimiuk’s conjecture and Barański–Murawski–Nayar–Oleszkiewicz conjectures; quadratic stability at p=3; bounds retain sparsity and effective dimension; coefficient-sensitive tail bounds via Laplace transform; applications to Rademacher projections and randomly signed errors mentioned.

Conclusion: The work settles central conjectures in the area, delivering sharp, coefficient-sensitive moment and tail bounds for Rademacher sums and clarifying extremizers; practical implications for sparse representations and random projections, though applications are not developed here; the proofs leveraged ChatGPT-assisted discovery.

Abstract: Let $\varepsilon_1,\ldots,\varepsilon_n$ be independent Rademacher signs and let $a=(a_1,\ldots,a_n)\in\R^n$ satisfy the normalization below. For the normalized Rademacher sum, we determine how its higher moments depend on the fourth-order mass. Combining a sharp fixed-q moment envelope with a separate argument below the convexity threshold gives the Gaussian stability inequality for the full range $p\geq4$ of this linear-in-q bound. The same fourth-order framework determines the sharp finite dimensional $L_p/L_4$ Khintchine constant for $p\geq5$, with the flat coefficient vector as the extremizer. These results settle the conjectures of Jakimiuk and of Barański, Murawski, Nayar, and Oleszkiewicz stated below. We also prove Jakimiuk's conjectured quadratic stability estimate at $p=3$. The resulting bounds retain information about sparsity and effective dimension, with applications to Rademacher random projections and randomly signed errors; those applications are not developed further here. Their Laplace-transform form also gives coefficient-sensitive tail bounds. The proofs are discovered with substantial assistance from ChatGPT 5.6 Sol.

</details>


### [150] [An Empirical Study of Reward Specification and Benchmark Reliability in GRPO-based LLM Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17804)
*Rubén Balbastre,Juan Manuel Orduña,Mariano Pérez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Optimization for unlearning target-specific knowledge in LLMs (RWKU-GRPO) with four reward designs reveals that optimization success does not guarantee true behavioral unlearning; endpoint-focused evaluation and reward-hacking risks complicate conclusions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practical LLM unlearning requires suppressing target leakage while preserving non-target utility; existing objective definitions may miss intermediate behaviors when prompts allow broader answers.

Method: Controlled LoRA-GRPO RWKU setup; four reward designs spanning lexical suppression, anti-refusal shaping, rubric-based broad answering, explicit refusal contrast; analyses with and without SFT warm-up; multiple evaluation metrics including RWKU forget scores, held-out completion audits, terminal rollout audits, and training dynamics.

Result: Optimization success is not equivalent to behavioral unlearning; different metrics yield different conclusions. Disagreements arise from reward-hacking endpoints, policy-support limits in GRPO, benchmark probes that miss endpoint changes, and rewards that encourage broad-topic answering with low semantic leakage while optimizing.

Conclusion: Robust unlearning evaluation requires multi-metric assessment and defenses against reward hacking; design reward signals and benchmarks that align with endpoint behavior and account for the possibility of broad, non-leakage but non-falsifiable answers; future work should address benchmark design, reward shaping, and evaluation protocol to reconcile divergent metrics.

Abstract: Practical LLM unlearning is usually evaluated through two objectives: suppress target-specific knowledge and preserve non-target utility. In generative QA, this leaves a third behavior underspecified: when a target-adjacent prompt admits a broader answer without target-specific leakage, the model should answer at that level rather than leak, evade, or refuse. We study this specification problem in a controlled LoRA-GRPO RWKU setting, comparing four reward designs that span lexical suppression, anti-refusal shaping, rubric-based broad answering, and an explicit refusal contrast, with and without SFT warm-up. The experiments show that optimization success is not equivalent to behavioral unlearning: RWKU forget scores, held-out completion audits, terminal training-rollout audits, and training dynamics can point to different conclusions. We trace these disagreements to reward-hacking endpoints, policy-support limits in GRPO, benchmark probes that miss endpoint changes, and rewards that can select broad-topic answering with low semantic leakage during optimization.

</details>


### [151] [MotoSafety: Edge-AI with Learned Temporal Importance for Two-Wheeler Collision Risk Assessment Under Time Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17823)
*Sumit S. Shevtekar,Chandresh K. Maurya,Gourab Sil,Subasish Das*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Large-scale PTW safety dataset and lightweight edge AI (MotoSafety) achieving high accuracy and low latency for collision risk under Time Pressure; strong transferability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap on cognitive stressors (Time Pressure) affecting collision risk for powered two-wheeler riders in LMICs; existing studies are scarce; a deployment-friendly, edge-capable risk assessment tool is needed.

Method: Dataset: 129k labeled multivariate time-series from 153 simulator rides, 51 participants, under No/Low/High Time Pressure, across 64 features (vehicle dynamics, control inputs, proximity, violations). Introduce MotoSafety, an edge-AI architecture based on Learned Temporal Importance. 1.15M parameters, 0.135 ms CPU latency. Evaluation: accuracy 94.97%, ROC AUC 99.33%, MSE 0.039, MAE 0.094; baselines include TimesNet and LLM4TS; time-LLM/iTransformer show 4.4x higher error. Ground-truth Time Pressure as inductive bias improves accuracy to 94.97%; predicted TP yields 94.82%. Using only 21 IMU+GPS features yields 93.91% accuracy. Transferability: 97.66% to human activity domain, 99.65% to clinical domain.

Result: Dataset and model performance demonstrating strong predictive accuracy and low-latency inference; resource-efficient edge deployment; improved performance with inductive bias; practical feature subset and broad transferability.

Conclusion: MotoSafety advances PTW collision risk assessment within the Safe System framework; effective edge deployment on low-cost hardware; notable transferability to related domains and potential for real-world safety interventions.

Abstract: Powered two-wheeler riders face critical safety challenges in low- and middle-income countries, yet limited studies exist on how cognitive stressors such as Time Pressure influence collision risk. To address this gap, we introduce a large-scale dataset of over 129,000 labeled multivariate time-series sequences from 153 simulator rides by 51 participants under No, Low, and High TP, capturing 64 features across vehicle dynamics, control inputs, proximity, and behavioral violations. Building on this dataset, we propose MotoSafety, a novel edge-AI architecture grounded in the Learned Temporal Importance principle. MotoSafety achieves 94.97% accuracy and 99.33% ROC AUC, outperforming ten baselines, including TimesNet and LLM4TS, and achieves 0.039 MSE and 0.094 MAE for forecasting (4.4x lower error than Time-LLM and iTransformer). With only 1.15M parameters and 0.135 ms latency, it is suitable for edge deployment on low-cost CPU hardware. Using ground truth TP as an inductive bias improves accuracy from 94.09% to 94.97%, while predicted TP achieves 94.82%. Using only 21 IMU+GPS features, it achieves 93.91% accuracy, indicating practical deployment. Beyond PTW safety, the architecture shows better transferability to human activity (97.66%) and clinical (99.65%) domains. This lightweight framework advances PTW collision risk assessment, supporting the Safe System Approach for Intelligent Transportation Systems.

</details>


### [152] [Leveraging Association Context Retrieval in Knowledge Edit- ing to Build White-Box Attacks on LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17836)
*Roman Maksimov,Vladimir Aletov,Vladimir Solodkin,Dmitry Bylinkin,Daniil Medyakov,Aleksandr Beznosikov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A white-box attack on LLMs using a locate-then-edit framework augmented with the model's associative knowledge to extend constraint removal from prompts to entire thematic categories; achieves stronger attack effectiveness than prior methods with limited collateral damage to general performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs gain autonomy, it is crucial to study methods that can induce unsafe behavior. Edit-based approaches reveal vulnerabilities by giving the model unusually high probability to edit targets; extending this to thematic categories broadens the attack surface.

Method: Modify the knowledge-editing framework to retrieve associative knowledge from the model and apply constraint removal to an entire thematic category rather than a predefined dataset; evaluate across architectures to assess attack efficacy.

Result: Experiments show improved attack effectiveness over competing methods without substantially harming general model performance across different architectures.

Conclusion: Category-level, associative knowledge–driven edits expose deeper vulnerabilities in LLMs and suggest that defenses should consider not only prompt-level controls but also internal associative knowledge retrieval and category-wide constraints.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) are granted increasing autonomy, it is essential to investigate methods that can induce unsafe behavior. We propose a novel white-box attack inspired by locate-then-edit approaches from the field of Knowledge Editing. Our choice is motivated by the observation that models edited with such schemes tend to assign unusually high prediction probabilities to the edit target, a property that is particularly advantageous when designing attacks. We modify the editing framework by incorporating as- sociative knowledge retrieved from the model, thereby extending constraint removal to an entire thematic category rather than being limited to prompts from a predefined dataset. Experiments with various archi- tectures demonstrate improved attack effectiveness over competing methods without dealing critical damage to general model performance.

</details>


### [153] [MoRAX: Mobility-based Representation Augmentation for Geospatial Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17848)
*Ya Wen,Jixuan Cai,Yulun Zhou,Alec Kirkley*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MoRAX augments geospatial foundation models with human mobility-derived functional structure to improve urban representations; teacher models use mobility to outperform GFMs, while a mobility-free student approaches teacher performance; zero-shot transfer across unseen cities and countries demonstrates generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GFMs trained on Earth-observation data underrepresent human activity and functional connectivity. Human mobility reveals inter-regional relationships necessary for transferable urban representations, enabling zero-shot deployment in cities without mobility data.

Method: MoRAX introduces a lightweight framework that injects mobility-derived functional structure into geospatial embeddings. A teacher model observes mobility data to learn augmented representations; a student model is trained to imitate the teacher without using mobility data, preserving GFM coverage and spatial consistency. The approach supports zero-shot deployment in unseen cities and transfers across countries, with modulation conditioned on mobility flows to ground geospatial foundations in the human dimension.

Result: Across four target cities in two countries, the mobility-informed teacher consistently beats GFMs and strong urban baselines on eight socioeconomic and environmental prediction tasks. The mobility-free student closely approaches the teacher on most tasks. Cross-country transfers show that mobility-conditioned modulation provides a general mechanism for grounding geospatial representations in human activity.

Conclusion: Incorporating mobility-derived functional structure into GFMs yields more accurate, human-aligned urban representations and enables zero-shot transfer, while preserving GFM coverage. The modulation by mobility flows offers a general strategy for grounding geospatial foundations in the human dimension of cities.

Abstract: Geospatial Foundation Models (GFMs) are emerging as a powerful paradigm for learning semantically rich and geographically consistent visual and physical representations. However, their reliance on Earth-observation (EO) data leaves information about human activity largely underrepresented. Human mobility data reveals the functional and relational structure between regions that is missing from EO data, but is often limited only to the city where it is observed, making it challenging to use for transferable urban representation learning. We introduce MoRAX, a lightweight framework for augmenting geospatial embeddings with functional structure derived from human mobility. MoRAX preserves the coverage and consistency of a GFM while providing information about the functional connectivity among urban regions, permitting zero-shot deployment in unseen cities with or without available mobility data. Across four target cities spanning two countries, the MoRAX teacher model, which observes mobility, consistently outperforms GFMs and strong urban representation baselines in eight socioeconomic and environmental prediction tasks. Meanwhile, the student model, which never takes mobility data as input, approaches the teacher in performance on most tasks. Transfer results across countries further demonstrate that modulation conditioned on mobility flows provides a general mechanism for grounding geospatial foundations in the human dimension of cities.

</details>


### [154] [Efficient Resource Optimization for Split Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17849)
*Wei Wei,Xianhao Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Efficient optimization framework for split federated learning (SFL) under resource constraints: optimal model splitting in polynomial time; a (1+ε)-approximate method for joint splitting and resource allocation, enabling scalable energy–latency tradeoffs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SFL's discrete model-splitting and resource-allocation decisions form a hard mixed-integer problem that hinders scalability to large user populations; existing methods are heuristic or too slow.

Method: 1) Develop a polynomial-time algorithm to obtain the global optimum for the model-splitting subproblem. 2) Extend to joint model splitting and resource allocation by formulating a two-dimensional master problem and designing an efficient (1+ε)-approximation algorithm.

Result: The splitting algorithm achieves global optimality with polynomial time. The joint problem admits an efficient approximation with a (1+ε) guarantee. Experiments demonstrate effective energy–latency tradeoffs and scalability.

Conclusion: The framework provides scalable, near-optimal solutions for SFL in resource-constrained networks, enabling efficient energy–latency optimization for large-scale deployments.

Abstract: Split federated learning (SFL) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for model training at the edge. However, SFL inherently involves discrete decision variables for model splitting and resource allocation, resulting in a challenging mixed-integer problem. Consequently, prior optimization schemes for SFL are either \textit{heuristic} or \textit{computationally inefficient}, which cannot handle large-scale user populations. To address this limitation, this work establishes an efficient optimization framework for SFL under resource-constrained networks. Our framework jointly optimizes model splitting and resource allocation to minimize training cost, which is defined as the weighted sum of latency and energy costs. We first study the model splitting problem and develop a polynomial-time algorithm that achieves the global optimum. Then, we extend the approach to the joint model splitting and resource allocation problem. In this case, we formulate it as a two-dimensional master problem and develop an efficient approximation method with a $(1+ε)$-approximation guarantee. Extensive experiments show that the proposed approach provides efficient solutions to strike the optimal energy--latency tradeoff.

</details>


### [155] [Dynamic Compression in Recurrent Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17896)
*Jyothish Pari,Ryan Bahlous-Boldi,Pulkit Agrawal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dynamic compression enables recurrent models to revisit and revise their fixed-size state by selectively re-reading past tokens, reducing memory requirements at the cost of extra computation and improving scalability when storing multiple in-context functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the one-pass compression bottleneck in fixed-state RNNs, where every input must be encoded for all potential future uses, limiting fidelity across a growing set of tasks.

Method: Introduce a dynamic compression mechanism that allows additional recurrent updates to revisit parts of the history and refine the fixed-size state. Evaluate in a controlled setting where the model learns multiple in-context functions and, later, few-shot tasks requiring one of those functions.

Result: Dynamic compression substantially reduces the recurrent state needed to reuse stored functions and scales more favorably as the number of stored functions grows.

Conclusion: Selective re-scanning enables a computation–memory tradeoff: by spending more computation revisiting history, the model makes more effective use of a fixed-size state for reuse tasks.

Abstract: Recurrent models process long contexts efficiently by compressing their history into a fixed-size state, but modern architectures typically do so in a single causal pass over the sequence. Each input must therefore be compressed before the model knows how it will later be used, forcing a limited state to compromise across possible future demands. We introduce dynamic compression, which allows a recurrent model to selectively revisit past tokens and revise its fixed-size state through additional recurrent updates. The model need not preserve every part of the history at uniformly high fidelity in its recurrent state, because lower-fidelity information can be revisited from the retained raw sequence when it becomes relevant. We study this in a controlled setting where the model first learns multiple functions in-context and, later in the same sequence, encounters a series of few-shot tasks that each require it to identify and reuse one of those functions. A single-pass model must preserve every function at sufficient fidelity for any future task, whereas selective re-scanning allows the model to revisit and refine only the function currently needed. We find that dynamic compression substantially reduces the recurrent state required for accurate reuse and scales more favorably as the number of stored functions grows. These results demonstrate a computation--memory tradeoff in which recurrent models can spend more computation revisiting their history to make more effective use of a fixed-size state.

</details>


### [156] [Hybrid ML for Lightweight Pre-Route Delay Estimation in Open-Source IC Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17914)
*Marvin Castro Castro,Erick Carvajal Barboza*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight hybrid ML model (decision tree + linear regression) improves pre-routing delay estimation from OpenLane, achieving substantial error reduction, while being compact, fast, and highly explainable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for accurate STA with limited physical design information and open-source tooling gaps. OpenLane's estimates can be imprecise during early design stages; a lightweight, interpretable estimator is desirable.

Method: A hybrid model that combines a decision tree with linear regression (likely leaf-wise linear models) to predict delays for RTL-to-GDSII OpenLane outputs, aiming to be both accurate and explainable with minimal data requirements.

Result: 80% reduction in error compared to OpenLane's estimates; 71% improvement even without OpenLane-specific parameters; model is over 300x smaller, ~2x faster, and more explainable than heavier ML approaches.

Conclusion: The approach offers a practical, interpretable alternative to traditional delay propagation and complex ML models, suitable for early-design delay estimation with limited design information.

Abstract: Static Timing Analysis (STA) is a critical step in the design flow of digital integrated circuits, however, obtaining accurate delay estimations can represent a challenge when limited information regarding physical design is available. In response, this work presents a hybrid and light-weight machine learning (ML) based approach that combines a decision tree with linear regression to improve pre-routing delay estimations generated by the open-source RTL-to-GDSII tool OpenLane. The proposed model achieves an 80\% reduction in error compared to OpenLane's estimates, demonstrates a 71\% improvement even without utilizing OpenLane-specific parameters. Overall, this method offers an alternative to traditional delay propagation techniques and more complex machine learning models that is not only accurate, but is also over 300 times smaller, 2 times faster and offers a higher explainability.

</details>


### [157] [Efficient RLVR Scheduling via Graph-Structured Online Difficulty Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17941)
*Zhizhao Liu,Zhiliang Tian,Xi Wang,Zhihua Wen,Yihang Xiong,Zhiquan Lai,Dongsheng Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A graph-based online difficulty estimator for RLVR that shares rollout feedback across related samples to adapt exploration without dedicated probing, using a Potts prior and state-level Beta-Binomial modeling with online mean-field updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR requires expensive rollout exploration. Uniform budgets waste resources on easy samples and under-explore hard but learnable ones. Existing online difficulty estimators struggle with cold-start, stale feedback, or ignoring sample relations, limiting efficiency.

Method: Construct a difficulty-aware sample graph using semantic and reasoning similarities. Introduce latent difficulty states with a Potts prior to encourage neighboring samples to share states. Use a state-level Beta-Binomial model to aggregate rollout outcomes per state. Apply an online mean-field variational algorithm to update latent-state assignments and state-level difficulty as new feedback arrives. The estimator is plug-and-play for sample-selection and rollout-allocation schedulers.

Result: Empirical evaluation across multiple base models, RL schedulers, and benchmarks shows improved performance with better exploration efficiency and reduced reliance on dedicated probing.

Conclusion: The proposed graph-based online difficulty estimator mitigates cold-start and stale feedback by sharing information across related samples, enabling difficulty-adaptive exploration without dedicated probing and is compatible with various RLVR schedulers.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models but relies on costly rollout exploration. Assigning the same exploration budget to samples with different difficulty levels is inefficient: easy samples may receive redundant rollouts, whereas difficult but learnable samples may receive too little exploration. Existing adaptive schedulers address this mismatch through curriculum-based sample selection or non-uniform rollout allocation based on estimated sample difficulty. However, obtaining reliable online difficulty estimates remains challenging: dedicated probing adds substantial generation overhead, whereas history-based estimators face a cold start with no initial observations and stale feedback, and typically ignore relations among samples. To address these limitations, we propose a plug-and-play graph-based online difficulty estimator that shares rollout feedback across related samples and continuously updates their difficulty estimates, mitigating cold start and staleness without dedicated probing. Specifically, we first construct a difficulty-aware sample graph based on semantic and reasoning similarities. Based on this graph, we introduce latent difficulty states and use a Potts prior to encourage neighboring samples to share the same state. We then employ a state-level Beta-Binomial model to aggregate the rollout outcomes associated with each state. Finally, we use an online mean-field variational algorithm to continuously update the latent-state assignments and state-level difficulty as new feedback arrives. Our framework can be integrated into sample-selection and rollout-allocation schedulers, enabling difficulty-adaptive exploration without dedicated probing. Experiments across multiple base models, RL schedulers, and benchmarks demonstrate that our framework achieves better performance.

</details>


### [158] [SIGMA: SHAP-Guided Implicit-Trajectory Generation for Metadata-Free LLM-Based AutoFE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17948)
*Xuan Zheng,Kento Uchida,Shinichi Shirakawa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SIGMA uses SHAP-guided, metadata-free feature generation with constant-context prompting, leveraging EXIT to encode trajectory; achieves SOTA-like performance with far fewer prompts and drastically lowers duplicate features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcoming lack of semantic metadata in AutoFE and mitigating context-window growth in long-horizon prompting, while maintaining stability and efficiency.

Method: Introduce SHAP-enhanced implicit-trajectory generation (SIGMA) that uses SHAP values as task-aware guidance for group feature generation without semantic metadata; adopt EXposed-feature Implicit Trajectory (EXIT) where prompt-exposed features implicitly encode trajectory, enabling near-constant prompt length.

Result: SIGMA attains performance comparable to state-of-the-art LLM baselines with nearly constant prompt length; EXIT reduces feature duplicates from 37.2% to 6.8%; SIGMA matches traditional SOTA with an average of 5.4 features.

Conclusion: The approach delivers scalable, metadata-free AutoFE with improved efficiency and stability. EXIT effectively conveys trajectory information within a fixed prompt, and SHAP substitutes semantic metadata to guide feature generation.

Abstract: Recent research has leveraged Large Language Models (LLMs) to enhance Automated Feature Engineering (AutoFE) through semantic descriptions and trajectory-based prompting. However, there exist two challenges that limit their applicability and scalability in long-horizon optimization: (1) semantic metadata is unavailable in many practical settings, and (2) trajectory accumulation increases the risk of exceeding the context window, while without it, the generation process can become unstable, leading to becoming stuck in the local optima and a high duplicate rate of generated features. To this end, we propose a SHAP-enhanced Implicit-trajectory Generation for Metadata-free AutoFE (SIGMA), a scalable constant-context optimization framework. SIGMA leverages SHAP values to provide task-aware signals for guiding group feature generation instead of semantic information. In addition, we adopt an EXposed-feature Implicit Trajectory (EXIT) approach, where the exposed features in the prompt implicitly represent the trajectory. Empirical results demonstrate that SIGMA achieves performance comparable to the state-of-the-art (SOTA) LLM baselines with a nearly constant prompt length. Notably, EXIT significantly reduces the duplicate ratio of generated features from 37.2% to 6.8%. At the same time, SIGMA matches traditional SOTA performance with only 5.4 features on average, demonstrating substantial efficiency gains in feature utilization.

</details>


### [159] [An Omitted Mode Is a Rare Rule: The Sampling-Verification Danger Law in Continuous Code World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17956)
*Javier Aguilar Martín*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Acceptance in Code World Model for continuous control certifies only sample consistency, not broader model fidelity; the informative gate fraction is small (about two percent of planner queries).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Questioning what a success/certification in the Code World Model pipeline guarantees in continuous control, and quantifying the risk contributed by gate rollouts and acceptance sampling; derive theoretical bounds and evaluate identifiability and repairability of mode-based constructs.

Method: Theoretically factorizes risk as (1−r)^N for N i.i.d. gate rollouts; derive a localization budget at model boundary points for Lipschitz models; contrast with discontinuous reset modes; empirically evaluate LLM-generated world models (GPT-5.x) on 1D clamps and 2D regions, plus targeted interventions and controls; develop a version-space certificate to assess identifiability; re-score a large artifact set against independent samples.

Result: The delay-free factorization shows that an independent acceptance sample increases the exponent (i.e., less risk) but cannot eliminate miss-probability; at boundary points, Lipschitz models with limited Lipschitz constant and region, there is a provable lower bound on region disagreement; 1D clamp repair succeeded on 105 of 111 mode-containing draws; exactness on 50 of 56 instrument-stream blocks (95% CI [0.781, 0.960]); in 2D, no artifact recovered the rule (0/156); eight targeted interventions failed to fix; positive controls indicate a located rule is not induced, yet constants align with theory; a version-space certificate demonstrates class-relative identifiability: declares fit succeeds in 20/20 blocks and every sample-consistent circle is within tolerance in 18/20; re-scoring 1034 artifacts on independent samples confirms acceptance certifies sample consistency and nothing more, with gate-informative instances covering about 2% of the planner’s queries.

Conclusion: Acceptance certifies only sample-consistency; the measurable identifiability of entry rules exists but is instrument-dependent; in practice, informative gates are rare, and many failures persist despite synthesis repairs. The framework exposes the limits of acceptance-based protection in continuous-control pipelines and highlights the need for stronger or alternative certificates.

Abstract: In the Code World Model paradigm an LLM synthesizes an executable world model that a classical planner searches, and the model is accepted when it reproduces sampled transitions. We ask what that acceptance certifies in continuous control. We define the pipeline's danger as an expected risk and isolate its exact factor: the probability that N i.i.d. gate rollouts all miss a critical event of probability r is exactly (1-r)^N; an independent acceptance sample adds its budget to the exponent. On three hybrid instruments the accepted mode-blind model is exploited: the planner is pinned at the mode boundary at a regret of nearly the whole attainable return. We prove a localization budget, valid at boundary points: models with Lipschitz constant at most L differing by eta at a point disagree above tolerance eps on a region of volume at least kappa((eta-eps)/L)^(d+m); the discontinuous reset modes studied pay no such budget. With real LLM synthesis, GPT-5.x repairs an omitted 1D clamp in 105 of 111 mode-containing draws -- every attempt exact on 50 of 56 instrument-stream blocks (95% CI [0.781, 0.960]). On 2D regions no artifact recovers the rule (0/156); eight targeted interventions leave the failure in place, and positive controls locate it: a located rule is not induced, while given form and location the constants follow exactly. A version-space certificate proves identification is class-relative: at the widest dose the declared fit succeeds in 20/20 blocks and every sample-consistent circle is within tolerance in 18/20. We prove a class of entry rules exactly consistent with every sample yet harmless at play, so identifiability is a measurable property of the instrument. Re-scoring all 1034 artifacts on independent samples confirms acceptance certifies sample consistency and no more: where the gate is provably informative it covers about two percent of the exploited planner's queries.

</details>


### [160] [Understanding the Surprising Generalization Properties of Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17957)
*Nour Shaheen,Junwei Ma,Alex Labach,Frank Hutter,Valentin Thomas,Anthony L. Caterini*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Self-supervised pretraining on a single real table can yield strong transfer for Tabular Foundation Models (TFMs); model usefulness is driven more by feature count than data size, and a task-centric, column-aware design with retrieval-based in-context generalization is beneficial.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what drives generalization and transfer in TFMs, and whether large synthetic or real-data corpora are necessary; to propose a task-centric, retrieval-based framework for tabular pretraining and in-context learning.

Method: Experiments on self-supervised pretraining with a single real table; assessment of cross-table transfer, analysis of factor importance (features vs instances), evaluation of per-column preprocessing versus dataset filtering/deduplication, and investigation of retrieval-based in-context generalization.

Result: Surprisingly strong transfer from a single real table; tables tend to be either broadly useful or broadly poor across tasks; number of features is a stronger predictor of usefulness than number of instances; fine-grained column-level preprocessing improves downstream performance; dataset-level filtering/deduplication yields no improvement; in-context generalization appears largely retrieval-based.

Conclusion: A task-centric, retrieval-based perspective provides a strong framework for designing TFMs and corpora; prioritize increasing task diversity/quality and per-column processing, and study retrieval mechanisms to guide future model and corpus design.

Abstract: Tabular Foundation Models (TFMs) increasingly rely on in-context learning, where a model receives labelled examples at inference time and predicts labels for new inputs without updating its weights. Existing TFMs are typically trained on either massive synthetic corpora or very large collections of real datasets. In contrast, we show that surprisingly strong transfer can emerge from self-supervised pre-training on just a single real table. In this setting, we also find that tables tend to be either broadly useful or broadly poor regardless of downstream prediction task, and that the strongest predictor of usefulness is the number of features rather than the number of instances. This leads to a task-centric interpretation of tabular pre-training: the number and the quality of tasks are essential for the pre-training of TFMs.
  We show that the same task-centric perspective can help corpus design at scale: fine-grained column-level pre-processing consistently improves downstream performance, while no improvements are observed when we filter or deduplicate at the dataset level.
  Finally, we offer a new perspective for how TFMs generalize: we believe that tabular in-context generalization is largely retrieval-based, and good models are those that learn to identify relevant examples in the provided context and aggregate them well. The mechanics of TFMs have been relatively understudied; our task-centric, retrieval-based perspective offers a new framework to guide future model and corpus design.

</details>


### [161] [Too Sure to Be Safe: Model Calibration for Reliable Log Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17965)
*Bin Li,Dongdong Wang,Siyang Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LoRD is a lightweight post-hoc calibration framework for LM-based log anomaly detectors that uses route-specific reliability via latent reconstruction distances to recalibrate high-risk predictions, boosting confidence reliability without hurting detection performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LM-based log anomaly detectors exhibit poorly calibrated confidence, frequently yielding overconfident incorrect predictions, especially under class imbalance; conventional calibration metrics can obscure this reliability gap, creating risks for operational monitoring.

Method: LoRD learns prediction-route-specific reliability models from latent representations of correctly classified validation samples and uses route-wise reconstruction distances to estimate reliability; it then selectively recalibrates high-risk predictions in a post-hoc, lightweight manner to suppress overconfident errors while preserving reliable predictions.

Result: Experiments on four large-scale log benchmark datasets and multiple language-model detectors show that LoRD consistently improves confidence reliability and reduces overconfident anomaly-related errors without sacrificing anomaly detection performance.

Conclusion: LoRD provides robust, selective post-hoc calibration for log anomaly detection, delivering more reliable confidence estimates across datasets and detectors without compromising detection accuracy.

Abstract: Online log anomaly detection is critical for maintaining the reliability of large-scale computing systems. Although recent language model-based log anomaly detectors achieve strong detection performance, their confidence estimates remain poorly calibrated. We show that these detectors frequently assign excessive confidence to incorrect predictions, particularly for anomalous logs under severe class imbalance. Moreover, confidence on erroneous predictions remains persistently high even when conventional calibration metrics indicate good calibration, creating a critical reliability gap for operational monitoring systems. To address this issue, we propose Log Reconstruction and Distance (LoRD), a lightweight post-hoc calibration framework for reliable log anomaly detection. LoRD learns prediction-route-specific reliability models from latent representations of correctly classified validation samples and estimates prediction reliability through route-wise reconstruction distances. Based on the estimated reliability, LoRD selectively recalibrates high-risk predictions to suppress overconfident errors while preserving reliable predictions. Extensive experiments on four large-scale log benchmark datasets and multiple language model-based detectors demonstrate that LoRD consistently improves confidence reliability and substantially reduces overconfident anomaly-related errors without sacrificing anomaly detection performance.

</details>


### [162] [Evaluating and improving crop-yield forecasting methods during extreme drought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17971)
*Shrey Gupta,Yi Ming,George Mohler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: In the 2012 drought context, deep learning (VITA) can outperform traditional ML for county-level corn yield forecasting, while simple modifications (sample weighting and feature selection) improve ML models but do not beat VITA; the study highlights distribution shift (train/test dissimilarity) and data sparsity as key challenges in climate-crop forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how covariate shift and spatial-temporal data sparsity under extreme drought affect crop-yield forecasting and to compare deep vs. non-deep learning approaches, plus practical modifications for ML.

Method: Using 16 meteorological predictors to forecast county-level corn yield for the 2012 drought year. Compare non-deep ML models with a deep-learning model (VITA). Address data issues by applying sample weighting and feature selection to mitigate distribution shift and sparsity (spatial: missing yields; temporal: limited daily values).

Result: Non-deep ML models gain from sample weighting and feature selection, showing improved forecasting accuracy. The deep-learning model VITA shows little to no improvement from these modifications, yet overall VITA outperforms the ML models with or without modifications.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that covariate shift between training and testing data and data sparsity critically influence forecast performance. While ML models benefit from simple modifications, a well-constructed DL model (VITA) can surpass ML, underscoring the need to consider distribution shift and data irregularities when selecting forecasting approaches.

Abstract: The impact of climate variability on food production has led to the creation of various forecasting models that uses machine learning (ML), numerical weather predictors (NWP) or a hybrid of ML-NWP models to identify structural and physical relationships between meteorological drivers and crop growth, in order to predict crop yield. Droughts, for example the 2012 Midwestern US (Corn Belt) drought, are extreme events that affect crop production and test the limits of these forecasting models. Using 16 meteorological drivers as predictors, we compare ML (non-deep learning) and deep learning forecasting models to predict the county-level corn yield for the extreme drought year, 2012. This forecasting problem is characterized by a dissimilarity between the feature distributions of the training and test data, where the meteorological conditions of the extreme drought year fall outside the range of historically observed values. Additionally, the dataset consists of spatial and temporal irregularities where counties with missing yields introduce spatial sparsity and the use of only a subset of daily values per year introduce temporal sparsity. To overcome this, we use sample weighting and feature selection as modifications to improve our forecasting models. These modifications lead to an improvement for ML models; however, the deep learning model VITA shows little to no improvement. While VITA outperforms the ML models with or without modifications, our current study sheds light on the effect of dissimilarity between train and test feature distributions on forecasting models, compares deep learning versus non-deep learning models, and introduces modifications that are effective for non-deep learning models.

</details>


### [163] [Recirculation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17981)
*Michael C. Mozer,Shoaib Ahmed Siddiqui,Danny Sawyer,Sunny Sanyal,Rosanne Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Inference-time architectural enhancement via recirculation introduces recurrence to foundations models, improving perplexity and accuracy without extra generation latency; training-free and auto-tuned in an adaptive variant; demonstrates performance gains on Gemma3 and GSM8k.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the depth-bound progression of state updates in feedforward transformers by enabling dynamic belief-state tracking at inference time without retraining.

Method: Introduce recirculation: a controlled form of recurrence that makes the model behave like a dynamical system. Distinguish from chain-of-thought and from depth-recurrence/looping; propose an adaptive variant with light hyperparameter tuning while freezing weights; incurs serial prefill processing but negligible generation latency.

Result: Relative to baselines, 23% perplexity reduction on Gemma3 data suite; 21% accuracy gain on GSM8k; reliable improvements on other downstream tasks.

Conclusion: Shows that training-free architectural evolution guided by a trained network’s properties can yield meaningful performance gains with minimal runtime impact, suggesting a general strategy for inference-time augmentation without retraining.

Abstract: We describe an inference-time architectural enhancement for off-the-shelf foundation models that markedly reduces perplexity and boosts accuracy across generation and reasoning tasks. Our approach incurs essentially no additional latency during generation, though it requires serial processing in the prefill phase. Motivated by the fundamental limitation that state updates in feedforward transformers are bounded by model depth, our technique, recirculation, introduces a specific form of recurrence that allows the model to act as a dynamical system and track belief states. We distinguish this technique from chain-of-thought computation---which is better reserved for complex inferences rather than basic state tracking---as well as from popular depth-recurrence techniques (looping) and the costly training of recurrent transformers. We also propose and evaluate an adaptive variant of recirculation which requires only light tuning of hyperparameters while freezing the original model weights. Relative to the off-the-shelf baseline, adaptive recirculation achieves remarkable gains on the Gemma3 family, including a 23% reduction in perplexity on a suite of datasets, a 21% increase in accuracy on GSM8k, and reliable improvements in accuracy on other downstream tasks. Our training-free approach succeeds by leveraging the model itself to inform architectural modifications, suggesting a route to architectural evolution guided by a trained network's properties rather than forced, arbitrary design choices.

</details>


### [164] [Composing Flow-Matching Energies with Known Physics: Generation, OOD Detection, and Inversion on PDE Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18004)
*Yixuan Sun,Anirban Samaddar,Sandeep Madireddy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Flow-matching models with a potential-induced velocity produce an explicit, time-dependent energy function whose gradient matches the learned score, enabling energy-based interpretations of probabilistic PDE modeling. This yields energy-based data generation, OOD detection via the energy, and compositional posterior sampling for inverse problems, with improved PDE residuals when using energy-augmented MCMC over the standard flow ODE baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To combine data-driven priors with known physical structure (e.g., PDEs) in a tractable training and sampling framework. Energy-based models (EBMs) are natural but suffer from intractable partition functions; deriving an explicit energy within a flow-matching setting provides an accessible, physics-informed probability model for fields.

Method: Introduce potential-induced velocity in a flow-matching framework to obtain a scalar energy at all transport times. The energy gradient equals the learned score, and at the population optimum this energy matches the marginal negative log-density. Energies are obtained purely from the matching regression objective on an independent linear Gaussian interpolation, without variational forms or additional MCMC steps, while sampling preserves the flow ODE. The trained energy is used for: energy-corrected data generation, energy-based OOD scoring, and energy-compositional posterior sampling in inverse problems; explicit energy enables general MCMC within predictor-corrector sampling, reducing PDE residuals and spectral distance relative to the baseline.

Result: The explicit energy permits applying general MCMC samplers in the predictor-corrector framework and yields reduced PDE residual and spectral distance compared to using only the flow ODE. Combining data energy with physics-based energy (e.g., PDE residuals) improves OOD detection accuracy. For inverse problems, composing the energy with a quadratic observational likelihood produces a posterior energy that can guide inference targets during sampling.

Conclusion: Deriving an explicit energy within a flow-matching EBM framework provides a practical and effective approach for integrating data-driven scores with physical priors, enabling improved sampling, OOD detection, and inverse-problem inference without requiring variational objectives or extra MCMC steps.

Abstract: Probabilistic modeling of physical fields benefits from both a data-driven prior and known physical structure such as the governing equations. Energy-based models (EBMs) are a natural fit since energies compose additively, which enables augmenting physics information during inference. However, EBMs have been difficult to train and sample from due to the intractable partition function. We show in this work that flow matching models with a potential-induced velocity yield an explicit scalar energy at all transport times, whose gradient is exactly the converted learned score and which recovers the marginal negative log-density at the population optimum. The time-dependent energy functions are obtained purely from the matching regression objective on an independent linear Gaussian interpolation, without a variational form or additional MCMC steps, and the sampling retains the flow ODE. Access to the energy function from a trained model serves three roles: energy-corrected data generation, energy as a scoring function for out-of-distribution (OOD) detection, and energy compositional posterior sampling for inverse problems. In particular, we show the explicit energy permits general MCMC samplers in the predictor-corrector sampling framework, reducing PDE residual and spectral distance compared to the flow ODE baseline. Furthermore, we demonstrate utilizing the data energy and physics-based energy (e.g., PDE residuals) as complementary mechanisms to improve detection accuracy for OOD tasks. In addition, we explore the connection to MCMC-based inference for inverse problems by composing the energy with a quadratic observational likelihood that yields a posterior energy, used as an explicitly chosen family of inference-time targets.

</details>


### [165] [Policy-Invariant Reward Shaping from LLM Feedback: A Framework for Hybrid RL Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18008)
*Christophe D. Hounwanou,John Emeka Eze,Yaé U. Gaba*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Bounded potential-shaped LLM progress scores in Goal-Augmented MDP preserve optimal policies and are robust to score inaccuracies; validated numerically including adversarial configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address theoretical status of LLM-derived rewards in hybrid LLM-planner and RL-controller architectures; provide guarantees beyond generic LLM-as-reward by using potential-based shaping.

Method: Formalize architecture as a Goal-Augmented MDP; prove that when per-state LLM progress score is a bounded potential function, the shaping term preserves the optimal policy set; perform numerical verification on a small MDP across four configurations including adversarial scaling.

Result: Theoretical guarantee—policy optimality preserved under inaccurate LLM scores; stronger than generic LLM-as-reward; numerical results showing robustness to substantial score manipulation.

Conclusion: LLM-derived progress-based potential shaping offers a robust integration of LLMs with RL, ensuring policy optimality even with imperfect scores; supports safe deployment of LLM-guided planning in hybrid systems.

Abstract: Combining large language models with reinforcement learning is increasingly explored, yet the theoretical status of LLM-derived reward signals is often left implicit. We formalize the hybrid LLM-planner and RL-controller architecture as a Goal-Augmented Markov Decision Process and show that when the LLM per-state progress score is used as a bounded potential function, the resulting shaping term preserves the optimal policy set even when the LLM scores are inaccurate. This guarantee is stronger than what general LLM-as-reward approaches provide. We verify the result numerically on a small MDP under four potential configurations, including an adversarial one scaled to twenty times the base reward magnitude.

</details>


### [166] [Revisiting WEASEL 2.0: Reproduction, Sensitivity, and an Adaptive Ensemble-Size Rule](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18021)
*Cian Higgins,Gerard Carrigan,Pinar Sungu Isiacik,Georgiana Ifrim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Replication of WEASEL 2.0 on 114 UCR datasets confirms original accuracy; sensitivity tests identify the maximum ensemble-size rule as the main source of resource over-provision. An adaptive rule based on series length and class count reduces peak memory and fit time, with negligible accuracy impact, especially for long series.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To verify the robustness of WEASEL 2.0's design choices, justify or replace heuristic thresholds, and improve efficiency for long time series by enabling an adaptive ensemble sizing strategy.

Method: Reproduce WEASEL 2.0 on 114 UCR datasets; compare mean/median accuracy to published values using Wilcoxon signed-rank test; perturb four design choices (downstream classifier, feature weighting, maximum window-size rule, maximum ensemble-size rule); propose an adaptive rule tying max ensemble size to series length and number of classes; evaluate adaptive rule on fixed-length datasets in terms of memory, runtime, and accuracy.

Result: Mean accuracy 0.865 and median 0.928 on replication, congruent with published values (p=0.655). The downstream classifier, absence of feature weighting, and window-size rule are robust to perturbations. The maximum ensemble-size rule is over-provisioned for long series. Adaptive sizing reduces peak fit memory by median 37 MB (mean 395 MB) and fit time by 0.4 s (mean 4 s) with a median accuracy change of 0% (mean -0.11%), with larger benefits on long-series datasets.

Conclusion: An adaptive ensemble-size policy maintains accuracy while substantially reducing resource usage, especially for long time series. This suggests revisiting fixed thresholds in WEASEL 2.0 and highlights the value of data-driven resource allocation in dictionary-based time-series classifiers.

Abstract: WEASEL 2.0 is a dictionary-based time series classifier that combines dilated sliding windows with a randomised hyperparameter ensemble and a fixed-size dense feature representation. Two of its hyperparameter choices, the maximum ensemble size and the maximum window size, are specified by simple thresholding rules whose chosen thresholds are not empirically justified in the original paper. In this work we reproduce WEASEL 2.0 on 114 UCR datasets, achieving a mean accuracy of 0.865 and median of 0.928, closely matching the published values (Wilcoxon signed-rank, p = 0.655). We then test the sensitivity of four design choices: the downstream classifier, the absence of feature weighting, the maximum window-size rule, and the maximum ensemble-size rule. The first three are robust to perturbation. The fourth is over-provisioned for long-series datasets, motivating an adaptive rule that sets the maximum ensemble size from series length and number of classes. Evaluated on fixed-length datasets, the adaptive rule reduces peak fit memory by a median of 37 MB (mean 395 MB) and fit time by a median of 0.4 s (mean 4 s), with a median accuracy change of 0% (mean -0.11%). Memory and time savings concentrate on long-series datasets where the original rule allocates the largest ensemble size.

</details>


### [167] [Why GPT-Style Models Do Not Directly Transfer to Symbolic Music: Compression in the Wrong Coordinate System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18025)
*Yi Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework ties tokenization to predictive compression in symbolic music, arguing that effective coordinates reveal stable predictability while preserving relational freedom; controlled experiments show that how tokens are defined (boundary interfaces) matters more than sheer sequence length, explaining limited cross-modality transfer of GPT-like systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand why tokenization interfaces matter for predictive modeling in music and what makes a tokenization effectively compressive without constraining relational structure.

Method: Propose the Effectiveness–Losslessness Framework with two boundaries (Fact–Token Boundary and Token–State Boundary). Conduct controlled symbolic-music experiments to evaluate how coordinate choice vs. relational projections affect predictive compressibility and contextual modeling.

Result: Effective coordinate construction improves predictive compressibility; fixed relational projections constrain contextual modeling. Sequence compaction alone does not guarantee predictive compression; preserving relational freedom enables higher-order musical organization to emerge without explicit structural labels.

Conclusion: Tokenization must discover representations that are predictively effective while preserving relational freedom from which contextual structure can emerge. This explains why GPT-style model transfer across modalities is limited: architectures transfer, but tokenization interfaces do not; tokenization should expose predictive regularities while leaving computation of context-dependent relations to model states.

Abstract: GPT-style models achieve strong performance by representing language with finite vocabularies of reusable discrete tokens. This success has motivated symbolic music tokenizations to treat recurring musical structures, such as chords, motifs, and phrases, as reusable units analogous to linguistic tokens. However, tokenization derives its advantage not from reusable combinations alone, but from compression: effective compression requires coordinates in which recurring regularities form stable and predictable conditional distributions. The key problem is therefore not to find larger musical combinations, but to discover the coordinate system in which musical facts become predictively compressible. We formulate the Effectiveness--Losslessness Framework and define tokenization as the construction of a predictively effective and relationally lossless coordinate system. The Predictive Effectiveness Principle defines the Fact--Token Boundary: decoupling and denesting construct coordinate interfaces that expose predictive regularities. The Relational Losslessness Principle defines the Token--State Boundary: tokenization stops before context-dependent relations are fixed, leaving their computation to model states. Controlled symbolic-music experiments validate these boundaries. Effective coordinate construction improves predictive compressibility, while fixed relational projections constrain contextual modeling. Sequence compaction alone does not guarantee predictive compression, while preserving contextual freedom allows higher-order musical organization to emerge without explicit structural labels. These results reveal why GPT-style models do not transfer directly across modalities: architectures transfer, but tokenization interfaces do not. Tokenization must discover effective representations while preserving the relational freedom from which contextual structure can emerge.

</details>


### [168] [TabNSM: Neural Sparse Mixer for Tabular Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18026)
*Ali Eslamian,Qiang Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TabNSM is a scalable deep tabular regression framework that combines Sparse Interaction learning with feature-token mixing for near-linear complexity, enhanced by a multi-stage regression head, GridLoss, and RISE sampling, achieving strong performance on high-dimensional tabular data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tree ensembles are robust but struggle with end-to-end representation learning; deep models learn features but face costly interaction modeling and sensitivity to noisy features. A scalable, selective interaction approach is needed for regression on large, heterogeneous tabular data.

Method: Introduction of Adaptive Sparse Interaction Module (ASIM) that unifies foreground feature discovery, sparse local interaction encoding, and Feature-Token Mixing; plus three components—Multi-Stage Regression Head for progressive refinement, GridLoss (ordinal-aware soft-binning aligned with target structure), and RISE (Reweighted Instance Sampling by Error) for difficulty-aware sampling.

Result: Across nine real-world regression benchmarks, TabNSM shows strong predictive performance and practical scalability, with consistent gains on high-dimensional and heterogeneous datasets.

Conclusion: Selective interaction modeling, structured regression supervision, and difficulty-aware sampling form an effective and scalable paradigm for deep tabular regression.

Abstract: Large-scale, high-dimensional tabular regression remains challenging: tree-based models are robust but lack end-to-end representation learning, while deep models enable flexible feature learning but often incur costly interaction modeling and sensitivity to noisy or redundant features. We propose TabNSM, a scalable regression framework that extends our earlier sparse-attention and mixer architectures. At its core, the Adaptive Sparse Interaction Module (ASIM) integrates foreground feature discovery, sparse local interaction encoding, and Feature-Token Mixing, providing near-linear complexity under fixed sparse configurations. For regression, TabNSM introduces three complementary components: a Multi-Stage Regression Head for progressive prediction refinement; GridLoss, an ordinal-aware soft-binning objective that incorporates target structure into representation learning; and RISE (Reweighted Instance Sampling by Error), a difficulty-aware sampling strategy based on loss-quantile bins. Across nine real-world regression benchmarks, TabNSM delivers strong predictive performance and practical scalability, with particularly consistent gains on high-dimensional and heterogeneous datasets. These results demonstrate that selective interaction modeling, structured regression supervision, and difficulty-aware sampling provide an effective and scalable approach to deep tabular regression.

</details>


### [169] [Optimize Your Sampling: Tuned Diffusion Sampling with Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18040)
*Travis Zhang,Christian Belardi,Justin Lovelace,Jin Peng Zhou,Saebyeol Shin,Carla P. Gomes,Kilian Q. Weinberger*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Black-box Bayesian optimization of diffusion-sampling timesteps (OYS) to directly maximize image quality metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion sampling is expensive due to many forward passes; reducing timesteps without retraining can lower cost while maintaining quality.

Method: Treat timestep selection as a black-box optimization problem and optimize the target quality metric directly using Bayesian optimization. Works with various samplers (Euler, DPM-Solver++) and models, including distilled ones; requires no additional training.

Result: OYS outperforms default schedules and Align Your Steps on text-to-image generation, and also improves inpainting and other tasks; gains are observed in both quantitative metrics and human evaluations. A 5-step OYS schedule achieves 89–94% of the 50-step quality at ~10x inference-time reduction.

Conclusion: Direct, training-free timestep optimization via Bayesian optimization is effective and broadly applicable for diffusion-model sampling, delivering substantial speedups with minimal quality loss.

Abstract: Sampling from a diffusion model typically requires many forward passes through a large neural network, making generation computationally expensive. While much work has focused on efficient solvers and samplers, comparatively little attention has been paid to selecting the sampling timesteps themselves. A recent line of work optimizes theoretically derived surrogates for sample quality rather than the quality metric itself. We propose Optimizing Your Sampling (OYS), which instead treats timestep selection as a black-box optimization problem, optimizing the target metric directly with Bayesian optimization. OYS outperforms both the default schedules and those of Align Your Steps on text-to-image generation, and improves over the default schedules on inpainting and other image tasks, in both quantitative and human evaluations. OYS requires no additional training, is applicable even to distilled models, and improves both simple and sophisticated samplers such as Euler and DPM-Solver++. A 5-step OYS schedule retains 89%-94% of the quality of a 50-step schedule while reducing inference cost by 10x.

</details>


### [170] [The concentration game: Bayesian updating, regret, and information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18061)
*Akshay Balsubramani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An information-theoretic, exact decomposition of exponential-weights regret in a two-player zero-sum game, unifying Bayesian updating and concentration phenomena; Gibbs/Bayes weights are the unique Bellman equalizer; regret splits into information loss, retempering drift, and comparator information; standard bounds are looser relaxations; a general framework subsuming bandits, posterior sampling, aggregation, and boosting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To unify regret analyses across learning under information constraints by casting regret in a single information-theoretic decomposition, tying Bayesian updating, entropy constraints, and log-partition functions to a universal framework.

Method: Model a repeated zero-sum game between a learner and nature with a fixed prior and a per-round information budget; derive Bellman equations whose solution uses Gibbs/Bayes weights; identify the log-partition function as a value function; perform an exact three-term decomposition of regret (information loss, retempering drift, comparator information); show that standard deviation-based bounds are looser relaxations; extract strategies from each decomposition term; connect to large-deviation bounds and via specialization to bandits, posterior sampling, aggregation, and boosting.

Result: Exact regret decomposition into three components with Gibbs/Bayes weights as the unique equalizer and log-partition functions as value functions; an information-theoretic ledger emerges from repeated play; standard concentration bounds arise as looser special cases; the framework subsumes multiple learning paradigms under a single geometric/compositional principle.

Conclusion: The comparator-class geometry and information-theoretic decomposition provide a unifying lens for regret analyses, linking Bayesian updating, entropy-constrained decisions, and concentration phenomena, and enabling cross-domain methods (bandits, posterior sampling, aggregation, boosting) as natural specializations.

Abstract: We give a two-player zero-sum repeated game between a learner and nature whose value identity generates Bayesian updating and an exact accounting of exponential-weights regret at once, and supplies the comparator-class variational form that a wide class of concentration phenomena share. The terminal payoff is the most a comparator can gain at fixed relative entropy from the prior, and the one-step constraint is an information budget on nature's move under the learner's mixed action. With the learner's move otherwise unrestricted, Gibbs/Bayes weights emerge as its unique Bellman equalizer -- the mixed action that makes the per-round loss independent of which direction nature moves -- with log-partition functions playing the role of value functions. The regret decomposes exactly into three parts: a per-round information loss reflecting the variation in observed outcomes, an additive retempering drift that accounts exactly for any change of measurement scale between rounds, and the information the comparator carries relative to the prior. The variance and bounded-range proxies that drive standard regret bounds are looser relaxations of this decomposition, which holds generally and governs them all. Both players' strategies are read off from the decomposition term by term, and repeated play yields an information-theoretic ledger of self-play in place of the usual quadratic-variation surrogate. The same comparator-class geometry accounts for the classical large-deviation bounds, and methods across bandits, posterior sampling, aggregation, and boosting are specializations of the one regret decomposition.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [171] [VLCP: Vision Language Control Policy Closed-Loop Code Replanning for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16978)
*Dhia Naouali,Minghan Wu,Claudia Wong,Abhinav Puthran,Omar G. Younis*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A frozen vision-language model controls a robot by writing and rewriting a short Python control function in-episode, achieving strong training-free performance via closed-loop code replan, without fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To avoid discarding the model's reasoning during adaptation to robotics by fine-tuning, and to fix failures at the code level within a single episode rather than restarting tasks.

Method: Keep the VLM frozen and have it generate a Python control function. At every K steps, re-observe from multi-view RGB, proprioception, and a state delta, then rewrite the control function based on the latest observations. No demonstrations or fine-tuning. Replans operate in open-loop within the episode, with results cached for reuse and a cross-episode skill library built from rewritten blocks.

Result: On a 57-task MuJoCo/RoboVerse sweep, the training-free policy achieves 35.1% pooled success versus 3.5% for the identical system queried once per episode. The gap is consistent across scene families. The improvement stems from a 27.3% within-episode recovery rate on failed grasps. The approach is efficient: a median 84% of input tokens are cached, an episode uses ~10 compact queries, and rewritten control blocks persist to a cross-episode skill library.

Conclusion: Training-free, closed-loop policy via adaptive code rewrites significantly improves robotic task success and efficiency compared to single-pass policies, leveraging re-planning at the code level with minimal compute and enabling reusable skills.

Abstract: Turning a frontier vision-language model into a robot policy usually means fine-tuning it to emit an action representation it never saw in pretraining, which throws away much of the reasoning that made the model worth reaching for. We go the other way and keep the VLM frozen. It writes the policy as a short Python control function, with no demonstrations and no fine-tuning. Writing that code once is open-loop, though. Existing closed-loop methods react at the wrong level: they retry a fixed policy or pick a different subtask, but never rewrite the code that failed. VLCP closes the loop where the failure actually lives, on the control code, within a single episode. Every $K$ steps the VLM re-observes the scene from multi-view RGB, proprioceptive state, and a state delta, then rewrites the control function from what it just saw, so a failure is caught before it compounds.
  We evaluate on a 57-task MuJoCo/RoboVerse sweep. This training-free policy reaches $35.1\%$ pooled success, against $3.5\%$ for the identical system queried once per episode. That tenfold gap holds with non-overlapping confidence intervals in every scene family. The gain traces to a $27.3\%$ within-episode recovery rate on failed grasps: a miss an open-loop controller would carry to the end of the episode gets re-observed and fixed at the next replan. And the loop stays cheap. A median $84\%$ of input tokens hit cache, an episode needs only about $10$ compact queries, and control blocks written during any replan persist to a cross-episode skill library reused in later prompts.

</details>


### [172] [FetchMan: Learning Visual Humanoid Loco-Manipulation Policies from Simulated Experiences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17027)
*Omar Rayyan,Zhi Li,Max Argus,Yuxin Jiang,Chang Yu,Chenfanfu Jiang,Yuchen Cui*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A sim-to-real loco-manipulation pipeline (FetchMan) that combines cloning, RL, and Flow-GRPO refinement to achieve high real-world performance and generalization across unseen scenes/objects, including multi-object extensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the data-hungry nature of deep visual loco-manipulation, the difficulty of real-world data collection for tabletop and humanoid robots, and the need for robust sim-to-real transfer to enable generalist loco-manipulation policies.

Method: 1) Baseline behavioral cloning of synthetic demonstrations shows a low performance ceiling. 2) Reinforcement learning breaks through the ceiling. 3) Refine the cloned policy with Flow-GRPO on a single sparse reward. 4) Build an end-to-end sim-to-real pipeline spanning 150k+ scenes to train FetchMan. 5) Evaluate on FetchMan-Bench (simulation) and deploy zero-shot on a real Unitree G1 with a single-object reach-and-pick. 6) Extend to multi-object training as an initial step toward loco-manipulation generalists at this data scale.

Result: Cloned policies underperform; RL improves performance; Flow-GRPO refinement yields performance that cloning cannot match. Real-world zero-shot reach-and-pick succeeds at 73.3% across unseen scenes. The approach scales to 150k+ simulated scenes and extends to multi-object training.

Conclusion: A staged sim-to-real pipeline (clone → RL → Flow-GRPO refinement) enables effective real-world generalization for loco-manipulation, with strong zero-shot real-world performance and scalability toward generalist policies.

Abstract: Visual loco-manipulation policies that can generalize to novel scenes and objects have long been a goal of robotics research. However, today's data-hungry algorithms make collecting sufficient demonstrations a struggle for tabletop manipulation, and even more so for humanoids that must also walk and balance. Learning from simulated data and transferring that behavior to the real world, as is commonly done in locomotion, sidesteps this struggle, so we replicate that recipe for loco-manipulation. In doing so, we find that cloning synthetic demonstrations results in a low performance ceiling no matter the amount of training data.
