Reinforcement learning breaks through it, and refining the cloned policy with Flow-GRPO on a single sparse reward yields performance that synthetic behavior cloning cannot match. Together, these stages form our end-to-end sim-to-real pipeline spanning more than 150,000 scenes, which we use to train FetchMan. We evaluate it on FetchMan-Bench, a simulation benchmark we release, and deploy it zero-shot on a real Unitree G1, where our single-object reach-and-pick policy walks to and grasps a target across unseen scenes at 73.3% success. Finally, we extend this recipe to multi-object training, a first step toward loco-manipulation generalist policies at this data scale.

</details>


### [173] [Lambda-Hold Control: Human-Like Movement Emerges from a Minimal Task Reward in Predictive Musculoskeletal Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17030)
*Jun Hyuk Lee,Chihyeong Lee,Jooeun Ahn*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces a λ-hold controller for muscle-driven locomotion, leveraging the EP (equilibrium-point) hypothesis to reduce exploration in high-dimensional muscle actuation spaces, enabling learning of human-like sprinting with minimal reward within about an hour of training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Massive actuation and redundancy in musculoskeletal models create highly inefficient exploration when using reinforcement learning; there is a need for physiology-informed control to improve sample efficiency and realism in learned locomotion.

Method: Propose a λ-hold controller where the policy's control variable is the per-muscle EP threshold length λ. A stretch-reflex recruitment law computes muscle excitations automatically. Holding each λ over a gait-phase interval reduces the frequency of policy queries, enabling efficient exploration by grounding control in EP physiology and using intermittent control and optimal feedback concepts.

Result: Demonstrates, for the first time, that a muscle-actuated skeletal model can learn human-like sprinting using only a minimal reward within an hour of training.

Conclusion: The approach is not merely an engineering trick but a physiology-grounded method that integrates the EP hypothesis, intermittent control, and optimal feedback control, contributing to predictive simulation and the development of a learnable model of the human motor controller.

Abstract: The massive overactuation in the human musculoskeletal system makes it challenging to train musculoskeletal models to generate human-like motion via reinforcement learning, primarily because exploration in the resulting high-dimensional and redundant action space is extremely inefficient. To address this problem, we propose the $λ$-hold controller, inspired by the equilibrium-point (EP) hypothesis, which has been widely supported by extensive evidence from human motor control studies. The policy's control variable is the per-muscle EP threshold length $λ$, from which a stretch-reflex recruitment law computes the muscle excitations automatically. Holding each $λ$ over an interval of the gait phase also sharply reduces the frequency at which the policy must be queried. Consequently, the controller, to our knowledge for the first time, enables a muscle-actuated skeletal model to learn human-like sprinting using only a minimal reward within an hour of training. The efficient exploration through the proposed $λ$-hold controller is not merely an engineering trick but an approach grounded in physiology, bringing together the EP hypothesis, intermittent control, and optimal feedback control. Beyond encapsulating human-like behavior in predictive simulation, this achievement contributes to developing a learnable model of the human motor controller.

</details>


### [174] [Terrain-Aware Local Path Planning with Global DEM Data Integration for Autonomous UGV Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17038)
*Devender Singh,Issah Nazif Suleiman,Paul Mitten,Glenn Cutler,Vinicius Prado da Fonseca,Matthew Hamilton*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Hybrid planning framework combining low-resolution DEM-based global planning with LiDAR-driven local sensing for adaptive navigation in outdoor terrains; shows substantial improvements in obstacle avoidance and slope metrics in Gazebo simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between coarse global maps and real-time sensor feedback for unmanned ground vehicles operating on uneven outdoor terrain by leveraging DEM priors with real-time LiDAR data.

Method: Global path planning using a DEM-informed A* algorithm (low-resolution DEM). Local navigation adapts the path using real-time LiDAR obstacle detection and terrain analysis; local corrections guided by sensor feedback to negotiate dynamic changes. Evaluation performed in Gazebo against a baseline.

Result: Obstacle avoidance rate of 95% in simulation; average traversed slope reduced from 8° to 2.7° on custom terrain; reported improvements in path efficiency and terrain traversability.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that integrating coarse DEM-driven global planning with LiDAR-based local adaptation enhances robustness and efficiency of autonomous navigation on dynamic outdoor terrains; supports real-time adaptation, with simulation-level validation; real-world validation recommended.

Abstract: Autonomous navigation in complex outdoor terrains presents critical challenges for unmanned ground vehicles (UGVs) due to the inherent disconnect between global mapping and real-time sensor feedback. This work proposes a hybrid framework that integrates low-resolution Digital Elevation Model (DEM) data with real-time LiDAR-based obstacle detection and terrain analysis for efficient path planning. A global path is initially computed using a preprocessed DEM-based A* algorithm. Subsequently, local sensor data drives adaptive path correction, enabling the UGV to negotiate sudden environmental changes while maintaining safety and efficiency. Simulation results in Gazebo demonstrate significant improvements over a baseline approach, achieving a 95\% obstacle avoidance rate and reducing the average encountered slope from $8^\circ$ to $2.7^\circ$ in custom terrain. This integration enhances path efficiency and terrain traversability and supports robust real-time adaptation, paving the way for more reliable autonomous navigation in dynamic outdoor environments.

</details>


### [175] [PDDL-ART: Autonomous Symbolic Abstraction From Demonstration For Long-Horizon Robotic Manipulation Using Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17146)
*Disha Kamale,Dmitry Berenson*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A vision-language model (VLM)–driven system, PDDL-ART, generates task-specific PDDL domain and problem descriptions from a single demonstration and a natural language description, using a multi-stage correction pipeline with execution-guided grounding to align planning with the task; it outperforms a baseline planner.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce manual engineering in creating PDDL domains for long-horizon manipulation by leveraging VLMs to translate demonstrations and natural language into executable PDDL without domain templates or fine-tuning.

Method: The framework takes a single expert demonstration, a natural-language task description, and a library of high-level action names to autonomously generate PDDL domain and problem descriptions; it avoids domain templates or specified action signatures or fine-tuning. It employs a multi-stage correction pipeline (syntactic, semantic, execution) and an execution-guided component that uses symbolic predicate grounding. It leverages VLMs’ tool-use capabilities to incorporate geometric and temporal reasoning and decides autonomously when to invoke tools and how to interpret outputs.

Result: Evaluated on challenging manipulation tasks in engine maintenance and household domains, including tasks requiring memory, abstract predicate inference, and visually indistinguishable goal states. Achieved an average success rate of 93.3%, vs. 78.3% for a baseline VLM-based planner.

Conclusion: PDDL-ART demonstrates the feasibility and effectiveness of automatically generating PDDL domain and problem descriptions from minimal inputs with execution-guided grounding, achieving substantial performance gains over a baseline and reducing the need for domain templates and expert domain knowledge.

Abstract: Symbolic planning with PDDL offers a principled framework for long-horizon robot manipulation, but constructing accurate PDDL domain and problem descriptions remains a significant bottleneck, typically requiring substantial domain expertise. We present a Vision-Language Model (VLM)-based approach called PDDL-ART, a framework that autonomously generates task-specific PDDL domain and problem descriptions from a single expert demonstration, a natural language task description, and a library of available high-level action names. PDDL-ART does not require any domain templates, action signatures, or fine-tuning. To ensure the generated descriptions are not only syntactically valid but semantically aligned with the demonstrated task, PDDL-ART introduces a multi-stage correction pipeline operating at syntactic, semantic, and execution levels. A key component of execution-guided correction is symbolic predicate grounding. Instead of relying solely on visual observations, PDDL-ART leverages the tool-use capabilities of modern VLMs to incorporate geometric and temporal reasoning for evaluating relational predicates that are not directly discernible from images alone. Critically, the model autonomously determines when to invoke these tools and how to interpret their outputs. We evaluate PDDL-ART on challenging manipulation tasks in engine maintenance and household domains, including tasks that require memory, abstract predicate inference, and goal states that are visually indistinguishable from the initial state. PDDL-ART achieves an average success rate of 93.3%, compared to 78.3% for a baseline VLM-based planner.

</details>


### [176] [Teach and Grow: An Agent-Centered Architecture for General Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17209)
*Chang Nie,Zhe Liu,Hesheng Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Teach-and-Grow Learning (TGL) is an agent-centered robotics framework that converts few demonstrations into reusable Skill Blocks, stores them in a Skill Library with Experience Memory, and enables continual, task-agnostic improvement without task-specific policy retraining; LIBERO shows strong performance and a scaling-law for reusable experience guiding future learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the retraining tax in end-to-end vision-language-action and world-action robotics when faced with unfamiliar objects, sensors, embodiments, or contacts beyond validated coverage, by enabling reuse of learned skills and ongoing adaptation.

Method: A multimodal agent derives executable Skill Blocks from demonstrations; in new scenes it grounds, composes blocks, selects tools, observes outcomes, and revises plans. A Skill Library stores behaviors; Experience Memory tracks successes, failures, and repairs. New tasks are learned without task-specific policy retraining.

Result: LIBERO achieves state-of-the-art performance with controlled studies highlighting skill induction, persistent reuse, and agent-directed adaptation.

Conclusion: Proposes the Teach-and-Grow scaling-law: effective reusable experience X drives future-task error and teaching demand toward irreducible floors via power laws; deployment becomes continual learning where one task eases the next.

Abstract: End-to-end vision-language-action (VLA) and world-action models offer an elegant route to general-purpose robotics, but their reliability is bounded by validated physical coverage. When an unfamiliar object, sensor, embodiment, or contact falls outside that coverage and no validated fallback exists, correcting the failure requires new robot data, a policy update, and regression testing. This recurring burden is the retraining tax. Unlike text, embodied data must often be created by operating machines. We present Teach-and-Grow Learning (TGL), an agent-centered architecture for general robot learning. In its general form, a multimodal agent turns a few successful demonstrations into reusable Skill Blocks: closed-loop behaviors for meaningful subgoals. In a new scene, the agent grounds and composes these blocks, selects learned or geometric tools, observes the physical outcome, and revises the route when execution departs from intent. A Skill Library stores executable behavior, while structured Experience Memory carries forward success, failure, and repair. New tasks are acquired without task-specific policy retraining. Our LIBERO evaluation attains state-of-the-art performance; controlled studies expose skill induction, persistent reuse, and agent-directed adaptation. Finally, we propose the Teach-and-Grow scaling-law hypothesis: if X denotes effective reusable experience, future-task error and teaching demand should approach irreducible floors as power laws in X. The architecture therefore treats deployment as a period of continued learning, in which one task can make the next easier.

</details>


### [177] [Robust Brachiation on a Life-Sized Dual-Arm Robot Using Waypoint-Guided Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17320)
*Ayumu Iwata,Kento Kawaharazuka,Keita Yoneda,Takahiro Hattori,Kei Okada*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Waypoint-Guided Reinforcement Learning (WGRL) enables robust life-sized dual-arm brachiation by guiding end-effector trajectories with sparse waypoints while RL generates whole-body motion; designed for Sim-to-Real transfer; demonstrated in Sim-to-Sim and hardware with failure recovery, expanding arm-based locomotion capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Brachiation requires highly coordinated, timing-critical, whole-body coordination; achieving robust life-sized robotic brachiation is challenging due to scarcity of reliable imitation data and sim-to-real gaps.

Method: Introduce WGRL: sparse waypoint guidance for end-effector trajectories to steer a reinforcement learning policy; whole-body motion emerges from RL; rewards incorporate task success and mechanical energy; training environment tailored for Sim-to-Real transfer; tested in Sim-to-Sim monkey-bar scenarios with geometric variation and on hardware; includes failure-recovery behaviors and provides design guidelines.

Result: The approach achieves forward progression and motion stability in both simulated and hardware experiments; demonstrates robust brachiation across variations and recoveries; confirms the method’s potential to extend the traversable workspace of life-sized arm-based robots.

Conclusion: WGRL offers a practical learning framework for arm-based locomotion on life-sized robots, delivering non-linear, coordinated brachiation behavior and providing actionable guidelines to design learning systems that generalize from simulation to real hardware.

Abstract: Brachiation is a form of locomotion in which primates move primarily using their arms, enabling traversal in environments without footholds. However, this motion requires highly coordinated whole-body movement and precise timing control for bar grasping and release. As a result, achieving robust behavior on life-sized robotic platforms remains challenging. In this study, we present a reinforcement learning-based method to realize brachiation on a life-sized dual-arm robot. The core of the proposed approach is Waypoint-Guided Reinforcement Learning (WGRL), a learning framework for inducing non-linear and complex motions. For high-difficulty tasks where imitation learning data are unavailable, WGRL guides behavior acquisition by sparsely specifying waypoints for the end-effector trajectory, while whole-body motion is generated through reinforcement learning. In addition, by integrating the waypoint-following guidance with rewards based on task success and mechanical energy, and training in an environment designed for Sim-to-Real transfer, the proposed method achieves both forward progression and motion stability. The acquired behavior is evaluated through Sim-to-Sim experiments under monkey-bar environments with geometric variations and hardware experiments, confirming robust brachiation including failure recovery behavior. This study provides effective learning design guidelines for realizing arm-based locomotion on life-sized robotic hardware and expanding the traversable workspace of robots.

</details>


### [178] [ORPA: Online Residual Policy Adaptation for Robot Manipulation Control with Human Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17323)
*Muhammad A. Muttaqien,Tomohiro Motoda,Ryo Hanai,Yukiyasu Domae*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ORPA adds a lightweight, feedback-conditioned residual module to a pretrained imitation-learning policy, enabling real-time correction in joint space without retraining, and improving robustness to perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation-based manipulation policies perform well under ideal conditions but degrade with small execution errors or distribution shifts; dataset aggregation and full retraining are costly and unsuitable for real-time deployment.

Method: Introduce Online Residual Policy Adaptation (ORPA): a compact residual module conditioned on runtime feedback that predicts joint-space action residuals to be added to the base policy's output. The module does not modify base policy parameters, allowing immediate runtime adaptation.

Result: Evaluation on precision-sensitive manipulation tasks using the ALOHA platform shows higher success rates and better recovery from small perturbations compared to baseline policies and rule-based inverse kinematics corrections.

Conclusion: ORPA enables immediate, runtime corrections without retraining, enhancing robustness and practicality of imitation-learning-based robotic manipulation.

Abstract: Robotic manipulation policies trained via imitation learning, such as Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT), can achieve strong performance under ideal conditions but often remain sensitive to small execution errors and distribution shifts. Correcting these failures typically requires dataset aggregation and full-policy retraining, which is computationally expensive and unsuitable for real-time deployment. In this work, we propose Online Residual Policy Adaptation (ORPA), a framework that enables immediate, feedback-driven correction of robot actions without modifying the underlying policy parameters. ORPA augments a pretrained control policy with a lightweight, feedback-conditioned module that predicts residual adjustments directly in joint space, allowing the system to adapt its behavior at runtime. We evaluate ORPA on a set of precision-sensitive manipulation tasks using the ALOHA platform, demonstrating improvements in success rate and recovery from small perturbations compared to baseline control policies and rule-based inverse kinematics corrections.

</details>


### [179] [Reconfiguration-Complete Motion Primitives with Constructive Planning for Deformable Planar Modular Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17324)
*Jie Gu,Tingting Wang,Hongrun Gao,Yirun Sun,Zhihao Xia,Chunxu Tian,Dan Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A square-cell abstraction maps deformable rhombus modules to a fixed grid to enable fixed, constructive reconfiguration planning. It uses two local primitives (pivoting and shearing) to transform any non-straight edge-connected configuration with N≥7 into a fixed staircase canonical form, with reversible motions ensuring mutual reachability. A staircase-canonicalization planner and a boundary-to-delivery lookahead selector yield a complete, efficient reconfiguration workflow. Experiments show reduced planning time and improvements over a prior framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The lack of a fixed representation for continuously deformable modular robots hinders reliable reconfiguration planning and analysis. A fixed, interpretable abstraction is needed to guarantee completeness and enable efficient planning.

Method: Introduce a square-cell abstraction that maps deformable rhombus modules to grid cells while preserving local motion semantics via two primitives: pivoting and shearing. Prove that every non-straight edge-connected configuration with N≥7 can be transformed to a fixed staircase using admissible primitives. The proof is constructive, yielding a staircase-canonicalization planner that moves removable boundary modules while preserving connectivity. Add a boundary-to-delivery lookahead selector to prioritize high-level decisions without breaking completeness.

Result: All non-straight edge-connected configurations with N≥7 are transformable to a fixed staircase canonical form via reversible primitives, enabling mutual reconfigurability within this class. The planner implements staircase canonicalization, and the lookahead selector substantially reduces planning time. Comparisons against a prior framework show lower planning times for similar shared module counts.

Conclusion: The authors provide a complete, constructive framework for reconfiguration of deformable rhombus-based modular robots using a fixed staircase canonical form. The approach yields reversible motions, guaranteeing mutual reconfigurability, and an efficient planner enhanced by a lookahead selector that preserves completeness while reducing planning time.

Abstract: The continuously deformable geometry of modular robots makes it difficult to define a fixed representation for reconfiguration planning and analysis. This letter introduces a square-cell abstraction that maps deformable rhombus modules to fixed-size grid cells while retaining physically interpretable local motions through two primitives, pivoting and shearing. Under this abstraction, we prove that every non-straight edge-connected configuration with $N \geq 7$ can be transformed to a fixed canonical staircase using only admissible primitive motions. Since these motions are reversible, any two configurations in this class are mutually reconfigurable. The proof is constructive and directly yields a staircase-canonicalization planner that transports removable boundary modules while preserving connectivity. As a practical enhancement, we further introduce a boundary-to-delivery lookahead selector that ranks admissible high level choices without affecting the completeness guarantee. Experiments demonstrate the constructive reconfiguration process and show that the selector substantially reduces planning time, while reference comparisons indicate lower planning times than the prior framework over the shared module counts.

</details>


### [180] [MANIGUARD: A Benchmark and Data Suite for Specification-Grounded Safety Evaluation and Improvement of Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17386)
*Yiyan Peng,Philip Wang,Simon Sinong Zhan,Yiqi Lyu,Zhenyang Ni,Jixin Yan,Fiorelli Wong,Ruochen Jiao,Hang Yin,Xinyu Cao,Huajie Shao,Manling Li,Ruohan Zhang,Qi Zhu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ManiGuard provides a specification-grounded safety evaluation and improvement framework for foundation-model manipulation, featuring ManiGuard-Bench (200 locked tasks across six families) and a safety-annotated trajectory pipeline. Evaluation includes in-distribution and single-axis perturbations with LTLf-based monitors, 8000 demonstrations, and 23k rollouts demonstrating safety gaps that persist despite fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation emphasizes task success in robotic manipulation, but safety under spec constraints is under-explored. A specification-grounded, scalable framework is needed to systematically assess and improve safe behavior of foundation-models in manipulation, including simulation-to-physical transfer and observability-neutral safety predicates.

Method: Construct ManiGuard-Bench with six contact-rich task families organized into 200 locked base tasks; apply one in-distribution plus four single-axis distributional perturbations (1000 locked scenarios total). Use LTL_f-grounded automaton monitors over physics-grounded predicates to runtime-check safety in simulation and on a Franka robot. Pair automated motion-planning with human teleoperation, annotated by the same safety monitors, enabling safety-aware fine-tuning. Release 8,000 safety-annotated demonstrations (40 per base task). Evaluate zero-shot and fine-tuned VLAs across >23,000 rollouts to assess safety vs task success.

Result: Key findings: (i) safety must be measured independently of task success since 6-21% of successful rollouts violate the spec. (ii) fine-tuning improves safe task completion from near-zero to 7.5-29.8% and engaged-and-safe behavior from 16-40% to 51-72%. (iii) still a substantial gap with 21-42% of engaged rollouts violating and two of six families with near-zero safe success, with these failures persisting under distribution shift and hardware tests.

Conclusion: Specification-grounded safety evaluation is essential and reveals safety gaps that task-success metrics miss. While coarse-grained fine-tuning improves safety metrics, a non-trivial portion of engaged rollouts remain unsafe, indicating the need for stronger or different safety-aligned training, more robust distributional generalization, and continued development of runtime safety monitors and benchmarks for robotic manipulation.

Abstract: Foundation-model policies for robotic manipulation are advancing rapidly on task success, but rigorous evaluation of whether they succeed safely is still lacking. We introduce ManiGuard, a specification-grounded framework for evaluating and improving the safety of foundation-model manipulation, comprising the ManiGuard-Bench task suite and a paired safety-annotated trajectory-generation pipeline. ManiGuard-Bench organizes six contact-rich household task families into 200 locked base tasks along a skill $\times$ constraint taxonomy, with safety specified independently of task success. Each task is evaluated under one in-distribution and four single-axis out-of-distribution perturbations that hold the safety specification fixed, giving 1,000 locked scenarios. Every rollout is runtime-checked by LTL$_f$-grounded automaton monitors over physics-grounded predicates rather than learned classifiers or LLM judges, in simulation and on a physical Franka platform. The pipeline pairs an automated motion-planning generator with human teleoperation, annotated by the same per-step monitor, and directly supports safety-aware fine-tuning; we release 8,000 safety-annotated demonstrations, 40 per base task. Benchmarking zero-shot and fine-tuned VLAs across more than 23,000 rollouts, we find: (i) safety must be evaluated independently of task success, as 6-21% of successful rollouts violate the specification; (ii) fine-tuning on our suite raises safe task completion from near zero to 7.5-29.8% and engaged-and-safe behavior from 16-40% to 51-72%; but (iii) a gap remains that scaling demonstrations does not close, with 21-42% of engaged rollouts still violating, two of six families below 2% safe success for every policy, and these failures persisting under distribution shift and on hardware.

</details>


### [181] [Bi-Layer Ant Colony Optimization for Multi-Robot Task Allocation and Routing in Delivery Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17416)
*Le Na Nguyen,Thanh Long Nguyen,Thanh Thao Ton Nu,Quan Le,Manh Duong Phung*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A unified MRTA framework using a bi-layer ant colony optimization (ACO) that optimizes task assignment and routing jointly, outperforming MILP and PSO in travel distance and completion time for varying task sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To efficiently solve multi-robot task allocation by merging task assignment and routing into a single optimization problem and enabling scalable, reliable delivery solutions.

Method: Introduce a new cost function to unify MRTA into one optimization objective and implement a bi-layer ACO with two interdependent decision layers operating within one colony to co-optimize task allocation and route planning across multiple robots.

Result: Experiments against MILP and PSO show the bi-layer ACO achieves the shortest total travel distance and fastest completion time across all task sizes, reducing travel distance by up to 17.7% and completion time by about 20% relative to baselines.

Conclusion: The bi-layer ACO is efficient, scalable, and reliable for multi-robot delivery tasks, effectively solving integrated task assignment and routing challenges.

Abstract: This paper addresses the multi-robot task allocation (MRTA) problem, which is essential for delivery and logistics applications. Our approach first defines a new cost function that transforms the MRTA into a unified optimization problem capturing both task assignment and routing. A bi-layer ant colony optimization (ACO) algorithm is then introduced, integrating two interdependent decision layers within a single colony process to solve the problem. This hierarchical framework enables simultaneous optimization of task allocation and route planning across multiple robots. Comparative experiments with mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) and particle swarm optimization (PSO) demonstrate that the proposed bi-layer ACO achieves the shortest total travel distance and fastest completion time across all task sizes. Specifically, it reduces total travel distance by up to 17.7% and completion time by nearly 20% compared with baseline methods. These results confirm the efficiency, scalability, and reliability of the proposed bi-layer ACO for multi-robot delivery tasks.

</details>


### [182] [Prism-GRPO: Faster VLA Policy Optimization via Splitting Same-outcome Groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17423)
*Zeyun Deng,Yuzhe Lu,Yawei Wang,Linbo Liu,Qing Ping,Han Ding,Guande Wu,Panpan Xu,Jun Huan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Prism-GRPO augments GRPO with a trajectory-level execution-quality score to differentiate same-outcome groups, improving sample efficiency and robustness. It reduces wasted rollouts, increases success rates within a fixed rollout budget (up to 56% fewer rollouts to reach target), and transfers cleaner behavior to real robots, while mitigating reward hacking. The approach is validated across RoboTwin tasks with ablations over multiple quality signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRPO eliminates the need for a critic but incurs a sampling cost because group-relative advantages require multiple rollouts per scene. In early training, many groups share binary outcomes (all success or all failure) and have zero advantage, leading to discarded data and wasted rollout budget. Prism-GRPO introduces a trajectory-level quality signal to recover training signal without task-specific progress rewards.

Method: Augment binary outcome reward with a weighted trajectory-level execution-quality score. Split same-outcome groups into a spectrum of quality levels so that successes outrank failures even when outcomes are identical. Derive quality signals from simulator contacts, executed actions, or visual observations (VLM), avoiding task-specific progress rewards. Prove Prism-GRPO does not increase the chance of discarding a group due to zero advantage and establish a gradient-alignment condition under which the combined update is a local ascent direction. Evaluate on four RoboTwin tasks with varying horizons and coordination patterns; ablations analyze different quality signals.

Result: Prism-GRPO yields higher success and quality at matched rollout budgets and reduces rollouts needed to reach target success by up to 56%. It suppresses a reward-hacking shortcut, with cleaner behavior transferring to real robots. Across ablations, gains persist for contact-, smoothness-, and VLM-derived quality signals.

Conclusion: Quality-aware augmentation in Prism-GRPO provides a more informative training signal for GRPO-based VLA policies, improving sample efficiency and robustness without trusting noisy binary outcomes. The approach generalizes across horizons and coordination patterns and is compatible with multiple quality-signal sources.

Abstract: GRPO is increasingly used for reinforcement learning of vision-language-action (VLA) policies because, unlike PPO, it does not require training a critic. This simplification comes with a sampling cost: group-relative advantages require multiple rollouts from each scene. Under binary success rewards, groups whose rollouts all succeed or all fail have zero advantage and are discarded by dynamic sampling. These groups are especially common early in training, when most rollouts fail, wasting much of the expensive robotic rollout budget. We introduce Prism-GRPO, which augments binary outcome reward with a weighted trajectory-level execution-quality score. By splitting same-outcome groups into a quality spectrum, Prism-GRPO recovers training signal while ensuring that every success still outranks every failure. Quality scores can be derived from simulator contacts, executed actions, or visual observations, avoiding task-specific progress rewards. We prove that Prism-GRPO never increases the probability that a sampled group is discarded for having zero advantages, and derive a gradient-alignment condition under which its combined update remains a local ascent direction for task success. Across four RoboTwin tasks spanning different horizons and coordination patterns, Prism-GRPO improves success and quality at matched rollout budgets and reaches target success rates with up to 56% fewer rollouts. It also suppresses a reward-hacking shortcut, with the cleaner behavior transferring under direct deployment to a real robot. Through ablations, we show consistent gains across contact-, smoothness-, and VLM-derived quality signals.

</details>


### [183] [UniReflex: Plug-and-Play Force Control for Pretrained Generative Policies via Fast-Slow Reflex](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17432)
*Yan Huang,Shoujie Li,Ziwu Song,Wenbo Ding*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: UniReflex is a plug-and-play framework that endows frozen generative policies with variable impedance control (VIC) for contact regulation by tapping force-direction intent from demonstrations, without backbone fine-tuning; it uses a fast reflex network to separate force exertion from interaction response and achieves substantial latency reductions and improved contact stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative imitation policies excel at trajectory planning but lack closed-loop force regulation; incorporating force modalities usually requires redesigning or retraining networks. A universal, non-invasive VIC addition is needed to improve contact robustness without retraining.

Method: UniReflex intercepts deep latent representations from the action head of frozen generative policies and uses them to drive a fast reflex network that decouples active force exertion from external interaction response. It predicts normalized anisotropic stiffness directions for directional compliance and includes an adaptive gating mechanism to switch between position-dominant planning and force-dominant execution. It operates as a plug-in with no slow-backbone fine-tuning and relies on force-direction intent from demonstrations.

Result: Real-world bimanual experiments show significantly improved contact stability and success rates while preserving original position accuracy. Per-step backward latency is 25-66x lower than joint training strategies across evaluated backbones.

Conclusion: UniReflex offers a universal, efficient, non-invasive method to elevate frozen generative policies with VIC, delivering robust contact regulation and large latency improvements without retraining.

Abstract: Generative imitation learning policies excel at trajectory planning but lack closed-loop force regulation, while directly incorporating force modalities often requires redesigning or retraining the network. We present UniReflex, a universal plug-and-play framework that equips frozen generative policies with variable impedance control (VIC) for contact regulation, guided by force-direction intent collected during demonstration, without further slow-backbone fine-tuning. By non-invasively intercepting deep latent representations from the action head, UniReflex drives a fast reflex network that decouples active force exertion from external interaction response. This scheme predicts normalized anisotropic stiffness directions for directional compliance allocation. Furthermore, UniReflex integrates an adaptive gating mechanism that enables seamless transitions between position-dominant planning and force-dominant execution. Real-world bimanual experiments demonstrate that UniReflex significantly improves contact stability and success rates while preserving original position accuracy. Our approach achieves 25-66x lower per-step backward latency relative to joint training strategies on the evaluated backbones.

</details>


### [184] [EATR-Stereo: Embodiment-Aware Routing of Paired Stereo Evidence for Humanoid Vision-Language-Action Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17453)
*Songwei Wu,Rui Zhao,Fan Yang,Zhongqiang Nie,Zhiduo Jiang,Wandong Sun,Yuwei Li,Yang Liu,Hong Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Embodiment-aware, cross-view token routing (EATR-Stereo) preserves primary-view tokens while selectively incorporating synchronized stereo cues via CVATs and proprioceptive routing, enhancing long-horizon humanoid VLA control with a frozen pretrained model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon humanoid vision–language–action (VLA) control requires integrating complementary stereo views without disrupting pretrained vision-language representations or the primary-view processing. Existing interfaces often discard stereo evidence or misalign auxiliary data with robot embodiment.

Method: Introduce EATR-Stereo: a token-routing framework that retains primary-view tokens, builds primary-aligned Cross-View Auxiliary Tokens (CVATs) from synchronized auxiliary-view tokens, and uses a body-segmented proprioceptive encoder to condition auxiliary usage on robot configuration history. The routed cross-view stream augments a frozen pretrained VLA’s context (language + primary vision) without modifying the model.

Result: Empirical evaluation on a 33-DoF humanoid with 37-D proprioceptive state across 9 configurations and 100+ search–grasp–place–return tasks yields 60.0% full-task success, 100.0% grasp success, and 80.0% stage success. Under severe asymmetric occlusion, recovery improves to 80% versus 30% for CVAT alone. Ablations show preserving primary tokens and coupling cross-view auxiliary features with proprioceptive routing are key.

Conclusion: Selective routing of stereo evidence, aligned with primary view and conditioned on robot state, improves spatial grounding and reliability in long-horizon humanoid VLA control using a frozen pretrained model.

Abstract: Long-horizon humanoid vision--language--action (VLA) control with head-mounted stereo cameras requires visual interfaces that can exploit complementary views while maintaining compatibility with pretrained representations. Existing interfaces often discard complementary stereo evidence or fuse additional observations without preserving the native primary-view pathway and adapting auxiliary information to robot embodiment. We present EATR-Stereo, an embodiment-aware token-routing framework that retains primary-view tokens and constructs primary-aligned Cross-View Auxiliary Tokens (CVATs) by querying the synchronized auxiliary-view token sequence. A body-segmented proprioceptive encoder further conditions token-wise auxiliary usage on robot configuration history, enabling selective incorporation of stereo evidence during action generation. The routed auxiliary stream augments the language and primary-visual context of a pretrained VLA while keeping its vision--language model frozen. On a 33-DoF physical humanoid with a 37-D proprioceptive state, we evaluate nine configurations in over-100-s search--approach--grasp--place--return tasks. EATR-Stereo achieves 60.0% full-task success, 100.0% grasp success, and 80.0% stage success. Under severe asymmetric occlusion, it improves recovery to 80% compared with 30% for CVAT alone. Ablation studies further show the importance of preserving primary tokens and combining cross-view auxiliary features with structured proprioceptive routing. These results demonstrate that selectively routed paired stereo evidence improves spatial grounding for reliable long-horizon humanoid VLA control.

</details>


### [185] [Reuse Before You Retrieve: Diagnosing Headroom and Complementarity for Test-Time Augmentation of Embodied Multimodal Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17484)
*Yuhwan Jeong,Kuk-Jin Yoon*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces recoverable headroom and retrieval complementarity to guide test-time augmentation of frozen VLA policies; demonstrates a retry selector and retrieval that improve success rates across backbones and domains, with up to 21 points gains and cross-domain transferability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is little guidance on when to apply test-time interventions to frozen vision-language-action policies. The work seeks to quantify when sampling additional behaviors or external priors can improve performance by distinguishing recoverable internal capability from missing external priors.

Method: Define two measurable factors (recoverable headroom and retrieval complementarity). Evaluate an episode-level retry selector under retryable or parallel execution. Apply retrieval across multiple frozen VLA policies and environments. Experiments conducted on LIBERO across different robots and simulators, including degraded observations. Use autoregressive OpenVLA to illustrate headroom vs ranking, and study retrieval behavior.

Result: The retry selector recovers substantial latent capability across VLA backbones, yielding up to 21.0 percentage-point gains in success rate, closely tracking recoverable headroom. Demonstrates transfer to different robot/simulator and robustness under degraded observations. Retrieval improves the policy with the largest action-prior gap and provides additional gains when combined with selection. Findings distinguish between recoverable headroom and ranking ability, and collectively offer empirical guidance on when to use sampling vs retrieval at test time.

Conclusion: Empirically grounds test-time augmentation by separating what can be recovered from the frozen policy versus what priors must be introduced externally, enabling more informed decisions about intervention strategies for VLA policies.

Abstract: Frozen vision-language-action (VLA) policies are increasingly improved at test time by sampling additional policy behaviors or introducing external demonstrations. Yet there is little guidance for deciding which intervention a deployed policy actually needs. Additional sampling is useful only when better behavior already exists within the policy's stochastic rollouts and can be identified, whereas retrieval is most useful when the relevant action prior is not reliably represented by the policy. We study this decision through two measurable factors, recoverable headroom and retrieval complementarity, which characterize how much useful behavior is already available to recover and whether an external action prior fills a measurable gap. We evaluate an episode-level retry selector under retryable or parallel execution, together with retrieval across multiple frozen VLA policies and environments. The selector consistently recovers substantial latent capability across all tested VLA backbones on LIBERO, with gains of up to 21.0 success-rate points that closely track recoverable headroom. It also transfers to a different robot and simulator and remains effective under degraded observations, while experiments with autoregressive OpenVLA illustrate the distinction between available headroom and the ability to rank candidate rollouts. Retrieval behaves differently, improving the policy with the largest measured action-prior gap and providing further gains when combined with selection. Together, these results provide an empirical basis for characterizing test-time augmentation opportunities by separating capability that can be recovered from the frozen policy from behavioral priors that may need to be introduced externally.

</details>


### [186] [Calibrated Predictive Safety for Heterogeneous Robots: An Action-Conditioned JEPA Framework with Model-Based Safety Shields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17496)
*Kaiming Zhong,Tianhua Liu,Yue Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Hybrid action-conditioned JEPA world model with embodiment-aware safety shield for receding-horizon planning; improves success and reduces collision false negatives in simulation; real-world deployment remains future work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language-action policies lack execution-time guarantees and generalization. A hybrid approach seeks to predict task progress and physical risk for action chunks and enforce safety via a deterministic shield, enabling deployable pipelines for heterogeneous robots.

Method: A receding-horizon pipeline: (1) proposer outputs K candidate action chunks; (2) action-conditioned JEPA rolls out each candidate in a frozen-encoder latent space conditioned on an embodiment embedding; (3) calibrated risk and progress heads score each rollout with uncertainty; (4) a deterministic per-embodiment safety shield filters inadmissible candidates; (5) a fallback ladder handles empty-admissible-set cases; learned ranking only reorders admissible candidates; guarantees come from shield and ladder.

Result: Evaluated in a pre-registered simulation protocol (LIBERO-Long) across 600 episodes; full framework improved success over a shield-only baseline and reduced collision false negatives at matched recall; deployment-efficiency measurements on target on-robot and edge accelerators included; real-robot experiments and offline reranking significance test are future work.

Conclusion: Coupling a learned, action-conditioned model with a deterministic safety shield yields a deployable pipeline with execution-time guarantees when the shield is active; real-world validation and more rigorous statistical tests are planned for future work.

Abstract: Vision-language-action policies generalize broadly but provide no execution-time guarantees; classical model-based planners respect kinematic and geometric constraints but generalize poorly. We study whether an action-conditioned Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) world model can predict, before execution, both task progress and physical risk for candidate action chunks, and whether coupling these predictions to an embodiment-specific model-based safety shield yields a deployable pipeline for heterogeneous robots.
  We propose a receding-horizon decision pipeline: (1) a proposer produces K candidate action chunks; (2) an action-conditioned JEPA rolls each candidate forward in a frozen-encoder latent space conditioned on an embodiment embedding; (3) calibrated risk and progress heads score each rollout and report uncertainty; (4) a deterministic per-embodiment safety shield filters inadmissible candidates; (5) a fallback ladder handles empty-admissible-set cases. The learned ranking only reorders admissible candidates; enforcement guarantees come from the deterministic shield and fallback ladder.
  We evaluate with a pre-registered protocol in simulation (LIBERO-Long). In 600-episode configurations the full framework improved success over a shield-only baseline and reduced collision false negatives at matched recall. Deployment-efficiency measurements on target on-robot and edge accelerators are included. Real-robot experiments and an offline reranking significance test remain future work; see the paper for disclosures.

</details>


### [187] [Embodied-Navigator: Point, Think, Memorize, and Align for Efficient Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17512)
*Hongyan Feng,Sunlai Chen,Xuanyu Liu,Miao Pan,Yangfan Xie,Yuxiang Cui,Zhongxiang Zhou,Rong Xiong,Wenqi Zhang,Jianwei Yin,Yueting Zhuang,Xuhong Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TAMP-Nav is a unified embodied-navigation framework combining Pixel-to-3D Action (Point), selective reasoning with anchor-trajectory memory (Think and Memorize), and a two-level alignment paradigm (Align) with GRPO, achieving state-of-the-art results with efficient training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLM-based embodied navigation faces misalignment between 2D pre-training priors and 3D action spaces, rigid reasoning timetables, and inefficient memory use. A cohesive framework that preserves 2D priors while enabling robust 3D control and efficient memory is needed.

Method: Point: reformulates navigation as 2D visual prompting where VLM selects 2D pixels, which are projected to 3D coordinates for a low-level SLAM controller. Think and Memorize: dynamic, selective trigger of Chain-of-Thought and retention of high-fidelity memory at critical nodes, with compression of redundant trajectories into Space-Time Indicators. Align: Two-Level Alignment via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), combining global outcome rewards with dense process rewards for tighter cognitive-to-physical alignment.

Result: Empirical results show state-of-the-art performance, e.g., 66.2% Success Rate on R2R-CE, and high runtime and sample efficiency, requiring only 90k training trajectories.

Conclusion: TAMP-Nav substantively integrates 2D VLM priors with 3D embodied control, memory-efficient reasoning, and dense reward shaping to enhance navigation performance and efficiency.

Abstract: Although Large Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have significantly advanced embodied navigation, their direct deployment remains challenging, as existing methods often force VLMs into unnatural action spaces that misalign with their 2D pre-training priors, compounded by rigid reasoning schedules and inefficient memory management. To overcome these limitations, we propose TAMP-Nav, a unified framework for efficient embodied navigation. First, we introduce a Pixel-to-3D Action Formulation (Point) that reformulates navigation into 2D visual prompting. Specifically, the VLM merely selects 2D pixels, which are then projected into 3D coordinates for a low-level SLAM controller. This design naturally aligns embodied execution with the VLM's inherent 2D visual capabilities. Second, we propose an integrated Selective Reasoning and Anchor-Trajectory Memory mechanism (Think and Memorize), which dynamically triggers Chain-of-Thought and retains high-fidelity memory only at critical nodes, compressing redundant trajectories into lightweight Space-Time Indicators, thereby preserving critical historical information and enhancing spatio-temporal perception. Finally, we design an efficient Two-Level Alignment Paradigm (Align) via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). By superimposing global outcome rewards with fine-grained process rewards, this dense supervision tightly aligns the agent's cognitive planning with physical environmental feedback, endowing the model with adaptive reasoning capabilities. Experiments demonstrate that TAMP-Nav achieves state-of-the-art performance (e.g., 66.2% SR on R2R-CE) with high runtime and sample efficiency (requiring only 90k training trajectories).

</details>


### [188] [Scalix: Uncertainty-Aware Scale-Consistent Monocular SLAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17553)
*Sebastian Barbas Laina,Tianyi Zhang,Panagiotis Petropoulakis,Simon Schaefer,Simon Boche,Jaehyung Jung,Cedric Le Gentil,Stefan Leutenegger*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Scalix is a real-time monocular SLAM system that achieves metric-scale state estimation by fusing learned depth cues with uncertainty into a probabilistic factor-graph. It treats per-pixel depth uncertainty and per-frame scale uncertainty as independent measurements, improving scale consistency across views. It reports state-of-the-art performance on metric and up-to-scale benchmarks while maintaining real-time operation and generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monocular SLAM suffers from scale ambiguity. Depth maps from deep models are noisy and often scale-inconsistent across frames. A solution is needed to obtain reliable metric scale from monocular data without extra sensors.

Method: Augment monocular depth models with per-pixel depth uncertainty and per-frame scale uncertainty. Integrate scale predictions as independent measurements within a probabilistic factor-graph optimization, leveraging multi-view data associations to improve scale consistency. Preserve real-time operation.

Result: Experiments on large-scale outdoor and indoor environments show state-of-the-art performance on both metric and up-to-scale benchmarks, with real-time operation and strong generalization.

Conclusion: Scalix demonstrates that metric-scale monocular SLAM is achievable by probabilistic fusion of learned depth cues and uncertainty in a factor-graph framework, enabling reliable scale-consistent mapping and robust real-time performance.

Abstract: Cameras are ubiquitous sensors in robotics due to their compact form factor and the perceptual richness captured through visual information. Monocular SLAM enables robots to understand the environment with a minimum setup, however, it inherently suffers from scale ambiguity. A common solution is to provide multi-modal sensor configurations, such as visual-inertial systems, where scale is observable unless the robot navigates under a constant-velocity motion, a common scenario in mobile robotics. With the advent of deep-learning, geometric foundation models have been used to address this problem, but the depths maps are often noisy and scale-inconsistent across frames. In this paper, we propose Scalix, a real-time monocular SLAM framework that achieves metric-scale state estimation by integrating learned depth cues into a probabilistic factor-graph formulation. By augmenting existing monocular depth models with both per-pixel depth uncertainty and per-frame scale uncertainty, Scalix treats scale predictions as independent measurements within its optimization, leading to improved scale consistency through multi-view data associations. Experiments in large-scale outdoor and indoor environments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on both metric and up-to-scale benchmarks while maintaining real-time operation and generalization.

</details>


### [189] [HODAgent: Towards On-Demand, Responsive Humanoids for Physical World Human Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17584)
*Wang Warren Chen,Jiahao Zhang,Zhenjiang Li,Mingxu Wang,Lei Yi,Yuchen Kang,Shuo Sun,Ziping Chen,Jie Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A System-2 embodied humanoid agent (HODAgent) integrates Env-Interactor, Planner, Executor, and hierarchical Memory to manage situated intent, adaptive execution, mid-task revision, and outcome verification across simulation and real-world humanoid robots, achieving notable improvements over baselines on both simulated and physical tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address coherent interaction, planning, and task state maintenance in service robotics, enabling adaptation to new requests mid-episode and grounding task completion in execution outcomes, while bridging simulation and hardware via a shared interface.

Method: Proposes a semi-duplex architecture with four components: Env-Interactor, Planner, Executor, and hierarchical Memory. A shared interface links simulation environments and a physical humanoid (Unitree G1). Evaluation in interactive simulation (164 cases) with two VLM backbones; physical robot tests measuring pass rates across atomic, composite, and complete tasks; benchmarks across multiple embodied tasks.

Result: In simulation, 84.8% and 91.5% Joint Success for two VLM backbones, outperforming baselines by 9.8 and 18.9 points. On Unitree G1, pass rates are 92% (atomic), 72% (composite), 63.3% (complete). Across multiple embodied benchmarks, improvements of 0.7–9.0 points over baselines.

Conclusion: A unified System-2 agent enables adaptive humanoid service across simulation and reality, demonstrating robust task grounding, revision, and outcome verification in real-world and simulated settings.

Abstract: We propose HODAgent, a System-2 embodied agent for humanoid robots in service settings, addressing situated intent, responsive execution, task revision, and outcome verification. Its semi-duplex architecture integrates an Env-Interactor, Planner, Executor, and hierarchical Memory to maintain coherent interaction, planning, and task state during service episodes. This allows handling new requests during motion, retaining progress, revising actions, and grounding closure in execution outcomes. A shared interface connects simulation and physical robots (Unitree G1), isolating platform-specific control. In an interactive simulation with 164 cases, HODAgent achieves 84.8% and 91.5% Joint Success under two VLM backbones, outperforming baselines by 9.8 and 18.9 points. On physical robots, pass rates are 92% (atomic), 72% (composite), and 63.3% (complete tasks). On multiple embodied benchmarks, it improves over baselines by 0.7-9.0 points. Results show a unified System-2 agent enables adaptive humanoid service across simulation and reality.

</details>


### [190] [tinyDSM: A Framework for Skill Modeling and Development for Resource-Constrained Millirobots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17596)
*Markus D. Kobelrausch,Michael Miedler,Axel Jantsch*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes a developmental framework for cm-sized millirobots that learns autonomously via tinyDSM, integrating intrinsic motivation and fitness-based assessment to enable open-ended skill acquisition with minimal hard-wired priors, validated on a 36 cm^3 robot using RP2040 and simulated analyses; demonstrates progression from basic motor skills to complex geometric patterns in ~15 minutes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable autonomous exploration, learning, and long-horizon adaptation for resource-constrained millirobots, aiming for generic applicability with minimal a-priori knowledge while still supporting structured skill organization.

Method: A developmental mechanism with intrinsic motivation embedded in a cognitive architecture (knowledge, reasoning, learning), implemented with tinyDSM that combines intrinsic motivation and fitness-based evaluation. Uses a hierarchical knowledge graph and kinematic reasoners to model and assess skills. Reinforcement learning guides skill acquisition and adaptation.

Result: Experimental validation on a 36 cm^3 millirobot (RP2040-based) showing autonomous progression from elementary motor skills to complex geometric patterns within 15 minutes. Adds simulation-based analysis for systematic comparisons across learning algorithms and intrinsic motivation parameters.

Conclusion: The framework supports open-ended, resource-efficient skill development for small robots by starting from minimal priors and letting system-specific dependencies emerge, with demonstrated rapid skill acquisition and a methodology adaptable to generic application domains.

Abstract: In this study, we investigate developmental mechanisms that enable small, resource-constrained systems such as cm-sized millirobots to autonomously explore, learn, and adapt their capabilities throughout their lifespan. Reinforcement learning algorithms guide the agent's skill acquisition and adaptation through the interplay of our proposed tinyDSM, which integrates intrinsic motivation and fitness-based assessment. We strive for minimal, hard-wired skills while encouraging the open-ended development of new skills. A key emphasis in our approach is to encode minimal a-priori general knowledge, which serves as a foundational starting point for the system as it further learns system-specific dependencies from the initial knowledge provided. Thus, by design, our approach attempts to cover very generic application domains. The methodology is based on (a) developmental mechanism with intrinsic motivation, and (b) a cognitive architecture (knowledge, reasoning, learning), while (c) utilizing minimal resources. It uses a hierarchical knowledge graph and kinematic reasoners to model and evaluate simple and advanced motion related skills. In our experiments, we use a resource-constrained millirobot with a volume of 36 cm^3 with a Raspberry Pi Pico 32-bit microcontroller (RP2040) that integrates all described features and capabilities except the camera system in 9 kB. Starting with learning the most elementary motor skills the millirobot autonomously progresses from simple linear and angular movements to complex geometric patterns within 15 minutes. To complement the physical experiments, we perform a simulation-based analysis that enables systematic comparisons across learning algorithms and intrinsic motivation parameters.

</details>


### [191] [LIBERO-VIFO: Benchmarking the Capability and Safety of Visual Cue Following in Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17600)
*Zhengyan Qian,Rui Yan,Alex Jinpeng Wang,Jinhui Tang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LIBERO-VIFO benchmarks visual cue following in Vision-Language-Action models, assessing both capability and safety across eight cue families and two-part protocols; reveals gaps between cue understanding and execution and risks of unauthorized following; validated on seven models and in real-robot settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As VLA models are deployed in real-world scenarios, ensuring they follow authorized cues and ignore unauthorized ones is crucial for safety. Current evaluations emphasize task success and neglect visual-safety aspects; a comprehensive benchmark is needed to evaluate both capability and safety of visual cue following.

Method: Define LIBERO-VIFO with eight visual cue families. Two-part protocols: Part I tests cue understanding and authorized following; Part II tests unauthorized cue following under language-cue conflict and empty language conditions. Evaluate seven VLA models; include extended experiments on scene-instantiated cues, safety-critical settings, and real-robot deployment.

Result: Cue understanding does not reliably translate to execution; VLAs can execute cue-indicated tasks without language instruction, enabling unauthorized cue following; results reinforced by scene-instantiated cues, safety-critical tests, and real-robot deployment.

Conclusion: LIBERO-VIFO provides a systematic framework to evaluate both capability and safety of visual cue following, highlighting visual-centric safety as a new direction for the VLA community.

Abstract: Visual cues are increasingly adopted to guide robot learning, but whether Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models can reliably follow authorized cues while disregarding unauthorized ones remains unclear. Existing work covers only a narrow range of cue forms and focuses on final task success, providing only a coarse assessment of cue-following capability. Treating all visual cues as authorized also leaves safety risks of unauthorized following unexplored. To address these gaps, we introduce LIBERO-VIFO, a benchmark to evaluate both the capability and safety of visual cue following in VLA models. LIBERO-VIFO defines eight visual cue families spanning diverse forms. A total of four protocols in two parts are defined: Part I tests cue understanding and authorized following, while Part II evaluates unauthorized visual cue following under language-cue conflict and empty language conditions. Evaluating seven VLA models reveals that although visual cue understanding does not reliably translate into execution, current VLAs are able to execute cue-indicated tasks without language instruction, exposing an emerging risk of unauthorized visual cue following. Extended experiments on scene-instantiated cues, safety-critical settings, and real-robot deployment corroborate these findings. LIBERO-VIFO brings both the capability and safety of visual cue following into systematic evaluation, establishing visual-centric safety as a new perspective for the VLA community.

</details>


### [192] [Physics-Informed Sliding-Window Particle Filtering for Tactile-Only In-Hand 6-DoF Object Pose Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17601)
*Lingjun Shao,Ying Zhang,Xiangfei Li,Xiangyang Li,Huan Zhao,Zhenyu Wang,Han Ding*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A physics-informed SE(3) particle-filter for tactile-only 6-DoF pose refinement of grasped objects, featuring temporal fusion, symmetry-aware resampling, and a potential-guided proposal, achieving improved ADD-S on a 5-object Allegro Hand study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision is unavailable or heavily occluded during in-hand manipulation; tactile measurements are partial, sparse, and ambiguous under symmetry. Robust belief maintenance and multimodal inference are needed for accurate pose estimates.

Method: A SE(3) particle filter uses dense whole-hand tactile data. The likelihood combines active-contact signed-distance consistency, force-normal alignment, friction-cone feasibility, zero-force negative evidence, and optional feasibility guards. A sliding-window log-likelihood fuses recent frames; a potential-field-guided proposal reduces hand–object penetration; symmetry-aware resampling preserves multiple plausible modes.

Result: Experiments on an Allegro Hand V5 with five objects show lower normalized ADD-S than tactile-only geometric, particle-filter, and learning baselines. Ablations confirm benefits of temporal fusion, potential guidance, and mode preservation.

Conclusion: The proposed physics-informed tactile-filtering approach improves tactile-only pose refinement under partial observability, with temporal fusion and symmetry-aware mechanisms essential for reliable multimodal beliefs.

Abstract: This paper studies tactile-only 6-DoF pose refinement and belief maintenance for grasped objects in static and short quasi-static in-hand configurations where vision is unavailable or heavily occluded. The key difficulty is tactile partial observability: whole-hand taxel contacts are sparse, intermittent, and ambiguous under limited excitation and object symmetries. We propose a physics-informed particle filter on $\mathrm{SE}(3)$ that updates pose beliefs from dense whole-hand tactile measurements. The likelihood combines active-contact signed-distance consistency, force-normal alignment, friction-cone feasibility, zero-force negative evidence, and optional feasibility guards. A sliding-window log-likelihood fuses recent tactile frames to reduce single-frame ambiguity, while a potential-field-guided proposal steers particles away from hand--object penetration. Symmetry-aware resampling preserves multiple plausible modes. Experiments on an Allegro Hand V5 with five objects show lower normalized ADD-S than tactile-only geometric, particle-filter, and learning baselines, and ablations confirm the benefits of temporal fusion, potential guidance, and mode preservation.

</details>


### [193] [Iterative Grasp Pose Refinement: A Deep Reinforcement Learning Approach for 2D Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17628)
*Amir Arsalan Nematollahi,Shayan Ahmadi,Mehdi Tale Masouleh,Ahmad Kalhor*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RL-based grasp refinement using keypoint representations and a DQN to turn geometrically suggested grasps into successful ones; validated in simulation on 300 Dex-Net objects and transfer to a Delta robot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need compact, interpretable, and generalizable representations for object manipulation; overcome limitations of purely geometric grasp methods by integrating learning for refinement and enabling sim-to-real transfer.

Method: Combine keypoint-based object representations with a Deep Q-Network. Start from 2D overhead images in simulation; a geometric algorithm provides initial grasp candidates; the RL-based refinement iteratively converts failed grasps into successful ones.

Result: Achieves 100% success on objects previously deemed ungraspable by geometric methods for 300 Dex-Net objects with a UR5; demonstrated sim-to-real transfer with a Delta robot where a refined grasp manipulates an object previously ungraspable.

Conclusion: RL-based grasp refinement is effective and scalable for contact-rich manipulation, offering generalization and sim-to-real transfer capabilities in robotic grasping.

Abstract: Developing robots capable of understanding and manipulating objects requires compact, interpretable, and generalizable representations. This work proposes a reinforcement learning-based framework for robotic grasp refinement, integrating keypoint-based object representations with a Deep Q-Network (DQN). Using 2D overhead images captured in a simulated environment, a geometric-based algorithm generates initial grasp candidates, which are iteratively refined by the proposed framework, transforming failed grasps into successful ones. Experiments conducted on 300 objects from the Dex-Net dataset using a UR5 manipulator demonstrate the framework's effectiveness, achieving a 100% success rate on objects previously deemed ungraspable by geometrical methods. The framework's sim-to-real transferability is further validated through physical experiments on a Delta parallel robot, where a refined grasp successfully manipulates an object that was previously ungraspable. The findings underscore the effectiveness of reinforcement learning in addressing challenges in robotic grasping, offering a scalable and adaptable solution for contact-rich manipulation tasks.

</details>


### [194] [OVIP-SG: Open-Vocabulary Instance-Preserving Scene Graphs for Mapping and Retrieval of Small, Fine-Grained Objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17633)
*Tianjing Hao,Haiyu Lan,Angsong Li,Cheng Chen,Enyu Li,Jiarui Yang,Yuning Su,Peiwen Lin,Wang Chuang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A unified open-world 3D semantic mapping framework (OVIP-SG) that preserves instance-level fidelity while enabling language-guided, open-vocabulary detection and retrieval, improving detection metrics and search efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To leverage vision-language models (VLMs) for open-vocabulary perception in 3D scene graphs while maintaining instance-level mapping fidelity and enabling robust target retrieval and absence detection in embodied navigation.

Method: Integrates VLM-based scene-category enumeration with symmetric 3D IoU association and area-weighted feature fusion to preserve small instances. VLM-inferred object functions partition the scene into compact functional search regions. A four-stage cascaded retrieval pipeline with voxel voting determines target absence and supports robust retrieval.

Result: On Replica, OVIP-SG outperforms ConceptGraphs by 6.31 points in class-mean accuracy (mAcc) and 5.15 points in frequency-weighted mIoU (F-mIoU), while achieving a class-agnostic native-instance Panoptic Quality (PQ) of 0.398. It reduces the search area to 21.8% of indoor floor space and attains 0.773 balanced accuracy for object-presence classification. Real-world robotic experiments validate practicality.

Conclusion: The framework delivers improved open-world perception with preserved instance integrity and effective language-guided retrieval, enabling more efficient and robust embodied navigation and exploration.

Abstract: Integrating open-vocabulary perception into object-level 3D scene graphs is a double-edged sword. While vision-language detectors recover long-tail categories and small, fine-grained objects overlooked by closed-set models, they also tend to fragment large surfaces and merge small objects into larger neighboring objects, compromising instance-level consistency and undermining mapping fidelity. Moreover, existing methods struggle to retrieve previously unmapped targets or determine whether a queried object is absent, hindering robust embodied open-world navigation and exploration. We present OVIP-SG, a unified framework for instance-preserving semantic mapping, functional scene partitioning, and language-guided small, fine-grained object retrieval. OVIP-SG uses a vision-language model (VLM) to enumerate scene-specific categories for robust open-world detection. Symmetric 3D Intersection over Union (IoU) association and area-weighted feature fusion preserve small independent instances, while VLM-inferred object functions partition scenes into compact functional search regions. A four-stage cascaded retrieval pipeline further incorporates voxel voting and determines target absence from exploration coverage. Under a unified evaluation protocol on Replica, OVIP-SG outperforms ConceptGraphs by 6.31 points in class-mean accuracy (mAcc) and 5.15 points in frequency-weighted mIoU (F-mIoU) while achieving a class-agnostic native-instance Panoptic Quality (PQ) of 0.398. It reduces the search area to 21.8% of the indoor floor space and reaches 0.773 balanced accuracy for object-presence classification. Real-world robotic experiments further demonstrate its practical effectiveness.

</details>


### [195] [Collective Ranking of Environmental Signals through Gaussian Belief Propagation in a Patrolling Robot Swarm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17690)
*Zachary R. Madin,Connor York,Jonathan Lawry,Edmund R. Hunt*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GBP on the patrol graph yields robust distributed ranking for multi-robot patrolling, outperforming simple and visit-count averaging in both simulation and hardware experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend patrolling to a continuous-valued ranking (generalizing best-of-n) under sensor noise and environmental monitoring/security needs, leveraging the patrol graph as a natural structure for distributed inference.

Method: Model the patrol topology as a factor graph with unary measurement factors at visited nodes and pairwise smoothness factors along edges; apply Gaussian Belief Propagation (GBP) to infer the collective ranking; compare GBP against simple averaging and visit-count-weighted averaging across varying noise levels; validate in simulation and on four Leo Rovers tracking a propagating radio signal in an office lobby.

Result: GBP achieves higher ranking accuracy, lower mean squared error, and faster time-to-consensus than baselines; degradation with increasing noise is graceful for GBP, while baselines deteriorate more substantially; hardware trials reproduce the same performance ordering as simulations.

Conclusion: GBP is an effective, robust approach for distributed collective ranking in multi-robot patrolling, illustrating the dual role of the patrol graph as both movement topology and a factor graph for spatial beliefs; results support practical deployment in security/environmental monitoring tasks.

Abstract: Multi-robot patrolling requires a team to visit all areas of an environment at regular intervals, typically minimising idleness. A practical extension, motivated by security and environmental monitoring, is to additionally form a collective ranking of all patrol locations by some measured signal, a generalisation of the best-of-n problem to the many-option, continuous-valued regime. We observe that the patrol graph admits a natural dual interpretation: it is simultaneously the topology that dictates agent movement and a factor graph over which spatial beliefs can be propagated. Exploiting this equivalence, we apply Gaussian Belief Propagation (GBP), a graph-based algorithm, to collective ranking using unary measurement factors at visited nodes and pairwise smoothness factors along patrol edges. We compare GBP against simple and visit-count-weighted averaging across a range of sensor-noise conditions in simulation, and validate the approach on four Leo Rovers tracking a propagating radio signal in an office lobby. GBP outperforms both baselines on ranking accuracy, mean squared error, and time to consensus. We find that as noise increases and the task becomes harder, GBP degrades gracefully in simulation while both averaging methods degrade substantially. Hardware trials reproduce the same performance ordering on a real propagating radio signal, supporting the practical relevance of the simulated results.

</details>


### [196] [Force-Based Offset Estimation for Keyed Peg-in-Hole Assembly Using Local Gaussian Process Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17691)
*Chandra Yuvesh Aubeeluck,Abilash Philip Madavath,Augustin Raju,Nicolas Pyschny,Felix Hackelöer,Florian Zwanzig*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A perception-validation-insertion pipeline using a local KNN-Gaussian Process hybrid regressor estimates residual misalignment from wrist force/torque to improve keyed peg-in-hole insertion, achieving 67%→87% success in chamfered, keypoint-based pick-and-place with a collaborative robot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Key-keyway assemblies demand precise contact geometry and are highly sensitive to grasp pose deviations in uncertain environments; existing approaches struggle to maintain alignment under uncertainty in tactile/visual sensing.

Method: Estimate residual radial offset from wrist force/torque using a local KNN-Gaussian Process hybrid regressor, with two regime-specific models for hard collision and guided chamfer insertion. Regime is classified via a contact-window duration threshold. Enhance KNN with a deterministic search guided by post-grasp monocular visual validation, integrated into a perception-validation-insertion pipeline.

Result: Improved regressor accuracy; insertion success rate increased from 67% to 87% in experiments using a collaborative robot arm’s force/torque sensor, demonstrating accurate radial offset estimation in chamfered peg insertion within a pick-and-place framework.

Conclusion: The approach robustifies keyed peg-in-hole assembly in uncertain environments by fusing force sensing with monocular validation; effective for chamfered insertions and keypoint-detection-based pick-and-place tasks.

Abstract: Key-keyway assembly tasks impose strict geometric constraints and are highly sensitive to grasp pose deviations in uncertain environments. This work presents a force-based offset estimation method for keyed peg-in-hole assembly, embedded within a perception-validation-insertion pipeline. Residual misalignment is estimated directly from wrist force/torque measurements using a local KNN-Gaussian Process hybrid regressor. The framework distinguishes between two contact regimes, hard collision and guided chamfer insertion, and routes inference to a dedicated model for each. Regime classification is achieved via a contact-window duration threshold. KNN combined with a deterministic search using the results of a post-grasp monocular visual validation contributes to an increased accuracy of the regressor model. This approach achieves accurate radial offset estimation in chamfered peg insertion, during a keypoint detection-based pick and place application. Experiments using the integrated force/torque sensor of a collaborative robot arm showed an increase in insertion success rate from 67% to 87% after the pipeline was applied.

</details>
