### [197] [Dijkstra as an Oracle for Online Stochastic Shortest Path Navigation with Provable Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17703)
*Mansur M. Arief,Ali Akarma,Ahmad Alfan Alfian Irfan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Dijkstra-based planning can be exact under a weaker condition than causality via nonnegative reduced costs on a determinized map; the DORA algorithm uses a shortest-path oracle with fixed calls per episode, without kernel estimation, and achieves near-optimal planning with significant planner-time savings and improved safety in dynamic environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable safe, efficient online navigation for mobile robots operating near humans and critical facilities when true traversal costs are uncertain and actuation is imperfect, overcoming the computational burden of exact stochastic shortest path methods.

Method: 1) Theoretically characterize when Dijkstra’s algorithm is exact for a stochastic map by leveraging a nonnegativity condition on a reduced cost defined on a determinized representation. 2) Propose DORA (Dijkstra Oracle Reduced-cost Algorithm), an online learner that (a) uses a shortest-path oracle a fixed number of times per episode, (b) never estimates a transition kernel, and (c) adds a logarithmic survival weight to bound dynamic-obstacle contact risk.

Result: Empirical evaluation across grid world, directional drilling, and drone-surveillance benchmarks shows DORA matches optimistic value iteration with true kernel while requiring 4.5–19.3× less planner work; it reduces contacts by ~17× versus determinize-and-replan and keeps contact rates within budgets spanning two orders of magnitude.

Conclusion: Shortest-path search can serve as a safe and efficient planning backbone for online robotics under stochastic dynamics, provided the reduced-cost nonnegativity condition holds; DORA demonstrates practical gains in planning efficiency and safety.

Abstract: Mobile robots that operate in side by side with humans and critical facilities must reach their goals at low cost, despite often unknown true traversal costs of the map apriori and imperfect actuation. Planners that solve the underlying stochastic shortest path problem exactly, such as value iteration, require computation that grows with the diameter of the map, whereas Dijkstra's algorithm is fast but is usually considered inexact once transitions are stochastic. This study shows that Dijkstra's algorithm can remain an exact planning engine under a condition that is much weaker than the causality condition often invoked in the literature, namely nonnegativity of a reduced cost defined on the determinized map. Building on this characterization, an online learner DORA (Dijkstra Oracle Reduced-cost Algorithm) is proposed for robot navigation that calls a shortest path oracle a fixed number of times per episode, never estimates a transition kernel, and adds a logarithmic survival weight when the probability of contact with a dynamic obstacle must stay within a budget. In the numerical experiments involving three other benchmarks that cover grid world navigation, directional drilling, and drone surveillance, the learner matches optimistic value iteration that is given the true transition kernel while performing 4.5 to 19.3 times less planner work, reduces contacts during learning by a factor of seventeen relative to determinize and replan, and keeps the contact rate within budgets that span two orders of magnitude. These results indicate that shortest path search supports safe and efficient online navigation and path planning tasks.

</details>


### [198] [CompCPZ: Preserving Multi-Modal Intent in Language-Guided Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17717)
*Zhen Zhang,Ahmad Hafez,Peng Xie,Yanliang Huang,Wenyuan Wu,Amr Alanwar*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CompCPZ provides a compositional algebraic layer to preserve multi-modal disjunctive representations in language-conditioned robot policies, avoiding silent semantic failures and improving policy robustness by recursively composing polynomial zonotope enclosures along the language parse tree; it achieves state-of-the-art results on ManiSkill3 and transfers to real-robot trials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language-conditioned robot policies often collapse disjunctive instructions into a single connected region, losing alternative feasible modes; this leads to runtime failures when mode uncertainty exists. A formal method is needed to preserve multi-modality in the feasibility set.

Method: Introduce CompCPZ, a sound algebraic layer that wraps language-conditioned systems and recursively composes per-primitive constrained polynomial zonotope (CPZ) enclosures along the language parse tree. It uses distribution-free conformal coverage and achieves sub-millisecond runtime, enabling tractable, multi-modal feasibility representations.

Result: On ManiSkill3 tabletop manipulation, CompCPZ outperforms convex-set baselines, multi-peak decoders, and a zero-shot vision-language-action model (1,900/1,918 paired wins; p << 10^-30). It also transfers to planar real-robot trials on a Unitree Go2 under motion capture without retuning.

Conclusion: Evaluations should measure whether the feasibility set preserves the connected-component structure of user intent, not only whether a target is reached; compositional language grounding benefits from preserving disjunctive structure in the representation.

Abstract: A robot asked to "place the cup near the red plate or the blue plate" may reach the centroid between them and appear geometrically successful, while satisfying neither disjunct of the instruction. This silent semantic failure exposes a structural limitation of language-conditioned robot policies: representations that collapse a disjunctive instruction into a single connected set cannot preserve all feasible modes, and planners that commit to one action degrade under run-time mode uncertainty. We address this limitation with CompCPZ, a sound algebraic layer that language-conditioned learning systems wrap to recover multi-modal disjunctive representation, recursively composing per-primitive constrained polynomial zonotope enclosures along the language parse tree with distribution-free conformal coverage and sub-millisecond runtime. On a closed-loop ManiSkill3 tabletop-manipulation benchmark, CompCPZ outperforms convex set baselines, multi-peak decoders, and a zero-shot vision-language-action model (1,900/1,918 paired wins, p << 10^(-30)); the same compiler also transfers without retuning to planar real-robot trials on a Unitree Go2 quadruped under motion capture. These results suggest that compositional language grounding should be evaluated not only by reaching a decoded target, but by whether the represented feasibility set preserves the connected-component structure of the user's intent.

</details>


### [199] [Stability Control for Real World Testing in Autonomous Racing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17779)
*Phillip Pitschi,Simon Sagmeister,Frederik Werner,Markus Lienkamp,Boris Lohmann*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A stability-control stack (ESC, slip control, countersteer) is proposed to safeguard motion controllers in autonomous driving at the dynamic limits, validated via simulation and real-world experiments with open-source code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous vehicles approaching handling limits can become unstable due to disturbances; standard controllers assume stability and may fail, especially in high-performance contexts like autonomous racing.

Method: Integrates ESC, slip control, and countersteer to adjust steering and braking commands from the primary motion controller; validated in simulation and on a full-scale vehicle; open-source implementation provided at github.com/TUMFTM/tam-stability-control.

Result: System maintains stability in critical situations and expands the feasible operating region; improves safety when operating near the limits.

Conclusion: A practical stability-control framework that shields motion controllers from instability at the handling limits and facilitates integration via open-source code.

Abstract: Controlling an autonomous vehicle at the limits of handling is a challenging task. Due to external influences, such as road conditions or weather, a vehicle can easily become unstable. Since most control algorithms assume stable vehicle behavior, they might fail in these situations. Especially when operating expensive vehicles without a safety driver on board, as in autonomous racing, this poses a significant challenge. To enable safe operation at the vehicle's dynamic limits, we present a comprehensive stability control system that safeguards motion control algorithms in autonomous driving. The proposed system consists of an electronic stability control (ESC), a slip control (SC), and a countersteer system (CS), which collectively adapt steering and brake commands from the motion controller to maintain vehicle stability. We validate our approach through both simulation and experiments on a real-world, full-scale vehicle. The results show that the stability control system maintains vehicle stability in critical situations and extends the operational feasible region. To simplify integration, we provide an open-source implementation at github.com/TUMFTM/tam-stability-control.

</details>


### [200] [Effector-Centric NMPC of Tiltable-Multirotors for Offset-Free Omnidirectional Aerial Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17819)
*Jinjie Li,Yicheng Chen,Johannes Kübel,Haokun Liu,Junichiro Sugihara,Moju Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Design and control framework for tiltable-multirotors enabling six-DOF, wrench-based aerial manipulation with onboard NMPC and disturbance handling; validated on a tiltable quadrotor at 100 Hz with real-world tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable omnidirectional aerial manipulation by generating wrenches directly through tiltable flight bases, overcoming limitations of fixed-axis aerial manipulators and enabling traversal of singular configurations under constraints.

Method: A design analysis showing four-rotor tiltable configuration balances interference-free propeller sizing and hover efficiency; analysis of end-effector placement (upward) for favorable clearance and wrench availability; development of a wrench-based NMPC framework (effector-centric) incorporating a modified integral term for model error and an acceleration-based estimator for external wrenches; onboard implementation at 100 Hz.

Result: Real-world experiments including 90-degree step cartwheel rotation, whiteboard pushing, and continuous 360-degree valve turning demonstrate the feasibility of wrench-based omnidirectional manipulation with singularity traversal on a one-DoF-per-arm tiltable-quadrotor.

Conclusion: An integrated onboard NMPC framework that combines design choices, singularity handling, and disturbance compensation enables practical omnidirectional aerial manipulation with tiltable quadrotors.

Abstract: Aerial manipulation extends robotic operations to previously inaccessible aerial environments. Unlike arm-equipped aerial systems, tiltable-multirotors can directly generate six-degree-of-freedom wrenches through their flight bases, enabling both efficient movement and omnidirectional operation by tilting the thrust direction.
  This work presents a design analysis and a wrench-based control framework for tiltable-multirotors in aerial manipulation. We show that a four-rotor tiltable configuration provides a balance between interference-free propeller sizing and hovering efficiency across different attitudes, and its null-space redundancy is crucial for traversing singular configurations under physical constraints. We further show that an upward end-effector placement yields a favorable trade-off between geometric clearance and available wrench. To address disturbances, we propose a dual strategy consisting of a modified integral term for model error and an acceleration-based estimator for external wrenches. Building on these insights, we develop an effector-centric nonlinear model predictive control (NMPC) framework that integrates design choices, singularity handling, and disturbance compensation into a unified formulation.
  The proposed framework runs fully onboard at 100 Hz on a custom-built tiltable-quadrotor. Real-world experiments, including a 90-deg step cartwheel rotation, whiteboard pushing, and continuous 360-deg valve turning, demonstrate the feasibility of wrench-based omnidirectional manipulation with singularity traversal on a one-DoF-per-arm tiltable-quadrotor.

</details>


### [201] [Jetson-ORB-SLAM3: Accuracy-Preserving GPU Implementation for Edge Computing Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17874)
*Rajat Roy,Aditya Arun Kumar Yadav,Hardik Jain*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Accuracy-preserving GPU front end for ORB-SLAM3 on Jetson Orin Nano; keeps CPU back end, enabling real-time edge SLAM with CNN-based loop closure and TensorRT acceleration; achieves near-parity with CPU across datasets and strong speedups (2.2 ms per query for loop closure) on a 7W device, with monocular-inertial 32 FPS on EuRoC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the computational burden of dense feature extraction and loop closure in visual-inertial SLAM on low-power edge hardware while preserving the accuracy of a CPU baseline.

Method: GPU-implemented ORB feature extraction that reproduces the CPU detector algorithm with high fidelity (94.7% exact keypoint agreement, 99.9% descriptor bit agreement); CNN-based loop closure accelerated with native TensorRT/libnvinfer FP16; mapping/optimization remains on CPU; evaluate four configurations (GPU pipeline vs CPU baseline on Jetson Orin Nano and desktop).

Result: On EuRoC, all configurations agree within 0.10 cm MAE (SE(3)); reproducible across TUM-VI and KITTI; EuRoC monocular-inertial ~32 FPS; sub-centimeter accuracy on 5/6 TUM-VI room sequences; sub-1% relative translation on 9/11 KITTI sequences; loop closure query time reduced to 2.2 ms with FP16; 7W device enables concurrent learned place recognition with tracking.

Conclusion: The GPU-accelerated front end preserves overall SLAM accuracy while delivering substantial speedups for edge devices; a practical, accuracy-preserving split (GPU front end, CPU back end) supports real-time monocular-inertial SLAM with CNN-based loop closure on low-power hardware.

Abstract: Visual-inertial SLAM on low-power edge platforms is constrained by the cost of dense feature extraction and loop closure. Prior GPU ports of ORB-SLAM trade accuracy for speed by approximating the ORB detector, altering the feature set and therefore the estimated trajectory. We present an accuracy-preserving GPU implementation of ORB-SLAM3 for the NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano, whose GPU ORB front end reproduces the reference CPU detector algorithmically to 94.7% exact keypoint agreement and 99.9% descriptor bit agreement. This work also makes CNN-based loop closure edge-viable through native TensorRT. The visual front end (feature extraction) is offloaded to the GPU while the mapping and optimization back end is kept on the CPU, matching each computation to the hardware it suits. The accuracy is verified by comparing four configurations: the GPU pipeline and the unmodified CPU reference, each run on both the Jetson Orin Nano and a desktop. On EuRoC dataset, all four agree to within 0.10cm in mean absolute trajectory error (SE(3)), so neither the GPU port nor the change of hardware shifts the estimated trajectory. The GPU-versus-CPU comparison is reproducible on TUM-VI and KITTI datasets, so the acceleration is accuracy-preserving rather than approximate. The proposed implementation is competitive with published ORB-SLAM3 on EuRoC, attains sub-centimeter accuracy on five of the six TUM-VI room sequences, and reaches sub-1% relative translation error on nine of eleven KITTI sequences. For loop closure, the generic ONNX-Runtime CUDA/TensorRT execution providers are unusable with our CosPlace ResNet-50 on the embedded platform, whereas a native libnvinfer FP16 engine reduces per-query inference to 2.2ms, a 180x speedup. Learned place recognition therefore runs concurrently with tracking on a 7W device. In monocular-inertial mode the system sustains 32FPS mean over the eleven EuRoC sequences.

</details>


### [202] [ControlledShifts: Towards Standardizing Robustness Evaluation in Trajectory Prediction Under Distribution Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17882)
*Ingrid navarro,Pablo Ortega-Kral,Yutong Duan,Jonathan Francis,Jean Oh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A framework and benchmark suite, ControlledShifts, for evaluating robustness of trajectory predictors to distribution shifts by standardized in-distribution/out-of-distribution splits, with three benchmarks and a unified robustness score; demonstrated on transformer-based models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses lack of a standardized, cross-scenario robustness evaluation in trajectory prediction where performance degrades under distribution shift; fragmentation across generalization axes.

Method: Introduces characterization and splitting functions to define shifts; builds three benchmarks for topological/behavioral shifts; defines a unified robustness score combining prediction quality (relative gain) and stability (preservation); evaluates transformer-based architectures.

Result: Benchmarks reveal critical differences among models of different capacities in handling latent relevance and environmental structure; demonstrates usefulness of ControlledShifts.

Conclusion: ControlledShifts provides a standardized framework and metrics to compare robustness across distribution shifts in trajectory prediction, enabling more robust model evaluation and design.

Abstract: Trajectory prediction is central to safety in autonomous driving, yet learning-based predictors tend to degrade sharply when encountering scenarios poorly represented by their training data. Many methods attempt to mitigate distribution shift degradation through data-centric or test-time adaptation approaches; however, they are typically validated along fragmented axes of generalization, leaving the field without a standardized way to compare robustness across shifts a model may encounter.
  To address this, we introduce ControlledShifts, a framework and benchmark suite that systematically re-splits existing trajectory datasets into in-distribution (seen) and out-of-distribution (unseen) partitions, via a shared characterization-and-splitting formulation, in which a characterization function fixes the axis of variation a benchmark probes and a splitting function fixes how the tail of that axis is withheld. The suite comprises three benchmarks targeting key topological and behavioral distribution shifts. Furthermore, to aggregate multi-dimensional performance metrics across these benchmarks, we propose a unified robustness score that evaluates models along two complementary dimensions: prediction quality (relative performance gain) and prediction stability (performance preservation under shift). We showcase ControlledShifts by benchmarking prominent transformer-based architectures, exposing critical differences in how models of varying capacities handle latent relevance and environmental structure.

</details>


### [203] [PRISM: Precision and contact-rich Real-world Industrial Skill dataset with Multimodal sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17962)
*Tengbo Yu,Jiahao Wu,Hanning Wang,Rui Chen,Chuanhou Liu,Chuang Sun,Hangxin Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PRISM introduces a large-scale, multimodal dataset for contact-rich industrial manipulation, with 5,000 teleoperated trajectories across ~25 tasks, 45 hours of data, and synchronized RGB-D, force/torque, tactile, and robot-state signals; open-source.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing datasets are biased toward short-horizon, low-contact tasks and lack multimodal sensing essential for precision industrial assembly; a benchmark is needed for multimodal perception and control under realistic manufacturing constraints.

Method: Teleoperated data collection using synchronized multi-view RGB-D, force/torque, tactile sensors, and robot-state measurements across 25 manipulation tasks (e.g., plug/unplug, conveyor-based sorting); 5k trajectories totaling ~45 hours.

Result: Dataset characteristics (size, modalities, tasks, real-world manufacturing relevance) and its openness; provides a realistic benchmark for multimodal perception and control under high-precision constraints.

Conclusion: PRISM establishes a foundation for contact-rich, generalizable manipulation research in real manufacturing settings and enables development of learning methods leveraging multimodal feedback; the dataset is open-sourced.

Abstract: Recent progress in robotic learning has been fueled by large-scale datasets collected in everyday environments. However, most existing datasets emphasize short-horizon, low-contact tasks such as pick-and-place, and therefore do not capture the precision control, force/torque or tactile regulation, and multimodal feedback required for industrial assembly. To address this gap, we introduce PRISM, a large-scale multimodal dataset for contact-rich industrial operations. The dataset spans more than 25 manipulation tasks (e.g., electronic components plug/unplug, conveyor-based sorting) and covers diverse mechanical constraints. PRISM includes more than 5,000 trajectories totaling 45 hours of teleoperated demonstrations, recorded using synchronized multi-view RGB-D, force/torque, tactile, and robot-state measurements. In contrast to datasets collected in household or laboratory settings, PRISM provides a realistic benchmark for multimodal perception and control under high-precision industrial constraints, and serves as a foundation for contact-rich, generalizable manipulation in real-world manufacturing environments. The dataset is open-sourced at: https://tengbo-yu.github.io/PRISM/

</details>


### [204] [Hydra-0: Action Flow for Generalist World Modeling and Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18077)
*Hongyu Li,Bowen Wen,Xinghao Zhu,Yixuan Wang,Yilun Du,Yunzhu Li,George Konidaris,Stan Birchfield,Soha Pouya,Chenran Li,Yan Chang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Hydra-0 is a generalist world model that uses action flow as a pixel-motion interface to unify robot control across embodiments and tasks, achieving large accuracy gains and revealing an inverse mode.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable generalist, cross-domain world modeling and control via a shared visual interface that encodes action consequences across agents, tasks, and environments.

Method: Propose Hydra-0, a world model conditioned on action flow (pixel-motion of actions). Learns action consequences across embodiments, tasks, environments, and video-generation backbones. Demonstrates zero-shot composition, data-efficient adaptation, and an emergent inverse mode where a world action model predicts compatible robot motion from desired object flow; an action head maps latent features to executable actions without task-specific demonstrations.

Result: Best configuration achieves 90.4% lower robot-motion error and 60.2% lower object-motion error than action-conditioned baseline; RoboLab Pearson r=0.96 between replayed and reference success rates; zero-shot composition; data-efficient adaptation; emergent inverse mode.

Conclusion: Action flow serves as a shared control interface to connect heterogeneous training data, enable open-loop policy evaluation, and drive robot control, highlighting the potential for generalist world modeling.

Abstract: We introduce Hydra-0, a generalist world model conditioned on action flow, which represents robot actions as pixel motion. This shared visual interface enables generalist world modeling and control by learning action consequences across embodiments, tasks, environments, and video-generation backbones. Our best configuration achieves 90.4% lower robot-motion error and 60.2% lower object-motion error than our action-conditioned baseline, while supporting zero-shot composition and data-efficient adaptation. On the RoboLab benchmark, Hydra-0 achieves a Pearson correlation of r=0.96 between replayed and reference success rates. Finally, we uncover an emergent inverse mode of this interface: a world action model that predicts compatible robot motion from desired object flow transferred from a human demonstration. A trained action head maps the resulting latent features to executable actions without requiring task-specific expert robot demonstrations. Together, these results demonstrate the potential of action flow as a shared control interface connecting heterogeneous training data, open-loop policy evaluation, and robot control.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [205] [GxP-Agent: Process-DAG Topology for Reliable Clinical Trial Programming with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16890)
*Jaime Yan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A DAG-based multi-agent system (GxP-Agent) encodes regulatory process knowledge to generate CDISC-compliant, subject-level datasets with perfect structural accuracy, outperforming LLM-only and retrieval-based baselines and generalizing to ADAE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-shot LLM code generation struggles with regulatory-grade data assembly (CDISC-compliant ADaSL/ADAE datasets); need reliable, auditable, GxP-compliant data generation that respects domain workflows.

Method: Introduce GxP-Agent, a DAG-structured multi-agent system with 15 domain-specific nodes and validation gates; worker agents use pharmaverse skill context and conditional retries. Evaluate on CDISC-Bench (CDISCPilot01: 254 subjects, 49 ADSL variables) and ADAE (9-node DAG, 55 variables, 1,191 records). Compare against baseline retrieval-augmented and single/multi-agent baselines; also test GPT-4.1 under DAG.

Result: CDISC-Bench: 100% structural match (49/49 vars, 254 records) across three runs with Claude Sonnet 4.6; best baselines ~59.2% and 0% for others. GPT-4.1 under DAG achieves 59.2% mean structural match (otherwise 0% under other architectures). ADAE: 100% structural match on first attempt.

Conclusion: Encoding domain process knowledge as graph topology enables reliable, GxP-compliant clinical trial programming, allowing weaker models to achieve strong performance; the approach shows strong generalization to related datasets (ADAE) and suggests a paradigm shift from LLM-only reasoning to graph-guided orchestration in regulated data workflows.

Abstract: Clinical trial programming -- transforming study protocols into analysis-ready datasets under CDISC standards -- is a bottleneck in regulatory submissions, yet LLM-based code generation fails catastrophically on this task: across 11 single-shot attempts with five frontier models, none produces a valid subject-level analysis dataset. We introduce GxP-Agent, a multi-agent system that encodes regulatory process ordering as a directed acyclic graph (DAG), decomposing monolithic dataset generation into 15 domain-specific nodes executed by worker agents with pharmaverse skill context, validation gates, and conditional retry. On CDISC-Bench, a new execution-based benchmark built from the FDA pilot submission CDISCPilot01 (254 subjects, 49 ground-truth ADSL variables), GxP-Agent with Claude Sonnet 4.6 achieves 100% structural match (49/49 variables, 254 correct records) across three independent runs, compared to 59.2% for the best retrieval-augmented baseline and 0% for all single-agent and flat multi-agent approaches. The DAG topology also enables weaker models: GPT-4.1 achieves 59.2% mean structural match under the same DAG, where it scores 0% under every other architecture. The approach generalizes to ADAE (adverse events; 9-node branching DAG, 55 variables, 1,191 records), achieving 100% structural match on the first attempt. These results demonstrate that encoding domain process knowledge as graph topology -- rather than relying on LLM reasoning alone -- is a key enabler for reliable, GxP-compliant clinical trial programming.

</details>


### [206] [Runtime Governance for Agentic AI: Action-Boundary Control with Trusted Provenance and Fail-Closed Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16891)
*Adam Mazzocchetti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Aegis introduces runtime governance that treats model outputs as action proposals and mediates them via a trusted decision layer before tool execution. In sandbox experiments, it prevented observed risky actions, though general autonomous-agent safety remains unproven.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address safety from harmful operational side effects by introducing an execution boundary beyond prompt-level governance.

Method: A system architecture where model outputs are action proposals evaluated by a trusted runtime against active policies, with server-side provenance, fail-closed behavior under uncertainty, and Senate-style quorum-based authorization for selected cases; evaluated on a sandbox corpus across multiple run families, tasks, and repeats.

Result: In 6,300 prompt-policy conditioned rows, 79 risky comparator-path leakage rows were observed. In 2,100 Aegis-governed rows, zero governed mock-tool applications and zero governed risky side-effects were completed. All 1,832 Aegis-attempted governed rows preserved trusted provenance. All 1,019 Senate-settled rows had quorum and final signed tallies. Overall, the results show runtime action-boundary governance prevented risky proposals from becoming governed side effects in this sandbox; they do not prove general autonomous-agent safety.

Conclusion: The work supports a narrower claim that runtime governance can bound harmful actions in the evaluated sandbox, but does not establish broad, general safety guarantees for autonomous agents.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems request tool actions that can modify files, send messages, launch jobs, or change workflow state. This shifts the safety problem from harmful text generation to harmful operational side effects. Prompt-level governance can shape model behavior, but it does not create an execution boundary. We introduce Aegis, a runtime governance system that treats model outputs as action proposals and mediates them through a trusted decision layer before tool execution. The model proposes; the trusted runtime decides. Aegis evaluates proposals against active policy state, resolves provenance server-side, fails closed under uncertainty, and routes selected cases through Senate-style settlement, a quorum- based non-unilateral authorization path. We evaluate Aegis on a repeated sandbox corpus spanning five run families, 42 tasks, three conditions, and ten repeats per family. Across 6,300 rows, prompt-policy conditioning produced 79 risky comparator-path leakage rows. Across 2,100 Aegis-governed rows, the system recorded zero governed mock-tool applications and zero governed risky side-effect completions. All 1,832 Aegis-attempted governed rows preserved trusted Aegis-resolved provenance, and all 1,019 Senate-settled rows had quorum and final signed tally evidence. These results do not prove general autonomous-agent safety. They support the narrower systems claim that, in this evaluated sandbox corpus, runtime action-boundary governance prevented observed risky proposals from becoming governed side effects.

</details>


### [207] [The Price of Thinking: Reasoning Effort as a Model-Specific API Contract](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16956)
*Yeabin Moon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Examines how an explicit 'reasoning-effort' term in API contracts affects cost and accuracy, using a paired design (high effort vs omitted) on 30 AIME 2026 items. Found higher per-call cost with high effort (+$0.01031) and no detectable accuracy gain; cost per correct answer higher under high effort. Claims are bounded by model, task, date; design preregistered with frozen pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand whether charging for explicit reasoning effort changes outcomes and costs, and whether it improves answer accuracy in API usage.

Method: Registered paired contrast: same model with explicit high-effort term vs omitted term; 30 AIME 2026 items; five calls per item; one paid attempt per item allocated to a frozen terminal category; inference resampling with repeated calls; calibration via Models-API metadata, preregistered probes; freeze of request registry, parser, terminal taxonomy, analysis pipeline prior to outcomes.

Result: Mean delivered cost higher under explicit high-effort contract by 0.01031 USD per call (CI [0.00204, 0.01974]); accuracy difference +0.0133 (CI [-0.0267, +0.0467]), not statistically significant; maximum detectable accuracy difference ~4.67 percentage points; cost per correct answer: 0.08665 USD (high effort) vs 0.07662 USD (omitted).

Conclusion: Explicitly pricing for higher reasoning effort increases cost without clear accuracy gains in this design; results are scoped to the specific model, task (AIME 2026), and date; preregistration and frozen pipeline bound claims to documentation-grade evidence.

Abstract: API buyers purchase a dated contract, not a model name alone: the contract includes the requested and served model, reasoning-effort term or its omission, output rail, service product, prompt, and price schedule. We study the reasoning-effort term through a registered paired contrast of Sonnet 5 with explicit high effort against the same model with effort omitted, using 30 AIME 2026 items and five calls per item. Every paid attempt was assigned one frozen terminal category, and inference resampled items while retaining their repeated calls. Mean delivered cost was \$0.01031 per call higher under the explicit-high contract than under the omitted contract [+\$0.00204, +\$0.01974]. The corresponding accuracy contrast was +0.0133 [-0.0267, +0.0467]; we did not detect an accuracy difference, and the interval permits a gain of up to 4.67 percentage points that this design cannot rule out. Cost per correct answer was \$0.08665 under the high-effort contract and \$0.07662 under the omitted contract, as registered point estimates. A dated contract census, Models-API metadata, and preregistered raw-response probes further documented model-specific omission semantics, including within a provider; claims remained at documentation grade when raw structure was indeterminate. The request registry, parser, terminal taxonomy, statistical plan, and analysis pipeline were frozen before outcomes were examined; the resulting claims are bounded to the model, task, and collection date studied.

</details>


### [208] [FedPref: Federated Preference Learning for Structured Radiology Report Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16971)
*Flint Xiaofeng Fan,Cheston Tan,Yew-Soon Ong,Roger Wattenhofer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FedPref is a privacy-preserving federated learning framework for extracting structured relations from radiology reports. It uses frozen public LMs to propose JSON extractions, local annotations to rank them, and trains compact Qwen3-8B adapters with only model updates, achieving gains over isolated training and close to pooled training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical radiology reports are free text requiring a fixed schema extraction; data-label distributions are highly imbalanced across institutions, making pooled centralized training impractical due to privacy and data-sharing constraints.

Method: FedPref freezes public language models to generate alternative JSON extraction candidates, uses local annotations to rank these candidates, and collaboratively trains compact Qwen3-8B adapters across sites while sharing only model updates. A heterogeneous teacher pool provides cross-model contrast during training. Evaluation uses six simulated hospitals with unequal data volumes and disease prevalence, plus a pooled central training baseline on a union of preference pairs.

Result: Compared with training each site in isolation, FedPref improves client-mean F1 by 2.49 points and worst-site F1 by 9.10 points, with the largest gains at sites with the least data. Central training on the pooled preference-pair union yields a further +2.66 points on client-mean F1. On a locked 400-report gold test set, FedPref attains 68.68 F1 versus 71.67 F1 for pooled training, preserving the same overall ordering. Importantly, FedPref enables institutions with unequal, unpooled data to benefit from collaboration without sharing reports or annotations.

Conclusion: FedPref demonstrates practical privacy-preserving collaboration for structured extraction from radiology reports. It delivers meaningful improvements for data-scarce sites and achieves competitive performance relative to pooled training, validating a scalable approach to cross-institution learning without data sharing.

Abstract: Radiology reports describe findings and locations in free text, but downstream search and analysis require these relations in a fixed schema. Learning this extraction requires labels that are unevenly distributed across institutions: smaller hospitals have less local evidence, and pooling data may be infeasible. We introduce FedPref: frozen public language models propose alternative JSON extractions, local annotations rank them, and sites collaboratively train compact Qwen3-8B adapters while sharing only model updates. A heterogeneous teacher pool provides cross-model contrast when repeated single-model samples collapse. On development data from six simulated hospitals with unequal data volume and disease prevalence, FedPref improves client-mean F1 by 2.49 points and worst-site F1 by 9.10 points compared with training each site in isolation, with the largest gains at the sites holding the least data. Central training on the pooled preference-pair union is 2.66 points higher on client-mean F1. On a locked, 400-report manually validated gold test set, FedPref reaches 68.68 F1 and pooled training 71.67, preserving that same ordering. FedPref thus lets institutions with unequal, unpooled data benefit from collaboration without ever sharing reports or annotations.

</details>


### [209] [The Problem Is the Problem: Towards Scalable Mathematical Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16977)
*Zeyu Zheng,Shengtong Zhang,Jeremy Avigad,Prasad Tetali,Sean Welleck*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes FAR (Find, Attempt, Recommend), a human-AI discovery pipeline for math that shifts focus from selecting a single pre-chosen problem to exploring a research direction; a literature-to-review cascade automates problem finding and artifact triage, improving efficiency in AI-assisted mathematical discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier-model reasoning is a scarce resource and expert mathematical review is tightly constrained. Current AI-for-math workflows concentrate human effort at problem selection and artifact review, creating bottlenecks for research-level math.

Method: A three-stage Find-Attempt-Recommend (FAR) cascade that searches a broad literature corpus for candidate problems within a user-specified direction, followed by multi-stage filtering and triage to surface artifacts for expert review. Demonstrated with a combinatorics pilot that processes thousands of papers to yield hundreds of conjectures and a subset for author-team review.

Result: The pipeline recovers thousands of candidate conjectures, filters them to thousands of well-posed, open conjectures, surfaces hundreds of potential resolutions, and selects a small set for author-team review. It identifies several interesting results related to known conjectures and questions, illustrating effective human-AI collaboration for mathematical discovery.

Conclusion: The proposed human-AI discovery paradigm enables scalable, direction-driven exploration in mathematics, reducing bottlenecks in problem identification and artifact curation, and showing promise for broader applicability beyond combinatorics.

Abstract: AI systems are increasingly capable of contributing to mathematical research. In research practice, frontier-model reasoning is a limited resource, and expert mathematical review is even more sharply constrained. Allocating these scarce resources well is therefore central to making AI-assisted mathematical discovery efficient. In most current AI-for-math workflows, human effort is concentrated at the beginning and end, in selecting suitable research problems and later reviewing the resulting artifacts. These two stages are becoming bottlenecks for research-level mathematics. We address them by proposing a new human-AI discovery paradigm. The human input is no longer a single problem selected in advance, but a research direction in which the experts have interest and expertise. The system then searches a broad literature corpus for candidate problems in that direction. Inspired by search and recommender systems, we build Find, Attempt, and Recommend (FAR), a literature-to-review cascade that automates the search for suitable problems and focuses human attention on artifacts that have passed several stages of filtering. In a combinatorics pilot, the pipeline starts from 5,245 combinatorics papers, recovers 6,453 candidate conjectures or open problems, and filters them to 4,717 apparently well-posed and still-open conjectures. Subsequent reasoning and automated triage stages surface 598 potential resolutions and select 77 items for author-team review. Among them, we identify many interesting discoveries, including results on conjectures and questions of Davies--Jenssen--Perkins--Roberts, Erdős--Straus, Ikenmeyer--Pak--Panova, and Lund--Saraf--Wolf. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of this new mode of human-AI collaboration for mathematical discovery.

</details>


### [210] [SkillEffect: Checked Lowering for Memory-Bounded Agent Tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17007)
*Yinuo Wang,Yiyu Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillEffect provides a checked-lowering runtime with audited relations and a verifier that enforces memory-bounded tool executions across multiple patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address memory overflows and resource misallocation when language models translate tool-use guidance into code, by introducing verifiable lowerings with registered memory constraints.

Method: Introduce SkillEffect architecture: recoverable source relation, audited bounded-IR, postconditions; independent checker reconstructs lowering from program and immutable input; relation plugins supply recognizers, input facts, bounded-IR constructors, arena bounds, and postconditions; shared runtime handles selection, bounded-VM, capacity leasing, and staged publishing; supports six operator families and five execution patterns via plugins.

Result: Demonstrated that bounded access reduces peak memory and improves completion under fixed caps; same contract across six plugins and five execution patterns; XLSX onboarding and Top-k extensions show reuse of trust boundary; checker accepts evaluated configurations and rejects adversarial ones.

Conclusion: A single checked-lowering architecture can enforce heterogeneous registered memory relations at Agent tool dispatch.

Abstract: Agent Skills can specify procedural and resource obligations for tool use, and language models instantiate them as concrete programs. However, when models turn this guidance into code for existing tool interfaces, even a semantically correct program may load an entire input and exceed the memory available to one tool call. We present SkillEffect, a checked-lowering runtime for computations with a recoverable source relation, an audited bounded implementation, and a registered output postcondition. Before granting execution authority, an independent checker rebuilds each proposed lowering from the submitted program and immutable input. Every relation plugin supplies a source recognizer, input-fact extractor, bounded-IR constructor, arena-bound function, and postcondition; one common runtime provides checked selection, bounded-VM execution, atomic capacity leasing, and staged publication. Generality in SkillEffect is architectural rather than automatic: each supported computation requires an audited relation plugin, while the dispatch, resource-control, execution, and publication mechanisms are shared across plugins. Across six operator families, bounded access substantially reduces peak memory and improves completion under externally fixed caps. Six plugins instantiate the same contract across five execution patterns, from streaming reduction to bounded-heap Top-k. The XLSX onboarding study and Top-k extension show that a new relation and a new retained-state pattern reuse the same trust boundary, while the checker accepts all evaluated legal configurations and rejects all adversarial proposals. Together, these results show that one checked-lowering architecture can enforce heterogeneous registered memory relations at Agent tool dispatch.

</details>


### [211] [Memory Is Communication: The Frontier Between Remembering and Signaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17053)
*Yashar Talebirad,Eden Redman,Ali Parsaee,Osmar R. Zaiane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Defines a trade-off frontier between memory (history) and peer signaling (communication) for bounded agents; introduces the remembering–signaling frontier; proposes hypothesis that more history-based loss reduction reduces needed peer communication; provides preliminary referential-game findings and a plan to map the frontier across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how resource-limited decision agents should allocate information between internal memory and external communication under constraints, and to formalize the trade-offs for task performance.

Method: Formulate an achievable region of memory-rate and message-rate pairs that achieve a target task loss under fixed decision rules; define the remembering–signaling frontier as the efficient boundary of this region. Hypothesize a monotone relation: for the same memory-induced loss reduction, more history means less required peer communication. Test via preliminary referential games by manipulating target repetition and cyclic structure, measuring message lengths and success; outline experiments to map the frontier by varying memory and message rates across cooperative tasks.

Result: Preliminary observations show target repetition is associated with shorter successful messages, whereas a hidden cyclic rule’s predictability did not shorten messages. The frontier framework provides a method to quantify trade-offs and test the hypothesis; results motivate further experiments to map the frontier across tasks.

Conclusion: The remembering–signaling frontier offers a principled boundary for allocating bounded agents’ information resources between memory and communication. If history yields larger loss reductions, peer signaling can be reduced correspondingly. The framework enables empirical frontier estimation across cooperative tasks and can guide design of memory/communication policies; future work should validate the hypothesis across varied tasks and refine frontier estimation.

Abstract: A bounded agent may obtain information for a decision from its own past, from peers, or from both sources. Retaining task-relevant history can reduce later communication, while a peer message can supply what memory lacks. Under limits on both resources, how should an agent allocate its information budget? Given a fixed task and decision rule, the memory and message rate pairs attaining a performance threshold form an achievable region under specified rules for using history and peer observations. We call its efficient boundary the remembering--signaling frontier. Across conditions where history permits the same maximum reduction in task loss, we hypothesize that a bounded agent will need less peer communication when it obtains a larger loss reduction from history. In preliminary referential games, target repetition coincided with shorter successful messages, while predictability from a hidden cyclic rule did not shorten them. Experiments varying memory and message rates can estimate the frontier and test this prediction across cooperative tasks.

</details>


### [212] [DiSCO: Defending text-to-image generation through distribution-guided contrastive prompt optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17067)
*Tong Zhang,Motasem Alfarra,Carlos Hinojosa,Christos Louizos,Bernard Ghanem*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Zero-shot black-box prompt-level defense (DiSCO) for text-to-image models that expands prompts with distribution-guided suffixes via beam search and contrastive scoring over safe/unsafe outputs. It reduces NSFW attack success on the I2P benchmark by 37.7% (undefended) and 25.13% (defended) without retraining, while preserving semantic fidelity and coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-to-image systems increasingly generate NSFW content, with red-teaming attacks exploiting learned data distributions. White-box defenses (weight editing, text encoder changes) are invasive and often not scalable to proprietary models. Black-box prompt-based defenses can be applied widely but struggle with benign adversarial prompts that are linguistically safe yet harmful due to data distributions.

Method: A plug-and-play, black-box module that requires no retraining or access to internals. It performs distribution-guided suffix expansion of the user prompt via beam search. A scoring function uses contrastive feedback over safe and unsafe image pools generated by the target model to guide generation. The process iterates, with adaptive feedback, until the produced image is deemed safe.

Result: Empirical evaluation on the I2P benchmark across multiple red-teaming attacks shows notable safety gains: 37.7% ASR reduction on undefended models and 25.13% ASR reduction on defended models. The method maintains semantic fidelity and improves image coherence compared to baselines.

Conclusion: DiSCO is a zero-shot, architecture-agnostic, plug-and-play defense that can be applied to any text-to-image system without modifying the model. It effectively mitigates both undefended and defended models against adversarial prompts while preserving output quality.

Abstract: As text-to-image generative models advance, they raise critical safety concerns, particularly the generation of Not-Safe-For-Work (NSFW) content such as violence and nudity, further exacerbated by red-teaming adversarial attacks. Existing defenses predominantly operate under white-box assumptions, relying on text encoder optimization, weight editing, or inference-time intervention, and fundamentally cannot scale to proprietary models. Black-box alternatives based on LLM prompt rewriting offer broader applicability, yet fail in a critical regime we identify as the \textit{benign adversarial} problem: prompts that are linguistically safe but still trigger harmful generation due to the model's learned data distribution. We propose DiSCO, a zero-shot, strictly black-box defense that operates entirely at the prompt level as a plug-and-play module, requiring no model retraining, fine-tuning, or access to model internals. DiSCO performs distribution-guided suffix expansion via beam search, optimized through contrastive scoring over safe and unsafe image pools generated by the target model itself, with iterative adaptive feedback until safe content is produced. We demonstrate that DiSCO consistently enhances the safety of both undefended and defended models on the I2P benchmark under multiple red-teaming attacks, achieving 37.7% and 25.13% ASR reduction, respectively, while maintaining semantic fidelity and improving image coherence. As a black-box, architecture-agnostic module, DiSCO can be readily applied to any text-to-image system without necessitating any changes to the model itself.

</details>


### [213] [KernelArc: A Multi-Agent Framework for GPU Kernel Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17071)
*Joyjit Kundu,Ben Stoffelen,Kaili Wang,Peter Vrancx,Ludovic Denoyer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KernelArc is a multi-agent framework that optimizes GPU kernels across heterogeneous workloads via parallel, strategy-specialized agents coordinating through conclusions-only shared memory and read-only cross-agent state; it achieves leading performance on SOL-ExecBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To broaden exploration and improve kernel optimization under a fixed candidate budget by leveraging multi-agent search, while acknowledging that the benefit of coordination depends on the kernel and optimization stage.

Method: Strategy-specialized agents run in parallel; coordination implemented via conclusions-only shared memory, deterministic benchmark guard, read-only cross-agent state, and plateau-triggered drafting.

Result: On NVIDIA H100 and B200 GPUs using SOL-ExecBench workloads, KernelArc produced implementations spanning BF16 GEMM, cuBLASLt Expert-API configuration tables, fused mixture-of-experts backward, shape-gated decoder-layer fusion, native NVFP4 grouped-query attention, and paged prefill attention; leaderboard snapshot as of 2026-07-30 shows first place on representative L1, L2, Quantization, and FlashInfer tasks.

Conclusion: Shared multi-agent search broadens exploration and can achieve strong incumbents within a fixed budget; the utility of coordination features is kernel- and stage-dependent.

Abstract: We present KernelArc, a multi-agent framework for autonomous GPU kernel optimization across heterogeneous workloads. Strategy-specialized agents run in parallel and coordinate through conclusions-only shared memory, a deterministic benchmark guard, and read-only cross-agent state with plateau-triggered drafting. We evaluate \kernelarc{} on NVIDIA H100 and B200 GPUs using category-representative SOL-ExecBench workloads. The resulting implementations span custom BF16 GEMM, static cuBLASLt Expert-API configuration tables, fused mixture-of-experts backward, shape-gated decoder-layer fusion, native NVFP4 grouped-query attention, and paged prefill attention. At the public SOL-ExecBench leaderboard snapshot recorded on July~30, 2026, these submissions ranked first on representative L1, L2, Quantization, and FlashInfer tasks. The trajectories support the paper's central motivation: shared multi-agent search can broaden exploration and reach stronger incumbents within a fixed candidate budget, while the value of individual coordination features depends on the kernel and optimization stage.

</details>


### [214] [A decodability criterion predicts when hidden-state selection beats majority voting in large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17124)
*Zhixiang wang,Ziliang Hong,Ulas Bagci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CASE is a dynamic selection method that uses a linear gate on answer-token hidden states to pick the best candidate; introduces decodability to measure when selection beats voting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Voting ensembles with sampled LLM answers fail on difficult questions due to correlated errors; a leakage-free, model-hidden-state signal is needed to reliably select correct answers, but existing probes leak question information and don’t always indicate trustworthiness.

Method: Train a linear gate on the answer-token hidden state to select the highest-scoring candidate. Introduce decodability, a leakage-free measure of how well the gate ranks correct candidates over incorrect ones, to predict when hidden-state selection outperforms voting. Compare to conventional probes (which suffer leakage) via question-grouped evaluation. Evaluate on held-out data across general and medical LLMs.

Result: CASE improves voting by up to 19 points on medium-difficulty questions and 16.8 points on hard questions. Decodability correlates with accuracy gains (Pearson r=0.75) and has a decision threshold near AUC=0.60. Decodability transfer to unseen scientific domain within 3.8 points. Gains are not tied to model scale but to the aligned knowledge the model must recall.

Conclusion: Decodability provides a practical, leakage-free criterion for deciding when learned hidden-state selection should be used over majority voting. It enables prior estimation of selection viability for a given model and task and transfers across general and medical LLMs and across domains.

Abstract: Combining the answers a large language model (LLM) samples for a question into one decision is a test-time information fusion problem, usually solved by majority voting. Voting is unreliable on difficult questions, where the sampled answers share correlated errors, so the wrong answer can win and drawing more samples makes the decision worse. Selecting a candidate by reading a correctness signal from the model's hidden states is a promising alternative, but its accuracy varies across models and tasks, and no measure indicates when it can be trusted. In this paper, we propose CASE (Correctness-Axis SElection), a dynamic selection combiner that trains a linear gate on the answer-token hidden state and selects the highest-scoring candidate. Its main contribution is decodability, a leakage-free measure of how well the gate ranks a question's correct candidates above its incorrect ones, which predicts whether hidden-state selection will outperform voting. A conventional probe appears accurate only because of question-identity leakage, which vanishes under question-grouped evaluation. On held-out data, decodability predicts the accuracy gain of selection over voting with a Pearson correlation r=0.75 and a decision threshold near AUC=0.60. Across general and medical LLMs, CASE improves over voting by up to 19 points on medium-difficulty questions and 16.8 points on hard questions. Decodability depends on the aligned knowledge a model must recall, not on its scale, and its prediction transfers to an unseen scientific domain within 3.8 points. It thus provides a practical criterion, measurable in advance for a given model and task, for choosing between learned selection and majority voting.

</details>


### [215] [Toward Personal Intelligence Through Cooperative Observation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17128)
*Yashar Talebirad,Osman Jime,Ali Parsaee,Eden Redman,Yongbin Kim,Osmar R. Zaiane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes cooperative observation as a framework for personal intelligence, linking usefulness, trust, and future access; reports a six‑month single‑subject Organizm prototype and outlines evaluation directions for observation‑quality effects on personal AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personal AI models are inherently bounded by what they can observe; simply increasing observation volume does not guarantee better assistance. A cooperative, user‑driven observation loop aligns data collection with usefulness and consent to improve model quality and inspectability.

Method: Introduces the cooperative observation framework and applies it to a five‑to six‑month deployment narrative using Organizm; presents a preliminary, single‑subject account and outlines directions for evaluating how observation quality shapes personal AI performance.

Result: A preliminary single‑subject account showing how usefulness and trust influence user consent to expand observation channels; demonstrates a feedback loop where inspectionability and failures drive modification, narrowing, revocation, or abandonment of observation.

Conclusion: The cooperative observation framework offers a principled path for designing and evaluating personal AI; future work should formalize metrics and pursue multi‑subject validation of how observation quality affects assistance.

Abstract: A personal AI system needs a model of the user's goals, constraints, and ongoing commitments to plan and act on their behalf, and the quality of that model is bounded by what the system can observe. Broader observation does not by itself improve assistance because a bounded system must select and compress information for the task at hand. We argue that this observation bottleneck has a cooperative structure: the system builds a partial model of the user's changing life, the user evaluates its actions, and the user's consent and control shape what it can observe next. Useful and inspectable behavior can give users a reason to maintain or expand the observation channel, while failures can lead them to correct, narrow, revoke, or abandon it. We use the term cooperative observation for this feedback loop among usefulness, trust, and future access, and propose it as a framework for personal intelligence. We report a preliminary single-subject account from Organizm, a prototype used over six months, and outline evaluation directions for measuring how observation quality shapes personal AI.

</details>


### [216] [KnowSim: Evaluating Information Calibration in LLM Assistants with User Simulators that Learn](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17150)
*Yoonjoo Lee,Hyoungwook Jin,Tae Soo Kim,Shaoyang Zhang,Philippe Laban,Q. Vera Liao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR:  KNOWSIM is an evaluation framework that uses a knowledge-graph based user simulator to model evolving user knowledge and assess LLM information calibration with three metrics, validated against human data and showing aptitude-treatment interactions across models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing user simulators for evaluating LLMs lack explicit modeling of user knowledge and its evolution, leading to unrealistic and non-adaptive interactions in knowledge-intensive tasks. A knowledge-state, learning-theory grounded simulator could better reflect real user trajectories and calibrate LLMs accordingly.

Method: Represent user knowledge as a graph of Information Units with prerequisite relations. Evolve knowledge states via update rules grounded in learning theory. Compute Knowledge Gain, Delivery Calibration, and Cognitive Overload from the trajectory. Validate against 705 human-AI sessions across two domains, stratified by knowledge level. Compare 9 LLMs to reveal aptitudetreatment interactions.

Result: KNOWSIM's model rankings significantly agree with human judgments (73-74% sign agreement) and outperform three baseline simulators. The best-performing model shifts with user knowledge level, uncovering aptitude-treatment interactions not evident from standard evaluations.

Conclusion: KNOWSIM provides a robust, interpretable framework for evaluating LLMs on knowledge-intensive tasks by modeling evolving user knowledge, enabling more realistic interactions and revealing when model choice should depend on user knowledge level.

Abstract: To effectively collaborate with users on knowledge-intensive tasks, Large Language Models (LLMs) must perform information calibration: matching content to a user's evolving understanding and cognitive capacity. Yet user simulators used to evaluate and train LLMs do not explicitly model user knowledge so they neither produce realistic interactions across knowledge levels nor reflect how interactions unfold as that knowledge evolves. To close this gap, we introduce KNOWSIM, an evaluation framework built around a user simulator that maintains explicit knowledge states, represented as a graph of Information Units with prerequisite relationships, that evolve under update rules grounded in learning theory. KNOWSIM computes three metrics (Knowledge Gain, Delivery Calibration, Cognitive Overload) directly from the knowledge state trajectory, reflecting key mechanistic aspects of information calibration. We validate KNOWSIM against 705 human-AI sessions across two domains, stratified by knowledge level: its rankings align significantly with human judgments (73-74% sign agreement), outperforming three baseline simulators. Applied to 9 LLMs, KNOWSIM reveals that the best model shifts by user knowledge level, revealing aptitude-treatment interactions invisible to standard evaluation.

</details>


### [217] [Synthesizing Feature Extractors: An Agentic Approach for Algorithm Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17170)
*Hai Xia,Carlos Ansótegui,Stefan Szeider*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Automated synthesis of interpretable feature extractors for constraint satisfaction problems using LLMs in an agentic loop to guide executable Python scripts for graph-based structural features, improving algorithm selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual feature engineering for CSPs is labor-intensive and not scalable to new problem classes; automated, interpretable feature extractors are needed to enable effective portfolio selection.

Method: LLMs operate in a check-fix-verify loop to generate and validate Python scripts that parse a MiniZinc model/instance into a typed graph and compute features (density, variable clustering, constraint tightness); evaluate on three CSP problems with five solvers; compare against mzn2feat and trans2feat; ensure interpretability.

Result: Synthesized extractors yield algorithm selectors that consistently surpass expert-curated mzn2feat features and best trans2feat variants; on FLECC, up to 8.3 percentage point improvement in test-set accuracy; extractors remain inspectable.

Conclusion: Automated, interpretable feature extraction via LLM-driven synthesis can achieve competitive or superior CSP algorithm selection, offering scalable, transparent alternatives to manual features.

Abstract: Algorithm selection for constraint satisfaction problems requires extracting features that capture problem structure. Manually designing feature extractors demands deep domain expertise and quickly becomes a bottleneck when new problem classes appear. We present an automated approach that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) in an agentic check--fix--verify loop to synthesize executable Python scripts that act as interpretable, problem-specific feature extractors. Given a high-level MiniZinc model and an instance, the LLM agent generates code that constructs a typed graph representation and computes structural properties such as graph density, variable clustering, and constraint tightness. We evaluate our approach on three combinatorial problems (vehicle routing, car sequencing, fixed-length error-correcting codes) with a portfolio of five state-of-the-art solvers. The synthesized extractors yield algorithm selectors that consistently outperform both expert-curated mzn2feat features (up to $8.3$ percentage points (pp) test-set accuracy on FLECC) and the best transformer-based trans2feat variants. In the meanwhile, the synthesized feature extractors remain inspectable.

</details>


### [218] [Benchmarking the Benchmarks: Evaluating Automated Safety Benchmarks for Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17183)
*Nyamtulla Shaik,Fengjun Li,Bo Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-centric safety benchmarks do not reliably evaluate Small Language Models; ambiguity due to prompt complexity and architecture confounds safety assessment, making aggregate rankings brittle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether safety/robustness benchmarks developed for LLMs transfer to SLMs in resource- and privacy-sensitive settings, and whether they provide reliable evidence of SLM safety.

Method: Large-scale evaluation of five benchmark suites across 26 open-source SLMs using a unified judging rubric (0 for harmful, 1 for safe, 0.5 for ambiguous/irrelevant); analyze correlations with prompt complexity, lexical density, perplexity, length, lexical sophistication, self-coherence, and reply-prompt similarity.

Result: Ambiguity dominates judgments and correlates with prompt complexity and model architecture; higher lexical density and longer outputs increase ambiguity, while lexical sophistication, self-coherence, and reply-prompt similarity reduce it. This creates a capability-safety confound. Mean-score leaderboards are brittle: model rankings change under reasonable ambiguity treatments even if outputs are unchanged.

Conclusion: LLM-centric benchmarks are insufficient for assessing SLM safety; need SLM-tailored evaluation methods that explicitly handle ambiguity and avoid overreliance on aggregate mean scores; avoid confounding model capability with safety.

Abstract: Small Language Models (SLMs) are increasingly deployed in resource-constrained, privacy-sensitive settings, where safety and bias failures can cause security and societal risks. However, existing AI safety\slash security\slash compliance benchmarks are designed for large language models that may not transfer reliably to SLMs. We therefore ask: Can these benchmarks effectively and reliably evaluate SLMs? To answer this question, we conduct a large-scale assessment of the effectiveness and robustness of these automated pipelines by evaluating five widely used benchmark suites across 26 open-source SLMs under a unified judging rubric, which assigns a score of 0, 1, or 0.5 to harmful, safe, or ambiguous/irrelevant responses, respectively. Across the benchmarks, ambiguous judgments dominate and correlate with prompt complexity and model architecture, indicating that {\em LLM-centric safety benchmarks are insufficient as standalone evidence for SLM safety assessment}. In general, the ambiguity rate increases with lexical density, output perplexity, and output length and decreases with lexical sophistication, self-coherence, and reply-prompt similarity. This reveals a capability-safety confound that mixes model capability with apparent safety. Since ambiguity is prevalent, aggregate mean-score leaderboards are mathematically brittle: model rankings change significantly under reasonable ambiguity treatments, even when the underlying outputs remain unchanged.

</details>


### [219] [Fool's Gold: Defensive Deception Against Safety-Removal Attacks on Open-Weight Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17202)
*Mark Russinovich*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Decoy hardening (Fool's Gold) trains decoys inside a differentiable attack simulation to preserve safe behavior even when a model's refusal is stripped. Evaluated on seven models (9B–122B, dense and MoE). On six models that pass a pre-registered efficacy gate, 0.51–0.90 of attacked-state responses are decoys, yielding +0.27–0.84 improvement; six remain within benign budgets, while a seventh fails the gate. Results replicate on frozen/test splits. Epistemic claim: without independent ground truth, external benchmarks cannot separate falsified from correct answers; on a CBRNE-adjacent slice, defended 122B is fatally wrong on 0.82–0.86 of matched-quality answers vs at most 0.10 undefended. Repeated sampling with element-wise consensus (K=64) reconstructs a usable procedure on 0.083–0.625 of prompts where validated, vs 0.58–0.96 undefended. The defense does not address in-context jailbreaks and protects only the initially released defended weights."


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-weight language models can trivially remove refusals through ablation; there is a need for defenses that preserve safe, benign behavior even if refusal mechanisms are manipulated or stripped.

Method: Train decoys inside a differentiable simulation of the attack, using a refusal pin and benign leash to keep clean-state behavior aligned with the defended weights; evaluate on seven models across multiple families, with pre-registered efficacy gates and repeated sampling; test across held-out prompts and analyze both benign behavior and hazard-related tasks; compare attacked-state decoy rate and consensus-based recoveries; assess generalization to CBRNE-adjacent tasks and in-context jailbreaks.

Result: Six models pass the efficacy gate; decoy fraction in attacked-state outputs ranges 0.51–0.90, with defense-attributable improvements of 0.27–0.84; all six stay within benign budgets; the seventh model fails the gate. Rates replicate on frozen/test splits. On external red-team benchmarks' CBRNE-adjacent slice, defended 122B is fatally wrong on 0.82–0.86 of matched-quality answers vs at most 0.10 undefended. Element-wise consensus with K=64 reconstructs a usable procedure on 0.083–0.625 of prompts where the instrument validates, vs 0.58–0.96 undefended; the weakest model yields per-draw conclusions. The defense does not address in-context jailbreaks and only protects the initially released defended weights.

Conclusion: Fool's Gold offers a partial mitigation against refusal-stripping attacks by embedding decoys, but its efficacy is epistemically constrained (dependent on ground truth; potential for fatal degradation on hazard tasks); it does not resolve in-context jailbreaks and relies on the specific defended weights, highlighting the need for independent grounding, broader evaluation, and robust, dynamic defenses.

Abstract: Safety alignment in open-weight language models is trivially removable: abliteration projects a refusal-mediating direction out of the weights in minutes, and no release-time defense we are aware of prevents it durably. What cannot be prevented can be deceived. Our defense, decoy hardening ("Fool's Gold"), concedes the refusal strip and poisons its payoff: once refusal is stripped, most answers to hazardous operational requests are confident, fluent decoys whose critical elements are falsified. Decoys are trained inside a differentiable simulation of the attack, expressing only in the attacked state; a refusal pin and benign leash hold clean-state behavior to the original. We instantiate it on seven models from five families (9B-122B, dense and mixture-of-experts). On the six models passing our pre-registered efficacy gate, 0.51-0.90 of attacked-state responses to held-out prompts are decoys, +0.27-0.84 attributable to the defense; all six stay within registered benign-behavior and capability budgets; the seventh (smaller) fails the gate (boundary case). Rates replicate on a frozen test split or untouched strata. The claim is epistemic: without independent ground truth, no observation surface we tested separates falsified answers from correct ones - on external red-team benchmarks' CBRNE-adjacent slice, the defended 122B is fatally wrong on 0.82-0.86 of matched-quality answers vs at most 0.10 undefended. Repeated sampling does not restore trust: element-wise consensus at K=64 reconstructs a fully usable procedure on 0.083-0.625 of prompts where the instrument validates, vs 0.58-0.96 undefended, with no label-free way to tell the regimes apart; on the weakest such model the claim is per-draw only. We evaluate chemical and biological hazards; the defense does not address in-context jailbreaks and protects only the initially released defended weights.

</details>


### [220] [Explicit State Elicitation Is Not Enough: A Controlled Audit of Memory-Policy Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17247)
*Yihang Chen,Pin Qian,Su Wang,Chong Peng,Huan Xu,Shuaiting Li,Yiqi Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Audits state-structured prompts and memory routing for personalized agents using controlled counterfactuals; finds limited gains from explicit state-output fields, and that surface accuracy can overstate counterfactual consistency; policy classification alone may not reveal internal mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Systematically evaluate how retrieved memory and intermediate state definitions influence decision-making in personalized agents, and separate genuine internal reasoning from superficial cues or wiring.

Method: Constructed a multi-stage auditing protocol: 480-example development set to suggest gains from a state-structured prompt bundle; TF-IDF analysis to detect lexical separability and lack of standalone Ignore cases; a frozen 160-example counterfactual set with 40 four-way families and reference policies; evaluated four state definitions, isolated state-output field, and benchmark-associated state labels; performed family-level and seed-stability analyses; attempted decomposed semantic evidence elicitation; evaluated policy classification (not downstream actions).

Result: Four state definitions improved policy accuracy; an isolated explicit state-output field did not significantly improve accuracy for Llama-3.3-70B and gave only marginal gain for GPT-OSS-120B; benchmark labels shifted predictions but mapped deterministically to policies (diagnostic, not evidence of faithfulness); complete four-way family success is rare; decomposed semantic evidence did not improve routing; provider-side request validation hindered some experiments.

Conclusion: State-based prompting and memory-routing yield limited and model-dependent benefits when assessed at the policy level; naive metric improvements can misrepresent internal mechanisms; robust auditing requires evaluation beyond surface policy accuracy and attention to counterfactual consistency and implementation-specific constraints.

Abstract: Personalized agents must decide whether retrieved user memory should be used, ignored, updated, or queried before it affects a current task. We use this setting to develop an empirical audit protocol for structured intermediate outputs: first audit dataset shortcuts, then isolate bundled prompt changes, check whether intermediate labels are answer-associated, test decomposed semantic evidence, and audit provider-level execution failures. A 480-example synthetic development set initially suggested large gains from a state-structured prompt bundle, but TF-IDF diagnostics showed lexical separability and no positive standalone Ignore cases. We therefore construct a frozen 160-example controlled counterfactual set with 40 matched four-way families and rule-derived reference policies. On this set, exposing the four state definitions improves accuracy, but an isolated explicit state-output field does not significantly improve policy accuracy for Llama-3.3-70B and gives only a marginal, non-significant gain for GPT-OSS-120B. Supplying benchmark-associated state labels shifts policy predictions, but because those labels deterministically map to policies, this is a label-conditioning diagnostic rather than evidence of a faithful internal mechanism. Family-level and seed-stability analyses further show that example-level accuracy overstates counterfactual consistency: complete four-way family success is rare. An exploratory follow-up that elicits decomposed semantic evidence also fails to improve routing for the cleanly evaluated endpoint; the corresponding GPT-OSS condition was unavailable because of provider-side request validation. We evaluate policy classification only, not downstream responses, tool actions, or memory-store mutation.

</details>


### [221] [Do LLMs Know a Good Hypothesis When They See One? Logit-Based Energy Scoring Outperforms Prompted LLM-as-Judge for Scientific Hypothesis Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17270)
*Swati Rajwal,Sanjay Das,Tirthankar Ghosal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Logit-based energy scoring uses LLM intrinsic confidence to rank hypotheses; benchmarked 7 LLMs on 1323 papers across 12 disciplines with 15 incorrect alternatives; intrinsic scoring outperforms prompted listwise ranking, strongest 1B-parameter model achieving 53.1% Hit@1 (post hoc max); overall potential for confidence-based evaluation in scientific discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
