Motivation: Evaluating hypotheses generated by LLMs is challenging; existing methods rely on judges or semantic similarity, which can bias toward familiar ideas. A confidence-based, intrinsic evaluation could improve trustworthiness in AI-enabled scientific workflows.

Method: Compute logit-based energy scores from LLMs to assess hypotheses against 15 incorrect alternatives. Benchmark seven models on 1,323 papers across 12 disciplines. Compare intrinsic scoring to prompted listwise ranking. Report Hit@1 pooled across scorers and identify best configuration (noting post hoc selection).

Result: Intrinsic scoring yields 33.0% Hit@1 pooled across scorers, versus 16.6% for prompted listwise ranking. The strongest configuration—1-billion-parameter model with logit-based energy scoring—reaches 53.1% (maximum across 14 model-scorer combinations chosen post hoc). Overall, intrinsic model confidence shows potential for evaluating scientific hypotheses.

Conclusion: Intrinsic confidence-based evaluation is promising for trustworthy AI-enabled scientific discovery and motivates further research into confidence-based methods for hypothesis evaluation.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for scientific hypothesis generation. However, evaluating generated hypotheses remains a challenge for trustworthy AI-enabled scientific workflows. Existing approaches often use LLMs as judges or rely on semantic similarity, which can favor familiar ideas over novel ones. We propose a logit-based energy scoring method that evaluates hypotheses using a language model's intrinsic confidence rather than comparative judgment. We benchmarked seven language models on 1,323 papers across 12 disciplines. Each paper was paired with its hypothesis and fifteen incorrect alternatives. Intrinsic scoring reached 33.0% Hit@1 pooled across both scorers, compared with 16.6% for prompted listwise ranking. The strongest configuration, a 1-billion-parameter model using logit-based energy scoring, reached 53.1%, though this was the maximum across 14 model-by-scorer combinations selected post hoc. Overall, intrinsic model confidence shows potential for scientific hypothesis evaluation. This study also motivates future research on confidence-based methods for trustworthy AI-enabled scientific discovery.

</details>


### [222] [ASI-Bench: At the Dawn of Artificial Superintelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17271)
*Junwei Zhou,Zhen Sun,Binyu Li,Jiangyu Zhou,Yuexi Pan,Hengyu Wang,Honghe Ren,Xiaohan Jia,Xueyang Zhou,Xiaoyu Cao,Yongchao Chen,Yuanning Feng,Junhao Wu,Cheng Zhang,Sijia Chen,Haoyu Xue,Chengsong You,Huan Wang,Koutian Wu,Peigan Gao,Jiakun Wu,Wenzhe Li,Ergan Shang,Qingyuan Zheng,Jingjing Zhou,Ruixuan Jia,Yan Xu,Hongrui Zhang,Xiao-Han Ma,Zhengxiang Cheng,Yuexing Hao,Liting Mai,Xianglin Ji,Wenjun Zhang,Zhuofan Chen,Yixiao Huang,Chi Wang,Wenyue Hua,Yilun Hao,Yuantao Zhai,Ziyan Zhao,Jingyan Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASI-Bench is a new benchmark to evaluate AI's ability to conduct autonomous, end-to-end scientific research; results show current systems still rely heavily on human guidance, with significant performance decline when guidance is reduced.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move AI from exploiting existing knowledge to autonomous discovery and verification across domains; fill the gap in existing benchmarks that test only learned knowledge and guided tasks.

Method: 60 project-level research tasks across 11 domains; progressive withdrawal of methodological guidance; 18 agent-model configurations; tasks subjected to expert review, AI-assisted auditing, sandbox execution, and scorer validation.

Result: Average scores drop from 50.91 with full guidance to 29.10 with only the method specified and 26.62 when agents must determine the method themselves, indicating reliance on human guidance and limited autonomous capability.

Conclusion: ASI-Bench is open to community contributions to extend tasks and push toward autonomous scientific research; emphasizes current systems are far from fully autonomous project-level research.

Abstract: Artificial superintelligence (ASI) requires AI to move beyond mastering existing knowledge toward exploring the unknown, creating new knowledge, and turning new ideas into verifiable results. However, the capabilities of today's AI systems are still largely built on learning, compressing, and applying existing human knowledge. Accordingly, existing benchmarks primarily test whether AI can produce correct answers based on learned knowledge, or whether it can complete tasks under extensive human guidance. We therefore introduce ASI-Bench, the first benchmark to jointly evaluate AI systems' capabilities of innovative exploration and autonomous scientific execution across general research domains, and the first to progressively withdraw human methodological guidance within the same research project to test how far AI can proceed on its own. Built by over 40 experts with the cost of 31,000+ human hours, ASI-Bench contains 60 project-level research tasks across 11 scientific domains and progressively reduces methodological guidance to test whether AI can independently select methods, conduct research, and produce verifiable results. All tasks undergo expert review, AI-assisted auditing, sandbox execution, and scorer validation. Across 18 state-of-the-art agent--model configurations, the average score drops from 50.91 with full methodological guidance to 29.10 with only the method specified and 26.62 when agents must determine the method themselves. This sharp decline shows that current systems remain heavily dependent on human guidance and are still far from autonomously conducting end-to-end, project-level scientific research. ASI-Bench is open to the world. We invite researchers and builders everywhere to contribute new tasks, challenge the limits of today's AI, and help accelerate humanity's collective path toward artificial superintelligence at https://asibench.apexin.ai/submit.

</details>


### [223] [DeAR: Decentralized Agentic Reasoning via Capability Grounding and Collaborative Thought Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17282)
*Xing Wei,Changmeng Zheng,XiaoYong Wei,Xiufen Ye,Qing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Decentralized Agentic Reasoning (DeAR) replaces centralized protocols with peer-to-peer collaboration, using capability grounding, thought-map navigation, and topology updates to improve multimodal and text QA performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Centralized agentic systems suffer routing bottlenecks and static role allocations, hindering performance on complex multimodal queries; a decentralized, adaptive approach may enhance scalability and accuracy.

Method: DeAR introduces (1) decentralized capability grounding for query-dependent specialization, (2) thought map navigation to guide cross-peer interactions, and (3) topology update for adaptive error correction, evaluated on 9 multimodal/text QA benchmarks.

Result: Empirical evaluations show DeAR consistently outperforms recent baselines across diverse tasks, indicating the benefits of decentralized, adaptive agent collaboration for knowledge-intensive reasoning.

Conclusion: Decentralization with adaptive collaboration improves reasoning accuracy; the framework's proposed mechanisms effectively handle complex queries, and the code will be released upon acceptance.

Abstract: Existing agentic reasoning systems typically rely on centralized protocols. This design introduces routing bottlenecks and static role allocations that often fail when handling complex multimodal queries. We propose DeAR (Decentralized Agentic Reasoning), a framework that shifts from central control to autonomous peer-to-peer collaboration. DeAR is built on three mechanisms: (1) decentralized capability grounding for query-dependent agent specialization, (2) thought map navigation for targeted peer interactions, and (3) topology update for adaptive error correction. Evaluations across 9 diverse multimodal reasoning and text-based QA benchmarks indicate that DeAR consistently outperforms recent baseline methods, validating that decentralized and adaptive collaboration among agents enhances accuracy in knowledge-intensive reasoning tasks. The source code will be available at https://open_upon_acceptance.

</details>


### [224] [PlanPO: Group Planning-Aware Policy Optimization for Multi-Turn Agentic LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17289)
*Dayang Liang,Liyuan He,Xuan Feng,Shuxin Li,Bo An,Yunlong Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PlanPO is a group-relative RL method for LLMs that uses coarse-to-fine advantage signals to differentiate successful trajectories by interaction and generation efficiency, enabling generalizable planning; it outperforms GRPO by 27.2% on ALFWorld/WebShop/SciWorld with minimal cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current group-relative policy optimization often treats all successful trajectories the same, obscuring differences in interaction efficiency and leading to advantage collapse and bottlenecks. A method that distinguishes these trajectories can foster generalizable planning behaviors.

Method: PlanPO introduces coarse-to-fine advantage signals that capture relative trajectory length differences and turn-level response lengths within the set of successful rollouts for a given task. These signals are integrated into a group-relative optimization framework to learn planning and generation policies that generalize beyond task-specific high-quality examples, without simply minimizing length.

Result: Empirically, PlanPO achieves a 27.2% average improvement over GRPO on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SciWorld benchmarks, outperforming recent strong baselines, while incurring negligible additional training cost.

Conclusion: PlanPO provides a simple, effective RL approach that fosters generalizable planning in agentic LLMs by leveraging coarse-to-fine, trajectory-aware advantage signals within a group-relative framework.

Abstract: Group-relative policy optimization has emerged as a key paradigm for training agentic large language models (LLMs) on multi-turn interactive tasks. However, most existing variants fail to distinguish advantages among successful trajectories even when these trajectories differ substantially in their interaction efficiency. For instance, circuitous successes are often assigned the identical outcome reward, causing advantage collapse and severe performance bottlenecks. To this end, we propose Group Planning-aware Policy Optimization (PlanPO), a simple yet effective RL method for learning generalizable planning abilities beyond task-specific high-quality behavior patterns. Specifically, PlanPO introduces coarse-to-fine advantage signals, which capture the relative differences in trajectory-level lengths and turn-level response lengths conditioned on successful trajectories sampled for the same task. Within the group-relative optimization structure, this enables agents to actively learn generalizable and deliberate behaviors spanning interaction planning and textual generation from high-quality rollouts, without degenerating into vanilla length minimization. Experimentally, PlanPO improves over GRPO by 27.2\% on average across the challenging multi-turn benchmarks ALFWorld, WebShop, and SciWorld, outperforming recent powerful baselines while incurring negligible additional training cost.

</details>


### [225] [LiveHouse-TS: An Open-world Living Benchmark for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17299)
*Haomin Wen,Ziyu Zhou,Qingxiang Liu,Siru Zhong,Yuxuan Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LiveHouse-TS introduces an open-world, streaming benchmark for Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) that evaluates models prequentially on real future data, revealing that static rankings degrade under distribution shifts and over time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static, snapshot-based benchmarks for TSFMs fail to capture long-term robustness and performance under evolving, real-world conditions (seasonality, shifts, events). A continuous, open-world benchmark can measure temporal validity and ranking stability.

Method: Develop LiveHouse-TS as a live, open-world benchmark infrastructure that streams future data and evaluates model performance prequentially across 11 domains and 17 datasets, enabling continuous evaluation and longitudinal analysis of rankings and robustness.

Result: Static rankings are dramatically reshuffled under the live protocol; long-term evaluation reveals robustness and ranking effects not evident in snapshot benchmarks across multiple domains.

Conclusion: LiveHouse-TS provides a first open-world, living benchmark for TSFMs, enabling ongoing assessment of model robustness and ranking stability over time, highlighting the limitations of static benchmarks.

Abstract: Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) have recently emerged as a highly promising paradigm for cross-domain zero-shot forecasting. However, existing evaluation protocols predominantly rely on static benchmarks with fixed historical test windows. While these benchmarks provide a valuable baseline snapshot, they evaluate an average performance on a fixed history, failing to capture how models behave in continuously evolving real-world environments characterized by seasonal variations, distribution shifts, and unexpected events. To bridge this gap, we introduce LiveHouse-TS, the first open-world living benchmark infrastructure for TSFMs. By evaluating models prequentially on real future data in open-world environments, LiveHouse-TS shifts time series benchmarking from snapshot accuracy to continuous temporal validity. Rather than acting as a one-off leaderboard, our infrastructure serves as a continuous time series infrastructure designed to explore vital, long-term scientific questions: Can model rankings be maintained over the long term? Which models remain genuinely robust under distribution shifts? Extensive streaming evaluations across 11 domains with 17 datasets demonstrate that static rankings undergo a dramatic reshuffling under a live protocol.

</details>


### [226] [SignalReasoner: Assessing the Upper Bound of 3B Models for Signal Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17301)
*Guozheng Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RL fine-tuning with verifiable rewards and domain-aware chain-of-thought fine-tuning substantially improve signal-domain mathematical reasoning in LLMs; compares GRPO, GSPO, GMPO; best model reaches 39.12% accuracy (≈3.2x baselines).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend successful post-training CoT RL methods from broad mathematical reasoning to graduate-level signal processing math, assess domain-specific RL pipelines and policy optimization variants (GRPO/GSPO/GMPO) on WirelessMATHBench-XL.

Method: Two training paradigms applied to Qwen2.5-3B-Base: (i) direct reinforcement learning on WirelessMATHBench-XL with verifiable rewards; (ii) supervised fine-tuning on a distilled wireless-domain chain-of-thought corpus, followed by the same RL stage. Benchmarks compare three policy-optimization schemes: GRPO, GSPO, GMPO.

Result: Best model achieves 39.12% overall accuracy, more than triple the untrained Base model's 12.37% accuracy, indicating substantial gains from the combined SFT+RL approach and RL with verifiable rewards in domain-specific signal reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Domain-aware CoT SFT followed by RL can effectively initialize and improve domain-specific signal reasoning in LLMs; GSPO/GMPO have potential stability/accuracy advantages over GRPO in this setting, though further investigation is warranted to isolate their contributions and limitations.

Abstract: Post-training with supervised chain-of-thought fine-tuning and reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards has substantially improved the mathematical reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). However, their application to signal processing problems remains relatively under-explored. This report investigates reinforcement fine-tuning strategies for adapting Qwen2.5-3B-Base to graduate-level signal mathematical problems from WirelessMATHBench-XL, a comprehensive benchmark for mathematical reasoning in this domain. We examine two training paradigms: (i) direct reinforcement learning (RL) on WirelessMATHBench-XL with verifiable rewards; and (ii) supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on a distilled wireless-domain chain-of-thought corpus, followed by the same domain-specific RL stage. Across both paradigms, we benchmark Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), Group Sequence Policy Optimization (GSPO), and Geometric-Mean Policy Optimization (GMPO). We aim to assess whether domain-aware CoT SFT serves as an effective initialization for subsequent RL, and whether GSPO or GMPO offer advantages in stability or accuracy over GRPO for signal reasoning tasks. Our best model achieves an overall accuracy of 39.12\%, representing a more than threefold improvement over the untrained Base model (12.37\%).

</details>


### [227] [Wuying-Browser-Agent: Real-World Centric Fundamental Long-Horizon Browser Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17319)
*AIMAE Team,Tianxiang Chen,Yan Cheng,Zhangye Han,Xiaowei Li,Chang Liu,Cheng Liu,Zhongqiang Ma,Long Peng,Xiaobing Tu,Yinggui Wang,Hongliang Wei,Chen Wu,Daiping Xin,Kunyu Zhou,Pengyang Zhou,Peiyuan Chen,Ziyuan Chen,Yutao Deng,Chunyu Dong,Xiangyu Fu,Yicheng Feng,Ruian He,Haochen Li,Miancan Liu,Zhengqin Liu,Wei Peng,Jinkui Ren,Haoyu Tan,Dong Xiao,Rongkun Xue,Shujian Yang,Xianhang Ye,Ziqi Yuan,Ziyang Yu,Linghan Zhang,Xiantao Zhang,Xuanpu Zhao,Yinan Zhao,Zhenghui Zhao,Bin Zhu,Likai Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unified browser-agent framework (Wuying-Browser-Agent) aligns execution, supervision, optimization, and evaluation to tackle long-horizon, real-world web tasks, introducing a structured execution layer, recovery-focused training (RUIC-SFT), divergence-aware online GRPO, and a large bilingual benchmark, achieving new open-source state-of-the-art across multiple benchmarks and demonstrating generalization beyond browsers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world browser use requires sustaining dozens of decisions, error recovery, and complex UI navigation, which existing short demonstrations and benchmarks fail to capture. The paper argues that alignment across the full pipeline is necessary, not just scaling models.

Method: 1) Structured browser harness for stable execution primitives and context management; 2) Reflection and UI-specialized Curriculum SFT (RUIC-SFT) trained on recovery trajectories and complex-UI interactions; 3) Divergence-Aware Online GRPO (DAO-GRPO) with potential-based reward shaping and divergence-aware step weighting for long-horizon credit assignment; 4) BrowserBench, a bilingual real-web benchmark with 350 tasks (~38 steps average).

Result: Wuying-Browser-Agent-27B achieves 80.6% on WebVoyager, 66.7% on Online-Mind2Web, and 65.1% on BrowserBench (open-source state-of-the-art); the same pipeline transfers to non-browser tasks, achieving an average of 73.8 across Tau2-Bench, Claw-Eval, and BFCL-v4.

Conclusion: Alignment across execution, supervision, optimization, and evaluation yields robust long-horizon browser agents; the framework’s components collectively enable recovery, efficient learning, and scalable evaluation, with demonstrated generalization beyond browser domains and a substantial, realistic benchmark to expose long-horizon failures.

Abstract: Browser agents perform well on short, clean demonstrations, but real deployment is fundamentally different: agents must sustain dozens of decisions on live websites while recovering from mistakes and navigating complex UIs. We argue that closing this gap requires alignment at every level of the pipeline, including execution, supervision, optimization, and evaluation, rather than scale alone. We present Wuying-Browser-Agent, a unified framework that addresses each of these levels. A structured browser harness provides stable execution primitives and decision-oriented context management. Reflection and UI-specialized Curriculum SFT (RUIC-SFT) explicitly trains on recovery trajectories and complex-UI interactions. Divergence-Aware Online GRPO (DAO-GRPO) improves long-horizon credit assignment through potential-based reward shaping and divergence-aware step weighting. Finally, we introduce BrowserBench, a bilingual real-web benchmark of 350 tasks averaging 37.9 steps, because most existing benchmarks are too short to expose long-horizon failure modes. Wuying-Browser-Agent-27B achieves 80.6\% on WebVoyager, 66.7\% on Online-Mind2Web, and 65.1\% on BrowserBench, establishing a new open-source state of the art on browser-use benchmarks. The same pipeline also transfers beyond browser use, demonstrating strong general agentic ability and reaching an average score of 73.8 on Tau2-Bench, Claw-Eval, and BFCL-v4.

</details>


### [228] [LLMs for Medical Consultation Are Evaluated Too Late: The Preformulation Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17330)
*Yining Hua,Cyrus Ayubcha,Hongbin Na,Levi Lian,Alon Gorenshtein,Yiftach Barash,Eyal Klang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Instruction to enter care shifts LLM behavior at first contact: reduces premature self-care advice and increases structured handoffs, but does not reliably elicit decisive facts; study suggests measuring first-contact behavior rather than final-answer quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To examine pre-consultation behavior of LLMs in medical consultation and how entry-to-care instructions shape initial information gathering and handoffs, addressing limitations of evaluating only final diagnostic accuracy.

Method: Evaluation of three API models across four physician-authored, multi-turn vignettes under baseline and entry-to-care instructions (24 fixed-script transcripts); plus adaptive standardized-patient simulations in two cases (12 transcripts). Measured: pre-answer self-care advise, presence of structured handoff summaries, sequencing/documentation changes; analyzed in terms of whether decisive facts were elicited.

Result: Baseline: self-care advice before any patient answer in 9/12 cells; instruction: 0/12. Handoff summaries: 0/12 baseline, 10/12 instruction. Instruction altered sequencing/documentation but did not reliably elicit decisive facts.

Conclusion: The preformulation gap should be assessed via observable first-contact behavior rather than inferring from diagnostic accuracy or final answers; emphasizes the importance of initial interaction patterns in evaluating medical LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models for medical consultation are often evaluated after a clinical problem has already been made clear, although real consultations may begin with a vague, minimized, or misframed concern. We evaluated three API models across four physician-authored, multi-turn vignettes under baseline and entry-to-care instruction conditions, yielding 24 fixed-script transcripts; two cases also used adaptive standardized-patient simulation, yielding 12 transcripts. Self-care or home-management advice before any patient answer appeared in 9 of 12 baseline case-model cells and 0 of 12 instruction cells, while structured handoff summaries appeared in 0 of 12 and 10 of 12 cells, respectively. The instruction changed sequencing and documentation, although it did not reliably ensure elicitation of decisive facts. The preformulation gap should therefore be evaluated directly through observable first-contact behavior rather than inferred from diagnostic accuracy or final-answer quality.

</details>


### [229] [TileMix: Tile-Centric Mixed-Precision Attention for LLM Inference Acceleration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17336)
*Hanzhi Zhang,Qiao Zhang,Qinglei Cao,Heng Fan,Yan Huang,Kewei Sha,Yunhe Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TileMix introduces a tile-centric precision-routing kernel for long-context dense attention in LLMs. It partitions the attention matrix into hardware-aligned score tiles, uses compact bitmasks to route tile groups through FP16 or INT8 computations, and shares an online-softmax state. This preserves dense token connectivity, requires no training, and improves prefill throughput while offering a controllable accuracy-efficiency trade-off across model families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context prefill in LLMs is computationally expensive due to quadratic complexity of dense self-attention. Existing work either reduces precision uniformly or selectively prunes interactions, but fails to align precision routing with hardware tile boundaries and fuse operations, leading to suboptimal throughput and accuracy.

Method: Tile-level partitioning of the attention matrix into hardware-aligned score tiles, compact routing bitmasks indexing tile groups, and runtime dispatch to FP16 or INT8 score computations within a fused dense-attention kernel. All paths update a shared online-softmax state. Precision-grouping scales so a single routing bit can govern multiple adjacent key tiles, preserving tile-aligned compute and metadata. The approach supports grouped-query attention, variable-length batches, and INT8 key/value caches, and does not require training.

Result: In experiments on LongEval, LV-Eval, and A100 prefill benchmarks across LLaMA, Qwen, and Vicuna, TileMix recovers long-context quality lost under uniform INT8 and improves prefill throughput compared with FP16, delivering a tunable accuracy-efficiency frontier across model families.

Conclusion: TileMix offers a practical, non-training solution that maintains dense token connectivity while enabling mixed-precision execution aligned with hardware tiles, enabling efficient long-context prefill across diverse models and hardware.

Abstract: Long-context prefill in large language models (LLMs) incurs substantial computation and memory traffic because dense self-attention computes quadratic query-key scores. Existing methods either use a uniform low-precision path or select token interactions, leaving spatial precision routing over hardware-aligned score tiles outside fused dense attention. We introduce TileMix, a tile-centric precision-routing kernel that makes numerical precision an executable spatial decision over score-tile groups within fused dense attention. TileMix partitions the attention matrix into hardware-aligned score tiles, packs routing decisions into compact bitmasks, and dispatches each tile group through FP16 or INT8 score computation while both paths update a shared online-softmax state. Scalable precision grouping lets each routing bit govern multiple adjacent key tiles, preserving hardware-aligned compute tiles and compact metadata at long contexts. By routing all legal tile groups, TileMix preserves dense token connectivity, requires no training, and supports grouped-query attention, variable-length batches, and INT8 key/value caches. Across LongEval, LV-Eval, and A100 prefill benchmarks on LLaMA, Qwen, and Vicuna, TileMix recovers long-context quality lost under uniform INT8 and improves prefill throughput over FP16, yielding a controllable accuracy-efficiency frontier across model families. The implementation is available at https://github.com/HanzhiZhang-Ulrica/TileMix.

</details>


### [230] [LLM-Only PDDL Domain Repair with Open-Weight Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17341)
*Nader Karimi Bavandpour,Pascal Bercher*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Open-weight LLMs can improve automated repair of PDDL models over a symbolic baseline, but reliability is limited: high F1 yet low test-pass guarantees; domain-dependent failures (e.g., Thoughtful) reduce trust; not yet suitable for reliable automated model repair.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether open-weight large language models can perform PDDL model repair using positive/negative test plans, and compare their performance to a symbolic baseline.

Method: Experimental evaluation of an LLM-only repair approach versus a symbolic baseline on a PDDL model repair task using positive test plans (solutions) and negative test plans (failures); performance metrics include F1 score and test-pass rate across domains, including the Thoughtful domain; analyses include best-performing configurations with and without test traces.

Result: Symbolic baseline F1 = 0.49; best-performing LLM F1 = 0.87 (with high reasoning effort), an absolute improvement of 0.38; however, mean test-pass rate = 0.82, dropping to 0.06 in the Thoughtful domain; even the best setting with test traces reaches only 0.92.

Conclusion: Current open-weight LLMs cannot guarantee satisfaction of test constraints required for reliable automated PDDL model repair; reliability is domain-sensitive and falls short of consistent constraint satisfaction; integration with symbolic methods or improved training may be required.

Abstract: AI planning is concerned with finding a sequence of actions that achieves a specified goal. It relies on explicit models of the world, commonly represented in the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL). An active line of research investigates how errors in such models can be detected and repaired. For example, users may provide positive test plans that are solutions, and negative test plans that fail during execution. Automated repair methods then modify the PDDL model to satisfy these constraints. In this paper, we evaluate the ability of recent open-weight large language models to perform this repair task using an LLM-only approach. Our experiments show that the symbolic baseline achieves an $F_1$ score of $.49$, while the best-performing LLM reaches $.87$ with high reasoning effort, an absolute improvement of $.38$. However, that setting has a mean test pass rate of only $.82$, falling to $.06$ on the Thoughtful domain; even the best setting that includes the test traces reaches only $.92$. Thus, current open-weight models cannot guarantee satisfaction of the test constraints required for reliable automated model repair.

</details>


### [231] [Cognitive Graph Intelligence for Adaptive and Robust DDoS Attack Detection in Next Generation Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17352)
*Mohammad Arif Hossain,Yeahia Sarker,Md Jafrin Hossain,Most. Humayra Khanom Rime,Nirwan Ansari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GraphGAN for DDoS detection using a generator to synthesize minority attack samples and a GCN-based discriminator, with kNN graph construction from sliding windows to capture temporal relations; a separate GCN classifier on balanced data achieves state-of-the-art performance, especially under data scarcity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address severe class imbalance and non-stationarity in DDoS detection, by modeling relational and temporal structure among traffic flows and augmenting minority samples to improve detection of attacks.

Method: Sequential flows are converted into k-nearest neighbor graphs via sliding windows. A GAN generator synthesizes realistic minority (attack) samples; a Graph Convolutional Network (GCN) discriminator distinguishes real vs synthetic graph data. A separate GCN classifier, trained on a balanced dataset, performs the final detection decision.

Result: GraphGAN achieves superior accuracy, precision, and recall compared to state-of-the-art approaches across four benchmark datasets, with pronounced advantages in data-scarce scenarios.

Conclusion: Integrating temporal graph construction, adversarial augmentation, and GCN classification yields a topology-aware, robust intrusion detection framework suitable for data-constrained environments.

Abstract: Distributed Denial-of-Service (DDoS) attacks threaten network availability, requiring a cognitive detection process that senses traffic, infers intent, and supports an adaptive response under severe class imbalance and non-stationary conditions. This paper proposes a Graph-based Generative Adversarial Network (GraphGAN) that serves as the cognitive detection engine for this task. GraphGAN captures the relational structure among traffic flows while addressing imbalance through adversarial generation of synthetic samples. Sequential flows are converted into $k$-nearest neighbor graphs using sliding windows to preserve feature-similarity and temporal dependencies among flows. The generator learns the distribution of DDoS attacks to synthesize realistic minority samples, while a Graph Convolutional Network (GCN)-based discriminator distinguishes real from synthetic graph data. A separate GCN classifier, trained on the balanced dataset, performs the final detection decision. Evaluations on four benchmark datasets show that GraphGAN achieves superior accuracy, precision, and recall compared to state-of-the-art approaches, particularly in data-scarce scenarios. By integrating temporal graph construction, adversarial augmentation, and GCN classification, GraphGAN effectively models coordinated attack behaviors and mitigates class imbalance, providing a robust and topology-aware solution for intrusion detection in data-constrained environments.

</details>


### [232] [LEGO-RL: Harness-Native Reinforcement Learning for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17393)
*Yiming Du,Yuxin Jiang,Tao Yuan,Jianbo Dai,Shaowei Wang,Jierun Chen,Chaofan Tao,Xianzhi Yu,Lifeng Shang,Kam-Fai Wong,Xiaohui Li,Haoli Bai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LEGO-RL enables policy-gradient RL for coding agents by integrating native harnesses with in-process LLM proxying, scalable sandboxing, and observable training tools, achieving improved performance across multiple harnesses while preserving a strong rollout-training correlation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Native coding-agent harnesses have misaligned execution environments for policy-gradient training, leading to crashes, reward hacking, and mismatch between rollout and policy updates.

Method: Three pillars: (1) in-process LLM proxying to capture raw generation streams and recompute token-level log probabilities; (2) scalable sandbox orchestration with image caching and stage-wise defenses to mitigate reward hacking; (3) integrated validation/monitoring plugin and Live UI for granular trajectory diagnostics.

Result: Trained the sparse MoE model Qwen3.5-35B-A3B with GSPO across three native coding-agent harnesses. SWE-bench Verified scores improved from OpenHands SDK 64.0% to 70.4%, Claude Code 62.4% to 68.2%, and OpenCode 57.2% to 66.6%. Rollout-training probability correlation remained above 0.99.

Conclusion: LEGO-RL effectively bridges native coding-agent harnesses with scalable policy-gradient optimization, delivering faithful optimization, reliable execution, and observable training, demonstrated by robust, cross-harness gains on SWE-bench Verified.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for coding agents increasingly relies on long-running agent harnesses to manage tool integration, repository contexts, and execution feedback. However, the native execution environments of these harnesses are inherently misaligned with policy-gradient training: environmental crashes and reward hacking corrupt outcome signals, while train-inference discrepancies decouple rollout behavior from policy updates. To address this, we present LEGO-RL, a framework that bridges native coding-agent harnesses with scalable policy-gradient optimization without modifying their internal control flow. LEGO-RL is built upon three pillars: (1) faithful optimization via in-process LLM proxying that captures raw generation streams for token-level alignment and robust trainer-side log-probability recomputation, even under harness-side compaction or re-serialization; (2) reliable execution via scalable sandbox orchestration featuring image caching and stage-wise defenses to mitigate reward hacking; and (3) observable training through an integrated plugin that automates validation and monitoring, paired with a Live UI for granular trajectory diagnostics. We evaluate LEGO-RL by training the sparse MoE model Qwen3.5-35B-A3B with GSPO across three native coding-agent harnesses. LEGO-RL improves Qwen3.5-35B-A3B across OpenHands SDK (64.0% to 70.4%), Claude Code (62.4% to 68.2%), and OpenCode (57.2% to 66.6%) on SWE-bench Verified, while maintaining a rollout-training probability correlation above 0.99.

</details>


### [233] [Task-Aware Harness Provisioning for LLM Agents in Mission-Critical Infrastructure Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17433)
*Liangtao Lin,Qingang Zhang,Zhaomeng Zhu,Tianwei Zhang,Yonggang Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Task-specific harness provisioning for LLM agents in mission-critical infrastructure, via map-guided escalation to balance accuracy and cost; no universal optimum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce resource waste from uniform harness exposure by aligning information access with task requirements; identify optimal harness configurations through task-to-harness mappings derived from literature and controlled experiments.

Method: Classify MCI tasks by the mathematical representation of the underlying system; rank harness configurations by information exposure; construct task-to-harness mappings from literature and controlled execution; propose map-guided escalation that starts with task-specific harnesses and expands upon self-check failure; evaluate on two MCI tasks (liquid cooling, power grids) against full provision and Reflexion.

Result: In liquid cooling, accuracy improved from 0.652 (full provision) to 0.715 with map-guided provisioning and achieved accuracy comparable to Reflexion with 48% fewer tokens; in power grids, full provision remains optimal, but map-based provisioning offers lower-cost alternatives; overall, harness provisioning exhibits a domain-dependent Pareto frontier.

Conclusion: Harness provisioning for LLM agents in MCI should be domain-specific; there is no universal optimum—map-guided escalation provides effective trade-offs between accuracy and cost along a Pareto frontier.

Abstract: LLM agents have been widely adopted to operate mission-critical infrastructure (MCI). These agents normally rely on a harness that determines what information they can access, which tools they can use, and what actions they can take. Existing systems often expose the same comprehensive harness to every task, which may not be necessary and cause resource wastes. In this paper, we focus on the identification of optimal harness configurations, and view it as a resource-matching problem between what each task requires and what the harness provides. To measure this match, we classify MCI tasks based on the mathematical representation of the underlying system and rank harness configurations by the amount and type of information they provide. We then construct task-to-harness mappings from two sources: mining research literature and measuring controlled agent execution. Leveraging the measured mapping, we propose a new harness provisioning algorithm: map-guided escalation. It begins with a task-specific harness and expands to full provision only after a failed self-check. We evaluate our method in two representative MCI tasks: in liquid cooling, it improves the agent accuracy from 0.652 under full provision to 0.715 and achieves accuracy comparable to Reflexion with 48% fewer tokens; In power grids, full provision remains accuracy-optimal, while map-based provisioning offers lower-cost alternatives. These findings show that harness provisioning follows a domain-dependent accuracy-cost Pareto frontier rather than a universal optimum.

</details>


### [234] [Depth Enables Local Entropy: Quadratic Depth Dependence in Deep Variation-Norm ReLU Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17434)
*Tao Jiang,Minbo Gao,Shaowei Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Minimax risk for Gaussian regression with Parhi--Nowak deep-RBV^2 nets scales as at least ~L^2 w^2 log w * R^2 / n, with an upper bound ~~ L^2 w^2 R^2 / n; depth induces quadratic (up to log factors) dependence; a radius-dependent transition to representation-limited behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify how depth, width, and layer-sum constraints in a structured deep network affect statistical risk, establishing near-tight bounds and identifying intrinsic depth costs.

Method: Construct local packing with log-cardinality Omega(L^2 w^2 log w); develop bias-corrected bounded-coefficient approximation; balanced amplification to control coefficient growth with depth; translate to vector-valued RBV^2 blocks; apply Gaussian Fano to derive radius-explicit lower bounds; derive pseudodimension-based finite-net upper bound.

Result: Lower bound: minimax risk >= c * L^2 w^2 log w * R^2 / n under A=B=R, sigma ~ R, with a radius condition; upper bound: tilde O(L^2 w^2 R^2 / n) via finite nets; thus quadratic depth dependence up to log factors; transition to representation-limited regime at small radius.

Conclusion: Shows depth costs are intrinsic in this architecture; risk scales quadratically with depth and logarithmically with width; suggests design considerations and that upper and lower bounds are close up to logs; indicates representation constraints dominate in tight-radius regimes.

Abstract: We study Gaussian regression over the explicit vector-valued Parhi--Nowak deep-RBV^2 architecture with depth L, width w, layer-sum variation budget A, and output bound B. For this O(L w^2)-parameterized architecture, the known lower and upper bounds differ by one factor of depth. We construct a local packing showing that the quadratic depth dependence is intrinsic under an explicit sample-size-dependent radius condition. The packing has log-cardinality Omega(L^2 w^2 log w); its codewords lie in an O(lambda) L^2 ball and are pairwise Omega(lambda)-separated. The main ingredients are a bias-corrected bounded-coefficient approximation theorem and balanced amplification: multiplying a depth-D ReLU network by q can be implemented using one constant channel so that every coefficient grows by only q^(1/D). Translation to vector-valued RBV^2 blocks then has layer-sum cost O(D w^2 q^(1/D)). Gaussian Fano yields a radius-explicit lower bound governed by the output, testing, and representation scales. Under A=B=R, sigma proportional to R, and the stated radius condition, this gives minimax risk at least of order L^2 w^2 log(w) R^2/n. A pseudodimension-based finite-net upper bound gives O-tilde(L^2 w^2 R^2/n) for unbounded Gaussian responses. Thus the minimax risk has quadratic polynomial dependence on depth, up to logarithmic factors, and exhibits a transition to representation-limited behavior at smaller radius.

</details>


### [235] [Structure-Internalized Rule Language Model for Faithful Knowledge Graph Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17443)
*Xingrui Zhuo,Jiapu Wang,Manzong Huang,Gongqing Wu,Xindong Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes SIRLM, a structure-internalized LLM framework for Knowledge Graph Reasoning (KGR) that aligns LLM reasoning with KG structure via SIRG, KG tokenizer, and neuro-symbolic reasoner, achieving state-of-the-art results across multiple datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses reasoning evidence perception drift: mismatch between KG structural context and LLM parametric knowledge, which harms reasoning effectiveness and faithfulness; aims to tightly couple structural knowledge with faithful rule execution.

Method: Introduces Structure-Internalized Rule Generator (SIRG) with an in-context learning block and a structural relation memory; adds a KG tokenizer based on structural invariance learning; incorporates a neuro-symbolic reasoner with rule-constrained message propagation; designed to provide learnable structural representations and faithful feedback; integrates with standard LLM training paradigms (SFT, GRPO).

Result: Extensive experiments against 17 state-of-the-art KGR methods on 36 datasets demonstrate significant superiority of SIRLM.

Conclusion: SIRLM effectively grounds LLM reasoning in KG-grounded evidence, improving both effectiveness and faithfulness; compatible with existing training pipelines and underscores the benefit of integrating structural knowledge into LLM-based KGR.

Abstract: Knowledge Graph Reasoning (KGR) aims to discover latent facts by leveraging the structural evidence available in KGs, posing a challenge to the structural semantic understanding capability of KGR models. Recent studies have demonstrated that Large Language Models (LLMs) can achieve remarkable progress on KGR tasks via flexible in-context learning. However, the inherent representation inconsistency between KG structural context and LLM parametric knowledge remains inadequately addressed. This limitation prevents LLMs from effectively perceiving reasoning evidence that aligns with KG constraints, which undermines both the effectiveness and faithfulness of reasoning. We refer to this problem as reasoning evidence perception drift of LLMs over KGs. To address this problem, we propose a Structure-Internalized Rule Language Model (SIRLM), which centers on structural rule generation to couple the parametric learning of structural knowledge with the faithfulness evaluation of reasoning logic, enabling LLMs to anchor tightly to KG-grounded evidence. Specifically, we first design a Structure-Internalized Rule Generator (SIRG), which incorporates an in-context learning block augmented with a structural relation memory to coordinate structural and parametric knowledge. Furthermore, we equip SIRG with a KG tokenizer based on structural invariance learning and a neuro-symbolic reasoner based on rule-constrained message propagation. These components provide SIRG with learnable structural representations and faithful rule-execution feedback, respectively. Our SIRLM can be seamlessly integrated into standard LLM training paradigms, such as SFT and GRPO. Extensive experiments against 17 state-of-the-art KGR methods on 36 datasets demonstrate the significant superiority of SIRLM.

</details>


### [236] [SAGE: Self-Evolving Storyboard Skills via Attribution-Guided Rule Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17468)
*Maolin Ran,Xiaoyang Lu,Jiaqi Liu,Jian Wang,Weiwen Liu,Jianghao Lin,Yong Yu,Weinan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAGE uses Attribution-Guided Evolution to learn, attribute, evolve, and route directing knowledge from expert storyboard demonstrations into scenario-specific rule packs for automated storyboarding, enabling auditable and scalable control with performance gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address tacit, unevaluated, and non-scalable knowledge transfer from expert directors to automated storyboard generation.

Method: Contrast each training screenplay with its expert storyboard to derive episode-independent rules; during generation, record narrative-group rule adoption; apply localized feedback to iteratively update individual rules; evolve rules into scenario packages with a routing index; groups retrieve bounded rule sets appropriate to their situation without ongoing expert input.

Result: 18 test episodes across 3 genres yield 77.8 vs 77.1 (expert-validated rubric) in favor of SAGE; 14-day deployment produced 1,344 narrative-group outputs with 87.2% acceptance without substantive edits and >83% reduction in authoring time per episode; release of PROSE dataset (68 episodes).

Conclusion: SAGE demonstrates practically auditable, knowledge-grounded automation for storyboard generation with competitive quality and substantial efficiency benefits, supported by a public dataset for further research.

Abstract: Storyboards turn screenplays into visual shot plans for automated short drama production. Professional storyboarding relies on tacit directorial expertise and remains an industrial bottleneck. Large language models can automate this step, but methods for supplying directing knowledge face three challenges: (1) Knowledge acquisition: the craft remains implicit in exemplars or must be written manually. (2) Knowledge refinement: authored knowledge is not evaluated against execution outcomes, and opaque generation prevents feedback attribution to the knowledge behind each decision. (3) Knowledge injection: injecting all knowledge exceeds usable context, while manual selection for every narrative group does not scale. We present SAGE (Skill with Attribution-Guided Evolution), a deployed framework that learns, attributes, evolves, and routes directing knowledge from expert demonstrations. SAGE derives rules that are independent of episode content by contrasting each training screenplay with its expert storyboard. During generation, the model records each narrative group's adopted rules. Combining these records with localized feedback enables targeted updates to individual rules. Evolved rules form scenario packages with a routing index, so each group retrieves only a bounded set appropriate to its situation without expert intervention. On 18 test episodes across three genres, SAGE scored 77.8 on a rubric validated by experts, versus 77.1 for professional directors. Deployed for 14 days on Virtual Film Studio, SAGE produced 1,344 narrative group outputs; 87.2 percent were accepted without substantive edits, and the production team recorded over 83 percent less authoring time per episode. We release PROSE, the first public dataset pairing screenplays with storyboards by professional directors across 68 episodes: https://github.com/creDreams/PROSE.

</details>


### [237] [When AI Designs AI: Innovation or Imitation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17471)
*Yikang Yang,Zhengxin Yang,Luzhou Peng,Minghao Luo,Yanqi Kan,Wanling Gao,Jianfeng Zhan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agent-designed methods mostly reuse and recombine human-designed algorithmic choices, with occasional SOTA matches but weak generalization; overall novelty is limited to recombination within human-derived design spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantitatively compare agent-designed algorithms with human-designed ones by deriving task-specific design spaces from humans, mapping both sets into these spaces, and assessing performance and design differences.

Method: Construct task-specific algorithmic design spaces from human-designed methods; map human and agent-designed methods into these spaces at the module level; evaluate agents on a representative suite of open-ended, multi-modal AI tasks; quantify performance and algorithmic differences from humans.

Result: Agents occasionally reach or surpass human SOTA on 10 of 72 configurations; cross-task/agent generalization is limited. 96.8% of agent-designed methods fall within human-derived design spaces, with a large portion recombining existing human choices, and roughly half exactly matching a human design.

Conclusion: Current agents can match SOTA sporadically, but their algorithmic designs largely stay within human-derived spaces, indicating limited novelty and a tendency to reuse/recombine existing human designs; meaningful progress may require novel algorithmic constructs beyond current human-derived repertoires.

Abstract: Recent advances in LLM agents have made them increasingly capable of designing methods for complex AI tasks. This raises two central questions about agent-designed methods relative to human-designed methods: how well they perform, and how different their algorithmic designs are. To study these questions, this paper introduces an analysis that derives task-specific algorithmic design spaces from human-designed methods, maps both human- and agent-designed methods into these spaces, and quantifies their algorithmic differences at the module level. Widely used LLM agents are evaluated on a suite of representative, open-ended AI tasks spanning multiple modalities, and the methods they design are analyzed in terms of both task performance and algorithmic differences from human-designed methods. Experimental results show that current agents can occasionally match or surpass human state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance (10/72 configurations), but such success does not generalize reliably across tasks or agents. Moreover, 96.8% of agent-designed methods fall within human-derived algorithmic design spaces, largely recombining algorithmic choices found in human-designed methods, while nearly half exactly match an existing human algorithmic design. Taken together, these findings suggest that although current agents can occasionally match or surpass human SOTA performance, their algorithmic designs remain within human-derived algorithmic design spaces, reflecting the reuse and recombination of algorithmic choices.

</details>


### [238] [Towards Better Agents for Multi-Turn User Interaction: The Next User Turn Is More Than Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17499)
*Yiwen Zhao,Zhihao Wen,Yuchen Mao,Mingxuan Jiang,Yihao Hu,Pan Wang,Xin Zhang,Wei Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FACA introduces Feedback-Aware Credit Assignment for interactive RL in user-facing agents, aligning turn-level reactions with preceding segments to compute a local advantage that complements terminal outcomes, improving multi-turn dialogue performance without extra critics or rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current interactive RL often assigns a single terminal reward, miscrediting elicitation, errors, and repair. Next-turn user reactions provide localized, noisy evidence about the preceding dialogue and can guide credit assignment for multi-turn interactions.

Method: FACA aligns each reaction with the preceding user segment, derives a locally normalized reaction advantage, and adds it to the verified terminal advantage without requiring an extra critic or rollout.

Result: Compared to an outcome-only Interactive GRPO baseline across nine-domain τ-family tasks, FACA yields improvements of 5.91 and 10.22 percentage points in the 9-domain average at model sizes of 8B and 14B respectively, across three independently trained runs. Gains are concentrated in Telecom; at 8B, randomizing reaction polarity removes the Telecom gain. Zero-shot performance on Pare-Bench and Co-Gym is consistent with the ordering.

Conclusion: Next-turn user reactions provide actionable local credit that appreciably improves multi-turn user-interacting agents, with robust gains across domains and model scales using FACA’s locally normalized reaction advantage added to terminal outcome advantage.

Abstract: User-facing tool agents must coordinate dialogue and tool use as user goals unfold over multiple turns. Yet interactive reinforcement learning typically reduces each rollout to a terminal reward, assigning the same credit to effective elicitation, errors, and later repair. The next user turn is more than context: it also provides noisy, temporally local evidence about the preceding user-to-user segment. We introduce \textbf{F}eedback-\textbf{A}ware \textbf{C}redit \textbf{A}ssignment (\textsc{FACA}), which aligns each reaction with that segment, derives a locally normalized reaction advantage, and adds it to verified terminal outcome advantage without an extra critic or rollout. Against an outcome-only Interactive GRPO control matched in simulator, visible dialogue, initialization, rollout, and optimization, \textsc{FACA} improves the nine-domain $τ$-family average across three independently trained runs by 5.91 and 10.22 percentage points at 8B and 14B, respectively. Gains concentrate in Telecom; at 8B, randomizing reaction polarity removes the Telecom gain. The same ordering holds zero-shot on Pare-Bench and Co-Gym. These results demonstrate that next-turn user reactions provide actionable local credit for improving multi-turn user-interacting agents.

</details>


### [239] [SGHA: Evidence-Grounded Research Problem Discovery with Local Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17501)
*Sarvesh Gharat,Junpei Komiyama*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A local, corpus-first system (SGHA) discovers research problems by structuring literature into evidence-linked objects and a typed evidence graph, identifying gaps and formulating traceable problem families without frontier-model APIs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address auditability, privacy, and bias concerns in early-stage problem formulation driven by opaque proprietary models; enable fully local, transparent problem discovery.

Method: Construct evidence-linked paper objects and a typed evidence graph from literature, detect unresolved patterns, screen gaps before formulation, and produce problem families with assumptions, objectives, success criteria, and ambiguities; all components run on a locally served open-weight 9B LLM.

Result: Compared with AI Scientist-v2 across five ML domains, SGHA’s corpus structure and evidence-constrained reasoning yield promising, inspectable problem formulations without using frontier-model APIs.

Conclusion: Explicit corpus structure and evidence-constrained reasoning can support auditable, privacy-preserving research-problem formulation; SGHA demonstrates viability of fully local LLM-based discovery.

Abstract: Recent efforts toward fully automated AI scientists have demonstrated that language-model agents can generate hypotheses, execute experiments, and draft scientific manuscripts. However, during the early stages of research, when research problems are formulated, these AI scientists often rely heavily on proprietary frontier models. Their proposals are shaped by opaque parametric knowledge and by literature searches conditioned on the proposals themselves. Such knowledge is effectively a black box, and this dependence makes the evidential basis and validity of generated research problems difficult to audit and leaves the process vulnerable to model-specific hallucinations and biases. Furthermore, if proprietary research materials are transmitted to external APIs, the use of these models creates confidentiality, privacy, and data-governance concerns.
  We introduce the Structural Gap Hypothesis Agent (SGHA), a fully automated, corpus-first research-problem discovery system that runs entirely on a local LLM. SGHA structures a scientific literature corpus into evidence-linked paper objects and a typed evidence graph, detects unresolved structural patterns across papers, screens candidate gaps before formulation, and produces traceable research-problem families. In particular, it is able to output assumptions, objectives, success criteria, and remaining ambiguities. All LLM-based components of SGHA are executed using a locally served open-weight 9B language model, without requiring proprietary frontier-model APIs. We compare SGHA with the AI Scientist-v2 idea formulation module in five machine-learning domains. Our results suggest that explicit corpus structure and evidence-constrained reasoning can support promising, inspectable research-problem formulation without relying on frontier models during generation or verification.

</details>


### [240] [Agent Lightning v1.0: Towards Harnessed Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17528)
*Zhiyuan He,Siwei Zhang,Zhiwen Zhou,Yuqing Yang,Yu Kang,Yuge Zhang,Luna K. Qiu,Tin Yan Tsui,Jiahang Xu,Chong Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes harnessed agentic RL and Agent Lightning v1.0 to integrate arbitrary agent harnesses with RL training via an LLM endpoint proxy; demonstrates notable gains on SWE-bench with a small data budget and provides reproducible pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the underexplored role of deploy-time harnesses in RL training, where the harness controls environment interaction and introduces unique data-processing challenges, motivating a lightweight framework to study stability and effectiveness.

Method: Introduce Agent Lightning v1.0 (≈3.5k lines of code) enabling arbitrary harnesses; define harnessed agentic RL where the deploy-time harness participates in post-training; use an LLM endpoint proxy to connect agents to RL training; evaluate on instruction-following, search, and coding agents; use ~6k training examples; provide a complete reproducible workflow for coding-agent RL.

Result: RL improved Qwen3.5-9B on SWE-bench Verified from 41.8% to 56.4% (14.6-point absolute gain) with 6k training examples and modest compute; release of complete workflow and training scripts to enable reproducible research on harnessed agentic RL.

Conclusion: Harnessed agentic RL is a viable paradigm with distinct dynamics controlled by the deploy-time harness; performance depends on retokenization, sample merging, loss normalization, and backend scheduling; the work provides a practical testbed, reproducible pipeline, and a foundation for further study of harnessed RL.

Abstract: Modern agents operate inside agent harnesses that manage tools, context, and control flow, making the harness a critical part of the agent system. Our original Agent Lightning introduced a disaggregated architecture that connects arbitrary agents to RL training through an LLM endpoint proxy, an approach later adopted by frameworks such as verl Uni-Agent, AReaL 2.0, slime, and Polar. We refer to this paradigm as harnessed agentic RL, where the deploy-time harness directly participates in model post-training. Harnessed agentic RL differs fundamentally from traditional agentic RL: the harness, rather than the training engine, owns the environment interaction loop, while the trainer observes only sequences of LLM request-response pairs. This introduces challenges in retokenization, sample merging, advantage calculation, loss normalization, and backend scheduling, which can substantially affect training stability and effectiveness. We present Agent Lightning v1.0, a lightweight framework for harnessed agentic RL implemented in approximately 3,500 lines of code. It supports arbitrary agent harnesses and serves as a practical testbed for studying these challenges. We evaluate it on instruction-following, search, and coding agents, and provide a complete reproducible pipeline for coding-agent RL. Using only 6K training examples and modest compute, RL improves Qwen3.5-9B on SWE-bench Verified from 41.8% to 56.4%, a 14.6-point absolute gain. We release the complete workflow and training scripts to facilitate reproducible research on harnessed agentic RL.

</details>


### [241] [When to Review: Spaced Repetition for Continual Pre-Training of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17530)
*Alankar Atreya,Devesh Batra,Yoages Kumar Mantri,Geremy Bantug,Greig A Cowan,Raad Khraishi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Adaptive spaced-repetition schedule for continual pre-training (SRT) using per-example recall states and SM-2 to selectively replay past vs. new data, improving old-knowledge retention while maintaining or improving new-knowledge acquisition across language, vision, and tabular domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continual pre-training risks catastrophic forgetting of older knowledge when integrating new information. Uniform replay treats all past examples equally, ignoring that different examples decay at different rates. Incorporating cognitive-inspired scheduling aims to balance stability (retaining old knowledge) and plasticity (learning new information).

Method: Introduce Spaced Repetition Training (SRT). Maintain per-example recall state and map per-example perplexity to a recall-quality signal. Use the SM-2 algorithm to schedule which historical examples to rehearse and when, while keeping the model, objective, and optimizer unchanged. Tested on temporally separated Wikipedia and code corpora; extended experiments with vision and tabular data.

Result: SRT improves the stability-plasticity trade-off, recovering 5–37 percentage points of old-knowledge accuracy lost by naive continual pre-training across model scales. It preserves or enhances new-knowledge acquisition and, at larger scales, maintains broad benchmark performance where uniform replay degrades performance. The scheduling principle appears to generalize beyond language when paired with an appropriate recall signal.

Conclusion: Cognitive-inspired adaptive review scheduling (SRT) is a modular, effective approach to continual pre-training. Its per-example recall tracking and perplexity-based recall signal enable selective rehearsal, offering improved retention with minimal disruption to learning objectives; the underlying principle likely extends to other modalities given a suitable recall metric.

Abstract: Continual pre-training of large language models must acquire new information without erasing old knowledge. Existing replay methods often choose a global old/new mixture and sample uniformly, ignoring that examples differ in how quickly they are forgotten. We formulate continual pre-training as adaptive review scheduling: the training loop should decide not only how much history to replay, but which examples should return at each step. We introduce Spaced Repetition Training (SRT), a continual learning framework inspired by cognitive science, which schedules sample-rehearsal using the SuperMemo-2 (SM-2) algorithm. SRT maintains per-example review state, maps per-example perplexity to a recall-quality signal, and schedules historical examples for retention and new examples for consolidation while leaving the model, objective, and optimizer unchanged. On temporally separated Wikipedia and code corpora, SRT improves the stability-plasticity trade-off, recovering 5 to 37 percentage points of old-knowledge accuracy lost by naive continual pre-training across model scales while preserving or improving new-knowledge acquisition. At larger scale, SRT preserves broad benchmark performance that naive continual pre-training and uniform replay substantially degrade. Experiments with vision and tabular data further suggest that the scheduling principle extends beyond language when paired with an appropriate recall signal.

</details>


### [242] [Quantifying Risk Under Evolving Uncertainty: Belief-Dependent Robustness for Safe Sequential Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17574)
*Deep Kumar Ganguly,Jan Kretinsky*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RATTL ties caution to epistemic uncertainty via a Bayesian posterior over unknown dynamics and a Wasserstein ambiguity set whose radius is a monotone function of the posterior, yielding a continuum between worst-case robustness and risk-neutral total-reward maximization; it features a safety guarantee (Safety Sandwich) and a CVaR-like reduction in binary-hazard settings, with a threshold-driven action deferral and broad runtime-safety applicability to uncertain agents including LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety and performance under learning unknown dynamics; a principled risk-aware planning framework that adapts as evidence accumulates.

Method: Maintain a Bayesian posterior over unknown dynamics; optimize under a Wasserstein ambiguity set whose radius contracts with evidence; use a duality akin to Entropic Value-at-Risk to map a risk level to an ambiguity radius; establish well-posedness under transience and compactness; prove a Safety Sandwich bound; show that in a binary-hazard instance the criterion reduces to CVaR at a level set by posterior entropy; provide a worked example demonstrating threshold-based action deferral.

Result: Qualitative guarantees: RATTL value lies between an uninformed robust value and full-knowledge optimum, with a gap that vanishes as the posterior concentrates; in a canonical binary-hazard instance the criterion equals CVaR at a posterior-entropy determined level; the approach yields a runtime-safe policy that defers efficient actions until sufficient evidence is gathered.

Conclusion: RATTL offers a principled, tunable risk-aware planning framework for learning agents to ensure runtime safety under uncertainty, including LLM-based systems; it bridges robust worst-case and risk-neutral objectives by tying risk to epistemic uncertainty and evidence.

Abstract: How cautious should an agent be while it is still learning its environment? We propose RATTL (Risk-Adversarial Total-Reward Learning), which ties caution to epistemic uncertainty: the agent holds a Bayesian posterior over unknown dynamics and plans against a Wasserstein ambiguity set whose radius is a monotone function of that posterior. The radius contracts with evidence, so behaviour interpolates continuously between worst-case robustness and risk-neutral total-reward maximization. The design follows the duality underlying the Entropic Value-at-Risk, which converts the choice of a risk level into the choice of an ambiguity radius. We show the resulting planning problem is well posed under transience and compactness conditions, and prove a Safety Sandwich: the RATTL value lies between the uninformed robust value and the full- knowledge optimum, with a gap that vanishes as the posterior concentrates. In a canonical binary-hazard instance, the induced criterion reduces to Conditional Value-at-Risk at a level set by the posterior entropy. A worked example shows the agent deferring the efficient action until a sharp identification threshold. RATTL targets runtime safety for agents, including LLM-based systems, acting under uncertainty.

</details>


### [243] [TRUSS: Towards Task-Reliable and User-Safe Automated Agent Skill Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17588)
*Zhibo Zhang,Zhen Ouyang,Ling Shi,Kailong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRUSS is an evidence-guided framework that generates functionally effective and safety reliable Agent Skills by combining static safety checks with execution-provenance guided iteration, improving vulnerability detection, attack resistance, and task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome ambiguity in candidate Skills by not only inspecting artifacts but also examining executed behavior and side effects; ensure safety and functional reliability in automated Skill generation.

Method: Static gate evaluating functional claims against source/domain evidence using nine safety properties, followed by loading candidates into a shadow agent inside a Controllable Execution Environment with brokered tools that enforce policy and record provenance traces; iterative refinement links functional failures and property violations back to Skill content.

Result: TRUSS achieved 100% precision and recall in vulnerability detection across 168 SkillInject artifacts, 155 SkillSafetyBench cases, and 187 SkillGenBench tasks; repairs reduced attack success from 38.71% to 19.35% (GPT-5.5) and 46.45% to 29.68% (GPT-5.4) with no regression; Skill generation improved task effectiveness from 17.11% to 52.94% and increased security rate from 50.80% to 100.00%.

Conclusion: Execution evidence complements artifact inspection, exposing behavioral failures and guiding Skill generation toward outcomes that are jointly functionally correct and safety compliant.

Abstract: Agent Skills package reusable natural language procedures with executable resources, enabling software agents to acquire task specific capabilities without model adaptation. Automatically generating such Skills can improve task performance, yet evaluating a candidate solely from its artifact or final task outcome leaves unresolved which actions the equipped agent will perform and which side effects those actions will produce. We present TRUSS, an evidence guided framework for generating functionally effective and safety reliable Agent Skills. TRUSS first inspects functional claims against source and domain evidence while evaluating the complete artifact under nine predefined safety properties. Candidates admitted by this static gate are loaded by a shadow agent inside a Controllable Execution Environment, where brokered tools expose requested actions to policy enforcement and record their results as provenance preserving execution traces. Functional failures and property violations are linked back to the responsible Skill content and used to guide iterative refinement.
  We evaluate TRUSS on 168 SkillInject artifacts, 155 SkillSafetyBench cases, and all 187 tasks in SkillGenBench. TRUSS achieves 100.00\% precision and recall in vulnerability detection. Repair reduces attack success from 38.71\% to 19.35\% with GPT 5.5 and from 46.45\% to 29.68\% with GPT 5.4, with zero attack regression. For Skill generation, TRUSS raises task effectiveness from 17.11\% without Skills to 52.94\%, while increasing the benchmark Security rate from 50.80\% to 100.00\%. These results show that execution evidence can expose behavioral failures missed by artifact inspection and can guide Skill generation toward jointly verified functional and safety outcomes.

</details>


### [244] [MoNe: Modular Neural Memory for Efficient Long Context Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17616)
*Wonguk Cho,Kyubyung Chae,Tribhuvanesh Orekondy,Sunghyun Park,Hyoungwoo Park,Jeongho Kim,Arash Behboodi,Kyuwoong Hwang,Sungrack Yun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MoNe is a lightweight modular neural memory that attaches to frozen pretrained Transformers to enable long-context inference without retraining. It uses test-time learning of fast-weight memory networks to read context in fixed-size segments, generating keys/values from queries without re-reading context tokens. This yields O(N) preprocessing and O(1) query cost with memory that does not scale with N, and ~80% reduction in compute and peak GPU memory at 128K tokens with 6.4% parameter overhead. It generalizes beyond the backbone's native window and performs well on needle-in-a-haystack and RULER word-extraction benchmarks where ICL fails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional in-context learning (ICL) with fixed backbones suffers from costly long-context processing, retraining needs, and memory bottlenecks as context length grows. The work aims to enable scalable long-context inference without retraining by augmenting frozen transformers with a memory mechanism.

Method: Two-phase design: (1) test-time learning of fast-weight neural memory networks to read fixed-size context segments using layer-localized gradient updates; (2) inference uses memory to generate keys and values from the query tokens alone, without re-reading context tokens. This decouples inference cost from context length, yielding O(N) preprocessing and O(1) query cost, with peak GPU memory independent of N. The memory overhead is modest (~6.4% additional parameters).

Result: At 128K tokens, MoNe achieves ~80% reduction in compute and peak GPU memory compared to standard ICL, while maintaining competitive accuracy. It generalizes to context lengths far beyond the backbone window, and performs well on needle-in-a-haystack and RULER word-extraction benchmarks where ICL degrades.

Conclusion: MoNe provides an efficient, scalable approach to long-context inference by attaching a lightweight, test-time-learned memory to frozen transformers. It decouples inference cost from context length, enabling long-range reasoning with modest parameter overhead and strong benchmark performance.

Abstract: We present MoNe, a lightweight modular neural memory that attaches to any frozen pretrained Transformer to enable long-context inference without retraining. MoNe reads context in fixed-size segments via test-time learning of fast-weight neural memory networks with layer-localized gradient updates; at inference, the memory generates keys and values from the query tokens alone, with no context tokens re-read. This two-phase design decouples inference cost from context length, achieving $O(N)$ preprocessing and $O(1)$ query cost with peak GPU memory that does not grow with $N$. At 128K tokens, MoNe reduces both compute and peak GPU memory by approximately 80% compared to ICL with only 6.4% parameter overhead. MoNe generalizes to context lengths far beyond the backbone's native window, achieving strong performance on needle-in-a-haystack and word extraction benchmarks from RULER, where ICL degrades sharply.

</details>


### [245] [Validated Adaptation for Aerial Crowd Monitoring at Mass Gathering Scale: A Deployment Protocol, a Severity Law, and a Diagnostic for Label-Free Drone Crowd Counting, Toward the FIFA World Cup 2034 (Saudi Arabia)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17625)
*AlAnoud AllGhayth,AlJawharh AlOtaibi,Jude AlSubaie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Label-free adaptation improves drone-based crowd counting under distribution shifts, delivering up to ~42 MAE gain over a frozen model and recovering 31-49% of shift-induced error across conditions, validated on 525 controlled runs and full-resolution data with a safety intervention protocol.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable accurate, real-time crowd counting from UAVs in megacrowds (e.g., Hajj scale) without labeled data, with early warning for crush risk and a safety framework for deployment.

Method: Label-free adaptation with a physics-informed prior, normalization-driven signals, and a continuity residual; extensive falsification ablations (five); a full-resolution corpus; a flux-based risk module; 525 controlled runs; seed retraining to improve MAE; a six-point unconditional adaptation protocol with tail monitoring; a six-point safety/interlock protocol.

Result: 31-49% of shift-induced error recovered across four corruptions and five severities; strongest adaptation yields 41.8 MAE improvement over frozen source (CI [34.1, 49.6], p=7.5e-10, d=2.52); genuine +48 MAE aerial gap mitigated (source retrained to 14.6 validation MAE, 34% improvement); flux-based risk module fired on real congestion in 2 of 6 full-length clips; continuity residual invariant to domain shift with r≈1.0 in ablations; 58% of headroom sacrificed by a magnitude shift gate; five-condition safety interlock; six-point policy for unconditional adaptation with tail monitoring.

Conclusion: Demonstrates robust, label-free adaptation for drone-based crowd counting under domain shift and proposes a practical deployment protocol (six-point) that combines adaptation, physics-informed priors, and safety interlocks to prevent undercounting and unsafe inflows.

Abstract: Saudi Arabia will host the 2034 FIFA World Cup and already operates crowd management at Hajj scale. Drone-based counting must hold accuracy on footage unlike anything in its training corpus, without labels, and must warn of dangerous inflow before a crush forms. We deliver a validated answer built on 525 controlled runs, a full-resolution corpus study, five falsification ablations, and a five-condition safety-interlock evaluation. Label-free adaptation recovers 31-49% of shift-induced error across four corruptions and five severities, with the strongest method gaining 41.8 MAE over the frozen source (95% CI [34.1, 49.6], p=7.5x10^-10, d=2.52). We establish a severity law separating methods with a constant absolute margin from the one whose margin grows, and a stability budget identifying which configuration is safe to fly. On a full-resolution corpus carrying a genuine +48 MAE aerial gap (source retrained to 14.6 validation MAE, a 34% improvement), adaptation repairs the dense-scene undercounting that would otherwise under-report a forming crush, and the flux-based risk module fires on real congestion episodes in 2 of 6 full-length clips.
