We localise the recoverable error: in a regime built to favor a physics-informed conservation prior (300-frame clips at 200ms spacing, five times wider than standard), the adaptation signal is normalisation-driven, not flow-driven; the continuity residual is invariant to the proportional counting errors domain shift produces, confirmed by four on/off ablations correlated at r=0.999 and a 40% input corruption moving accuracy by only 0.05 MAE. A label-free shift gate shows shift magnitude and accuracy damage are rank-independent (Spearman rho=0.20; rho=-0.60 among genuine shifts), quantifying the 58% of headroom a magnitude gate forgoes. We establish unconditional adaptation with tail monitoring as policy, closing with a six-point protocol.

</details>


### [246] [Graph Surgery and the Do-Operator: A Precise Correspondence for Acyclic Structural Causal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17634)
*Satpreet Makhija*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Shows a precise equivalence between graphical do-operations and graph surgery in deterministic acyclic structural causal models by matching the dependency extraction Graph(F) with surgery Surg(Graph(F), T_ι); characterizes when the graph alone suffices, defines the intervened model, and proves how sequential interventions compose with outcomes depending only on actual dependency ancestors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reconcile two interpretations of interventions in causal models: (i) removing incoming edges graphically when applying a do-operator, and (ii) replacing mechanisms by constants in the functional description. The goal is to establish when these two views yield the same dependencies and how interventions propagate.

Method: Define Graph(F) as the dependency extraction from a mechanism family F. Prove the main theorem Graph(F^ι) = Surg(Graph(F), T_ι) for deterministic acyclic models with finitely many endogenous variables. Analyze models M=(G,F) with possible unused arrows to identify when Graph(F^ι) = Graph(F) under G, characterize when equality holds for all interventions, define the intervened model, analyze its run, and show that sequential interventions compose and that outcomes depend only on actual dependency ancestors.

Result: Main result: Graph(F^ι) equals Surg(Graph(F), T_ι), i.e., replacing target mechanisms by constants removes exactly the dependencies that graph surgery removes. For models with unused arrows, conditions are given when the equality with G holds for all interventions (iff G records the dependencies of F exactly). Also defines the intervened model, characterizes its run, shows how sequential interventions combine, and proves that outcomes depend only on interventions at actual dependency ancestors.

Conclusion: The paper formalizes the equivalence between do-operatorGraph surgery and dependency extraction in causal graphs, providing precise conditions under which graph-level dependencies capture the full effects of interventions and establishing that outcomes depend solely on actual dependency ancestors.

Abstract: The $\operatorname{do}$-operator is described graphically by deleting arrows into its targets and functionally by replacing their mechanisms with constants. To call these operations equivalent is not yet a mathematical statement: one returns a graph and remembers only the targets, whereas the other returns mechanisms and also remembers the imposed values. We make a dependency-level comparison precise for deterministic acyclic structural causal models with finitely many endogenous variables. If $\operatorname{Graph}(F)$ extracts the dependencies of a mechanism family $F$, our main theorem is $\operatorname{Graph}(F^ι)=\operatorname{Surg}(\operatorname{Graph}(F),T_ι)$. Thus replacing target mechanisms removes exactly the dependencies removed by graph surgery. For a model $M=(G,F)$ whose graph may contain unused arrows, we characterize when the same equality holds with $G$ in place of $\operatorname{Graph}(F)$; it holds for every intervention exactly when $G$ records the dependencies of $F$ exactly. We then define the intervened model, characterize its run, show how sequential interventions combine, and prove that an outcome depends only on interventions at its actual dependency ancestors.

</details>


### [247] [Beyond the Trace: Coupling an Interpretable Reasoning-State Readout to Native MoE Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17638)
*Kang Chen,Sihan Zhao,Yixin Cao,Yugang Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A two-level readout framework for mixture-of-experts reasoning: J64, a 64-axis semantic frame extracted from the model's reasoning, and R64, a low-overhead proxy from routing statistics; together they reveal latent process state, improve decision-time selection and voting, and can steer behavior via router edits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between produced reasoning traces and the actual internal process, providing readable, actionable latent state and improving performance with minimal overhead in mixture-of-experts models.

Method: Learn J64 from the model's own reasoning states to distill J-space into a 64-axis semantic frame; reconstruct J64 from native expert-routing statistics to produce R64, a low-overhead proxy; implement two-level internal readout with test-time decisions at two temporal resolutions; evaluate improvements in candidate selection, voting, and generation stop-and-resample policies across multiple models and families; investigate router edits targeting J64.

Result: J64 adds 0.096–0.135 held-out AUC over a baseline; median per-axis correlation between J64 and R64 ranges from 0.69 to 0.86 across three models and two families; on gpt-oss-20b, R64 preserves 95–100% of J64's predictive gain; J64 and R64 improve single-branch selection and R64-weighted voting improves plain majority voting in seven of eight settings; during generation, rolling readout windows drive a stop-and-resample policy; J64 improves accuracy by 1.1–5.9 points over a sibling-permuted control, with routing-only R64 retaining 0.9–3.2 points; router edits aimed at J64’s mechanism induce predicted reasoning behaviors and shift stall from numerical guessing toward exact symbolic execution.

Conclusion: J64 makes latent process state readable, while routing makes it deployable and actionable. Together, they enable interpretable and controllable two-level readouts for mixture-of-experts reasoning, with tangible gains and the potential to steer reasoning behavior through router edits.

Abstract: What a reasoning model writes is only a partial record of the process that produces it. We introduce a two-level internal readout for mixture-of-experts reasoning. We first distill vocabulary-scale J-space into J64, a 64-axis semantic frame learned from the model's own reasoning states. J64 reveals readable process state that the emitted trace does not show: it separates inference effort from problem-induced strain. It also adds 0.096 to 0.135 held-out AUC over a baseline that reads the same rollout as token occupancy and aggregates it in exactly the same way. We then reconstruct J64 from native expert-routing statistics. The result is R64, a low-overhead proxy: its median per-axis correlation with J64 is 0.69 to 0.86 across three models and two families, and on gpt-oss-20b it preserves 95 to 100% of J64's predictive gain. The readout supports test-time decisions at two temporal resolutions. Over completed candidate sets, J64 and R64 improve single-branch selection, and R64-weighted voting improves plain majority voting in seven of eight settings. During generation, rolling readout windows drive a cumulative stop-and-resample policy whose operating point is fixed on training questions alone. J64 improves accuracy by 1.1 to 5.9 points over a sibling-permuted control, and the routing-only R64 proxy retains 0.9 to 3.2 of those points. Finally, router edits aimed at the mechanism J64 names induce the predicted reasoning behaviors and shift a diagnosed stall from numerical guessing toward exact symbolic execution. Together, J64 makes latent process state readable, while routing makes it deployable and actionable.

</details>


### [248] [LLM-Derived Preference Judgments Are Not Self-Consistent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17644)
*Matthew T. Ford,Francis Bahk,Jingjing Wang,Adam S. Jovine,Tinghan Ye,David B. Shmoys,Peter I. Frazier*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-derived cardinal preference judgments often fail self-consistency tests; a single utility function cannot faithfully summarize preferences across items, as experiments across domains reveal persistent inconsistencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To validate the common assumption that LLM-based preference judgments can be summarized by a single utility function and used for decision-making, by testing self-consistency and measuring deviations.

Method: Develop statistical tests and interpretable metrics to quantify how far observed willingness-to-pay judgments deviate from the best-fitting self-consistent utility; apply to flight, apartment, and hotel scenarios across six LLMs.

Result: Large, persistent inconsistencies in LLM preference judgments; cannot be faithfully summarized by one utility function; suggests limitations of the utility-based pipeline for LLM preferences.

Conclusion: Caution against assuming self-consistency in LLM-derived preferences; future work should explore alternative representations (e.g., multiple utilities or non-utility models) and robust diagnostics for preference inference.

Abstract: Agents increasingly interpret a person's natural-language preferences by querying an LLM for numerical preference judgments, e.g., by asking how much the person would be willing to pay for an item. A growing body of work estimates a utility function from these judgments and then chooses actions based on their estimated utility. This pipeline assumes the judgments are approximately self-consistent: that a single utility function can reproduce them. But are they? To study this question, we measure the self-consistency of cardinal LLM preference judgments. For example, the difference in stated willingness-to-pay between two items should match the stated payment that makes a person indifferent to exchanging them. We develop statistical tests and interpretable measures of how far observed responses depart from the best-fitting self-consistent utility function. Experiments with flight, apartment, and hotel examples across six LLMs reveal large persistent inconsistencies. This suggests that LLM-derived preference judgments cannot be faithfully summarized by a single utility function.

</details>


### [249] [GraphWake: Group Polarization via Memory-Mediated Polarization Cascade in LLM-Agent Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17665)
*Haoran Bu,Zejian Chen,Litian Zhang,Xi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new threat, Memory-Mediated Polarization Cascade, leverages agent memory and public discussion to induce group polarization in LLM-driven agent communities. GraphWake demonstrates that memory retention and stance-neutral cues can trigger repeated retrieval/reproduction and propagation, increasing polarization across discussions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill a gap in security threat modeling for autonomous, agent-operated social platforms by identifying a memory-based mechanism for polarization that is difficult to counter with simple prompt defenses or echo-chamber designs.

Method: Model and instantiate a three-stage threat: (1) exposure and memory retention of targeted arguments; (2) retrieval and reproduction via stance-neutral discussion cues; (3) iterative propagation to untreated agents. Implement GraphWake with: (i) stance-support argumentation knowledge graphs; (ii) axiom-oriented triple selection for reliable retention/reproduction; (iii) stance-neutral memory cueing to trigger concurrent retrieval/reproduction and propagation.

Result: Empirical results show that GraphWake substantially increases group polarization across multiple discussions and memory systems, demonstrating a tangible community-level polarization risk.

Conclusion: Memory-mediated channels via structured knowledge and neutral cues can enable scalable polarization cascades in agent-based ecosystems; highlights the need for defenses addressing memory retention, retrieval cues, and propagation dynamics in autonomous social platforms.

Abstract: LLM-driven agents can autonomously exchange opinions on online platforms and form communities. Such agent-operated social platforms raise a new security concern: attackers may manipulate agents to induce group polarization. Existing methods manipulate agent prompts or construct echo chambers, both of which are difficult to realize in practice. We therefore formulate a new threat, Memory-Mediated Polarization Cascade, which uses agent memory as a persistence channel and public discussion as a propagation channel. This threat contains three stages. During exposure and memory retention, the attacker exposes a small set of target agents to arguments that reinforce their respective stated stances. The targets' memory systems then process and retain these arguments. During retrieval and reproduction, a shared stance-neutral discussion cues the targets to retrieve and reproduce their respective retained arguments. During iterative propagation, untreated agents influenced by the reproduced arguments restate and spread them. We instantiate this threat in GraphWake with three components: (i) stance-support argumentation knowledge graphs construct knowledge-based arguments; (ii) axiom-oriented triple selection distills them for reliable retention and reproduction; and (iii) stance-neutral memory cueing triggers concurrent retrieval and reproduction, initiating propagation. Experiments across multiple discussions and memory systems show that GraphWake substantially increases group polarization. These findings reveal a community-level polarization risk.

</details>


### [250] [Auditing Self-Evolution in Financial Agents: Capability Gains, Security Drift, and Execution-Interface Mismatch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17684)
*Jialong Li,Jialing Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Auditing self-evolving financial agents (SkillOpt, AWM, ReasoningBank) reveals that higher benign utility does not prevent rising attack exposure and unauthorized-state changes; post-evolution safety depends on more than accuracy, exposing execution-environment hazards and the need for tracking regressions, attack-surface contact, and artifact-executor compatibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivated by the disconnect between post-evolution accuracy and long-term safety/security in risk-sensitive domains like e-banking, the work aims to quantify whether evolving agents preserve prior correct behavior and uncover vulnerabilities.

Method: Simulated e-banking experiments with matched benign acquisition trajectories, sealed evaluation endpoints, execution-grounded checks, and independent state replay. Evaluations conducted on Qwen 3.7 Flash across three independently evolved lineages for SkillOpt, AWM, and ReasoningBank. Metrics tracked: benign utility, exposure to injected content, unauthorized-state changes, and attack success rate (ASR). Includes a post-hoc sensitivity test removing a harmful envelope to assess artifact-executor interactions and a separate envelope hazard revealed by AWM.

Result: SkillOpt raises benign utility from 0.741 to 0.837 while exposure rises from 0.820 to 0.943; conditional attack success after exposure falls from 0.605 to 0.562, but overall ASR rises from 0.496 to 0.530 and unauthorized financial-state changes rise to 0.685. Across three lineages, capability, exposure, and unauthorized-state changes increase in all three systems, while ASR increases in only two. ReasoningBank boosts utility to 0.859 without increasing aggregate ASR; unauthorized-state changes remain slightly above Static. AWM uncovers an execution-hazard: a literal WebArena text-action envelope disrupts tool execution in the native function-calling executor; post-hoc removal of this envelope restores utility from 0.319 to 0.756, while exposure rises from 0.299 to 0.909 and ASR from 0.195 to 0.575.

Conclusion: Auditing self-evolving financial agents requires monitoring regressions, attack-surface contact, unauthorized-state changes, and artifact-executor compatibility beyond accuracy; purely accuracy-centric evaluation is insufficient for safety in evolving agents.

Abstract: Self-evolving agents turn experience into reusable skills, workflows, or memories, but post-evolution accuracy alone does not show whether learned behavior preserves previously correct behavior or security. We audit SkillOpt, Agent Workflow Memory (AWM), and ReasoningBank in simulated e-banking using matched benign acquisition trajectories, sealed evaluation endpoints, execution-grounded checks, and independent state replay. On Qwen 3.7 Flash, SkillOpt raises benign utility from 0.741 to 0.837 while exposure to injected content rises from 0.820 to 0.943. Conditional attack success after exposure falls from 0.605 to 0.562, yet overall attack success rate (ASR) rises from 0.496 to 0.530 and unauthorized financial state changes rise to 0.685. Across three independently evolved lineages, capability, exposure, and unauthorized-state changes increase in all three, whereas ASR increases in only two. ReasoningBank raises utility to 0.859 without increasing aggregate ASR, although unauthorized state changes remain slightly above Static. AWM reveals a separate evaluation hazard: a literal WebArena text-action envelope disrupts tool execution in our native function-calling executor. In a post-hoc sensitivity test, removing only that envelope restores utility from 0.319 to 0.756, while exposure rises from 0.299 to 0.909 and ASR from 0.195 to 0.575. Auditing self-evolving financial agents therefore requires tracking regressions, attack-surface contact, unauthorized financial-state change, and artifact-executor compatibility, not accuracy alone.

</details>


### [251] [Mixture-of-Expert Blocks Contain Strong Hallucination Detection Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17687)
*Joao Fonseca,Rodrigo Rodrigues,Paolo Romano*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: InnerExpert uses MoE-specific signals for per-token hallucination detection in LLMs, achieving high AUROC with a single forward pass across multiple datasets and MoE architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hallucinations in LLMs; need per-token localization; MoE offers unique signals (router entropy, expert disagreement, usage patterns) unavailable in dense models; none previously exploited for this task.

Method: Develop InnerExpert that fuses routing-level MoE signals with standard transformer signals into per-token features, train a lightweight detector using LLM-as-a-judge labeled data; supports continuous updates without manual annotation; evaluated on five datasets and two MoE architectures; single forward pass.

Result: Outperforms existing methods on per-token detection; up to 0.91 answer-level AUROC and 0.76 token-level AUROC.

Conclusion: MoE-specific signals are valuable for precise hallucination detection; the approach provides efficient, scalable per-token detection and enables ongoing model updates; generalizes across datasets and MoE designs.

Abstract: Despite their widespread use, Large Language Models (LLMs) remain limited by a fundamental problem: the generation of plausible but false content, known as hallucinations. Most existing detection methods operate at the answer or sentence level, yet per-token detection is essential for localizing hallucinated spans and enabling fine-grained interventions. In this paper, we explore the use of the Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) paradigm to address this gap. In MoE architectures, a single forward pass activates a sparse subset of experts (i.e., distinct feedforward networks per layer) via a routing mechanism, producing internal signals (e.g., router entropy, expert disagreement, and expert usage patterns) that are unavailable in dense architectures and have not been previously exploited for hallucination detection. To this end, we introduce InnerExpert, the first method to leverage these MoE-specific signals for per-token hallucination detection. InnerExpert combines routing-level and standard transformer signals into compact per-token feature vectors, classified by a lightweight detector trained on labels produced by an LLM-as-a-judge pipeline, which enables continuous model updates without manual annotation. Our results show that InnerExpert outperforms existing methods across five datasets and two MoE architectures, achieving up to 0.91 answer-level and 0.76 token-level AUROC, while requiring only a single forward pass.

</details>


### [252] [Accuracy and Robustness of Model Cascades Under Data Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17711)
*Pallavi Mitra,Jai Kushwaha,Felix Biessmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Energy-efficient image classification with confidence-based cascades can drastically reduce energy use (up to 10x CO2 reduction) while preserving accuracy, but routing reliability under input degradations is fragile, revealing three failure modes that degrade deferral performance. Evaluation must include distribution shift and routing reliability beyond clean accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce AI energy consumption by cascading a small fast model and a larger accurate model using confidence-based routing; however, the approach hinges on reliable decision-making under distribution shifts, which is not guaranteed by degradations.

Method: Identify a Pareto-optimal model cascade balancing accuracy, routing quality, and energy; evaluate on image classification under static corruptions and sequential perturbations; analyze how routing decisions and deferral behave under distribution shift; categorize observed failure modes.

Result: The cascade achieves competitive predictive performance with up to 10x reductions in CO2 emissions. Under input degradations, routing decisions deteriorate: (1) static corruptions break the routing signal while the large model remains useful; (2) corruptions degrade both models so deferral no longer improves accuracy; (3) sequential perturbations stabilize predictions but suppress deferral, yielding stable yet unreliable outputs.

Conclusion: Energy-efficient cascades must be evaluated beyond clean accuracy, with explicit assessment of routing reliability under distribution shift. Robust design and testing of confidence-based deferral strategies are essential for dependable performance.

Abstract: Prediction cascades significantly reduce energy consumption of Artificial Intelligence (AI) models while maintaining high predictive performance. The idea is that easy inputs are routed through a lightweight small model, and difficult uncertain cases are deferred to a larger model. While this design can improve computational efficiency on clean data, its effectiveness depends on the reliability of confidence-based routing. Input degradations, such as static corruptions and sequential perturbations, can shift model confidence and routing decisions. In this paper, we study confidence-based cascade frameworks for image classification and investigate how such degradations affect their confidence-based deferral behavior. We select a model cascade at the pareto-optimum of accuracy, routing quality, and energy consumption that achieves competitive predictive performance with an up to 10-fold decrease in CO$_2$ emissions. We study the behavior of that model cascade under input corruptions and analyze how the cascade's routing decisions change when the input distribution shifts. Our analysis identifies three failure modes. Static corruptions either (1) break the routing signal while the large model remains useful, or (2) degrade both models so deferral no longer recovers accuracy. Sequential perturbations reveal a third mode: predictions stabilize but deferral suppresses, yielding stable but unreliable predictions. These findings demonstrate that energy efficient model cascades require evaluation beyond clean accuracy, with explicit attention to routing reliability under distribution shift.

</details>


### [253] [Beyond Suspicious Steps: Ontological Trust in Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17718)
*An He,Yao Wang,Haibin Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces ontological trust and RGE, an online monitor for trajectory-prefix alignment (Role, Goal, Evidence) in long-horizon agents. Shows strong drift-detection performance across cross-domain benchmarks and discusses pseudo-consistency challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing monitors focus on local compliance or final verdicts; drift can accumulate across prefixes rendering the action sequence misaligned with user task. A task-conditioned prefix-level trust estimator is needed to provide replayable, auditable trajectories.

Method: Define RGE monitor; decompose trust into Role, Goal, Evidence; leverage LLMs only to derive structured representations; deterministic trust-state updates, projections, and intervention decisions; build cross-domain trajectory corpus (OSWorld, FinanceBench, EICU-AC) with benign, drift, and pseudo-consistency cases; compare against adapted rule-, judge-, shield-style baselines; evaluate with two larger estimator models.

Result: RGE outperforms baselines on prefix-paired drift detection; with larger models achieves ~93% Drift F1 across benchmarks; benign coverage ≥95.8%; pseudo-consistency harder to detect, depending on external visibility of task completion; empirical characterization of this limit.

Conclusion: Establishes prefix-level, replayable trust monitoring for long-horizon agents; enables auditable oversight; highlights pseudo-consistency as a fundamental challenge and suggests directions for future work.

Abstract: Long-horizon agents increasingly operate across many steps, tools, and observa- tions. In this setting, the relevant oversight question is not only whether each action is locally valid, but whether the evolving trajectory still corresponds to the task the user authorized. Drift can accumulate quietly: an agent may call the right tool with plausible arguments at every step, while its prefix moves toward a broader role, an adjacent objective, or evidence the user never supplied. Existing monitors mostly check local compliance, deliver final-trace verdicts, or score generic risk; they do not directly estimate this prefix-level relation. We introduce ontological trust, a task-conditioned property of trajectory prefixes, and instantiate it as RGE, an online monitor that decomposes trust along Role, Goal, and Evidence. RGE uses LLMs only to derive structured task and step representations; trust-state updates, projec- tions, and intervention decisions are deterministic, so the output is a replayable and auditable trust trajectory rather than a single end-to-end judge verdict. We construct a cross-domain trajectory corpus from OSWorld, FinanceBench, and EICU-AC, covering benign executions, prefix-paired drift, and pseudo-consistency failures. On this corpus, RGE outperforms adapted rule-, judge-, and shield-style baselines on prefix-paired drift detection. With the two larger estimator models, it exceeds 93% Drift F1 on every benchmark while keeping benign coverage at or above 95.8%. Pseudo-consistency is harder: detection depends on whether task completion is externally visible, a structural limit we characterize empirically.

</details>


### [254] [Evaluating the Diversity of AI-Generated Content with Diversity Profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17731)
*Xiuyuan Hu,Xuege Hou,Guoqing Liu,Yang Zhao,Jieran Li,Dongbiao Sun,José Miguel Hernández-Lobato,Hao Zhang,Xue Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes diversity profiles, a curve-valued, condition-aware approach to evaluating AI-generated content diversity, addressing scalar metric biases and under-specification by using parameterized thresholds, scales, exponents, or orders across representations/distances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scalar diversity metrics for AI-generated content encode different biases and can yield contradictory rankings; high-dimensional embedding spaces exacerbate distance concentration and modality effects, making single-number summaries under-specified. A more transparent, resolution-aware framework is needed to robustly compare diversity across samples and settings.

Method: 1) Axomatic analysis showing no scalar metric can satisfy all desirable properties simultaneously. 2) Empirical analysis showing distance distributions in embeddings are concentrated and modality-dependent. 3) Introduce diversity profiles: curve-valued summaries that evaluate a parameterized diversity family across thresholds, scales, exponents, or orders for a given representation and distance/kernel. 4) Instantiate profiles for multiple metric families and demonstrate practical use in generative AI evaluation.

Result: Diversity profiles reveal robustness or dependence on parameter choices across resolutions, enabling more nuanced comparisons than single-number scores. Providing a transparent framework for evaluating diversity across conditions and metric families, with empirical demonstrations across several metrics.

Conclusion: Diversity profiles offer a transparent, resolution-aware framework that helps compare AI-generated content diversity more robustly than traditional scalar metrics, by exposing how conclusions may vary with parameter choices and representation.

Abstract: Diversity is a fundamental criterion for evaluating generative artificial intelligence (AI) systems, yet its measurement remains inherently ambiguous. Existing approaches typically represent generated samples in an embedding space, compute pairwise distances or similarities, and aggregate them into a single scalar score. Such scalar summaries are convenient, but they often encode different inductive biases and may yield contradictory rankings of the same sample sets. In this paper, we argue that diversity evaluation for AI-generated content is intrinsically under-specified when reduced to a single number. We first review representative diversity metrics, and then diagnose their limitations from two complementary perspectives: an axiomatic analysis showing that no representative scalar metric satisfies all desirable properties simultaneously, and an empirical analysis showing that high-dimensional representation spaces can induce concentrated, modality-dependent distance distributions. To address these issues, we propose diversity profiles: curve-valued, condition-aware summaries that evaluate a parameterized diversity family across a range of thresholds, scales, exponents, or orders under a specified representation and distance or kernel function. Diversity profiles reveal whether a comparison is robust across resolutions or instead depends on an arbitrary parameter choice. We instantiate profiles for several representative metric families and demonstrate their practical use in generative AI evaluation. Overall, diversity profiles provide a more transparent and resolution-aware framework for comparing the diversity of AI-generated content.

</details>


### [255] [Neuro-symbolic learning over OWL 2 DL via consequence-based compilation to differentiable circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17741)
*Olga Mashkova,Asaad Mohammedsaleh,Fernando Zhapa-Camacho,Robert Hoehndorf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Baobab compiles SROIQ ontologies with finite ABoxes into SDDs to enable Bayes-optimal neuro-symbolic learning, addressing reasoning shortcuts in non-Horn DLs; soundness verified in Lean 4; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current neuro-symbolic approaches for description logics either embed ontologies in continuous space, losing entailment, or restrict to the Horn fragment EL++, which omits rich DL features. There is a need to retain expressive DL capabilities while enabling perception-based learning.

Method: Saturate a propositional core under a consequence-based calculus to obtain a Sentential Decision Diagram (SDD); instantiate remaining SROIQ features (nominals, number restrictions, role axioms) over the active domain; train a perception network via the SDD's evidence-conditioned weighted model count (WMC) on real images under partial ABox supervision; handle multiple ontology-consistent completions by mixing justifications, seeded from enumerated completions to approximate Bayes posterior; compare against single-WMC and BEARS ensembles.

Result: On an ontology exercising distinctive SROIQ features, a CNN learns to read MNIST digits linked by a successor relation and recovers latent ontology concepts; mixtures over justifications can match calibrated posteriors beyond what independent perception or BEARS achieve; demonstrates mitigation of reasoning shortcuts in non-Horn DLs; Lean 4 machine-checked soundness; code available at the authors' repository.

Conclusion: Provides a sound compilation and representation framework enabling expressive DL reasoning within NeSy learning; shows practical Bayes-optimal posterior attainment in a non-Horn DL setting; first characterization and mitigation of reasoning shortcuts in non-Horn DLs.

Abstract: OWL 2 DL ontologies, grounded in the description logic $\mathcal{SROIQ}$, express large knowledge bases in biomedicine and the Semantic Web. Neuro-symbolic (NeSy) learners over description logics either embed the ontology in a continuous space, abandoning classical entailment, or restrict to the Horn fragment $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$, which has a single canonical model. We present Baobab, which compiles a $\mathcal{SROIQ}$ ontology with a finite ABox into a Sentential Decision Diagram (SDD): it saturates a propositional core under a consequence-based calculus and instantiates the remaining $\mathcal{SROIQ}$ features (nominals, number restrictions, and the role axioms) over the active domain. The SDD's evidence-conditioned weighted model count then trains a perception network to recognize real images under partial ABox supervision: on an ontology that exercises every distinctive $\mathcal{SROIQ}$ feature, a CNN learns to read MNIST digits coupled by a successor relation and recovers latent ontology concepts that an independent perception leaves at chance. When the supervision admits several ontology-consistent completions, an independent perception collapses onto one, a reasoning shortcut: we show that a mixture indexed by the query's justifications can represent the calibrated posterior no independent perception can, and that seeding it from the circuit's enumerated completions attains the Bayes-optimal posterior on a real-image MNIST task where single-WMC and learned mixtures (the BEARS-ensemble hypothesis class) do not: to our knowledge the first to characterize and mitigate reasoning shortcuts in a non-Horn description logic. Soundness of the compiler and the representation result are machine-checked in Lean 4. Code is available at https://github.com/bio-ontology-research-group/baobab.

</details>


### [256] [The Curious Case of Exploding DecPOMDPs: Containing the Fire through Policy Counting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17749)
*Nazlı Nur Karabulut,tanya Braun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Counting policies in DecPOMDPs enables tractable planning for multi-agent systems by exploiting policy symmetry, leading to a policy-counted dynamic programming approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DecPOMDPs exhibit exponential complexity in the number of agents. Symmetry among agents can be exploited, but counting agents keeps policy spaces large. Shifting focus to counting policies aims to restore tractability as the number of agents grows.

Method: Introduce policy-counted DecPOMDPs with a compact representation based on policy counts. Develop policy-counted dynamic programming that operates on this compact representation to solve DecPOMDPs efficiently.

Result: The approach reduces both model complexity and evaluation cost to a polynomial dependence on the number of agents, enabling tractable planning for large agent sets via the policy-counted representation and dynamic programming.

Conclusion: Counting policies, rather than agents, yields tractable planning in DecPOMDPs and can be effectively solved with policy-counted dynamic programming using the compact representation.

Abstract: Decentralised partially observable Markov decision processes (DecPOMDPs) provide a general framework for modelling multi-agent decision making under uncertainty. However, DecPOMDPs are known to suffer from exponential complexity in the number of agents. One way to combat this intractability in agent numbers is to look at partitions of agents that exhibit a form of symmetry among agents, allowing for a compact encoding by counting. However, a challenge arises as the policy space explodes, even though the model complexity and evaluation cost reduce to a polynomial dependence. In this paper, we redirect our focus from counting agents to counting policies, which actually enables tractability in agent numbers for so called policy-counted DecPOMDPs. Further, we present policy-counted dynamic programming using the compact representation to solve policy-counted DecPOMDPs efficiently.

</details>


### [257] [D$^2$ACCI: A Dual-Loop Diagnostic Protocol for Evidence-Preserving Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17756)
*Xule Liu,Yijun Liu,Chao Li,Shao Kun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A diagnostic-driven dual-loop protocol D^2ACCI with graded observability (DCR) and reusable artifacts (D^2ACCI-Eval) to localize, gate, and improve memory-system interventions in LLM agents, evaluated in MemStack with strong traceability and regression-aware evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end memory pipelines hinder localization of failures; existing evaluations lack paired statistics, slice-level checks, and traceable diagnostics.

Method: Introduce outer diagnostic gate and inner closed-loop with feature flags; protected-slice monitoring; trace-level localizability; DCR metric; D^2ACCI-Eval for gate replay; instantiate in MemStack; ablation studies.

Result: Benchmarks LoCoMo 93.59%, LongMemEval 90.93%, PersonaMem-V2 57.20%; five paired ablations show gains +1.9 to +3.7pp (p <= .003); BM25/RRF used as monitored flag; diagnostic traces improve root-cause agreement; diagnostic artifacts achieve DCR@3 98-100% vs 0% for results-only logs.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that robust, traceable, regression-aware memory-system iteration is achievable; D^2ACCI provides a practical protocol and artifacts to fill gaps in end-to-end evaluation; emphasizes localization and statistical grounding.

Abstract: Memory is a key capability of LLM agents. Persistent memory extends this across sessions---enabling recall, revision, and personalization. Yet its multi-stage pipeline (ingestion, retrieval, filtering, generation) makes failures difficult to localize: end-to-end evaluation reveals that an error occurred, but not which stage caused it. Existing evaluations often report aggregate performance without paired statistical comparisons, slice-level non-regression checks, or stage-level diagnostic traces. We propose D$^2$ACCI (Diagnostic-Driven Artifact-based Closed-loop Controlled Iteration), a dual-loop protocol whose outer diagnostic gate promotes, feature-flags, or rejects memory interventions based on paired evidence, protected-slice monitoring, and trace-level localizability. We further introduce DCR, a graded observability metric that measures whether failures remain localizable, and D$^2$ACCI-Eval, a reusable artifact for gate replay. We instantiate the protocol in MemStack and evaluate on three public benchmarks, achieving 93.59% on LoCoMo, 90.93% on LongMemEval, and 57.20% on PersonaMem-V2. Five paired ablations show that supplement extraction, session-memory retrieval, and Forget Guard yield statistically significant gains (+1.9 to +3.7pp, all p $\le$ .003). In contrast, BM25/RRF is retained as a monitored feature flag---a distinction invisible to aggregate-only evaluation. A diagnostic audit shows enriched traces substantially improve root-cause agreement over result-only relabeling. Diagnostic artifacts reach 98--100% DCR@3 versus 0% for results-only logs. These results establish that robust memory-system iteration demands traceable, statistically grounded, and regression-aware evidence---exactly the gap D$^2$ACCI fills.

</details>


### [258] [StartupBench: Benchmarking General-Purpose Agents on Market-Validated End-to-End Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17800)
*Liya Zhu,Xin Ma,Tao Liu,Haodong Wang,Ge Zhang,Jingzhe Ding,Qingshui Gu,Yongjie Zhong,Jinxiang Meng,Yuan Gao,Yunqiu Zhou,Hao Zhu,Jifeng He,Yongzhi Liao,Xinyi Zhang,Chaoxin Li,Yi Zhu,Xi Lin,Duju Zeng,Xiang Gao,Wen Zhang,Yunyang Wang,Duo Wang,Huan Zhou,Zuo Wang,Jin Chen,Kaiyuan Zhang,Chuqian Yu,Tianhao Yu,Longxiang Liu,Jianbo Xue,Huimin Che,Jiahao Wang,Yujia Qin,Jiaheng Liu,Shen Yan,Xiaolong Chang,Wenhao Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StartupBench defines an end-to-end agent benchmark built from market-validated AI startup workflows to measure real-world task completion by LLMs/agents; strongest models complete ~30% of tasks, with much partial progress, revealing gaps in complex instruction following and domain expertise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether advances in LLMs and agents translate to real-world user tasks beyond researcher-selected benchmarks by grounding evaluation in market-validated products and workflows.

Method: Translate adopted startup product workflows into complete deliverable-oriented tasks; evaluate across models using a unified agent harness and fine-grained rubrics that capture complex requirements.

Result: Across representative models, the strongest model completes only about 30% of StartupBench tasks, despite substantial partial progress on many; failures are linked to complex instruction following and domain-specific expertise.

Conclusion: StartupBench provides an empirical progress measure for end-to-end completion of real-world user tasks and highlights remaining gaps in current general-purpose agents.

Abstract: Recent advances in Large Language Models(LLMs) and agents have substantially improved the ability of AI systems to execute complex tasks. Yet existing benchmarks largely rely on researcher-selected tasks, leaving uncertain whether such progress extends to the work that real-world users actually demand from AI systems. We introduce \textbf{StartupBench}, an E2E agent benchmark grounded in market-validated AI startup products. Rather than defining tasks from pre-defined assumptions about useful agent capabilities, we systematically study AI products with demonstrated adoption, together with their product workflows and users, to identify real-world tasks for which AI has established practical demand across diverse professional domains. We translate these workflows into complete deliverable-oriented tasks and evaluate them with fine-grained rubrics capturing their complex requirements. Across representative models evaluated under a unified agent harness, even the strongest model successfully completes only approximately 30\% of StartupBench, despite making substantial partial progress on many tasks. Further analysis identifies aspects like complex instruction following and domain-specific expertise as major sources of failure. Our results reveal that many market-validated workflows remain beyond the reliable capabilities of current general-purpose agents, establishing StartupBench as an empirical measure of progress toward E2E completions of real-world user tasks.

</details>


### [259] [ARASH: Adaptive Retrieval And Shot Selection for Tabular Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17856)
*Samirasadat Jamalidinan,Yue Xu,Kazem Cheshmi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARASH: adaptive query-specific retrieval and shot selection for tabular foundation models to improve in-context learning efficiency; achieves substantial reduction in prompt length and memory with accuracy comparable to TabPFN.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TFMs are powerful but resource-intensive and impractical to retrain frequently; few-shot prompting offers a resource-efficient alternative, but selecting relevant shots for tabular data is challenging.

Method: ARASH uses local neighborhood analysis within the training set to perform adaptive, query-specific retrieval and shot selection to craft a compact, relevant prompt for TFMs.

Result: Significant efficiency gains: prompt length reduced by 1261.5x and memory usage by 2.56x, while maintaining comparable accuracy to TabPFN.

Conclusion: ARASH provides a practical, data-driven approach to optimize in-context learning for tabular foundation models, enabling resource-efficient deployment without sacrificing performance.

Abstract: Tabular prediction is a critical task across numerous applications. The recent success of large language models has sparked various approaches for adapting them to the tabular domain. A prevalent strategy involves training or fine-tuning specialized Tabular Foundation Models (TFMs) such as TabPFN. However, TFMs require substantial computational resources, and frequent model retraining is often impractical. In-context learning (ICL), specifically, few-shot prompting, offers a resource-efficient alternative to enhance performance. Yet, identifying the most relevant rows to serve as shots remains a challenge for tabular data. This paper introduces ARASH (Adaptive, query-specific Retrieval And Shot selection), a method that improves TFM efficiency by selecting optimal shots based on local neighborhood analysis within the training set. Our results demonstrate that ARASH reduces the prompt length and memory usage of TabPFN by 1261.5$\times$ and 2.56$\times$, respectively, while providing comparable accuracy.

</details>


### [260] [AutoResearch: Insight In, Hallucination Out](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17906)
*Yiming Ren,Xiang Liu,Qumeng Sun,Xiao Zhang,Jiahao Li,Haoyang Zhang,Junjie Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A two-stage autonomous research framework (Idea Generation + Idea Execution) that grounds ideas before experiments and conclusions before acceptance, improving reliability and progress across benchmarks; demonstrated lower audit events and improved recall on RSICD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To close the gap between automation and scientific grounding in long research workflows, by ensuring ideas are mechanistically grounded and conclusions are evidence-based.

Method: Idea Generation: continuous integration of emerging signals with domain knowledge, extraction of transferable mechanistic insights, multi-model generation and cross-review to produce grounded, testable research plans. Idea Execution: coordinated agents decompose plans into experiments, iterative implementation/diagnosis, and independent evidence-based review before accepting conclusions. Evaluation across cross-modal retrieval, systems optimization, and benchmark-driven ML.

Result: On RSICD benchmark, AutoResearch raises mean Recall from 32.84 to 34.69 and records only 5 audit-confirmed issue events versus 11–27 for comparable autonomous systems.

Conclusion: Insight In, Hallucination Out—meaningful insights are grounded before experimentation and conclusions are grounded before acceptance.

Abstract: Autonomous research systems are increasingly capable of executing long research workflows, yet automation alone does not ensure that the resulting process remains scientifically grounded. We introduce AutoResearch, a two-stage system that connects Idea Generation with Idea Execution to address both how research ideas are formed and how they are reliably established through experimentation. In Idea Generation, AutoResearch continuously integrates emerging research signals with accumulated domain knowledge, identifies transferable mechanistic insights, and uses multi-model generation and cross-review to produce grounded, testable research plans. In Idea Execution, coordinated agents decompose these plans into experiments, iteratively implement and diagnose them, and employ independent evidence-based review before accepting research conclusions. Across representative settings in cross-modal retrieval, systems optimization, and benchmark-driven machine learning, AutoResearch turns generated ideas into measurable progress, detects and corrects unreliable experimental results, and makes evidence-conditioned decisions to continue, revise, or terminate research directions. For example, on RSICD benchmark, an AutoResearch-generated idea improves mean Recall from 32.84 to 34.69, while recording only 5 audit-confirmed issue events compared with 11-27 for other autonomous research systems. These results demonstrate a research process in which meaningful insight is grounded before experimentation and conclusions are grounded before acceptance: Insight In, Hallucination Out.

</details>


### [261] [Adaptive Policy Portfolios for Robust Markov Decision Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17929)
*Kasper Engelen,Sebastian Junges,Guillermo A. Pérez,Marnix Suilen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies robust MDPs with adaptive policy portfolios—finite offline-synthesized, online selector-based—and provides complexity characterizations for certifying and synthesizing portfolios, showing high (∀R and ∃∀R) hardness even for simple cases, plus an offline construction approach suited for runtime specialization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce conservatism in robust MDPs by adaptively selecting among a small portfolio of policies after deployment; quantify the computational hardness of certifying and constructing such portfolios.

Method: Offline synthesis of a finite set of memoryless randomized policies; lightweight online selector to choose among them; formal complexity results (∀R-completeness, ∃∀R-completeness) for portfolio certification and synthesis; discussion of an offline portfolio construction method compatible with runtime specialization.

Result: Certification of a portfolio is ∀R-complete even for deterministic portfolios in acyclic (s,a)-rectangular RMDPs. Synthesis of a portfolio of unary-bounded size is ∃∀R-complete for general rational polytopes, even with fixed discount and acyclic dynamics. The single-policy case is itself hard. An offline portfolio construction is proposed that supports runtime specialization.

Conclusion: Portfolio certification and synthesis in robust MDPs are highly intractable in general, even for simplified models; nonetheless, the authors provide an offline construction approach that can be specialized at runtime, indicating a viable path for practical implementations despite the hardness results.

Abstract: Robust Markov decision processes optimize one policy against a set of plausible transition functions. This can be conservative when the unknown dynamics are fixed and become partially identifiable after deployment. We study adaptive policy portfolios: finite sets of memoryless randomized policies synthesized offline and paired with a lightweight online selector. Robust regret is a natural measure of portfolio quality: for each plausible environment, it measures the loss of the best portfolio member relative to the policy that would have been optimal had that environment been known. Related regret objectives were studied by Ghavamzadeh et al. (2016) with an emphasis on approximations and relaxations for safe policy improvement. We give a complexity-theoretic account of portfolio certification and synthesis. Certifying a given portfolio is $\forall\mathbb{R}$-complete already for deterministic portfolios in acyclic (s,a)-rectangular RMDPs. Synthesizing a portfolio of unary-bounded size is $\exists\forall\mathbb{R}$-complete for general rational polytopes, even with fixed discount and acyclic dynamics. The single-policy case is already hard, both combinatorially and algebraically. Finally, we present an offline portfolio construction that is amenable to runtime specialization.

</details>


### [262] [EvoTS-Agent: A Self-Evolving LLM Agent for Financial Time Series Change Point Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17933)
*Lei Jiang,Ye Wei,Xinyu Xi,Jordan Langham-Lopez,Yifan Bao,Raad Khraishi,Yihao Ang,Anthony K. H. Tung,Lukasz Szpruch,Hao Ni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A self-evolving LLM-based agent (EvoTS-Agent) for autonomous financial time-series change-point detection that uses validation-guided evolutionary operators (Revision, Alternative Strategy, Recombination) to adapt detection pipelines across datasets, achieving superior performance to existing LLM-based agents with 100% execution success across backbone LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial time series are non-stationary and heterogeneous, making change-point detection difficult. No single unsupervised method is robust across assets or regimes, so automated, adaptive pipelines are needed to reduce reliance on expert-driven tuning and improve scalability.

Method: An LLM-driven agent starts with curated exploratory data analysis to characterize data and initialize candidate detectors. It then evolves experiment trajectories via three operators: Revision (refines current best solution), Alternative Strategy (tries fundamentally different modeling directions when progress stalls), and Recombination (synthesizes evidence from high-performing trajectories). Validation feedback continuously guides trajectory evolution, enabling adaptation to dataset-specific statistics while preserving optimization reliability.

Result: Empirical evaluation on four benchmark datasets shows EvoTS-Agent consistently outperforms existing LLM-based agents and achieves 100% execution success across all evaluated backbone LLMs.

Conclusion: The approach provides an adaptive, scalable framework for autonomous CPD in financial time series, leveraging validation-guided evolution of detection pipelines. Important considerations include generalization to unseen regimes, explicit performance metrics, and computational overhead of the evolutionary loop.

Abstract: Financial time series exhibit non-stationary and heterogeneous statistical properties, making change-point detection challenging because no single unsupervised algorithm performs consistently across assets and market regimes. Conventional workflows consequently depend heavily on expert-driven model selection, feature design, and hyperparameter tuning, limiting their scalability and adaptability. We propose EvoTS-Agent, a validation-guided self-evolving LLM agent for autonomous financial time-series change-point detection. EvoTS-Agent first performs curated exploratory data analysis to characterize dataset properties and initialize candidate detection models. It then evolves executable experiment trajectories through three complementary operators: \textit{Revision} exploits the current best solution, \textit{Alternative Strategy} explores fundamentally different modeling directions when progress stagnates, and \textit{Recombination} synthesizes complementary evidence from high-performing trajectories. Validation feedback guides trajectory evolution throughout the search, enabling the agent to adapt its detection pipeline to the statistical characteristics of each dataset while preserving reliable optimization. Experiments across four benchmark datasets demonstrate that EvoTS-Agent consistently outperforms existing LLM-based agents while maintaining a 100\% execution success rate across all evaluated backbone LLMs.

</details>


### [263] [Procedural Content Metageneration via Program Search and Continual Abstraction Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17947)
*Matthew Siper,Ahmed Khalifa,Julian Togelius*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAD improves evolutionary search for procedural content generators by discovering reusable primitives from high-fitness programs and placing them into a run-specific library; demonstrated across Sokoban, Zelda, Dangerous Dave, and Lode Runner with LLM-driven mutation/crossover on Python generators; 160 runs show consistent gains and library reuse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance search efficiency in program-space exploration by reusing abstractions and utilities extracted from successful generators, enabling faster discovery of functional content across multiple domains.

Method: Evolve complete Python generators using language-model mutation and crossover. Introduce Continual Abstraction Discovery (CAD) to extract high-fitness primitives into a run-specific helper module. Use a 2x2 experimental design comparing CAD with/without a fixed hand-written domain API. Build a dataset of 160 complete runs, with at least ten 50-generation runs per cell.

Result: CAD raises mean final best fitness in all eight domain/API comparisons. Across CAD runs, learned libraries are adopted by most later programs and consistently rediscover validation, reachability, and structural utilities.

Conclusion: Discovering reusable primitives via CAD improves evolutionary program search for content generators, with libraries that are broadly reused and continually expanded across generations, suggesting generalizable benefits for evolved code-based generators.

Abstract: Large language models can generate executable programs, which makes it possible to search directly over procedural content generators rather than individual levels. We study this approach in Sokoban, Zelda, Dangerous Dave, and Lode Runner. Each run evolves complete Python generators through language-model mutation and crossover. We introduce Continual Abstraction Discovery, or CAD, which extracts reusable primitives from high-fitness programs into a run-specific helper module. A 2x2 experiment crosses CAD with access to a fixed hand-written domain API. The completed data set contains 160 complete runs, with at least ten 50-generation runs in every cell. CAD raises mean final best fitness in all eight domain and API comparisons. Across all CAD runs, learned libraries are adopted by most later programs and repeatedly rediscover validation, reachability, and structural utilities. These results support that discovering reusable primitives improves evolutionary program search for content generators.

</details>


### [264] [Towards Zero-Shot Task Transfer with Neurosymbolic World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17959)
*Isidoro Tamassia,Lennert De Smet,Giuseppe Marra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Neurosymbolic world models decouple observation reconstruction from reward prediction using structured latent components, enabling zero-shot generalization to new reward functions defined over the same symbolic state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome poor generalization of task-specific neural latent representations in model-based RL; enable zero-shot adaptation to unseen reward functions without additional environment interactions.

Method: Formulate a world model where reward depends only on a subset of structured, symbolic latent components; separate observation reconstruction from reward prediction; learn and utilize a symbolic latent state for planning, enabling neurosymbolic interpretation.

Result: Discusses advantages and challenges; provides empirical or conceptual evidence that this approach generalizes better than purely neural methods to new reward functions defined over the same symbolic state space.

Conclusion: Neurosymbolic world models can improve generalization and interpretability in model-based RL, enabling zero-shot adaptation to new rewards, but introduce complexity and design challenges.

Abstract: State-of-the-art model-based reinforcement learning methods learn neural world models that allow policy improvement by planning in a latent space, without assumptions on the structure of the underlying environment. While expressive, these models are generally task-dependent: they learn uninterpretable latent representations that are tied to the training task and thus hard to generalize to new tasks. In this work, we present a novel world model formulation where the reward prediction only depends on a subset of structured, symbolic components of the whole latent state. Decoupling observation reconstruction and reward prediction allows us to learn world models that can adapt zero-shot, i.e. without further environment interactions, to new reward functions defined over the same symbolic state space. We discuss the main advantages and challenges of learning these neurosymbolic world models and demonstrate the strong generalisation properties of our approach over purely neural methods.

</details>


### [265] [Can Large Language Models Explain Flight Safety Events? A Prior-Guided Semantic LLM-based Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18017)
*Lu Xu,Xu Li,Linjiang Zheng,Fan Li,Riquan Zhang,Jiaxing Shang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlightLLM proposes a prior-guided semantic LLM framework for interpretable flight safety analysis, combining feature engineering, semantic discretization, CatBoost as a classifier, contrastive few-shot learning, and structured aviation prompts; evaluated on 704 real-world A320 flights with hard landings; achieves competitive accuracy and provides interpretable event-cause explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explainable flight safety analysis requires not only detecting risk events but also interpretable, domain-aligned causal explanations. Traditional explainability (feature importance) is hard to translate into operational insights; LLMs offer language reasoning but face modal inconsistency, limited data, and domain knowledge gaps. A prior-guided, semantically discretized LLM framework can bridge these gaps.

Method: 1) Feature engineering to address modal inconsistency by combining statistical descriptors with physically meaningful flight indicators; 2) Semantic Discretization to translate numerical patterns into qualitative, language-friendly descriptions; 3) CatBoost as a statistical expert injected into prompts as prior guidance; 4) Contrastive few-shot learning to cope with limited data; 5) Structured prompts embedding aviation knowledge; 6) Evaluation using hard-landing as anchor on 704 real-world A320 samples.

Result: The approach achieves competitive classification performance while generating direct, reasonable explanations for event causes, demonstrating feasibility of interpretable flight safety analysis with FlightLLM on a real-world dataset.

Conclusion: FlightLLM demonstrates a viable, interpretable, prior-guided integration of LLM reasoning with traditional ML and aviation-domain knowledge, addressing modal inconsistency and data scarcity to produce both accurate risk classification and actionable explanations for pilot-control-level causes.

Abstract: Improving flight safety with flight data requires not only accurate detection of risk events, but more importantly, clear interpretation of their underlying causes at the level of pilot control behavior. Existing explainable AI techniques, such as feature importance maps, often require considerable domain knowledge to translate them into operationally meaningful explanations. Large Language Models (LLMs), which excel at language reasoning, bring a promising solution to this issue. However, applying LLMs in this domain presents key challenges such as modal inconsistency, limited classification ability, scarcity of task-specific data for fine-tuning, and lack of domain knowledge. To overcome these challenges, we propose FlightLLM, a prior-guided semantic LLM-based approach for interpretable flight safety analysis. Specifically, we first perform feature engineering to address modal inconsistency, combining statistical descriptors with physically meaningful flight indicators. This representation is further processed by a Semantic Discretization module, which converts abstract numerical patterns into qualitative descriptions that are more compatible with language reasoning. In addition, since LLMs are not inherently strong classifiers, CatBoost is incorporated as a statistical expert, and its prediction results are injected into the prompt as prior guidance. A contrastive few-shot learning strategy is further adopted to compensate for limited data. Finally, we design structured prompts to embed aviation-specific knowledge into the inference process. Using hard landing, a representative risk event with complex causal mechanisms, as an anchor point, we evaluate FlightLLM on a dataset of 704 real-world A320 flight samples. Experimental results show that the proposed approach achieves competitive classification performance while generating direct and reasonable explanations for event causes.

</details>


### [266] [StagedWorkspace: A Versioned Workspace for Knowledge-Work Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18050)
*Yining Hua,Hongbin Na,Yifan Zhou,Akshay Kalose,Cyrus Ayubcha,Levi Lian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes StagedWorkspace, a versioned workspace that ties parsed records and diffs to content hashes of native files, enabling explicit workspace-state tracking for knowledge-work agents; dual parsed/native access yields consistent performance gains; results across OfficeQA/APEX show notable improvements; advocates benchmarks that treat evidence, staged edits, and artifacts as explicit state transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge-work agents operate on diverse artifacts (PDFs, spreadsheets, slides, code, notebooks) whose versions diverge across views and edits. Existing repository-style contracts cover code but not non-code artifacts, leading to implicit state ambiguities. A formal workspace-state contract is needed to align views, edits, and submissions with evolving content.

Method: Introduce StagedWorkspace, a versioned workspace that binds parsed records and review diffs to content hashes of native files as they change. Conduct fixed-harness ablations on OfficeQA Pro and APEX-Agents to compare dual parsed/native access versus single-view approaches. Evaluate performance with model variants (e.g., SW-AGENT with Gemini 3.1 Pro and GPT-5.4 Nano on respective tasks) and perform a paired review-axis ablation on 57 file-editing tasks to assess the impact of visible diffs.

Result: Dual parsed/native access produced the highest scores across tested models: OfficeQA Pass@1 gains of 8.3–12.1 points and APEX mean rubric gains of 4.7–9.2 points relative to single-view baselines. SW-AGENT achieved 63.9% (Gemini 3.1 Pro on OfficeQA) and 42.1% (GPT-5.4 Nano on APEX) versus published same-model scores of 29.3% and 25.5. Visible diffs in the paired tasks further improved observed scores. 

Conclusion: Workspace state should be treated as an experimental variable in knowledge-work agents. The work motivates benchmarks that score evidence, staged edits, and submitted artifacts as explicit state transitions, enabling more reliable evaluation and control of evolving artifacts.

Abstract: AI agents increasingly perform knowledge work (i.e., produce and modify persistent digital artifacts such as code repositories, documents, spreadsheets, slides, reports), yet the parsed views they search, the native files they edit, the changes they review, and the artifacts they submit can refer to different versions of the same work product. We formulate this as a workspace-state contract: every view should be explicitly tied to a version of the evolving workspace state. Coding agents partly address this need through repository contracts for search, diffs, and tests, whereas an analogous contract is less explicit for PDFs, spreadsheets, slides, notebooks, and mixed-format project folders. We propose StagedWorkspace, a versioned workspace for knowledge-work agents. The workspace binds parsed records and review diffs to content hashes of the native files as they change. In fixed-harness ablations on OfficeQA Pro and APEX-Agents, dual parsed/native access has the highest point estimate for every tested model; relative to the more limiting single view, it improves OfficeQA Pass@1 by 8.3-12.1 points and APEX mean rubric score by 4.7-9.2 points. SW-AGENT scores 63.9% with Gemini 3.1 Pro on OfficeQA and 42.1 with GPT-5.4 Nano on APEX, compared with published same-model scores of 29.3% and 25.5, respectively. A paired review-axis ablation on 57 file-editing tasks further finds higher observed scores when diffs are visible. These results identify workspace state as an experimental variable in knowledge-work agents and motivate benchmarks that score evidence, staged edits, and submitted artifacts as explicit state transitions.

</details>


### [267] [HLSR: Hybrid Live Forecast Selective Dynamic Vehicle Rerouting for Real-Time Congestion Avoidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18056)
*Xiao Wang,Shun Ren Yang,Hui Nien Hung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HLSR is a selective hybrid live–forecast rerouting framework that reduces intervention scope by fusing live edge speeds with short-horizon forecasts, guided by dual-threshold congestion detection and upstream selection, with driver-tailored prediction and several innovations for scalable route planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Network-wide live rerouting is effective but impractical due to near-universal replanning in each decision period; a scalable approach with limited interventions is needed while leveraging forecasts.

Method: A hybrid framework that combines live edge speeds with short-horizon forecasts under limited interventions. Key components include dual-threshold congestion detection, calibrated upstream selection, driver-tailored travel-time prediction, approaching-vehicle expansion, travel-time-weighted k-shortest-path generation, and horizon-dependent hybrid segment speeds for multi-cost route allocation.

Result: The abstract does not provide quantitative results. It outlines the framework and its components, implying feasibility and potential performance gains via selective rerouting in simulations or networks, but no specific metrics are reported.

Conclusion: HLSR offers a scalable, selective alternative to full network-wide rerouting by integrating live and forecast information and targeted interventions; further empirical validation and performance evaluation are anticipated.

Abstract: Urban traffic congestion reduces productivity and increases travel cost and emissions. Network-wide live travel-time shortest-path rerouting can be highly effective in simulation, but assumes that essentially every on-road vehicle is replanned every decision period. We propose HLSR, a selective hybrid live--forecast vehicle rerouting framework that fuses live edge speeds with short-horizon forecasts under limited intervention scope. Building on dual-threshold congestion detection, calibrated upstream selection, and driver-tailored travel-time prediction, HLSR further introduces approaching-vehicle expansion, travel-time-weighted k-shortest-path generation, and a horizon-dependent hybrid live--forecast segment speed used in multi-cost route allocation.

</details>


### [268] [Delegation Asymmetry in Agentic Recommender Systems: Measuring Two-Sided Receptivity in Online Dating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18058)
*Daria Leshchikova,Valentina V. Kuskova,Dmitry Zaytsev,Valerii Klimov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agent receptivity for autonomous LLM agents in dating platforms comprises two distinct but highly correlated constructs (send vs receive) with a deployment-readiness asymmetry and strong reciprocity constraints, implying limited agent-mediated interactions and important design implications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate user acceptance and feasibility of agent-mediated communication in dating platforms by measuring receptivity to deploying an agent and to receiving agent-driven messages, and to quantify the effects of reciprocity and other design levers.

Method: Two large-scale surveys (N=2,894 on generative profile features; N=2,617 on autonomous agents) across two languages; latent-variable measurement model with graded response models and latent regression; model comparison; counterfactual simulations; cross-validation; AUC reported (0.88).

Result: Willingness to send and to receive are distinct yet highly correlated constructs (rho=0.92) with partial measurement invariance across languages (Delta BIC=52). Deployment receptivity threshold = -0.38; reception threshold = +0.32; engagement threshold = +1.39; mean deployment propensity ~3× mean engagement propensity. Under random-pairing counterfactual, only 4–13% of directed dyads align deployment with receiver engagement; significant gender-directional imbalance. Reciprocity constraints halve interactions; routing recipient receptivity triples per-contact engagement; results robust in out-of-sample validation (AUC 0.88).

Conclusion: Design implications emphasize disclosure, opt-in mechanics, and receptivity-aware matchmaking; to scale agent-mediated interactions, systems must address asymmetric receptivity and reciprocity requirements.

Abstract: Autonomous LLM agents that converse on a user's behalf are an emerging design pattern in matching platforms, yet their viability depends on a condition rarely examined: users must accept not only delegating conversation to an agent, but also receiving agent-mediated communication from others. We study this condition using two large-scale surveys of active users of a major dating platform (N=2,894 on generative profile features; N=2,617 on autonomous conversational agents, fielded in two languages). We develop a latent-variable measurement model of agent receptivity based on graded response models with latent regression, and show via model comparison that willingness to send and willingness to receive agent communication are distinct constructs: highly correlated (rho=0.92) but separable (Delta BIC=52), with partial measurement invariance across languages. The model quantifies a systematic delegation asymmetry: deploying one's own agent requires far lower receptivity (threshold -0.38) than engaging a counterpart's agent (+0.32; full engagement +1.39), and mean deployment propensity exceeds engagement propensity roughly threefold. Under a random-pairing counterfactual derived from stated receptivity, only 4-13% of directed dyads combine agent deployment with receiver engagement, with a pronounced gender-directional imbalance. Design counterfactuals quantify the levers: a reciprocity requirement cuts interaction volume by half or more by excluding nearly two-thirds of would-be deployment, while routing agent contacts on receive receptivity triples per-contact engagement, a lift that survives out-of-sample validation with the target item held out (AUC 0.88, 3.1x quartile lift under respondent-level cross-validation). We discuss implications for agentic recommender design, including disclosure, opt-in mechanics, and receptivity-aware matchmaking.

</details>


### [269] [On the Fragility of Self-Improving Agents: Variance, Task Order, and Underspecification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18066)
*Qinyuan Ye,Yu Li,Yada Pruksachatkun,Jiaxin Zhang,Chien-Sheng Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Memory-based self-improving agents show fragile reliability: variance across runs and strong dependence on task order undermine robustness; adding richer memory cues helps but does not fully solve fragility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reassess robustness of memory-based self-improving agents by expanding evaluation to multiple runs and random task order, probing underlying causes such as underspecification.

Method: Conduct multi-run experiments with shuffled task sequences; manually inspect memory; hypothesize underspecification; augment memory with detailed rubrics and environment feedback to improve specification and re-evaluate.

Result: Agent evaluation is inherently noisy in complex/multi-step tasks; improvement is highly dependent on task order; richer memory content partially alleviates degradation but large gaps remain; fragility persists.

Conclusion: Advocate for rigorous, multi-run evaluation and stress-testing; emphasize the need for human oversight interfaces to manage underspecification and prevent unforeseeable agent failures.

Abstract: Memory-based self-improving agents--those that learn from an online stream of tasks and improve over time by maintaining a textual memory bank--have shown great promise in recent literature. However, the reliability aspects of these methods have been critically overlooked. In this work, we conduct a comprehensive re-evaluation of two memory-based methods, broadening the scope of evaluation along two axes: (1) including multiple runs to quantify variance, and (2) randomly shuffling the tasks to investigate the effect of task order. Through these experiments, we make two observations that expose the fragility of current methods: First, agent evaluation is inherently noisy in complex environments and on multi-step tasks, and stacking a self-improving loop on top can further amplify this noise. Second, the agent's improvement is highly dependent on task order. Prior works often adopt default orderings that impose an implicit curriculum, acting as a hidden prerequisite for success.
  To better understand this fragility, we manually examine the agents' memory and hypothesize that task and environment underspecification contribute to this fragility. We validate this hypothesis by incorporating information that enables better specification, such as detailed rubrics and environment feedback, into the memory construction process. While this added information partially closes the performance degradation in previous experiments, significant gaps still remain, suggesting that other uncharacterized factors contribute to this fragility. Looking ahead, our work advocates for more rigorous evaluation protocols for self-improving agents by reporting results across multiple runs and stress-testing them under challenging conditions. Moreover, our findings on underspecification call for systems and interfaces that enable effective human oversight, preventing agents from failing in unforeseeable ways.

</details>
